DSA in Java — Interview Questions

Read notes

Answered the way you would say it in the room — first person, with an example every time. Tap a question to reveal the answer. Levels: Basic, Intermediate, Advanced.

Part 1 — Foundations

1. How to Approach a DSA Problem

BasicI've just read you the problem. What do you do in the first few minutes?

So I don't start coding. The first thing I do is ask questions until the problem is unambiguous, and then I write two or three concrete examples, including at least one edge case. Problems are often left vague on purpose, and the interviewer wants to see me notice that.

Let me show you with a quick example. Say you ask me "find two numbers that add up to a target." My questions would be:

  • Input: Is the array sorted? Can it be empty? Negatives? Duplicates? How big is n?
  • Output: Indices or values? Exactly one answer, or could there be none or many? What do I return if there's none?
  • Constraints: Can I use the same element twice? Can I modify the input?

Then I'd write examples like [2,7,11,15], 9 → [0,1], [3,3], 6 → [0,1] for duplicates, and [3,2,4], 6 → [1,2], which catches the "used the same index twice" bug before I've written a line.

The reason I do this is that the most expensive mistake in an interview is solving the wrong problem for twenty minutes. The size of n in particular tells me what complexity they're expecting. So in practice I spend around five minutes here, and I say my assumptions out loud so the interviewer can correct me early.

Interviewers look for: questions about empty input, duplicates, negatives and the size of n. Candidates who jump straight into code get marked down even when the code works.

BasicWhy would you describe a brute-force solution if you already know it's too slow?

So the brute force proves I understand the problem, it gives me a correct fallback if I run out of time, and it's the thing I optimise from. Most good solutions come from looking at the brute force and asking "what work am I repeating?"

Let me show you with a quick example: "does this array contain a duplicate?"

// Brute force: compare every pair. O(n^2) time, O(1) space.
static boolean hasDuplicateBrute(int[] a) {
    for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++)
        for (int j = i + 1; j < a.length; j++)
            if (a[i] == a[j]) return true;
    return false;
}

// The inner loop is really asking "have I seen a[i] before?"
// That's a lookup, so a HashSet makes it O(1). O(n) time, O(n) space.
static boolean hasDuplicate(int[] a) {
    Set<Integer> seen = new HashSet<>();
    for (int x : a) if (!seen.add(x)) return true;   // add returns false if present
    return false;
}
// [4,1,7,1] -> true, [1,2,3] -> false

What happens here is that saying "the inner loop is just a lookup" is literally the bottleneck step, and naming it points straight at the fix. I say the brute force and its Big-O in one or two sentences. I don't code it unless the interviewer asks.

So in practice: brute force out loud, name the bottleneck, then trade memory for time or sort first. And if time runs low, a clean working O(n²) beats a broken O(n).

IntermediateWalk me through Two Sum, from brute force to the optimal solution.

So the brute force tries every pair, which is O(n²). The optimisation comes from noticing the inner loop only asks one question: "does target - nums[i] exist somewhere I've already been?" A HashMap answers that in O(1) on average, so one pass is enough.

public int[] twoSum(int[] nums, int target) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();   // value -> index
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        int need = target - nums[i];
        if (seen.containsKey(need)) return new int[]{seen.get(need), i};
        seen.put(nums[i], i);                        // store AFTER checking
    }
    return new int[]{-1, -1};                        // no pair
}

Let me dry-run it on [2,7,11,15], target 9. At i = 0 I need 7, the map is empty, so I store 2 → 0. At i = 1 I need 2, it's in the map, so I return [0, 1].

One detail I always call out is storing after checking. If I stored first, then for [3,2,4] with target 6, the 3 would find itself and return [0,0]. Checking first means I only ever pair with an earlier index. It also handles [3,3] correctly, because the second 3 finds the first.

That's O(n) time and O(n) space. So I'm trading memory for time, and I'd say that out loud.

Interviewers look for: the follow-up "what if the array is sorted?" Then I'd use two pointers from both ends, which is O(n) time and O(1) space.

IntermediateHow do the constraints tell you what complexity to aim for?

So I work backwards from roughly 10⁸ simple operations per second. If n is up to 10⁵, an O(n²) solution is 10¹⁰ operations, which is far too slow, so they're expecting O(n log n) or O(n). The constraint on n is one of the biggest hints in the problem, and a lot of people ignore it.

Let me show you the mental table I use:

n up toTargetUsually means
10O(n!)All permutations, backtracking
20O(2ⁿ · n)All subsets, bitmask DP
500O(n³)Triple loop, interval DP
5,000O(n²)Double loop, 2D DP
10⁶O(n log n)Sort, heap, binary search
10⁹ and upO(log n) or O(1)Math, binary search on the answer

What happens in practice is that if I see "n ≤ 20", I immediately think about subsets or bitmasks, because 2²⁰ is only about a million. If I see "n ≤ 10⁵", I know my double loop won't pass, so I start thinking about sorting, a hash map or a sliding window.

If the interviewer hasn't given constraints, I ask for them. And if they say "assume it's large", I aim for O(n log n) or better and say so.

IntermediateYou're stuck and don't see the optimal approach. What do you do?

So I keep talking and go back to something concrete. I solve a tiny example by hand and watch what my brain does, because that's often the algorithm. Then I walk through a short list of patterns and ask whether each one fits.

The questions I literally ask myself:

  • What if the input were sorted? That suggests two pointers or binary search.
  • What if I had a HashMap of everything I've seen? That turns repeated lookups into O(1).
  • Is it a contiguous range? Then a sliding window or prefix sums.
  • Is it "top k" or "smallest so far"? Then a heap.
  • Am I solving the same subproblem again? Then memoisation or DP.

I also simplify the problem: 1D before 2D, no duplicates before duplicates, k = 1 before general k. Solving the simple version often shows me the general one.

One thing I've learned is that silence hurts more than being stuck. If I say "I'm thinking a heap might work here because we only care about the smallest k, but I'm not sure how to handle updates", the interviewer can nudge me. They give hints to people who are visibly reasoning.

So in practice, if time is running out, I code the brute force cleanly and explain how I'd improve it.

Interviewers look for: whether you can use a hint. Taking a hint well and running with it is scored positively, not negatively.

AdvancedYour solution uses a HashMap. What if the input is 500 GB and doesn't fit in memory?

So the in-memory HashMap stops working, and I'd switch to an approach that processes the data in chunks that do fit. The two standard tools are hash partitioning and external sorting. Both turn one huge problem into many small ones I can solve with the normal algorithm.

Let me show you with "find duplicate IDs in a 500 GB file" on a machine with 8 GB of RAM:

Pass 1 (partition): stream the file once.
  for each id: write it to bucket file  (hash(id) % 100)
  -> 100 files of about 5 GB each.
  Equal ids always land in the SAME file.

Pass 2 (solve each piece): for each bucket file,
  load it into a HashSet and report duplicates.
  Each piece fits in memory, so this is the normal O(n) algorithm.

The reason this is correct is that duplicates can only be in the same bucket, so I never need to compare across files. The total cost is two sequential passes over the disk, and sequential I/O is what disks are good at.

The alternative is an external merge sort: sort chunks in memory, write them out, then k-way merge them with a heap. After sorting, duplicates are adjacent. That's O(n log n) but also works for things like "top k" and "merge sorted logs".

So in practice I'd also ask whether an approximate answer is fine. If it is, a Bloom filter or a count-min sketch gives me a result in fixed memory, with a small, known error rate.

Interviewers look for: "equal keys go to the same partition", and awareness that one skewed bucket might still be too big, so you'd re-partition it with a different hash.

2. Big-O & Complexity

BasicWhat is Big-O, and why do we drop constants?

So Big-O describes how the running time or memory of an algorithm grows as the input grows. It ignores the machine and the exact seconds and keeps only the shape of the growth. That's why 3n + 5 is O(n) and n² + 100n is O(n²).

Let me show you with a quick example:

// Two separate passes: n + n = 2n steps -> still O(n)
static int maxMinusMin(int[] a) {
    int max = Integer.MIN_VALUE, min = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
    for (int x : a) max = Math.max(max, x);
    for (int x : a) min = Math.min(min, x);
    return max - min;
}

What happens here is that doubling the input doubles the work, whether I do one pass or two. The constant 2 doesn't change the shape, it just depends on hardware and implementation details that Big-O isn't trying to measure. And the smaller terms vanish because when n is a million, n² is a trillion while 100n is only a hundred million.

Formally, O is an upper bound, Ω is a lower bound, and Θ is a tight bound. In interviews people say "Big-O" but mean the tight worst-case bound, and that's what I give unless asked otherwise.

So in practice I use Big-O to compare approaches, but I don't pretend constants never matter. An O(n) solution with a HashMap can be slower than an O(n log n) sort on small inputs because of boxing and hashing overhead.

BasicWhat is the time complexity of this code? 
int ops = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
    for (int j = 1; j < n; j *= 2)
        ops++;

So this is O(n log n). The outer loop runs n times, and the inner loop doubles j each time, so it only runs about log₂ n times. Nested loops multiply, so I get n × log n.

Let me verify with numbers. For n = 8 the inner loop runs for j = 1, 2, 4, which is 3 times, so the total is 8 × 3 = 24. For n = 1024 it's 1024 × 10 = 10,240. I ran both and got exactly those counts.

The rules I apply to any snippet like this:

  • Sequential blocks add. Nested blocks multiply.
  • If the loop variable is multiplied or divided by a constant, that loop is logarithmic.
  • Different inputs get different letters. Looping over a string of length n inside a list of length m is O(n·m), not "O(n)".
  • Hidden loops count: list.contains, substring, and String += all cost more than O(1).

So I always look at what the loop variable does, not just how many loops there are.

Interviewers look for: the trap version where the inner loop depends on the outer one, like for (i = 1; i < n; i *= 2) for (j = 0; j < i; j++). That's 1 + 2 + 4 + … which sums to under 2n, so it's O(n), not O(n log n). For n = 1024 it's exactly 1023 steps.

IntermediateWhat does "amortised O(1)" mean? Use ArrayList.add as the example.

So amortised means averaged over a long sequence of operations. An individual add can be O(n) when the backing array is full and has to be copied, but that happens so rarely that the average cost per add stays constant.

Let me show you with a quick simulation of ArrayList's growth rule: start at capacity 10 and grow by 1.5x when full.

long copies = 0; int cap = 10, size = 0;
for (int k = 0; k < 1_000_000; k++) {
    if (size == cap) {             // full: copy everything into a bigger array
        copies += size;
        cap = cap + (cap >> 1);    // grow by 1.5x
    }
    size++;
}
// copies = 2,430,972 for 1,000,000 adds -> about 2.4 copies per add

What happens here is that each resize copies a lot, but the resizes get further and further apart. The total copy work is a geometric series, bounded by a constant times n, so each add costs O(1) on average.

The key detail is multiplying the capacity. If it grew by a fixed +10 slots each time, I'd copy 10 + 20 + 30 + … + n, which is O(n²) total, so each add would be O(n) on average.

So in practice amortised O(1) is fine for throughput, but a single call can still be slow. If I know the size up front, I pass it to the constructor, new ArrayList<>(n), and skip the resizes entirely.

Interviewers look for: the difference between amortised and average-case. Amortised is a guarantee over any sequence. Average-case, like HashMap's O(1), depends on the input being well-distributed.

IntermediateWhat's the space complexity of a recursive function that creates no objects?

So it's not O(1). Every active call holds a stack frame with its parameters and locals, so the space is the maximum recursion depth. A recursion that goes n levels deep uses O(n) space even if it never allocates anything.

// O(n) time AND O(n) space: n frames are alive at the deepest point
static long sumRec(int[] a, int i) {
    if (i == a.length) return 0;
    return a[i] + sumRec(a, i + 1);
}

// Same job, O(1) space
static long sumIter(int[] a) {
    long total = 0;
    for (int x : a) total += x;
    return total;
}

The rule I use is: time is the total number of calls times the work per call, and space is the height of the recursion tree. Those are different numbers. Naive Fibonacci is the classic example. It makes about 2ⁿ calls, but at any moment only one root-to-leaf path is on the stack, so the space is only O(n).

For trees the same rule gives O(h), where h is the height. That's O(log n) for a balanced tree, but O(n) for a skewed one that's really a linked list.

So in practice I always mention the stack when I give space for recursive code. And on a very deep input, like a 10⁶-node linked list, I'd switch to iteration, because Java will throw StackOverflowError long before it runs out of heap.

IntermediateQuicksort is O(n²) in the worst case. Why do people still call it O(n log n)?

So people are quoting its average case. With a good pivot, each partition splits the array roughly in half, giving log n levels of O(n) work. The O(n²) case only happens when the pivot is always the smallest or largest element, and randomised pivots make that astronomically unlikely.

Let me show you the cases side by side:

AlgorithmBestAverageWorstWorst happens when
QuicksortO(n log n)O(n log n)O(n²)Pivot is always the min or max
HashMap getO(1)O(1)O(log n) in Java 8+Many keys collide in one bucket
Insertion sortO(n)O(n²)O(n²)Input is reverse sorted
Merge sortO(n log n)O(n log n)O(n log n)Never worse, but O(n) extra space

What happens with a naive "always pick the first element" pivot on an already sorted array is that every partition peels off just one element. That gives n levels instead of log n, so n + (n−1) + … = O(n²).

This is also why Java uses different sorts. Arrays.sort(int[]) uses dual-pivot quicksort, which is fast in practice. Arrays.sort(Object[]) uses TimSort, which is stable and guaranteed O(n log n).

So in practice, when nobody specifies, I give the worst case and mention the average if it's different. For hashing and quicksort I always mention both.

AdvancedDerive the complexity of merge sort from its recurrence.

So merge sort's recurrence is T(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(n): two halves, plus a linear merge. There are log₂ n levels in the recursion tree, and each level does O(n) total work, so it's O(n log n).

Let me show you the recursion tree, because that's how I'd explain it on a whiteboard:

Level 0:  1 call  on n          -> merge work n
Level 1:  2 calls on n/2        -> 2 * n/2 = n
Level 2:  4 calls on n/4        -> 4 * n/4 = n
  ...
Level k:  2^k calls on n/2^k    -> n
Stops when n/2^k = 1, so k = log2 n levels.

Total = n per level * log2 n levels = O(n log n)

What happens is that the number of calls doubles at each level while the size of each call halves, so every level costs the same. That's the "tie" case of the Master Theorem. For T(n) = aT(n/b) + f(n) I compare f(n) with n^(log_b a). Here that's n^(log₂ 2) = n, which matches the merge work, so the answer is n × log n.

The same tool covers the others quickly. Binary search is T(n/2) + O(1), a tie at 1, so O(log n). A tree traversal is 2T(n/2) + O(1), where the leaves win, so O(n). Quickselect on average is T(n/2) + O(n), where the root wins, so O(n).

So in practice I rarely quote the theorem by name. I just say "log n levels, O(n) per level" and draw the tree, which is the reasoning the theorem packages up.

Interviewers look for: the space answer too. Merge sort on an array needs O(n) extra for the merge buffer, plus O(log n) stack.

3. Recursion & the Call Stack

BasicWhat is recursion? Write factorial recursively.

So recursion is a method solving a problem by calling itself on a smaller version of the same problem, until the problem is small enough to answer directly. Every recursive method needs a base case that stops it, and a recursive case that shrinks the input toward that base case.

static long factorial(int n) {
    if (n <= 1) return 1;              // base case
    return n * factorial(n - 1);       // smaller input, then combine
}
// factorial(5)  = 120
// factorial(20) = 2432902008176640000
// factorial(21) = -4249290049419214848   (overflowed long!)

The way I write it is the "leap of faith". I assume factorial(n - 1) already works and only ask how to build my answer from it. I don't try to trace every level in my head while writing it.

My checklist is: state the base case first, make sure every call shrinks the input, and decide exactly what the method returns. If I forget the base case or don't shrink the input, it recurses until the stack overflows.

One gotcha I call out is that factorial(21) silently overflows long and comes out negative. If they wanted bigger values I'd use BigInteger, or they probably want the answer modulo 10⁹ + 7.

That's O(n) time and O(n) stack space. So in practice I'd write factorial as a loop, but it's the cleanest way to show the two-part structure.

BasicWhat actually happens on the call stack when a recursive method runs?

So each thread in the JVM has a call stack. Every method call pushes a new frame holding that call's parameters, locals and return address. When the method returns, its frame is popped and the caller continues from where it paused. Recursion just means many frames for the same method.

Let me trace factorial(3):

Winding (push):
  main          paused at  x = factorial(3)
  factorial(3)  paused at  3 * factorial(2)
  factorial(2)  paused at  2 * factorial(1)
  factorial(1)  base case, returns 1

Unwinding (pop):
  factorial(2)  computes 2 * 1 = 2, returns 2
  factorial(3)  computes 3 * 2 = 6, returns 6
  main          x = 6

What happens here is that no multiplication happens on the way down. Each frame is paused mid-expression, waiting. All the work happens during the unwind. That's why the maximum depth, four frames here, is the space cost.

Each frame has its own copy of n, which is why the three calls don't interfere with each other. Objects passed in are different, though. The reference is copied, but the object is shared, so if I mutate a list in a deeper call, the caller sees it. That's exactly what backtracking relies on.

So in practice this model explains both the O(depth) space cost and StackOverflowError. The stack is a fixed size, and if depth goes too far, it runs out.

IntermediateWhat's the complexity of naive recursive Fibonacci, and how would you fix it?

So naive Fibonacci is O(2ⁿ) time, because each call branches into two, and the same subproblems get recomputed over and over. The space is only O(n), because the stack only holds one path of the tree at a time.

static long fib(int n) {                       // O(2^n) time, O(n) stack
    if (n <= 1) return n;
    return fib(n - 1) + fib(n - 2);
}

static long fibMemo(int n, long[] memo) {      // O(n) time, O(n) space
    if (n <= 1) return n;
    if (memo[n] != 0) return memo[n];          // already solved
    return memo[n] = fibMemo(n - 1, memo) + fibMemo(n - 2, memo);
}

static long fibIter(int n) {                   // O(n) time, O(1) space
    if (n == 0) return 0;
    long prev = 0, cur = 1;
    for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++) { long next = prev + cur; prev = cur; cur = next; }
    return cur;
}
// fib(10) makes 177 calls; fib(30) makes 2,692,537.
// fibMemo(50) = fibIter(50) = 12586269025, instantly.

What happens in the naive version is that fib(5) computes fib(3) twice and fib(2) three times, and it gets exponentially worse. Memoisation caches each answer the first time, so each fib(k) is computed once.

The iterative version is the bottom-up DP. I only ever need the last two values, so I keep two variables instead of an array.

So in practice I'd go naive, then memo, then iterative. That's the standard path from recursion to dynamic programming.

Interviewers look for: the time-vs-space distinction. Saying "O(2ⁿ) space" for naive Fibonacci is a very common slip. Space is the tree's height, not its size.

IntermediateWhat causes a StackOverflowError, and does tail recursion fix it in Java?

So a StackOverflowError happens when recursion goes deeper than the thread's stack can hold. That's usually a missing base case, or a correct recursion on a very deep input. And no, tail recursion doesn't help in Java, because the JVM doesn't do tail-call optimisation.

// Tail-recursive: the call is the very last thing
static long sumTail(int n, long acc) {
    if (n == 0) return acc;
    return sumTail(n - 1, acc + n);
}

// Same logic as a loop
static long sumIter(int n) {
    long acc = 0;
    while (n > 0) { acc += n; n--; }
    return acc;
}
// sumTail(100, 0)       = 5050
// sumTail(1_000_000, 0) -> StackOverflowError
// sumIter(1_000_000)    = 500000500000

A tail call means there's nothing left to do after it returns, so in theory the frame could be reused. Scala with @tailrec and Kotlin's tailrec do that, but Java still pushes a new frame for every call, so a million-deep tail recursion still overflows.

The default stack is roughly 512 KB to 1 MB per thread. I can raise it with -Xss, but that's treating the symptom.

So in practice the fix is converting to iteration. Tail recursion becomes a plain loop, where the parameters turn into loop variables. General recursion, like DFS, uses an explicit ArrayDeque as a stack, which lives on the heap and can grow much larger.

IntermediateImplement pow(x, n) faster than O(n).

So I'd use fast exponentiation. xⁿ is (x^(n/2))², times one extra x if n is odd. That halves the problem each call, so it's O(log n) time and O(log n) stack.

static double myPow(double x, long n) {
    if (n < 0) return 1 / myPow(x, -n);   // long: -Integer.MIN_VALUE overflows an int
    if (n == 0) return 1;
    double half = myPow(x, n / 2);         // solve half the problem ONCE
    return (n % 2 == 0) ? half * half : half * half * x;
}
// myPow(2, 10)  = 1024.0
// myPow(2, -2)  = 0.25
// myPow(-1, Integer.MIN_VALUE) = 1.0

What happens for 2¹⁰ is 10 → 5 → 2 → 1 → 0, so five calls instead of ten multiplications. For n around a billion it's about 30 calls.

There are two gotchas I call out. First, I must store half and reuse it. If I wrote myPow(x, n/2) * myPow(x, n/2), that's two calls per level, and the recursion tree has n leaves again, so it falls back to O(n).

Second, negative exponents. If n were an int and equal to Integer.MIN_VALUE, then -n overflows back to itself and the recursion never ends. Taking the parameter as long avoids that.

So in practice the same squaring idea shows up in modular exponentiation and matrix power for Fibonacci in O(log n).

Interviewers look for: the Integer.MIN_VALUE edge case and the "call it once" detail. Missing either is the usual way people fail this one.

AdvancedHow do you convert a general recursive DFS into an iterative one?

So I replace the call stack with my own explicit stack. I push the starting state, then loop: pop, process, and push the next states. The only subtle part is the push order, because a stack reverses things.

Let me show you with a preorder traversal of a binary tree:

record Node(int val, Node left, Node right) {}

static void preorderRec(Node n, List<Integer> out) {
    if (n == null) return;
    out.add(n.val);
    preorderRec(n.left, out);
    preorderRec(n.right, out);
}

static List<Integer> preorderIter(Node root) {
    List<Integer> out = new ArrayList<>();
    if (root == null) return out;
    Deque<Node> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    stack.push(root);
    while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
        Node cur = stack.pop();
        out.add(cur.val);
        if (cur.right != null) stack.push(cur.right);   // right first...
        if (cur.left != null) stack.push(cur.left);     // ...so left pops first
    }
    return out;
}
// Tree 1(2(4,5),3): both give [1, 2, 4, 5, 3]

What happens here is that each stack entry is a "call I still need to make." I push right before left because the stack is last-in, first-out, so left gets processed first, just like the recursive version.

It gets harder when the recursive version does work after a child returns, like postorder or backtracking. Then I push a small state object, such as a node plus a "visited" flag or the index of the next child, so I know where to resume.

So in practice I write the recursive version first because it's clearer. I convert when depth could hit around 10⁴ or more, like a skewed tree or a flood fill on a 1000×1000 grid.

Interviewers look for: the push-order reasoning, and knowing that the explicit stack lives on the heap, so it isn't limited by -Xss.

4. Java Toolkit for DSA

BasicWhy is String immutable in Java? And why must you compare strings with equals?

So a String can never change after it's created. Every "modification" returns a new object. Java made it immutable for safety and sharing: strings can be pooled, cached, used as HashMap keys and shared across threads without anyone being able to change them underneath you.

Let me show you with a quick example.

String s = "hi";
String t = s;
s = s + "!";                 // builds a NEW string; t still points at the old one
// t = "hi", s = "hi!"

String u = "hello";
u.toUpperCase();             // result thrown away, u is unchanged
// u = "hello"

String a = "java", b = "java", c = new String("java");
a == b;          // true   both literals share one object in the string pool
a == c;          // false  new String makes a separate object
a.equals(c);     // true   same characters
a == c.intern(); // true   intern() returns the pooled copy

The reasons I'd give are:

  • String pool: identical literals can share one object only because nobody can change it.
  • Hashing: String caches its hashCode. If a key could change after being put in a HashMap, it would sit in the wrong bucket forever.
  • Security: file paths, URLs and class names can't be changed after they've been validated.
  • Thread safety: immutable objects need no locking.

== compares references, meaning "is this the same object?" It happens to work for literals because of the pool, which is exactly why it's a trap. So in practice I always use equals for content.

Interviewers look for: the cost side of immutability. Every change is a copy, which leads straight into the StringBuilder question.

BasicString vs StringBuilder vs StringBuffer. When would you use each?

So String is immutable, StringBuilder is a mutable, growable char buffer, and StringBuffer is the old synchronized version of StringBuilder. For building a string in a loop I use StringBuilder, because += in a loop copies the whole string every time, which is O(n²) total.

// O(n^2): each += copies everything built so far
String s = "";
for (int i = 0; i < 100_000; i++) s += 'x';

// O(n): append is amortised O(1)
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
for (int i = 0; i < 100_000; i++) sb.append('x');
String result = sb.toString();

// Useful methods
StringBuilder b = new StringBuilder();
for (int i = 0; i < 5; i++) b.append(i).append(',');
b.setLength(b.length() - 1);   // drop last comma -> "0,1,2,3,4"
b.reverse();                   // in place        -> "4,3,2,1,0"

When I timed these on my machine, the += loop took about 480 ms and the StringBuilder loop about 2 ms, for the same 100,000-character result. That's the O(n²) vs O(n) difference showing up.

A single line like "a" + x + "b" is fine. The compiler handles that efficiently. The problem is only repeated concatenation in a loop.

MutableThread-safeUse when
StringNoYes (immutable)Keys, constants, values you pass around
StringBuilderYesNoBuilding strings in one thread (almost always)
StringBufferYesYes (synchronized)Legacy code; rarely needed today

So in practice I reach for StringBuilder any time I'm building output, reversing, or doing backtracking on characters. One cost to remember: insert(0, …) and deleteCharAt(0) are O(n) because they shift everything.

IntermediateWhy use ArrayDeque instead of Stack for a stack?

So java.util.Stack is a legacy class that extends Vector. Every method is synchronized, which is pointless overhead in single-threaded code, and because it is a Vector, you can insert into the middle, which breaks the whole idea of a stack. The Javadoc itself recommends Deque.

Stack<Integer> st = new Stack<>();
st.push(1); st.push(2); st.push(3);
System.out.println(st);        // [1, 2, 3]   top is at the END
st.add(0, 99);                 // legal! -> [99, 1, 2, 3]

Deque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();
dq.push(1); dq.push(2); dq.push(3);
System.out.println(dq);        // [3, 2, 1]   top is at the FRONT
dq.peek();                     // 3

// Empty behaviour
new ArrayDeque<Integer>().poll();   // null
new ArrayDeque<Integer>().pop();    // NoSuchElementException
new Stack<Integer>().pop();         // EmptyStackException

What I like about ArrayDeque is that it's one class for both stack and queue. It's a resizable circular array, so push, pop, offer and poll are all O(1), and it's faster than LinkedList because there's no node object per element.

One gotcha I've hit is that the iteration order differs. Printing a Stack shows the top last, while printing an ArrayDeque used as a stack shows the top first. So if I convert a stack to a list for output, I check which order I'm getting.

The other gotcha is that ArrayDeque rejects null with a NullPointerException, because poll uses null to mean "empty". If I need a null marker, like in level-order traversal, I use a sentinel object instead.

So in practice I declare it as Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>() and stick to push, pop and peek.

Interviewers look for: "Stack extends Vector" and "synchronized". The follow-up is usually "then when is LinkedList better?" and the honest answer is almost never, unless you need nulls or to remove elements during iteration.

IntermediateHashMap vs LinkedHashMap vs TreeMap. How do you choose?

So I choose by what order I need. HashMap has no defined order and is O(1) on average. LinkedHashMap is a HashMap plus a doubly linked list, so it keeps insertion order (or access order) at the same O(1). TreeMap is a red-black tree, so keys stay sorted and every operation is O(log n), but I get "nearest key" queries.

for (String w : List.of("pear", "apple", "fig", "banana")) { ...put(w, w.length()); }

HashMap        {banana=6, apple=5, pear=4, fig=3}   // some order, may change on resize
LinkedHashMap  {pear=4, apple=5, fig=3, banana=6}   // insertion order
TreeMap        {apple=5, banana=6, fig=3, pear=4}   // sorted by key

TreeMap<Integer, String> plans = new TreeMap<>(Map.of(100, "basic", 500, "pro", 1000, "enterprise"));
plans.floorEntry(750);    // 500=pro     largest key <= 750
plans.ceilingKey(750);    // 1000        smallest key >= 750
plans.floorKey(50);       // null        nothing qualifies
Orderget / putNull keyPick it for
HashMapNoneO(1) averageOne allowedCounting, lookups, the default
LinkedHashMapInsertion or accessO(1) averageOne allowedPredictable output, LRU cache
TreeMapSorted by keyO(log n)Not allowed (natural order)Floor/ceiling, ranges, sorted iteration

The reason I'd pick TreeMap is a question like "which price tier does 750 fall into?" or "what's the next free meeting slot after 10:30?" Those are floorKey and ceilingKey in O(log n), which a HashMap simply can't do.

One gotcha: the nearest methods return null when nothing qualifies, so unboxing straight into an int throws a NullPointerException.

So in practice HashMap is my default, LinkedHashMap when order matters or for an LRU cache with removeEldestEntry, and TreeMap when I need order-based queries.

IntermediateWhy does map1.get(k) == map2.get(k) sometimes return false when both values are 1000?

So it's the Integer caching trap. Collections store Integer objects, and == on two Integers compares references, not values. Java caches Integer objects from −128 to 127, so small values happen to be the same object and == works. Larger values are separate objects, so == is false.

Integer p1 = 127, p2 = 127, q1 = 128, q2 = 128;
p1 == p2;            // true   same cached object
q1 == q2;            // false  two different objects
q1.equals(q2);       // true
q1.intValue() == q2; // true   one side is int, so the other unboxes

Map<Character, Integer> m1 = new HashMap<>(), m2 = new HashMap<>();
m1.put('a', 1000); m2.put('a', 1000);
m1.get('a') == m2.get('a');               // false
m1.get('a').equals(m2.get('a'));          // true
Objects.equals(m1.get('a'), m2.get('a')); // true, and null-safe

int x = m1.get('z');   // NullPointerException: unboxing null

What makes this nasty is that tests with small numbers pass. I've seen this exact bug in "are these two strings anagrams using two frequency maps?" It works on "abc" and fails once a count goes above 127.

There are two related traps I watch for. Unboxing a missing key crashes, so I use getOrDefault. And on a List<Integer>, remove(1) removes index 1, while remove(Integer.valueOf(1)) removes the value 1. From [1, 2, 3], the first gives [1, 3].

So in practice, any time a value comes out of a collection, I compare with equals or unbox to int first.

Interviewers look for: the range −128 to 127 and the word "reference". Bonus if you mention that the upper bound of the cache can be raised with a JVM flag, which is exactly why you should never rely on it.

AdvancedWrite a Comparator to sort people by age descending, then name ascending. Why not (a, b) -> a - b?

So I'd chain the built-in comparator factories, which are readable and can't overflow. The subtraction trick breaks because a - b can overflow an int and flip the sign, which silently gives a wrong order.

record Person(String name, int age) {}

people.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(Person::age).reversed()
                      .thenComparing(Person::name));
// [Bob 30, Zoe 30, Adam 25]

// The overflow bug
int[][] pts = {{-2_000_000_000, 0}, {2_000_000_000, 0}, {5, 0}};
Arrays.sort(pts, (x, y) -> x[0] - y[0]);
// [[5, 0], [2000000000, 0], [-2000000000, 0]]   WRONG
Arrays.sort(pts, (x, y) -> Integer.compare(x[0], y[0]));
// [[-2000000000, 0], [5, 0], [2000000000, 0]]   right

// -2_000_000_000 - 2_000_000_000 evaluates to 294967296 (positive!)

What happens with subtraction is that the true difference is −4 billion, which doesn't fit in an int, so it wraps to +294,967,296. The comparator then claims the negative number is larger. With typical test values it works fine, which is what makes it dangerous.

A Comparator returns negative if the first argument comes first, zero if they're equal, and positive otherwise. It also has to be consistent. If compare says two different items are equal, a TreeSet or TreeMap will treat them as duplicates and drop one, which is a real bug I've seen with "sort by score" sets.

For heaps I write the same thing inline, for example a PriorityQueue of {id, distance} ordered by distance, then id:

PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((x, y) ->
    x[1] != y[1] ? Integer.compare(x[1], y[1]) : Integer.compare(x[0], y[0]));
// offer {3,2}, {1,5}, {2,2} -> polls {2,2}, {3,2}, {1,5}

So in practice I use Integer.compare or comparingInt every time, and thenComparing for tie-breaks.

Interviewers look for: the difference between Comparable (the class's natural order, compareTo) and Comparator (an external order you pass in), and that .reversed() applies to the whole chain before it.

5. Bit Manipulation

BasicHow do you check if a number is odd, or a power of two, using bits?

So for odd, I check the lowest bit: (n & 1) == 1. For a power of two, I use n > 0 && (n & (n - 1)) == 0, because a power of two has exactly one 1 bit, and n & (n - 1) clears the lowest 1 bit.

static boolean isOdd(int n)        { return (n & 1) == 1; }
static boolean isPowerOfTwo(int n) { return n > 0 && (n & (n - 1)) == 0; }

isOdd(-7);                   // true
-7 % 2;                      // -1, so "n % 2 == 1" says -7 is NOT odd
isPowerOfTwo(64);            // true   1000000 & 0111111 = 0
isPowerOfTwo(96);            // false  1100000 & 1011111 = 1000000
isPowerOfTwo(0);             // false  (guarded by n > 0)
isPowerOfTwo(Integer.MIN_VALUE); // false (guarded by n > 0)

What happens with n - 1 is that subtracting 1 flips the lowest 1 bit to 0 and every 0 below it to 1. ANDing with the original wipes out exactly that lowest bit. If nothing is left, there was only one bit.

The n > 0 guard matters. Zero has no bits, so the AND gives 0 and it would wrongly pass. Integer.MIN_VALUE is a single sign bit, so it would pass too without the guard.

One Java gotcha: I always put brackets around the AND. & binds weaker than ==, so n & 1 == 0 doesn't even compile.

So in practice (n & 1) is also the safe odd check for negatives, where n % 2 == 1 fails.

BasicWhat's the difference between >> and >>>, and how are negative numbers stored?

So Java stores ints in 32-bit two's complement. To get −x, you flip every bit of x and add 1, so ~x == -x - 1. >> is the signed shift: it copies the sign bit in from the left, so negatives stay negative. >>> is the unsigned shift: it always fills with 0.

-20       = 11111111111111111111111111101100
-20 >> 2  = 11111111111111111111111111111011  = -5   (sign bit copied)
-20 >>> 28 = 1111                              = 15   (zeros shifted in)
-1 >> 1   = -1
-1 >>> 1  = 2147483647

~5 = -6,   ~5 + 1 = -5
Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE) = -2147483648   (no positive twin!)
1 << 31 = -2147483648,  1 << 32 = 1,  1L << 32 = 4294967296

The reason two's complement is used is that addition works the same for positive and negative numbers and there's only one zero. The range is lopsided, −2³¹ to 2³¹ − 1, so Integer.MIN_VALUE has no positive version and Math.abs returns it unchanged.

A place >>> actually matters is the binary search midpoint. (lo + hi) / 2 overflows for large indices, and (lo + hi) >>> 1 fixes it because it treats the overflowed sum as unsigned. With lo = 2,000,000,000 and hi = 2,100,000,000, the naive version gives −97,483,648, and both >>> 1 and lo + (hi - lo) / 2 give 2,050,000,000.

So in practice I also remember that shift amounts are taken mod 32 for int, which is why 1 << 32 is 1. For 64-bit masks I write 1L << k.

Interviewers look for: ">> keeps the sign". Also that >> on a negative odd number rounds toward negative infinity, while / 2 rounds toward zero: -7 >> 1 is −4 but -7 / 2 is −3.

IntermediateEvery element appears twice except one. Find it in O(n) time and O(1) space.

So I XOR everything together. XOR has two properties that make this work: x ^ x = 0 and x ^ 0 = x, and it's commutative, so order doesn't matter. Every pair cancels out and only the single number survives.

static int singleNumber(int[] nums) {
    int x = 0;
    for (int v : nums) x ^= v;
    return x;
}
// [4, 1, 2, 1, 2] -> 4 ^ (1 ^ 1) ^ (2 ^ 2) = 4 ^ 0 ^ 0 = 4

Let me trace it: 0 ^ 4 = 4, 4 ^ 1 = 5, 5 ^ 2 = 7, 7 ^ 1 = 6, 6 ^ 2 = 4. The intermediate values look random, but the pairs cancel by the end.

The obvious alternatives are a HashSet, where I add and remove as I go (O(n) space), or sorting and checking neighbours (O(n log n)). XOR beats both on the constraints given.

The same trick solves "missing number": the array has 0..n with one missing, so I XOR every index and every value together, plus n. Everything present appears twice and cancels. For [3, 0, 1] that gives 2.

static int missingNumber(int[] nums) {
    int x = nums.length;
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) x ^= i ^ nums[i];
    return x;
}

So in practice, whenever I hear "appears twice" or "pairs cancel", XOR is the first thing I try.

Interviewers look for: the follow-up "what if every other element appears three times?" XOR doesn't cancel triples, so you count each bit position mod 3 instead.

IntermediateCount the number of 1 bits in an integer.

So the neat approach is Brian Kernighan's trick: n &= n - 1 clears the lowest set bit each time, so the loop runs once per 1 bit, not once per bit position. In real code I'd just call Integer.bitCount.

static int countBits(int n) {
    int c = 0;
    while (n != 0) {          // != 0, not > 0, so negatives work
        n &= n - 1;           // drop the lowest 1 bit
        c++;
    }
    return c;
}
// countBits(13) = 3     (1101)
// countBits(-1) = 32    (all ones)
// Integer.bitCount(13) = 3, Integer.bitCount(-1) = 32

Let me trace 13: 1101 & 1100 = 1100, then 1100 & 1011 = 1000, then 1000 & 0111 = 0. Three iterations, three bits.

The naive version checks n & 1 and shifts right 32 times. That's fine too, but there's a trap: if I shift with >> on a negative number, the sign bit keeps being copied in, so the loop never reaches 0. I'd have to use >>>.

Both versions are O(1) because an int has a fixed 32 bits. But Kernighan's is O(number of set bits), which is nicer for sparse values.

A common follow-up is "count bits for every number from 0 to n." There I'd use DP: bits[i] = bits[i >> 1] + (i & 1), which is O(n) total instead of O(n log n).

So in practice I mention Integer.bitCount, which the JIT turns into a single CPU instruction on most machines, and then write Kernighan's to show I understand it.

IntermediateGenerate all subsets of an array using a bitmask.

So with n elements there are 2ⁿ subsets, and each one maps to an n-bit number where bit i says whether element i is in or out. I loop mask from 0 to 2ⁿ − 1 and build each subset from its bits.

static List<List<Integer>> subsets(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    List<List<Integer>> out = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << n); mask++) {
        List<Integer> cur = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
            if ((mask & (1 << i)) != 0) cur.add(nums[i]);
        out.add(cur);
    }
    return out;
}
// [1,2,3] -> [[], [1], [2], [1, 2], [3], [1, 3], [2, 3], [1, 2, 3]]

Let me show you the mapping for a few masks: mask 5 is 101, so bits 0 and 2 are set, giving [1, 3]. Mask 6 is 110, giving [2, 3]. Mask 7 is everything.

That's O(n · 2ⁿ) time, because there are 2ⁿ masks and I check n bits for each. The output itself is that size, so I can't do better.

Compared to the recursive take-or-skip version, the bitmask version is iterative with no stack and very easy to get right. The recursive one is easier to prune, though. If I only want subsets whose sum is at most k, backtracking can stop a branch early, while the bitmask loop visits every mask.

So in practice bitmasks are my go-to when n ≤ 20, which is also the size where bitmask DP shows up, like "visit all cities" with dp[mask][city].

Interviewers look for: 1 << n as the count, and awareness that for n ≥ 31 an int mask overflows, so you'd need long, although 2³¹ subsets is already too many to generate.

AdvancedEvery element appears twice except two. Find both in O(n) time and O(1) space.

So XOR-ing everything gives me a ^ b, not the two numbers. The trick is that a ^ b is non-zero, so they differ in at least one bit. I pick the lowest such bit with xor & -xor and use it to split the array into two groups. Each group then contains exactly one of the singles, and every pair lands in the same group, so the plain XOR trick works on each group.

static int[] singleNumberIII(int[] nums) {
    int xor = 0;
    for (int v : nums) xor ^= v;          // xor = a ^ b
    int diff = xor & -xor;                // lowest bit where a and b differ
    int a = 0, b = 0;
    for (int v : nums) {
        if ((v & diff) == 0) a ^= v;      // group without that bit
        else b ^= v;                      // group with that bit
    }
    return new int[]{a, b};
}
// [1, 2, 1, 3, 2, 5] -> [5, 3]

Let me trace it. The XOR of everything is 3 ^ 5 = 011 ^ 101 = 110, which is 6. The lowest set bit of 6 is 2 (010). Numbers without bit 1 set are 1, 1 and 5, which XOR to 5. Numbers with it are 2, 3 and 2, which XOR to 3.

Why does x & -x isolate the lowest bit? Because -x = ~x + 1. Flipping every bit and adding 1 makes the carry ripple up to exactly the lowest 1 of x, and every bit above it is the opposite of x, so the AND keeps only that one bit. This is the same operation Fenwick trees use.

So in practice this is two passes, O(n) time, and three variables of space.

Interviewers look for: the explanation of why pairs can't be split across groups (both copies have the same bit), and the x & -x identity. The edge case where xor is Integer.MIN_VALUE still works, because MIN_VALUE & -MIN_VALUE is MIN_VALUE, a single bit.

Part 2 — Linear Data Structures

6. Arrays & Strings

BasicWhy is array access O(1), and when would you use an int[] over an ArrayList?

So an array is one contiguous block of memory where every slot is the same size. The address of element i is just base + i × elementSize, one multiply and one add, no matter how big the array is. That's why indexing is O(1).

Let me show you the trade-offs I think about:

int[]ArrayList<Integer>
SizeFixed at creationGrows (amortised O(1) add)
ElementsRaw ints, 4 bytes eachReferences to Integer objects (about 16 bytes each, plus the reference)
Cache behaviourValues sit next to each otherValues scattered on the heap
Lengtha.length (field)list.size() (method)
Insert in middleManual shift, O(n)add(i, x), still O(n)

What that means in practice is that an int[] is noticeably faster and smaller. It avoids boxing, where each int is wrapped in an object, and the CPU can prefetch contiguous memory.

So I use int[] when I know the size up front, like a DP table, a frequency array or a prefix-sum array. I use ArrayList when the size grows as I go, like collecting results. And if I'm inserting or removing at the front a lot, neither is right. I'd use an ArrayDeque.

Interviewers look for: "contiguous" and "same-size elements", and knowing that inserting at index 0 is O(n) for both because everything shifts.

BasicCheck if a string is a palindrome, ignoring case and non-alphanumeric characters.

So I'd use two pointers, one at each end, moving inward. I skip anything that isn't a letter or digit and compare the rest case-insensitively. That's O(n) time and O(1) space, with no cleaned-up copy of the string.

static boolean isPalindrome(String s) {
    int left = 0, right = s.length() - 1;
    while (left < right) {
        char l = s.charAt(left), r = s.charAt(right);
        if (!Character.isLetterOrDigit(l)) { left++; continue; }
        if (!Character.isLetterOrDigit(r)) { right--; continue; }
        if (Character.toLowerCase(l) != Character.toLowerCase(r)) return false;
        left++; right--;
    }
    return true;
}
// "A man, a plan, a canal: Panama" -> true
// "race a car" -> false
// "" -> true

What happens here is that each pointer only ever moves inward, so together they take at most n steps. The continue after skipping lets me re-check the loop condition, so I can't run past the other pointer.

The simpler version is to build a cleaned lowercase string with a StringBuilder, then compare it with its reverse. That's easier to read, but it uses O(n) extra space. I'd mention it and then write the two-pointer version.

So in practice I also clarify up front what counts as "alphanumeric". Some interviewers mean ASCII only, and Character.isLetterOrDigit also accepts Unicode letters.

IntermediateCheck if two strings are anagrams. What are the options and their costs?

So two strings are anagrams if they have the same characters with the same counts. For lowercase English letters, the best answer is a 26-slot count array: count up for one string, down for the other, and check everything is zero. That's O(n) time and O(1) space.

static boolean isAnagram(String s, String t) {
    if (s.length() != t.length()) return false;
    int[] freq = new int[26];
    for (int i = 0; i < s.length(); i++) {
        freq[s.charAt(i) - 'a']++;
        freq[t.charAt(i) - 'a']--;
    }
    for (int f : freq) if (f != 0) return false;
    return true;
}
// ("listen", "silent") -> true, ("rat", "car") -> false
ApproachTimeSpaceWhen
Sort both, compareO(n log n)O(n) for the char arraysQuick to write, any charset
int[26] countsO(n)O(1)Lowercase letters only
int[128]O(n)O(1)Any ASCII
HashMap<Character,Integer>O(n)O(k) distinct charsFull Unicode

The reason c - 'a' works is that a char is really a number, its character code. So 'a' becomes 0 and 'z' becomes 25. The length check at the top is a free early exit.

So in practice I ask about the character set first, because that decides between the array and the map. And if I did use two maps, I'd compare them with equals, never by comparing values with ==, because of the Integer cache.

IntermediateRotate an array to the right by k steps, in place.

So the clean O(1)-space trick is three reversals: reverse the whole array, then reverse the first k elements, then reverse the rest. And I take k %= n first, because rotating by n is the same as not rotating.

static void rotate(int[] nums, int k) {
    int n = nums.length;
    k %= n;
    reverse(nums, 0, n - 1);
    reverse(nums, 0, k - 1);
    reverse(nums, k, n - 1);
}
static void reverse(int[] a, int i, int j) {
    while (i < j) { int t = a[i]; a[i++] = a[j]; a[j--] = t; }
}

Let me trace it with [1,2,3,4,5,6,7] and k = 3:

reverse all        [7,6,5,4,3,2,1]
reverse first 3    [5,6,7,4,3,2,1]
reverse last 4     [5,6,7,1,2,3,4]   done

Why it works: after reversing everything, the last k elements are at the front, just backwards, and the first n − k are at the back, also backwards. Reversing each block puts them the right way round.

The alternatives are copying into a new array with out[(i + k) % n] = nums[i], which is O(n) extra space, or rotating by one step k times, which is O(n·k) and too slow.

So in practice this is O(n) time and O(1) space. The edge case I always test is k larger than n, like [1,2] with k = 5, which gives [2,1] only because of the k %= n.

Interviewers look for: the k %= n line. Forgetting it causes an index-out-of-bounds crash on the second reverse.

IntermediateProduct of array except self, without using division.

So the answer at index i is the product of everything to its left times everything to its right. I compute the left products in one forward pass into the output array, then sweep backwards with a running right product. That's O(n) time and O(1) extra space, not counting the output.

static int[] productExceptSelf(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    int[] out = new int[n];
    out[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) out[i] = out[i - 1] * nums[i - 1];  // left products
    int right = 1;
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        out[i] *= right;          // times everything to the right
        right *= nums[i];
    }
    return out;
}
// [1,2,3,4]      -> [24, 12, 8, 6]
// [-1,1,0,-3,3]  -> [0, 0, 9, 0, 0]

Let me trace [1,2,3,4]. After the forward pass, out is [1, 1, 2, 6], which is the product of everything left of each index. Going backwards, right takes the values 1, 4, 12, 24, so out becomes [24, 12, 8, 6].

The reason they ban division is zeros. "Total product divided by nums[i]" breaks on a zero, and you'd need special cases for one zero versus two. The prefix/suffix version handles zeros naturally, which you can see in the second example.

So in practice this "left pass, right pass" shape shows up a lot, for example in trapping rain water and in candy distribution.

AdvancedFind the length of the longest substring without repeating characters.

So I'd use a sliding window. I keep a window [left, right] with no repeats and remember the last index where I saw each character. When the new character was already seen inside the window, I jump left just past that earlier occurrence. Each character is visited once, so it's O(n).

static int lengthOfLongestSubstring(String s) {
    int[] last = new int[128];            // last index of each ASCII char
    Arrays.fill(last, -1);
    int best = 0, left = 0;
    for (int right = 0; right < s.length(); right++) {
        char c = s.charAt(right);
        if (last[c] >= left) left = last[c] + 1;   // repeat inside window
        last[c] = right;
        best = Math.max(best, right - left + 1);
    }
    return best;
}
// "abcabcbb" -> 3, "bbbbb" -> 1, "pwwkew" -> 3, "abba" -> 2

The subtle part is the last[c] >= left check. On "abba", when I reach the final 'a', its last index is 0, but left has already moved to 2 because of the 'b's. Without the check, left would jump backwards to 1 and I'd return 3, which is wrong. That's the test case I always run.

The brute force checks every substring for repeats, which is O(n³), or O(n²) with a set. The window works because once [left, right] is valid, every smaller window inside it is too, so I never need to move left backwards.

So in practice this is O(n) time and O(1) space for ASCII. For full Unicode I'd swap the array for a HashMap<Character, Integer>. If the input were a huge stream, this still works in one pass, because I only keep the window's metadata, not the string.

Interviewers look for: the "abba" case, and the general sliding-window template: expand right, shrink left until valid, record the answer.

7. Hashing

BasicWhy is HashMap get and put O(1) on average? When is it not?

So a HashMap turns the key into an array index with a hash function, then jumps straight to that bucket, just like array indexing. As long as keys are spread evenly and the table isn't too full, each bucket holds only a few entries, so a lookup does a constant amount of work regardless of the map's size.

Let me show you with the three pieces:

key "cat"
  -> hashCode()           98262
  -> spread (h ^ h>>>16)  98263
  -> index = hash & 15   7        (16 buckets)
  -> walk bucket 7, compare with equals()

What can break it is collisions: two keys landing in the same bucket. A few collisions are normal and cheap. The bad case is many keys in one bucket, from a poor hashCode or a deliberate attack. Then that bucket becomes a long chain and a lookup is O(n). Since Java 8, a bucket that grows past 8 entries turns into a red-black tree, so the worst case drops to O(log n).

Real collisions are easy to produce: "Aa".hashCode() and "BB".hashCode() are both 2112.

The other cost is resizing. When the map is 75% full it doubles and re-inserts everything, which is O(n) for that one put. But that's amortised O(1), like ArrayList.

So in practice I say "O(1) average, O(log n) worst case per bucket in Java 8+". And I remember that hashing a String key is O(length) the first time, so for long keys that's not free.

Interviewers look for: "average, not guaranteed", and the words collision, load factor and resize. That leads straight into the internals question.

IntermediateHow does Java's HashMap work internally?

So internally it's an array of buckets, Node<K,V>[] table, where each bucket is a linked list of nodes, or a red-black tree if it gets long. The capacity is always a power of two, starting at 16, and the table is created lazily on the first put.

Let me walk through a put(key, value):

  • Hash: call key.hashCode(), then spread it with h ^ (h >>> 16). That mixes the high bits into the low bits, because with 16 buckets only the lowest 4 bits pick the bucket.
  • Index: (capacity - 1) & hash. With a power-of-two capacity that's the same as hash % capacity, but faster and never negative.
  • Search the bucket: for each node, check node.hash == hash && (node.key == key || key.equals(node.key)). If found, replace the value. If not, append a new node.
  • Treeify: if that bucket's chain grows past the threshold of 8 nodes and the table has at least 64 buckets, convert it to a red-black tree. If the table is smaller than 64, it resizes instead. A tree shrinks back to a list at 6.
  • Resize: if size exceeds capacity × 0.75 (the load factor), double the capacity and redistribute.
// Capacity 16 -> 32: each entry stays at i or moves to i + 16,
// decided by a single bit of the hash (hash & 16)
"cat"  idx16 = 7    idx32 = 23   (bit set: moves by 16)
"dog"  idx16 = 13   idx32 = 29   (bit set: moves by 16)
"owl"  idx16 = 5    idx32 = 5    (bit clear: stays)
"emu"  idx16 = 12   idx32 = 12   (bit clear: stays)

get follows the same path: hash, index, then walk the bucket using hash and equals. The stored hash is compared first because comparing ints is much cheaper than calling equals.

A few details I'd add: one null key is allowed and goes to bucket 0, iteration order isn't defined and can change after a resize, and it's not thread-safe. Concurrent puts can lose updates, so across threads I use ConcurrentHashMap.

So in practice, if I know I'll store about a million entries, I pre-size it, HashMap.newHashMap(1_000_000) on Java 19+, to skip all the intermediate resizes.

Interviewers look for: the numbers 16, 0.75, 8 and 64, "power of two so the index is a bit mask", and the reason for treeification: protecting against collision attacks. The follow-up is usually the equals/hashCode contract.

IntermediateWhat happens if you override equals but not hashCode?

So hash-based collections break. The contract says if two objects are equal, they must have the same hashCode. If I only override equals, two equal objects still get different identity-based hash codes, land in different buckets, and the map never even calls my equals.

final class Point {
    final int x, y;
    Point(int x, int y) { this.x = x; this.y = y; }
    @Override public boolean equals(Object o) {
        return o instanceof Point p && p.x == x && p.y == y;
    }
    // no hashCode!
}
Set<Point> set = new HashSet<>();
set.add(new Point(1, 2));
set.contains(new Point(1, 2));   // false (almost always)

// Fix: add  @Override public int hashCode() { return Objects.hash(x, y); }
// Or just use a record, which generates both:
record P(int x, int y) {}
// set.contains(new P(1, 2)) -> true

The rules I keep in mind are: equal objects must have equal hash codes, but unequal objects may share a hash code. That's just a collision, and it's allowed. Both methods should use the same fields.

A related bug is mutating a key after putting it in the map. I've seen this with a List as a key: I put [1, 2], then added 3 to the same list. Now get with the list returns null, and so does get(List.of(1, 2)), because the entry is sitting in the bucket for the old hash. The map still says size 1, but the entry is unreachable.

So in practice my keys are immutable: Strings, boxed numbers, records, or an encoded long like (long) row * cols + col. And I never use int[] as a key, because arrays use identity equality.

Interviewers look for: the direction of the contract (equal implies same hash, not the reverse), and the mutable-key bug.

IntermediateGroup a list of words into anagram groups.

So the idea is to give every word a canonical key that's the same for all its anagrams, then group by that key in a HashMap. The simplest key is the word's letters sorted.

static Map<String, List<String>> groupAnagrams(String[] strs) {
    Map<String, List<String>> groups = new HashMap<>();
    for (String s : strs) {
        char[] k = s.toCharArray();
        Arrays.sort(k);
        groups.computeIfAbsent(new String(k), key -> new ArrayList<>()).add(s);
    }
    return groups;
}
// ["eat","tea","tan","ate","nat","bat"]
// -> {aet=[eat, tea, ate], ant=[tan, nat], abt=[bat]}

computeIfAbsent creates the list the first time a key appears and returns the existing one after that, so there's no "if not present, put" dance.

The cost with sorted keys is O(n · k log k), where n is the number of words and k is the maximum length. If words are long, I'd use a count-based key instead: count the 26 letters and use Arrays.toString(count) as the key. That's O(n · k), because building the key is linear.

One gotcha: I can't use the char[] or an int[] directly as the key. Arrays don't override equals and hashCode, so two identical arrays are different keys. Converting to a String fixes that.

So in practice this "canonical key + grouping map" pattern also solves things like grouping shifted strings or grouping points by slope.

IntermediateCount the subarrays whose sum equals k.

So I use prefix sums with a HashMap. The sum of a subarray (j, i] is prefix[i] - prefix[j]. I want that to equal k, so at each position I ask: how many earlier prefixes equal currentSum - k? A map from prefix sum to count answers that in O(1).

static int subarraySum(int[] nums, int k) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> prefixCount = new HashMap<>();
    prefixCount.put(0, 1);                 // the empty prefix
    int sum = 0, count = 0;
    for (int x : nums) {
        sum += x;
        count += prefixCount.getOrDefault(sum - k, 0);
        prefixCount.merge(sum, 1, Integer::sum);
    }
    return count;
}
// [1,1,1], k=2 -> 2
// [1,2,3], k=3 -> 2      ([1,2] and [3])
// [3,4,7,2,-3,1,4,2], k=7 -> 4

Let me trace [1,2,3], k = 3. Sums are 1, 3, 6. At sum 3, I look up 0, which is the seeded empty prefix, so I count 1 (the subarray [1,2]). At sum 6, I look up 3, which I've seen once, so I count another (the subarray [3]). Total 2.

The put(0, 1) seed is the line people forget. It represents "a subarray starting at index 0". Without it, [1,2] wouldn't be counted.

The reason a sliding window doesn't work here is negative numbers. With negatives, growing the window can make the sum smaller, so I can't decide which side to move. Prefix sums don't care about signs.

So in practice this is O(n) time and O(n) space, versus O(n²) for checking every subarray.

Interviewers look for: the seed and the "why not sliding window?" answer. Also, if values are large, the running sum should be a long.

AdvancedFind the longest run of consecutive integers in an unsorted array, in O(n).

So the obvious answer is to sort, which is O(n log n). To get O(n) I put everything in a HashSet, and then I only start counting from numbers that begin a run, meaning x - 1 isn't in the set. From each start I count upward.

static int longestConsecutive(int[] nums) {
    Set<Integer> set = new HashSet<>();
    for (int x : nums) set.add(x);
    int best = 0;
    for (int x : set) {
        if (set.contains(x - 1)) continue;   // not the start of a run
        int len = 1;
        while (set.contains(x + len)) len++;
        best = Math.max(best, len);
    }
    return best;
}
// [100, 4, 200, 1, 3, 2] -> 4   (1, 2, 3, 4)
// [0,3,7,2,5,8,4,6,0,1]  -> 9   (0..8)

It looks like a nested loop, so it looks like O(n²). But the continue is what makes it linear. The inner while only runs from the start of each run, and every number belongs to exactly one run, so across the whole loop each number is visited by the while at most once. That's at most n outer iterations plus n inner steps, so O(n) total.

Without that check, an input like 1..n would count from every number: n + (n−1) + … which is O(n²).

I iterate over the set, not the original array, so duplicates like the two zeros don't cause repeated work.

So in practice this is O(n) average time and O(n) space. On small inputs sorting might actually be faster because of hashing and boxing overhead, and I'd say that. An alternative is Union-Find, joining x with x + 1, but the set version is simpler.

Interviewers look for: a clear amortised argument for why the nested loop is O(n). Just asserting it isn't enough.

8. Linked Lists

BasicArrayList vs LinkedList. When would you actually use a LinkedList?

So an ArrayList is a dynamic array: O(1) access by index, amortised O(1) add at the end, O(n) insert in the middle. A LinkedList is a doubly linked list: O(1) insert and remove if you already hold the node, but O(n) to reach index i. In practice ArrayList wins almost everywhere.

ArrayListLinkedList
get(i)O(1)O(n), walks from the nearer end
add at endO(1) amortisedO(1)
add/remove at frontO(n), shifts everythingO(1)
Insert in middleO(n) shiftO(n) to find the spot, then O(1)
MemoryOne array of referencesA node object per element, plus two pointers

Let me show you why the table undersells it. I filled both with 100,000 Integers and read them with a for (int i…) list.get(i) loop. The ArrayList took about 13 ms. The LinkedList took about 8.7 seconds, because each get(i) walks the list, which makes the loop O(n²). That's an easy bug to write by accident.

Even when Big-O says LinkedList should win, like inserting in the middle, it often doesn't. Finding the spot is O(n) pointer-chasing, and scattered nodes are bad for the CPU cache, while ArrayList's shift is one fast memory copy.

So in practice I use ArrayList for lists and ArrayDeque for queues and stacks. The linked list as a concept matters a lot, though. It's the core of LRU caches, adjacency lists and hash-map buckets, where I hold node references directly.

Interviewers look for: "O(1) insert only if you already have the node", and the cache-locality point.

BasicReverse a singly linked list.

So I walk the list once with three pointers, prev, cur and next, and flip each node's arrow to point backwards. When cur falls off the end, prev is the new head. That's O(n) time and O(1) space.

static ListNode reverse(ListNode head) {
    ListNode prev = null, cur = head;
    while (cur != null) {
        ListNode next = cur.next;   // 1. save the rest
        cur.next = prev;            // 2. flip the arrow
        prev = cur;                 // 3. step prev forward
        cur = next;                 // 4. step cur forward
    }
    return prev;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5] -> [5,4,3,2,1],  null -> null

The order of those four lines is everything. If I flip cur.next before saving it, I've lost the rest of the list. So I always say "save, flip, step, step" out loud.

The recursive version reverses the rest first, then hooks the current node onto the end:

static ListNode reverseRec(ListNode head) {
    if (head == null || head.next == null) return head;
    ListNode newHead = reverseRec(head.next);
    head.next.next = head;      // the node after me now points back to me
    head.next = null;           // I become the tail
    return newHead;
}
// [1,2,3] -> [3,2,1]

The recursive one is elegant, but it's O(n) stack space, and on a 100,000-node list it can throw StackOverflowError.

So in practice I write the iterative version by default. I test it on an empty list, a single node and two nodes, because those are where pointer bugs show up.

IntermediateHow do you detect a cycle in a linked list, and find where it starts?

So I use Floyd's fast and slow pointers. Slow moves one step, fast moves two. If there's a cycle, fast eventually laps slow and they meet. If fast hits null, there's no cycle. That's O(n) time and O(1) space, versus O(n) space for a HashSet of visited nodes.

static ListNode detectCycle(ListNode head) {
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {
        slow = slow.next;
        fast = fast.next.next;
        if (slow == fast) {                  // they met inside the cycle
            ListNode p = head;
            while (p != slow) { p = p.next; slow = slow.next; }
            return p;                        // cycle start
        }
    }
    return null;                             // no cycle
}
// 3 -> 2 -> 0 -> -4 -> (back to 2)  returns the node 2
// 1 -> 2                             returns null

Why do they always meet? Once both are inside the cycle, the gap between them shrinks by exactly one each step, so fast can't jump over slow.

Why does the second phase find the start? Say the start is a steps from the head, and they meet b steps into the cycle. Slow walked a + b. Fast walked twice that, and the extra a + b is a whole number of laps. So walking a more steps from the meeting point also lands on the start. That's why a new pointer from the head and slow, moving one step each, meet exactly at the cycle start.

I compare nodes with == here, not values, because two different nodes can hold the same value.

So in practice the same slow/fast idea finds the middle of a list (slow is at the middle when fast reaches the end) and powers "find the duplicate number" on an array.

Interviewers look for: the loop condition fast != null && fast.next != null, which prevents a NullPointerException, and at least a sketch of the a + b proof.

IntermediateMerge two sorted linked lists.

So I use a dummy head node and a tail pointer. At each step I attach whichever list has the smaller front node, then advance. When one list runs out, I attach the rest of the other in one step. That's O(n + m) time and O(1) extra space, because I'm re-linking existing nodes, not copying.

static ListNode merge(ListNode a, ListNode b) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0), tail = dummy;
    while (a != null && b != null) {
        if (a.val <= b.val) { tail.next = a; a = a.next; }
        else                { tail.next = b; b = b.next; }
        tail = tail.next;
    }
    tail.next = (a != null) ? a : b;   // attach whatever is left
    return dummy.next;
}
// [1,2,4] + [1,3,4] -> [1,1,2,3,4,4]
// null + [0] -> [0]

The reason I use a dummy node is that without it, the first node is a special case. I'd need an if to decide which list provides the head. With the dummy, every node is attached the same way, and I just return dummy.next.

I use <= rather than < so that equal values come from the first list first. That keeps the merge stable, which matters when this is the merge step of merge sort on a linked list.

So in practice this is the building block for "merge k sorted lists". There I'd either merge in pairs (O(N log k)) or use a min-heap of the k front nodes, also O(N log k).

IntermediateRemove the nth node from the end of a list in one pass.

So I use two pointers with a fixed gap. I move fast n + 1 steps ahead of slow, then move both until fast falls off the end. At that point slow is right before the node to delete, so I can unlink it. A dummy node in front handles deleting the head.

static ListNode removeNthFromEnd(ListNode head, int n) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0);
    dummy.next = head;
    ListNode fast = dummy, slow = dummy;
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) fast = fast.next;   // gap of n + 1
    while (fast != null) { fast = fast.next; slow = slow.next; }
    slow.next = slow.next.next;                       // unlink the target
    return dummy.next;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5], n=2 -> [1,2,3,5]
// [1], n=1         -> []
// [1,2], n=2       -> [2]   (removes the head)

Let me trace the first example. fast starts at dummy and moves 3 steps to node 3. Then both move together until fast is null. That's 3 more steps, leaving slow at node 3. Its next is 4, the second from the end, and I skip it.

The reason for the gap of n + 1, not n, is that to delete a node in a singly linked list I need its predecessor. And the dummy node is why [1,2] with n = 2 works: the predecessor of the head is the dummy.

So in practice the two-pass version, counting the length and then walking to length - n, is also O(n) and perfectly fine. I mention it, but "one pass" is what they're asking to see.

Interviewers look for: the dummy node, and handling "remove the head" without a special case.

AdvancedDesign an LRU cache with O(1) get and put.

So I combine a HashMap with a doubly linked list. The map gives O(1) lookup from key to node. The list keeps nodes in recency order: most recent at the front, least recent at the back. On every access I move the node to the front, and when the cache is full I evict from the back. All of those are O(1) because the list is doubly linked and I already hold the node.

class LRUCache {
    private static class Node { int key, val; Node prev, next; Node(int k, int v) { key = k; val = v; } }
    private final int capacity;
    private final Map<Integer, Node> map = new HashMap<>();
    private final Node head = new Node(0, 0), tail = new Node(0, 0);   // sentinels

    LRUCache(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; head.next = tail; tail.prev = head; }

    int get(int key) {
        Node n = map.get(key);
        if (n == null) return -1;
        unlink(n); addFront(n);                  // now most recent
        return n.val;
    }
    void put(int key, int value) {
        Node n = map.get(key);
        if (n != null) { n.val = value; unlink(n); addFront(n); return; }
        if (map.size() == capacity) {
            Node lru = tail.prev;                // least recent is at the back
            unlink(lru); map.remove(lru.key);
        }
        n = new Node(key, value);
        map.put(key, n); addFront(n);
    }
    private void unlink(Node n) { n.prev.next = n.next; n.next.prev = n.prev; }
    private void addFront(Node n) { n.next = head.next; n.prev = head; head.next.prev = n; head.next = n; }
}
// capacity 2: put(1) put(2) get(1)=1 put(3) [evicts 2] get(2)=-1
//             put(4) [evicts 1] get(1)=-1 get(3)=3 get(4)=4

A few design choices I'd explain. The sentinel head and tail mean unlink and addFront never check for null. The node stores its key so that when I evict from the list, I know which map entry to remove. And it has to be doubly linked, because unlinking needs the previous node in O(1).

In production Java I'd just extend LinkedHashMap with access order, which is the same structure built in:

class LRU<K, V> extends LinkedHashMap<K, V> {
    private final int capacity;
    LRU(int capacity) { super(16, 0.75f, true); this.capacity = capacity; }  // true = access order
    @Override protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<K, V> e) { return size() > capacity; }
}
// put 1, put 2, get 1, put 3 -> keys [1, 3]

So in practice I'd mention that neither version is thread-safe. For a shared cache I'd wrap it with a lock or use a library like Caffeine.

Interviewers look for: why each piece is needed (map for lookup, doubly linked list for O(1) reordering), and the key stored in the node. The follow-up is often "now make it LFU", which needs a map of frequency to list.

9. Stacks

BasicWhat is a stack, and where have you actually used one?

So a stack is a collection where I only add and remove at one end, the top. The last thing I push is the first thing I pop, which is LIFO. Push, pop and peek are all O(1).

Let me show you with a quick example.

Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
stack.push(1);
stack.push(2);
stack.push(3);
System.out.println(stack);        // [3, 2, 1]  (top first)
System.out.println(stack.peek()); // 3, still there
System.out.println(stack.pop());  // 3, removed
System.out.println(stack);        // [2, 1]

The place I use a stack most is one I never write myself: the call stack. Every method call pushes a frame with its locals and return address, and returning pops it. That's why recursion works, and why very deep recursion throws StackOverflowError.

In code I write, I reach for a stack whenever the problem has nesting or "most recent first" behaviour. Matching brackets or HTML tags, undo history, the browser back button, evaluating postfix expressions, iterative DFS, and "next greater element" problems with a monotonic stack.

So my signal in an interview is: if the thing I opened last is the thing I need to close first, it's a stack.

BasicWhy do people say not to use java.util.Stack? What do you use instead?

So Stack is a legacy class that extends Vector. Every method is synchronized, which costs a lock even in single-threaded code, and because it's a Vector it exposes list methods that break the stack idea. The Java docs themselves recommend Deque, and I use ArrayDeque.

Let me show you with a quick example.

Stack<Integer> legacy = new Stack<>();
legacy.push(1); legacy.push(2); legacy.push(3);
System.out.println(legacy);   // [1, 2, 3]  (bottom first)
legacy.add(0, 99);            // allowed: insert under the bottom
System.out.println(legacy);   // [99, 1, 2, 3]

Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
stack.push(1); stack.push(2); stack.push(3);
System.out.println(stack);    // [3, 2, 1]  (top first)

What happens here is that Stack happily lets me insert at index 0, which no real stack should allow. It also iterates bottom to top, while ArrayDeque iterates from the top, and that difference has confused me when printing debug output.

One gotcha I've hit with ArrayDeque: push, pop and peek all work on the front. If I mix in offer or poll, which work on the other end, I've quietly built a queue. Also, peek() returns null on empty, while pop() throws NoSuchElementException. And it rejects null elements.

So in practice I always write Deque<T> stack = new ArrayDeque<>(), and stick to push, pop and peek.

IntermediateWrite a function to check if a string of brackets is valid.

So this is the textbook stack problem. The most recently opened bracket must be the first one closed, which is exactly LIFO. When I see an opener I push the closer I expect. When I see a closer, it has to match the top.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static boolean isValid(String s) {
    Deque<Character> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
        if (c == '(') stack.push(')');
        else if (c == '[') stack.push(']');
        else if (c == '{') stack.push('}');
        else if (stack.isEmpty() || stack.pop() != c) return false;
    }
    return stack.isEmpty();
}

isValid("({[]})");   // true
isValid("([)]");     // false, ] arrives while ) is expected
isValid("((");       // false, leftovers
isValid(")");        // false, nothing to match

Pushing the expected closer instead of the opener is a small trick I like, because the check becomes one comparison and I don't need a map.

The two edge cases people miss are both in there. A closer on an empty stack means invalid, so I check isEmpty() before popping. And after the loop, anything left on the stack is an unmatched opener, so I return stack.isEmpty(), not true.

It's O(n) time and O(n) space in the worst case, like a string of all openers. If there were only one bracket type, I'd drop the stack and just keep a counter.

Interviewers look for: both edge cases without prompting. A common follow-up is "only round brackets, O(1) space", which is the counter version.

IntermediateDesign a stack that returns the minimum element in O(1).

So the key insight is that a stack only ever changes at the top. The minimum of everything at or below a given element can't change while that element is still there. So I store the running minimum alongside each value.

Let me show you with a quick example.

class MinStack {
    private final Deque<int[]> stack = new ArrayDeque<>(); // {value, minSoFar}

    void push(int x) {
        int min = stack.isEmpty() ? x : Math.min(x, stack.peek()[1]);
        stack.push(new int[]{x, min});
    }
    void pop()   { stack.pop(); }
    int top()    { return stack.peek()[0]; }
    int getMin() { return stack.peek()[1]; }
}

// push 5, 2, 7, 1  -> getMin() = 1
// pop              -> getMin() = 2
