// pop, pop         -> getMin() = 5

What happens here is that when I pop the 1, the pair below it is {7, 2}, which already knows the min was 2 at that point. No searching and no recomputation, so every operation is O(1).

The cost is O(n) extra space, since every element carries its min. A common optimisation is a second stack that only pushes when the new value is less than or equal to the current min. The "or equal" matters. Without it, duplicates of the minimum break the pop logic.

So in an interview I write the pair version first because it's hard to get wrong, then mention the two-stack version as the space optimisation.

IntermediateYour recursive tree walk throws StackOverflowError on large input. How would you fix it?

So recursion uses the JVM call stack, which is small, typically enough for somewhere in the tens of thousands of frames depending on frame size and -Xss. A deep, skewed input blows past that. The fix is to manage my own stack on the heap, which is limited only by memory.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// recursive: one call frame per level of depth
static long sumRecursive(Node node) {
    long sum = node.val;
    for (Node child : node.children) sum += sumRecursive(child);
    return sum;
}

// iterative: the same walk with an explicit stack
static long sumIterative(Node root) {
    long sum = 0;
    Deque<Node> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    stack.push(root);
    while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
        Node node = stack.pop();
        sum += node.val;
        for (Node child : node.children) stack.push(child);
    }
    return sum;
}

// a chain 200,000 nodes deep:
// sumIterative -> 200000
// sumRecursive -> StackOverflowError

What happens here is that each "call" becomes a push, and each "return" becomes the loop picking the next item. Any recursive algorithm can be rewritten this way. When the recursion does work after the children return, like postorder, I push extra state, such as a "visited children" flag.

Raising -Xss also works, but it's a band-aid. It's a JVM-wide setting and it just moves the limit.

So in practice I keep recursion for balanced trees, where depth is about log n, and switch to an explicit stack when the depth depends on untrusted input, like parsing nested JSON or walking a file system.

Interviewers look for: that you know the heap-vs-stack memory difference. Also note that Java has no tail-call optimisation, so rewriting as tail recursion doesn't help.

AdvancedFor each element, find the next greater element to its right. Can you do better than O(n²)?

So the brute force scans right from every index, which is O(n²). The O(n) answer is a monotonic stack: I keep indices that are still waiting for a bigger value, and whenever a new value is bigger than the top, it's the answer for that top.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int[] nextGreater(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    int[] ans = new int[n];
    Arrays.fill(ans, -1);
    Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>(); // indices waiting for an answer
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        while (!stack.isEmpty() && nums[i] > nums[stack.peek()]) {
            ans[stack.pop()] = nums[i];
        }
        stack.push(i);
    }
    return ans;
}

nextGreater(new int[]{2, 1, 5, 3, 4, 1});  // [5, 5, -1, 4, -1, -1]

Walking through it: 2 waits, 1 waits on top of it. Then 5 arrives and pops both, so each gets 5. Next 3 waits, and 4 pops it. Then 1 waits on 4, and the loop ends. Whatever is still on the stack has no greater element, so it keeps -1.

The stack is always decreasing from bottom to top. That's the invariant. It looks like O(n²) because of the nested loop, but each index is pushed once and popped at most once, so the total work is O(n). That's amortised analysis, and I always say it out loud.

I store indices rather than values because many variants need distances. Daily Temperatures asks "how many days until warmer", which is i - index. The same pattern solves stock span and largest rectangle in a histogram.

Interviewers look for: the "each element pushed and popped once" argument. Follow-ups: circular array (loop twice with i % n), or next smaller (flip the comparison).

10. Queues & Deques

BasicWhat's the difference between a queue, a stack and a deque? How do you use them in Java?

So a queue is FIFO: add at the back, remove from the front, like a line at a counter. A stack is LIFO. A deque, the double-ended queue, lets me add and remove at both ends, so it can act as either one. In Java, ArrayDeque is my default implementation for all three.

Let me show you with a quick example.

Queue<String> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
q.offer("a"); q.offer("b"); q.offer("c");
q.poll();      // "a"  (first in, first out)
q.peek();      // "b"
q.clear();
q.poll();      // null on empty
q.remove();    // throws NoSuchElementException on empty

Each operation comes in two flavours. offer, poll and peek return a special value on failure. add, remove and element throw. I use the first set because an empty queue is usually a normal condition in my loop, not an error.

A few gotchas I've hit. ArrayDeque rejects null, so offer(null) throws NullPointerException. If I write int x = q.poll() on an empty Queue<Integer>, the unboxing of null throws too. And mixing ends: push adds at the front while offer adds at the back, so push plus poll is a stack, not a queue.

So in practice: queue for arrival order and BFS, stack for nesting, and a deque when I need both ends, like a sliding window.

BasicHow would you implement a fixed-size queue with an array?

So with a plain array, removing from the front would mean shifting every element left, which is O(n). The trick is a circular buffer: I keep a head index and a size, and wrap around with modulo so freed slots at the start get reused.

Let me show you with a quick example.

class CircularQueue {
    private final int[] data;
    private int head = 0, size = 0;

    CircularQueue(int capacity) { data = new int[capacity]; }

    boolean offer(int x) {
        if (size == data.length) return false;          // full
        data[(head + size) % data.length] = x;          // tail = head + size
        size++;
        return true;
    }
    Integer poll() {
        if (size == 0) return null;                     // empty
        int x = data[head];
        head = (head + 1) % data.length;
        size--;
        return x;
    }
}

// capacity 3: offer 1,2,3 -> true; offer 4 -> false (full)
// poll -> 1; offer 4 -> true (wraps into slot 0)
// poll x4 -> 2, 3, 4, null

What happens here is that after polling 1, slot 0 is free. The tail position is (1 + 2) % 3 = 0, so 4 goes there. No shifting, and every operation is O(1).

The reason I track size instead of a separate tail pointer is the classic ambiguity. With only head and tail, head == tail could mean empty or full. Using size removes that, without wasting one slot.

So this is the design behind ring buffers in logging, audio, and network I/O, and it's also what ArrayDeque does internally, except it grows instead of rejecting.

Interviewers look for: how you tell full from empty. This is LeetCode 622, Design Circular Queue.

IntermediateImplement a queue using two stacks. What's the complexity?

So I use an in stack for pushes and an out stack for pops. Pouring one stack into another reverses the order, which turns LIFO into FIFO. The key is that I only pour when out is empty.

Let me show you with a quick example.

class MyQueue {
    private final Deque<Integer> in = new ArrayDeque<>();
    private final Deque<Integer> out = new ArrayDeque<>();

    void push(int x) { in.push(x); }
    int pop()  { refill(); return out.pop(); }
    int peek() { refill(); return out.peek(); }
    boolean empty() { return in.isEmpty() && out.isEmpty(); }

    private void refill() {
        if (out.isEmpty()) {
            while (!in.isEmpty()) out.push(in.pop());   // reverses the order
        }
    }
}

// push 1, 2, 3; pop -> 1
// push 4; pop, pop, pop -> 2, 3, 4

Walking through it: after pushing 1, 2, 3, in has 3 on top. The first pop moves all three, so out has 1 on top, and it pops 1. Then I push 4 onto in. The next pops drain 2 and 3 from out first, and only then pour 4 across. Order is preserved.

Push is O(1). A single pop can be O(n) when it triggers a pour, but every element is moved from in to out exactly once in its life. So over any sequence of operations, pop is amortised O(1).

The bug I see most is pouring on every pop, even when out isn't empty. That interleaves new elements ahead of older ones and breaks FIFO.

Interviewers look for: the word "amortised" and why. The mirror question, "stack using queues", costs O(n) per push because you rotate the queue.

IntermediateArrayDeque or LinkedList as a queue: which would you pick and why?

So I pick ArrayDeque. Both give O(1) at both ends, but ArrayDeque is a growable circular array, so elements sit next to each other in memory. LinkedList allocates a node object per element with two extra pointers.

Let me show you with a quick comparison.

ArrayDequeLinkedList
Add/remove at endsO(1) amortisedO(1)
Memory per elementOne array slotA node: value, next, prev, header
Cache behaviourContiguous, fastPointer chasing
GC pressureLowOne object per add
Allows nullNoYes
Also a ListNoYes (index access is O(n))

The reason ArrayDeque wins in practice is the constant factor. The CPU prefetches contiguous memory, and there's no allocation per operation. The "amortised" part is the occasional resize, which doubles the array, same idea as ArrayList.

I'd only choose LinkedList if I genuinely needed null elements, or if I was removing from the middle through an iterator I already held. Both are rare.

So in practice, new ArrayDeque<>() is my queue, stack and deque, and I use PriorityQueue when order is by priority rather than arrival.

AdvancedFind the maximum of every window of size k in an array, in O(n).

So a heap would give O(n log k). For O(n) I use a monotonic deque of indices, with values decreasing from front to back. The front is always the current window's max.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int[] maxSlidingWindow(int[] nums, int k) {
    int[] result = new int[nums.length - k + 1];
    Deque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();   // indices, values decreasing
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        if (!dq.isEmpty() && dq.peekFirst() <= i - k) dq.pollFirst();        // left the window
        while (!dq.isEmpty() && nums[dq.peekLast()] <= nums[i]) dq.pollLast(); // can never win again
        dq.offerLast(i);
        if (i >= k - 1) result[i - k + 1] = nums[dq.peekFirst()];
    }
    return result;
}

maxSlidingWindow(new int[]{1, 3, -1, -3, 5, 3, 6, 7}, 3);  // [3, 3, 5, 5, 6, 7]

The insight is this: if a new value is bigger than an older one, the older one can never be a window max again, because the new value is bigger and stays in the window longer. So I throw it away from the back. The front leaves only when its index slides out.

That's why I need a deque: expired indices leave from the front, dominated ones leave from the back. Each index enters once and leaves once, so it's O(n) total, and O(k) space.

I store indices, not values, because I need to know when the front has expired. With duplicate values, storing values makes that check ambiguous.

AdvancedWhat if several threads produce and consume from the same queue?

So ArrayDeque isn't thread-safe, and wrapping it in synchronized blocks means writing my own wait and notify logic. I'd use java.util.concurrent. A BlockingQueue handles both the locking and the waiting: put blocks when full and take blocks when empty.

Let me show you with a quick example.

BlockingQueue<Integer> jobs = new ArrayBlockingQueue<>(100);

Thread producer = new Thread(() -> {
    try {
        for (int i = 1; i <= 5; i++) jobs.put(i);   // blocks if full
        jobs.put(-1);                                // poison pill: no more work
    } catch (InterruptedException e) { Thread.currentThread().interrupt(); }
});
Thread consumer = new Thread(() -> {
    try {
        while (true) {
            int job = jobs.take();                   // blocks if empty
            if (job == -1) break;
            System.out.println("processed " + job);
        }
    } catch (InterruptedException e) { Thread.currentThread().interrupt(); }
});
producer.start(); consumer.start();
// processed 1 ... processed 5

What I like here is the backpressure. A bounded queue means a fast producer can't fill the heap. It simply waits for the consumer. The "poison pill" is a common way to tell consumers to stop cleanly.

The options I choose between: ArrayBlockingQueue is bounded, with one lock. LinkedBlockingQueue has separate put and take locks, so it has more throughput, but it's unbounded by default, which is a memory risk. ConcurrentLinkedQueue is lock-free and never blocks, so it's good when threads shouldn't wait. PriorityBlockingQueue is for priority order.

So in practice, for a job pipeline I pick a bounded BlockingQueue, or just an ExecutorService, which uses one internally.

11. Heaps & Priority Queues

BasicWhat is a heap, and how is it stored?

So a heap is a complete binary tree with one ordering rule. In a min-heap every parent is less than or equal to its children, so the smallest value is always at the root. It is only partially ordered: siblings and cousins have no order between them.

Because it's complete (every level is full except the last, which fills from the left), there are no gaps. So I store it in a plain array, level by level, and find relatives with arithmetic instead of pointers.

index i:     parent = (i - 1) / 2
             left   = 2 * i + 1
             right  = 2 * i + 2

array  [1, 2, 3, 4, 9, 6, 7]

tree          1
            /   \
           2     3
          / \   / \
         4   9 6   7

Insert puts the value at the end of the array and "sifts up", swapping with its parent while it's smaller. Removing the min takes heap[0], moves the last element to the root, and "sifts down", swapping with the smaller child. Each moves along one root-to-leaf path, and a complete tree has height log n, so both are O(log n). Peek is O(1).

The array layout is a big practical win. No node objects, no pointers, and good cache locality.

So I think of a heap as "the cheapest structure that always knows the current minimum", not as a sorted collection.

Interviewers look for: the index formulas and the word "complete". A common trap is "is a heap a BST?" No: a heap only orders parent against child, not left against right.

BasicI added 5, 1, 4, 2, 3 to a PriorityQueue and printed it. Why isn't it sorted?

So toString and the iterator just walk the underlying heap array in index order. A heap only guarantees the root is the smallest. Only repeated poll() gives sorted order.

Let me show you with a quick example.

PriorityQueue<Integer> pq = new PriorityQueue<>();
for (int x : new int[]{5, 1, 4, 2, 3}) pq.offer(x);

System.out.println(pq);          // [1, 2, 4, 5, 3]   raw heap array

while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
    System.out.print(pq.poll() + " ");   // 1 2 3 4 5
}

What happens here is each offer sifts the value up only as far as it needs. The array ends up as a valid heap, with 1 at the root and every parent smaller than its children, but 5 and 3 are nowhere near sorted positions.

Here are the costs I keep in mind for PriorityQueue:

OperationCost
peek()O(1)
offer(), poll()O(log n)
remove(Object), contains()O(n), a linear scan
Build from a collectionO(n) heapify
IterationAny order

That remove(Object) line has bitten me. If I need to delete arbitrary elements often, or iterate in order, I'd switch to a TreeSet or TreeMap.

So if I need all items sorted once, I just sort. A heap is for when items keep arriving and I keep needing the current min.

IntermediateFind the kth largest element in an array. Why a min-heap and not a max-heap?

So I keep a min-heap of size k holding the k largest values seen so far. The top of that heap is the smallest of the top k, which is by definition the kth largest. It costs O(n log k) time and O(k) space.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int findKthLargest(int[] nums, int k) {
    PriorityQueue<Integer> heap = new PriorityQueue<>();   // min-heap
    for (int x : nums) {
        heap.offer(x);
        if (heap.size() > k) heap.poll();                  // evict the smallest
    }
    return heap.peek();
}

findKthLargest(new int[]{3, 2, 1, 5, 6, 4}, 2);   // 5

The reason it's a min-heap is eviction. When a new value arrives and the heap is over size, I want to throw out the weakest candidate, which is the smallest. A min-heap has it right on top. With a max-heap of everything I'd need O(n) space and k polls.

Compared to sorting, which is O(n log n), this wins when k is small. It also works on a stream, since I never need the whole array in memory. That's the answer when the interviewer says "what if the input doesn't fit in memory?"

If I have the whole array and want the best average, Quickselect is O(n) on average. It partitions like quicksort but only recurses into the side that contains index n - k. Its worst case is O(n²), so I'd shuffle or pick a random pivot.

Interviewers look for: O(n log k) versus O(n log n), and the streaming argument. Expect "can you do O(n)?" as the follow-up, which is Quickselect.

IntermediateHow do you make a max-heap in Java? What's wrong with (a, b) -> b - a?

So PriorityQueue is a min-heap by default, and I get a max-heap by passing a comparator. The safe way is Comparator.reverseOrder(). The subtraction lambda looks fine, but it overflows on large values and silently gives the wrong order.

Let me show you with a quick example.

PriorityQueue<Integer> bad = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> b - a);
bad.offer(1);
bad.offer(Integer.MIN_VALUE);
bad.peek();    // -2147483648   wrong, should be 1

PriorityQueue<Integer> good = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
// or (a, b) -> Integer.compare(b, a)

What happens here is that comparing MIN_VALUE with 1 computes Integer.MIN_VALUE - 1, which wraps around to 2147483647, a positive number. So the comparator says the opposite of the truth. It passes every small test and then fails on real data.

For objects I build comparators with the helper methods, which also read much better:

record Task(String name, int priority) {}

PriorityQueue<Task> tasks = new PriorityQueue<>(
    Comparator.comparingInt(Task::priority).reversed()
              .thenComparing(Task::name));
// offer email(1), deploy(5), alert(5)
// poll order: alert(5), deploy(5), email(1)

One more gotcha: if I change a field that the comparator reads while the object is inside the heap, the heap doesn't notice. I remove it, update it, and re-insert it.

So in practice I never subtract in a comparator. Integer.compare or the Comparator factories every time.

IntermediateWhy is building a heap O(n) and not O(n log n)?

So calling offer n times is O(n log n). Heapify is faster because it works bottom-up. I treat the array as a tree already and sift down every parent, from the last parent at n/2 - 1 back to the root. Most nodes are near the bottom, and they barely move.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static void heapify(int[] a) {
    for (int i = a.length / 2 - 1; i >= 0; i--) siftDown(a, i, a.length);
}

static void siftDown(int[] a, int i, int n) {
    while (true) {
        int smallest = i, l = 2 * i + 1, r = 2 * i + 2;
        if (l < n && a[l] < a[smallest]) smallest = l;
        if (r < n && a[r] < a[smallest]) smallest = r;
        if (smallest == i) return;
        int t = a[i]; a[i] = a[smallest]; a[smallest] = t;
        i = smallest;
    }
}

int[] a = {9, 4, 7, 1, 2, 6, 3};
heapify(a);   // [1, 2, 3, 4, 9, 6, 7]

The intuition for the math: half the nodes are leaves and do zero work. A quarter sit one level up and move at most 1 step. An eighth move at most 2 steps, and so on. The sum n/4 · 1 + n/8 · 2 + n/16 · 3 + … converges to about n. Only the single root pays the full log n.

Inserting one by one is the opposite. Each new item is a leaf that might sift all the way up, and half the items are leaves, so it's O(n log n).

In Java, new PriorityQueue<>(collection) uses heapify, so it's O(n). The same step is phase one of heap sort.

AdvancedDesign a structure that returns the median of a stream of numbers.

So I use two heaps. A max-heap low holds the smaller half and a min-heap high holds the larger half. I keep their sizes equal or let low have one extra. Then the median is either the top of low or the average of the two tops.

Let me show you with a quick example.

class MedianFinder {
    private final PriorityQueue<Integer> low = new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());
    private final PriorityQueue<Integer> high = new PriorityQueue<>();

    void addNum(int x) {
        low.offer(x);
        high.offer(low.poll());                                  // largest of low moves across
        if (high.size() > low.size()) low.offer(high.poll());    // rebalance sizes
    }
    double findMedian() {
        return low.size() > high.size() ? low.peek() : (low.peek() + high.peek()) / 2.0;
    }
}

// add 5 -> 5.0
// add 2 -> 3.5
// add 8 -> 5.0
// add 1 -> 3.5

The reason I always route through low first and then pass its max to high is that it keeps the ordering rule for free. Every number in low stays less than or equal to every number in high, without me comparing against both tops by hand.

addNum is O(log n), just a few heap operations. findMedian is O(1), and space is O(n).

The obvious alternatives are worse. Sorting on every query is O(n log n). Insertion into a sorted list is O(n) per add.

If the interviewer adds "and remove old numbers" (a sliding window median), heaps struggle because remove(Object) is O(n). There I'd use two TreeMap multisets, or lazy deletion with a map of pending removals.

Interviewers look for: the invariant stated clearly. Follow-up: "what if all numbers are in 0 to 100?", where a count array gives O(100) median queries.

Part 3 — Trees & Graphs

12. Binary Trees

BasicWhat's the difference between a full, complete, perfect and balanced binary tree?

So these words describe the tree's shape, and they matter because they decide the height, and most tree operations cost O(height). Let me give you each in one line, then say why I care.

TermMeaningWhy it matters
FullEvery node has 0 or 2 children, never 1Shows up in expression trees and Huffman trees
CompleteEvery level full except maybe the last, which fills from the leftFits in an array with no gaps, which is how heaps work
PerfectAll internal nodes have 2 children and all leaves are on one levelExactly 2h+1 − 1 nodes
BalancedAt every node, left and right heights differ by at most 1Height stays O(log n)
DegenerateEvery node has one childReally a linked list, height n − 1

I also keep depth and height straight. Depth counts edges from the root down to a node, so the root has depth 0. Height counts edges from a node down to its deepest leaf, so a leaf has height 0. One gotcha: LeetCode's "maximum depth" counts nodes, not edges, so it's height + 1.

The one that matters most in practice is balanced versus degenerate. The same 1,000,000 keys give a height of about 20 in a balanced tree and 999,999 in a degenerate one. That's the whole reason self-balancing trees exist.

So when I state a complexity for a tree algorithm, I say O(h), and then add "which is O(log n) if balanced, O(n) worst case".

Interviewers look for: complete versus full, which people mix up. Every perfect tree is both full and complete, but not the other way round.

BasicWalk me through the tree traversals. When would you use each one?

So there are three depth-first orders, named by when I visit the root relative to its children, plus level order, which is breadth-first. They all visit every node once, so each is O(n) time and O(h) space for the recursion.

Let me show you with a quick example.

//        1
//       / \
//      2   3
//     / \   \
//    4   5   6

static void preorder(TreeNode n, List<Integer> out) {
    if (n == null) return;
    out.add(n.val);             // root first
    preorder(n.left, out);
    preorder(n.right, out);
}
// preorder   [1, 2, 4, 5, 3, 6]   root, left, right
// inorder    [4, 2, 5, 1, 3, 6]   left, root, right
// postorder  [4, 5, 2, 6, 3, 1]   left, right, root
// level      [[1], [2, 3], [4, 5, 6]]

Here's how I pick. Preorder meets the parent before its children, so I use it to copy or serialize a tree. Inorder on a binary search tree gives sorted order, so it's for anything "kth smallest" or validation. Postorder finishes the children first, so it's for anything where a node needs its children's answers: height, diameter, or deleting a tree. Level order is for "per level" questions, like right side view, and for shortest distance from the root.

In practice, most tree problems I solve are really postorder. I write a helper that returns something about the subtree, and the parent combines the left and right answers.

So the question I ask myself is: does the node need information from above (preorder, pass it down as a parameter) or from below (postorder, return it up)?

IntermediateWrite level order traversal that returns each level as its own list.

So this is BFS with a queue. The one trick is to freeze the queue's size at the start of each level. That tells me exactly how many nodes belong to the current level before I start adding their children.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static List<List<Integer>> levelOrder(TreeNode root) {
    List<List<Integer>> levels = new ArrayList<>();
    if (root == null) return levels;
    Queue<TreeNode> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    queue.offer(root);
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int size = queue.size();              // freeze this level's size
        List<Integer> level = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
            TreeNode node = queue.poll();
            level.add(node.val);
            if (node.left != null)  queue.offer(node.left);
            if (node.right != null) queue.offer(node.right);
        }
        levels.add(level);
    }
    return levels;
}
// tree 1 / (2: 4, 5) / (3: -, 6)  ->  [[1], [2, 3], [4, 5, 6]]

What happens here is that at the start of each outer loop, the queue holds exactly one full level. The inner loop polls those nodes and offers their children, which become the next level.

The bug I see most often is writing for (int i = 0; i < queue.size(); i++). The size keeps growing as I add children, so levels blur together. Save it in a variable first.

It's O(n) time. Space is O(w), where w is the maximum width, which can be about n/2 for the bottom level of a perfect tree.

The same skeleton answers many variants: right side view (last node per level), zigzag (reverse every other level), average per level, and minimum depth (return at the first leaf).

Interviewers look for: the frozen size. Follow-up: "can you do right side view with DFS?", which works by visiting right first and recording the first node at each depth.

IntermediateFind the diameter of a binary tree.

So the diameter is the number of edges on the longest path between any two nodes, and that path doesn't have to pass through the root. Every path has one highest node where it bends, and the longest path bending at a node is its left height plus its right height. I compute that at every node in one postorder pass.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int best;

static int diameterOfBinaryTree(TreeNode root) {
    best = 0;
    height(root);
    return best;
}

// returns the number of nodes on the longest downward path
static int height(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return 0;
    int left = height(node.left);
    int right = height(node.right);
    best = Math.max(best, left + right);      // longest path bending here, in edges
    return 1 + Math.max(left, right);
}

// 1 / (2: 4, 5) / (3: -, 6)   ->  4   (4-2-1-3-6)

This is the pattern I call "return one thing, track another". The helper returns what the parent needs, the height, but it tracks the global answer in a field. The parent can't use a path that bends below it, because a path can't fork, so the two values really are different things.

The naive approach calls a separate height() at every node, which is O(n²) on a skewed tree. This version visits each node once, so it's O(n) time and O(h) space.

So once I see this shape I reuse it. Binary Tree Maximum Path Sum is the same code with sums and Math.max(0, …) to drop negative branches.

AdvancedFind the lowest common ancestor of two nodes in a binary tree.

So the LCA is the deepest node that has both p and q in its subtree, where a node counts as its own descendant. My recursion returns "what I found in this subtree": p, q, their LCA, or null. The first node where both the left and right calls return something is where the paths split, so that's the answer.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static TreeNode lowestCommonAncestor(TreeNode root, TreeNode p, TreeNode q) {
    if (root == null || root == p || root == q) return root;
    TreeNode left = lowestCommonAncestor(root.left, p, q);
    TreeNode right = lowestCommonAncestor(root.right, p, q);
    if (left != null && right != null) return root;   // p and q split here
    return left != null ? left : right;
}

//        1
//       / \
//      2   3
//     / \   \
//    4   5   6
// LCA(4, 5) = 2    LCA(4, 6) = 1    LCA(2, 5) = 2

The subtle case is LCA(2, 5). When the recursion reaches 2, it returns immediately without looking below. That's correct, because if 5 is under 2, then 2 is the LCA, and if it weren't, the other branch would report 5 and the split would happen higher.

That shortcut relies on an assumption: both nodes exist in the tree. If one might be missing, the function would wrongly return the one it found. In that case I do a full traversal that counts how many targets were found.

It's O(n) time and O(h) space. If the tree is a BST I'd use the ordering instead and walk down from the root in O(h), no recursion into both sides.

Interviewers look for: the "both must exist" assumption. Other follow-ups: nodes with parent pointers (it becomes intersection of two linked lists), or many LCA queries (binary lifting).

AdvancedHow would you serialize a binary tree to a string and rebuild it?

So I use preorder with a marker for null children. Preorder alone can't rebuild a tree, because many shapes share the same order. The null markers tell the reader exactly where each subtree ends, so reading the tokens in the same order rebuilds the same shape.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static String serialize(TreeNode root) {
    StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
    write(root, sb);
    return sb.toString();
}
static void write(TreeNode node, StringBuilder sb) {
    if (node == null) { sb.append("#,"); return; }
    sb.append(node.val).append(',');
    write(node.left, sb);
    write(node.right, sb);
}

static TreeNode deserialize(String data) {
    Iterator<String> tokens = Arrays.asList(data.split(",")).iterator();
    return read(tokens);
}
static TreeNode read(Iterator<String> tokens) {
    String t = tokens.next();
    if (t.equals("#")) return null;
    TreeNode node = new TreeNode(Integer.parseInt(t));
    node.left = read(tokens);
    node.right = read(tokens);
    return node;
}

// 1 / (2: 4, 5) / (3: -, 6)
// "1,2,4,#,#,5,#,#,3,#,6,#,#,"

The iterator is the important design choice. Both the left and right recursive calls consume from the same shared stream, so the left subtree eats exactly its tokens and the right call starts where it left off. No index math.

It's O(n) both ways. The string holds n values plus n + 1 null markers.

A level-order version also works and is what LeetCode displays, but it's more code. If the interviewer says "it's a BST", I can drop the null markers entirely: preorder alone is enough, because the value bounds tell me where each subtree ends.

So in real systems I'd probably just use JSON. But this question checks whether I understand why a traversal alone loses the shape.

13. Binary Search Trees

BasicWhat is a BST, and what's the complexity of search and insert?

So a binary search tree is a binary tree with one rule at every node: everything in the left subtree is smaller, and everything in the right subtree is larger. That lets me throw away half the remaining tree at each step, like binary search. Search and insert are O(h), where h is the height.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static TreeNode search(TreeNode node, int target) {
    while (node != null && node.val != target) {
        node = target < node.val ? node.left : node.right;
    }
    return node;   // null if not found
}

static TreeNode insert(TreeNode node, int val) {
    if (node == null) return new TreeNode(val);
    if (val < node.val) node.left = insert(node.left, val);
    else if (val > node.val) node.right = insert(node.right, val);
    return node;   // duplicates ignored
}

// insert 50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80
// inorder -> [20, 30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 80], height 2
// search(60) found, search(65) -> null

Insert is just a search that fails, then attaches a new leaf where the search fell off. Returning the node from the recursion is the neat part: the parent reassigns its child pointer, so I don't need a separate "parent" variable.

The important part is the "O(h)" wording. In a balanced tree h is about log n, so it's O(log n). But nothing in a plain BST keeps it balanced, so the worst case is O(n).

I also decide up front how to handle duplicates. Ignoring them makes it a set. For a multiset I'd store a count in the node rather than sending duplicates left or right, which messes up the ordering rule.

Interviewers look for: "O(h)" instead of a flat "O(log n)". Saying O(log n) without "if balanced" is the trap they're listening for.

BasicWhat happens if you insert already-sorted data into a BST?

So every new value is larger than everything before it, so it always goes right. The tree becomes a straight line, a degenerate tree that is really a linked list. Height becomes n − 1, and every operation drops to O(n).

Let me show you with a quick example.

TreeNode skew = null;
for (int v = 1; v <= 5; v++) skew = insert(skew, v);

// 1
//  \
//   2
//    \
//     3
//      \
//       4
//        \
//         5        height 4, not 2

// insert 1..10,000 in order -> height 9,999

What makes this nasty in real life is that sorted input is common, not rare. Auto-increment IDs, timestamps, and data loaded from a sorted file all hit the worst case. And with the recursive insert above, a big sorted input also gives a StackOverflowError, because the recursion depth equals the height.

There are three fixes I'd mention. Use a self-balancing tree, like red-black or AVL, which repairs its shape after every change. That's what TreeMap does. If I have all the data up front, I can build a balanced tree from the sorted array by always picking the middle. Or I can shuffle the input, since a random insertion order gives an expected height of O(log n).

So in practice I never use a hand-rolled plain BST in production. I use TreeMap or TreeSet, which guarantee O(log n).

IntermediateHow do you check whether a binary tree is a valid BST?

So the classic wrong answer is checking only that left.val < node.val < right.val at each node. The BST rule is about whole subtrees, not just children. The correct approach passes down a valid range: every node must sit strictly between a lower and an upper bound inherited from its ancestors.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static boolean isValidBST(TreeNode root) {
    return valid(root, Long.MIN_VALUE, Long.MAX_VALUE);
}
static boolean valid(TreeNode node, long low, long high) {
    if (node == null) return true;
    if (node.val <= low || node.val >= high) return false;
    return valid(node.left, low, node.val)       // left side: must stay below me
        && valid(node.right, node.val, high);    // right side: must stay above me
}

//   5
//    \
//     6
//    /
//   4
// local check: true  (6 > 5, 4 < 6)
// isValidBST:  false (4 is in 5's right subtree)

What happens here is that when I go right from 5, the lower bound becomes 5. When I then go left from 6, the upper bound becomes 6. So 4 must be in (5, 6), and it isn't.

I use long bounds on purpose. If I started with Integer.MIN_VALUE and MAX_VALUE, a tree containing Integer.MAX_VALUE would fail the >= check. Another clean option is Integer bounds with null meaning "no limit".

The alternative I also like is an inorder traversal that checks each value is strictly greater than the previous one. Same O(n) time and O(h) space.

Interviewers look for: the counterexample with the 4, and the Integer.MAX_VALUE edge case. Also whether duplicates are allowed, which changes <= to < on one side.

IntermediateHow do you delete a node from a BST?

So I first search for the node, then there are three cases. A leaf just disappears. A node with one child is replaced by that child. A node with two children is the interesting one: I copy in its inorder successor, the smallest value in its right subtree, and then delete that successor from the right subtree.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static TreeNode delete(TreeNode node, int key) {
    if (node == null) return null;
    if (key < node.val) node.left = delete(node.left, key);
    else if (key > node.val) node.right = delete(node.right, key);
    else {
        if (node.left == null) return node.right;    // leaf or only right child
        if (node.right == null) return node.left;    // only left child
        TreeNode succ = node.right;                  // two children:
        while (succ.left != null) succ = succ.left;  // smallest in right subtree
        node.val = succ.val;
        node.right = delete(node.right, succ.val);
    }
    return node;
}

// tree from 50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80
// delete 20 (leaf)          -> [30, 40, 50, 60, 70, 80]
// delete 30 (one child)     -> 40 takes its place
// delete 50 (two children)  -> 60 becomes the root

The reason the successor works is that it's the next value in sorted order. It's bigger than everything on the left and smaller than everything else on the right, so putting it at the node keeps the BST rule. And it has no left child by definition, so deleting it is always the easy case.

Notice the first two cases collapse into two lines. If the left is null I return the right, which is null for a leaf anyway.

It's O(h). The predecessor, the largest value on the left, works just as well.

So the thing I'm careful about is returning the node and reassigning child pointers all the way up. Forgetting node.right = … is the classic bug where the delete silently does nothing.

IntermediateFind the kth smallest element in a BST. What if the tree changes often?

So inorder traversal of a BST visits values in sorted order, so the kth node I visit is the answer. I do it iteratively with a stack so I can stop as soon as I hit k, without walking the rest of the tree.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int kthSmallest(TreeNode root, int k) {
    Deque<TreeNode> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();
    TreeNode cur = root;
    while (cur != null || !stack.isEmpty()) {
        while (cur != null) { stack.push(cur); cur = cur.left; }   // go left as far as possible
        cur = stack.pop();
        if (--k == 0) return cur.val;                              // kth in sorted order
        cur = cur.right;
    }
    throw new IllegalArgumentException("k is larger than the tree");
}

// tree from 50, 30, 70, 20, 40, 60, 80
// kthSmallest(root, 3) -> 40

What happens here is that I dive left, pushing the path, until there's nothing left. Popping gives the smallest remaining node. Then I move to its right subtree and repeat. The pops come out as 20, 30, 40, and I stop at the third one.

The cost is O(h + k): I pay the height to reach the first node, then roughly one step per pop. That's much better than collecting all n values into a list first.

For the follow-up, if the tree is updated often and I'm asked for kth many times, I'd store a size field in every node, the count of nodes in its subtree. Then at each node I compare k with left.size + 1 and go left, stop, or go right with k reduced. That's O(h) per query. It's called an order-statistic tree, and inserts and deletes just update sizes along the path.

Interviewers look for: the early stop, and the subtree-size idea for the follow-up. Java's TreeMap doesn't track subtree sizes, so this is custom.

AdvancedBuild a height-balanced BST from a sorted array.

So I pick the middle element as the root, then recursively build the left half into the left subtree and the right half into the right subtree. Everything before the middle is smaller and everything after is bigger, so the BST rule holds. Each split halves the range, so the tree stays balanced.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static TreeNode sortedArrayToBST(int[] nums) {
    return build(nums, 0, nums.length - 1);
}
static TreeNode build(int[] nums, int lo, int hi) {
    if (lo > hi) return null;
    int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
    TreeNode node = new TreeNode(nums[mid]);
    node.left = build(nums, lo, mid - 1);
    node.right = build(nums, mid + 1, hi);
    return node;
}

// [1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 6, 7]  -> root 4, height 2
//        4
//      /   \
//     2     6
//    / \   / \
//   1   3 5   7

At every level, the two halves differ in size by at most one, so left and right heights differ by at most one. That's exactly the height-balanced definition.

I write lo + (hi - lo) / 2 out of habit. It avoids overflow on huge indices, the same bug that once existed in the JDK's binary search.

It's O(n) time since each element becomes one node. Space is O(log n) for the recursion, plus the O(n) tree itself.

This is also the practical fix for the degenerate-BST problem. If I already have sorted data, I build balanced in one pass instead of inserting one by one. The harder variant is a sorted linked list. Finding the middle each time costs O(n log n), but I can get O(n) by building in inorder order while advancing a single list pointer.

14. Balanced Trees & TreeMap

BasicWhy do we need self-balancing trees? What does Java's TreeMap use?

So every BST operation costs O(height), and a plain BST can degrade to height n with sorted input. A self-balancing tree repairs its shape after every insert and delete, so the height always stays O(log n). That turns "O(h), maybe O(n)" into a guaranteed O(log n). TreeMap and TreeSet are red-black trees.

Let me show you the scale of it.

KeysBalanced height (about log2 n)Degenerate height
1,000about 10999
1,000,000about 20999,999
1,000,000,000about 30999,999,999

The way they repair themselves is with rotations. A rotation moves one node up and its parent down, in O(1), and it keeps the inorder order intact. Different trees just have different rules for when to rotate.

The reason this matters beyond interviews is worst-case guarantees. If a service stores keys from users, an attacker, or just a sorted import, can feed it sorted data. A self-balancing tree doesn't care about input order.

HashMap uses red-black trees too, since Java 8. When one bucket gets more than 8 entries (and the table has at least 64 buckets), that bucket is converted to a small red-black tree. That caps a bad bucket at O(log n) instead of O(n).

So in Java I never write a balanced tree myself. I use TreeMap or TreeSet, and in an interview I explain the idea and the rotations.

Interviewers look for: "red-black tree" by name, and the HashMap bucket treeification detail if it's a Java-heavy interview.

BasicHashMap vs TreeMap: when would you use each?

So HashMap is for fast lookup by exact key, O(1) on average, with no ordering at all. TreeMap keeps keys sorted, costs O(log n) for everything, and gives me ordered operations that a hash map simply can't do.

Let me show you with a quick comparison.

HashMapTreeMap
get / put / removeO(1) averageO(log n) guaranteed
Iteration orderNoneSorted by key
Min / max keyO(n) scanO(log n): firstKey, lastKey
"Closest key to x"Not possiblefloorKey, ceilingKey
Range queryNot possiblesubMap, headMap, tailMap
Key requirementhashCode and equalsComparable or a Comparator
null keyOne allowedNot with natural ordering

The trigger words for TreeMap are "closest", "next bigger", "previous smaller", "in a range", "sorted while changing", or needing both min and max while deleting arbitrary items, where a heap is weak.

One gotcha I've hit: TreeMap decides equality using the comparator, not equals. If my comparator says two different objects compare as 0, the map treats them as the same key and one overwrites the other.

A middle option is LinkedHashMap, which keeps insertion order at O(1). That's what I use for an LRU cache, not TreeMap.

So my default is HashMap, and I switch to TreeMap only when the problem needs order.

IntermediateExplain floorKey, ceilingKey, lowerKey and higherKey with a real use case.

So these are "nearest key" lookups, all O(log n). floor and ceiling include an exact match: the largest key <= x and the smallest key >= x. lower and higher are strict: the largest key < x and the smallest key > x. All return null when nothing qualifies.

Let me show you with a quick example, a pricing table where the price picks the plan tier.

TreeMap<Integer, String> prices = new TreeMap<>();
prices.put(100, "basic");
prices.put(250, "plus");
prices.put(500, "pro");
prices.put(1000, "enterprise");

prices.floorKey(300);      // 250   largest <= 300
prices.ceilingKey(300);    // 500   smallest >= 300
prices.floorKey(250);      // 250   equal counts
prices.lowerKey(250);      // 100   strictly less
prices.higherKey(250);     // 500   strictly greater
prices.floorKey(50);       // null  nothing below

prices.headMap(500);          // {100=basic, 250=plus}   keys < 500
prices.subMap(200, 600);      // {250=plus, 500=pro}     [200, 600)
prices.tailMap(500, false);   // {1000=enterprise}       keys > 500

Real places I've used this: mapping a timestamp to the config version active at that time (floorEntry), finding which IP range an address falls in, tier pricing like above, and consistent hashing, where I find the next server clockwise on the ring with ceilingEntry, wrapping to firstEntry.

My memory aid: floor and ceiling include equal, lower and higher don't. Methods ending in Key return just the key, and the Entry versions return key and value together, which saves a second lookup.

So one gotcha: floorKey returns an Integer, and assigning it to an int throws NullPointerException when nothing is found. I always null-check first.

Interviewers look for: the inclusive versus strict distinction, and that subMap is half-open by default.

IntermediateAVL tree vs red-black tree: what's the difference?

So both are self-balancing BSTs with O(log n) operations. AVL is strictly balanced: at every node the left and right heights differ by at most 1. Red-black is looser: it colours nodes and guarantees the longest path is at most twice the shortest. That trade makes AVL faster to search and red-black cheaper to update.

Let me show you with a quick comparison.

AVLRed-black
RuleHeight difference at most 1No red-red, same black count on every path
Max heightAbout 1.44 log nAbout 2 log n
LookupsSlightly faster (shorter)Slightly slower
Rotations per insertAt most 2At most 2
Rotations per deleteUp to O(log n)At most 3
Used byRead-heavy indexesJava TreeMap, C++ std::map, Linux scheduler

The red-black rules are: every node is red or black, the root is black, no red node has a red child, and every path from a node down to a null leaf has the same number of black nodes. The shortest possible path is all black and the longest alternates red and black, which gives the 2x bound.

Both use rotations to fix things. For AVL there are four cases. Left-left and right-right need one rotation. Left-right and right-left, the zig-zags, need two: first rotate the child to make it a straight line, then rotate the node.

So general-purpose libraries pick red-black, because inserts and deletes are cheaper and more predictable. I'd consider AVL only when the workload is mostly lookups.

IntermediateDesign a calendar that rejects overlapping bookings.

So I store bookings in a TreeMap from start to end. Because accepted bookings never overlap, sorting by start also sorts them by end. That means only two existing bookings can possibly clash with a new one: the one starting just before it and the one starting just after it.

Let me show you with a quick example.

class MyCalendar {
    private final TreeMap<Integer, Integer> bookings = new TreeMap<>(); // start -> end

    boolean book(int start, int end) {                                   // [start, end)
        Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> prev = bookings.floorEntry(start);    // starts at or before me
        Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> next = bookings.ceilingEntry(start);  // starts at or after me
        if (prev != null && prev.getValue() > start) return false;       // prev runs into me
        if (next != null && next.getKey() < end) return false;           // I run into next
        bookings.put(start, end);
        return true;
    }
}

// book(10, 20) true
// book(15, 25) false   overlaps [10, 20)
// book(20, 30) true    touching is fine, end is exclusive
// book(5, 10)  true
// book(8, 12)  false

What happens with book(8, 12): the floor of 8 is the booking at 5, which ends at 10. Since 10 is greater than 8, they overlap, so I reject it.

The naive version checks every existing booking, which is O(n) per call and O(n²) overall. This is O(log n) per booking.

The half-open interval [start, end) is a deliberate choice. It makes back-to-back meetings like 10–20 and 20–30 not count as a conflict, and the comparisons stay strict.

So when the interviewer extends it to "allow double booking but not triple", I switch to a sweep line: a TreeMap of time to +1/−1, where I add the event and walk the running sum.

AdvancedWhy do databases use B-trees instead of binary search trees?

So it comes down to the disk. Storage is read in pages, around 16 KB in InnoDB, and each page read costs far more than a memory access. A binary tree does one comparison per node, so looking up a key costs one page read per level. A B-tree packs hundreds of sorted keys into each node, so each page read cuts the search space by hundreds instead of by 2.

Let me show you the numbers for a billion keys.

TreeFan-outHeight for 109 keys
Balanced binary tree2About 30 levels, so about 30 page reads
B-treeAbout 500About 3 to 4 levels

And the top levels are small enough to stay cached in memory, so in practice a lookup is often just one or two actual disk reads.

Most databases use the B+ tree variant. The internal nodes hold only keys for routing, and all the actual rows or row pointers live in the leaves. The leaves are linked in sorted order. That link is what makes range queries like WHERE created_at BETWEEN … fast: find the first leaf, then scan sideways.

They stay balanced differently from red-black trees. Nodes split when they overflow and merge when they underflow, and the tree grows at the root, so all leaves are always at the same depth.

So this is also why index design matters. A composite index on (a, b) is sorted by a, then b, so it helps queries on a, or on a and b, but not on b alone.

Interviewers look for: "fewer, bigger nodes because of page I/O", and the linked leaves for range scans. The LSM tree, used by Cassandra and RocksDB, is the write-optimised alternative.

15. Tries

BasicWhat is a trie, and when would you use one over a HashSet?

So a trie, or prefix tree, stores strings letter by letter along paths from the root. Words that start the same way share their first nodes. Each node has children for the next possible letters and a flag that says "a word ends here".

Let me show you with a quick example.

insert "car", "card", "care"

root
 └ c
   └ a
     └ r  (end)
       ├ d  (end)
       └ e  (end)

search("car")      -> true   path exists and ends at a word
search("ca")       -> false  path exists but "ca" isn't a word
startsWith("ca")   -> true   path exists

The reason I'd pick a trie is prefix queries. "Does any word start with ca?" or "give me all words starting with ca" costs O(length of prefix) to find the spot. A HashSet would have to scan every word.

For exact lookups, a trie doesn't beat a HashSet. Both are O(L), since hashing a string also reads every character. And the trie uses much more memory, with one node object per character.

So I use a trie for autocomplete, spell-check suggestions, IP routing by longest prefix, and word games. It also shines when I search for many words while walking a structure, like a grid, because I can stop the moment the current path isn't a prefix of anything.

Interviewers look for: why you need the end flag, and an honest "a HashSet is fine for exact lookups."

BasicWhat's the complexity of insert, search and startsWith in a trie?

So all three are O(L), where L is the length of the word or prefix. The important point is that it doesn't depend on how many words are stored. Whether the trie holds 10 words or 10 million, looking up "card" takes four steps.

Let me show you with a quick breakdown.

OperationTimeExtra space
insert(word)O(L)Up to O(L) new nodes
search(word)O(L)O(1)
startsWith(prefix)O(L)O(1)
List all words with a prefixO(L + size of that subtree)Output size
Whole trieO(total characters) nodes in the worst case

With the array-of-26 design, each step is a direct index, children[c - 'a'], so it's truly constant per character. With a HashMap for children it's O(1) on average per character.

The space line is where tries hurt. If words share few prefixes, I get close to one node per character, and each node with a 26-slot array costs well over 100 bytes on a 64-bit JVM. So a million words can easily use hundreds of megabytes.

So in an interview I say "O(L) per operation, independent of n", then mention the memory cost before they ask.

IntermediateImplement a trie with insert, search and startsWith.

So each node holds an array of 26 children and an isEnd flag. Insert walks the word, creating nodes as needed, and marks the last one. Search and startsWith share the same walk. The only difference is whether I also require isEnd at the end.

Let me show you with a quick example.

class Trie {
    private static class Node {
        Node[] children = new Node[26];
        boolean isEnd;
    }
    private final Node root = new Node();

    void insert(String word) {
        Node node = root;
        for (char c : word.toCharArray()) {
            int i = c - 'a';
            if (node.children[i] == null) node.children[i] = new Node();
            node = node.children[i];
        }
        node.isEnd = true;
    }
    boolean search(String word) {
        Node node = walk(word);
        return node != null && node.isEnd;
    }
    boolean startsWith(String prefix) {
        return walk(prefix) != null;
    }
    private Node walk(String s) {
        Node node = root;
        for (char c : s.toCharArray()) {
            node = node.children[c - 'a'];
            if (node == null) return null;
        }
        return node;
    }
}

// insert car, card, care
// search("car") true, search("ca") false, startsWith("ca") true
// search("cards") false, startsWith("cat") false

Pulling out the shared walk helper is the design choice I'd point out. It removes duplicated loops and makes the difference between the two queries a single condition.

The bug I see most often is forgetting isEnd and returning true for "ca" just because the path exists. Another is assuming lowercase a to z without saying so. c - 'a' goes out of bounds on anything else.

So to support delete, I'd add a count of words passing through each node, decrement along the path, and prune children whose count hits zero.

Interviewers look for: stating the alphabet assumption. Common follow-ups: count words with a prefix (store a pass-through count) and wildcard search with . (DFS into all children at that position).

IntermediateShould trie children be an array or a HashMap?

So an array is faster and simpler when the alphabet is small and fixed, like lowercase English. A HashMap is better when the alphabet is large or unknown, like Unicode, URLs, or mixed case, because it only stores children that actually exist.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// array: fixed 26 slots per node
Node[] children = new Node[26];
node = node.children[c - 'a'];

// map: only real children, any character
class Node {
    Map<Character, Node> children = new HashMap<>();
    boolean isEnd;
}
node = node.children.computeIfAbsent(c, k -> new Node());

// map version inserting "héllo" works fine
// array version would throw ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException on 'é'

Here's how I weigh it:

Array[26]HashMap
Step costDirect index, very fastHash plus boxing a Character
Memory per node26 references, even if mostly emptyOnly existing children, but map overhead
AlphabetMust be known and smallAnything
Children in sorted orderFree: iterate 0 to 25Needs a TreeMap

The sorted-order point matters more than people expect. For "autocomplete in alphabetical order" or "lexicographically smallest word", the array gives it for free.

For a huge dictionary where memory is the problem, I'd go further. A radix tree, also called a compressed trie, merges chains of single-child nodes into one edge labelled with a whole substring. That cuts the node count a lot.

So my default in interviews is the array, and I say "I'd switch to a map if the character set isn't fixed."

AdvancedGiven a letter grid and a list of words, find every word that appears in the grid.

So running a separate grid search for each word repeats the same work for words that share prefixes. Instead, I put all the words into a trie and do one DFS from each cell, walking the trie alongside the grid. The moment the current path isn't a prefix of any word, I stop.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static class TNode { TNode[] next = new TNode[26]; String word; }

static List<String> findWords(char[][] board, String[] words) {
    TNode root = new TNode();
    for (String w : words) {
        TNode node = root;
        for (char c : w.toCharArray()) {
            if (node.next[c - 'a'] == null) node.next[c - 'a'] = new TNode();
            node = node.next[c - 'a'];
        }
        node.word = w;                       // store the word at its end node
    }
    List<String> found = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int r = 0; r < board.length; r++)
        for (int c = 0; c < board[0].length; c++)
            dfs(board, r, c, root, found);
    return found;
}

static void dfs(char[][] board, int r, int c, TNode parent, List<String> found) {
    if (r < 0 || c < 0 || r >= board.length || c >= board[0].length) return;
    char ch = board[r][c];
    if (ch == '#' || parent.next[ch - 'a'] == null) return;   // used, or no word goes this way
    TNode node = parent.next[ch - 'a'];
    if (node.word != null) { found.add(node.word); node.word = null; }  // report once
    board[r][c] = '#';                                          // mark as used on this path
    dfs(board, r + 1, c, node, found);
    dfs(board, r - 1, c, node, found);
    dfs(board, r, c + 1, node, found);
    dfs(board, r, c - 1, node, found);
    board[r][c] = ch;                                           // backtrack
}

// board oaan / etae / ihkr / iflv, words oath, pea, eat, rain
// -> [oath, eat]

A few choices here I'd explain. Storing the full word at its end node means I don't need to build a string during the DFS. Setting it to null after finding it avoids duplicates when the same word appears twice in the grid. And overwriting the cell with # is my visited marker, restored when I backtrack.

The worst case is O(R · C · 4 · 3L−1), where L is the longest word: four directions on the first step, then at most three, since I can't go back. In practice the trie pruning makes it far faster.

So the extra optimisation for big inputs is pruning found leaves from the trie, so later searches don't walk dead branches.

AdvancedHow would you build autocomplete for millions of search terms?

So the core is a trie, but a naive "walk to the prefix node, then DFS its whole subtree and sort" is too slow for short prefixes like "a", which could have millions of matches. The fix is to precompute: each node caches its top k suggestions, so a query is just O(prefix length) to reach the node and O(k) to read the list.

Let me show you with a quick sketch.

class Node {
    Node[] children = new Node[26];
    List<String> top = new ArrayList<>();   // best k completions through this node
}

// build: insert terms sorted by popularity (or alphabetically),
// and while walking each term's path, add it to node.top
// if that list has fewer than k entries
void insert(String term, int k) {
    Node node = root;
    for (char c : term.toCharArray()) {
        int i = c - 'a';
        if (node.children[i] == null) node.children[i] = new Node();
        node = node.children[i];
        if (node.top.size() < k) node.top.add(term);
    }
}

// query: walk the prefix, return node.top. O(prefix + k).

Because I insert in ranked order, the first k terms that pass through a node are the best k for that prefix. That's the same trick as LeetCode's "search suggestions system", which inserts sorted products and caches three per node.

Now, at real scale I'd talk about the system around it. Memory: I'd use a compressed radix tree and store term IDs instead of strings. Updates: popularity changes, so I'd rebuild the trie offline periodically and swap it in, rather than updating live. Distribution: shard by the first character or two, and put a cache in front, since a small set of prefixes gets most of the traffic.

Plenty of production systems skip the hand-built trie and use a search engine's completion feature, like Elasticsearch's completion suggester, which uses a compact automaton (an FST).

Interviewers look for: caching top k per node instead of searching the subtree per query, and awareness of memory and update cost.

16. Graphs & Traversal

BasicHow would you represent a graph? Adjacency list or matrix?

So my default is an adjacency list: for each vertex, a list of its neighbours. It uses O(V + E) space, and BFS or DFS over it costs O(V + E). A matrix uses O(V²) space no matter how many edges exist, but it answers "is there an edge u to v?" in O(1).

Let me show you with a quick example.

static List<List<Integer>> buildGraph(int n, int[][] edges) {
    List<List<Integer>> adj = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) adj.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        adj.get(e[0]).add(e[1]);
        adj.get(e[1]).add(e[0]);   // drop this line for a directed graph
    }
    return adj;
}

buildGraph(6, new int[][]{{0,1},{0,2},{1,3},{2,3},{3,4}});
// [[1, 2], [0, 3], [0, 3], [1, 2, 4], [3], []]
// node 5 has no edges, but it still exists
Adjacency listAdjacency matrix
SpaceO(V + E)O(V²)
Is there an edge u–v?O(degree of u)O(1)
List neighbours of uO(degree of u)O(V)
Best forSparse graphs, BFS/DFSDense graphs, small V, Floyd-Warshall

Most real graphs are sparse. A social network with a billion users, where each has a few hundred friends, would need 1018 cells as a matrix. So the list wins.

Two other forms come up a lot. An edge list, just an array of pairs, is often the input format, and it's what Kruskal's algorithm sorts. And implicit graphs, like grids or word ladders, where I never store edges at all and just generate neighbours on the fly.

So my first step in any graph problem is converting the input into an adjacency list, and remembering to create empty lists for isolated nodes.

BasicBFS vs DFS: how do they differ and when would you use each?

So BFS explores in rings, nearest nodes first, using a queue. DFS goes as deep as it can down one path, then backs up, using recursion or a stack. Both visit every reachable node once, so both are O(V + E). The difference is the order, and that decides which problems each one solves.

Let me show you with a quick example on the same graph.

// edges 0-1, 0-2, 1-3, 2-3, 3-4

static void dfs(List<List<Integer>> adj, int u, boolean[] seen, List<Integer> order) {
    seen[u] = true;
    order.add(u);
    for (int v : adj.get(u)) if (!seen[v]) dfs(adj, v, seen, order);
}

// DFS from 0:            [0, 1, 3, 2, 4]  dives 0 -> 1 -> 3 -> 2, backs up, then 4
// BFS distances from 0:  [0, 1, 1, 2, 3]  rings: {0}, {1, 2}, {3}, {4}

Here's how I choose. BFS for shortest path in an unweighted graph, "minimum number of moves", or anything spreading level by level, like rotting oranges or friend-of-friend distance. DFS for exploring everything, counting connected components, cycle detection, topological sort, and backtracking, where I need the full current path.

Memory differs too. BFS holds a whole frontier, which can be huge in a wide graph. DFS holds one path, which is small unless the graph is a long chain, and then recursive DFS risks a stack overflow. In that case I use an explicit stack.

For plain "can I reach X" or "count the islands", either works, and I pick whichever I can write fastest.

So the one rule I never break: if the question says "shortest" or "minimum steps" and edges are unweighted, it's BFS. DFS does not give shortest paths.

Interviewers look for: the shortest-path rule, and the memory trade-off for wide versus deep graphs.

IntermediateWhy does BFS find the shortest path in an unweighted graph? Where do you mark nodes visited?

So the queue processes nodes in order of distance: every node at distance 1, then every node at distance 2, and so on. The first time I reach a node, I've reached it by the fewest edges possible, because any shorter route would have been processed earlier. And I mark a node visited when I add it to the queue, not when I remove it.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int[] bfsDistances(List<List<Integer>> adj, int src) {
    int[] dist = new int[adj.size()];
    Arrays.fill(dist, -1);                 // -1 doubles as "not visited"
    Queue<Integer> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    dist[src] = 0;
    queue.offer(src);
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int u = queue.poll();
        for (int v : adj.get(u)) {
            if (dist[v] == -1) {           // mark when adding, not when polling
                dist[v] = dist[u] + 1;
                queue.offer(v);
            }
        }
    }
    return dist;
}

// edges 0-1, 0-2, 1-3, 2-3, 3-4, node 5 isolated
// bfsDistances(adj, 0) -> [0, 1, 1, 2, 3, -1]

Node 3 is the case I'd point at. Both 1 and 2 reach it. Because I mark it when 1 adds it, 2 skips it. If I marked on poll instead, 3 would go into the queue twice. The answer would still be right, but on a dense graph the queue can blow up to O(E) duplicate entries.

Using a dist array as the visited marker is a small habit I like. One array does two jobs, and unreachable nodes naturally stay at -1.

This breaks as soon as edges have weights. Then a path with more edges can be cheaper, so I need Dijkstra, which is BFS with a priority queue. For weights of only 0 and 1 there's 0-1 BFS with a deque.

Interviewers look for: "mark on enqueue". To rebuild the actual path, keep a parent array and walk back from the target.

IntermediateCount the number of islands in a grid of 1s and 0s.

So this is connected components on an implicit graph. Each land cell is a node, and edges go to its four neighbours. I scan every cell. When I find unvisited land, that's a new island, so I increment the count and flood-fill the whole island so I never count it again.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int numIslands(char[][] grid) {
    int count = 0;
    for (int r = 0; r < grid.length; r++)
        for (int c = 0; c < grid[0].length; c++)
            if (grid[r][c] == '1') {
                count++;                   // found an untouched island
                sink(grid, r, c);          // mark the whole island visited
            }
    return count;
}
static void sink(char[][] grid, int r, int c) {
    if (r < 0 || c < 0 || r >= grid.length || c >= grid[0].length || grid[r][c] != '1') return;
    grid[r][c] = '0';
    sink(grid, r + 1, c); sink(grid, r - 1, c);
    sink(grid, r, c + 1); sink(grid, r, c - 1);
}

// 11000
// 11000
// 00100
// 00011    -> 3

What happens here is that "sinking" land to '0' is my visited marker, so I don't need a separate boolean[][]. I'd mention that this mutates the input. If the caller needs the grid back, I'd use a visited array instead.

It's O(R × C) time, since each cell is sunk at most once and checked a constant number of times. Space is O(R × C) in the worst case for the recursion, when the whole grid is one snake-shaped island.

That worst case is real. A 1000 by 1000 grid of all land can recurse up to a million deep and overflow the stack. For big grids I'd switch the flood fill to BFS with a queue.

So the same skeleton solves max area of island, surrounded regions, and number of provinces. Union-Find is the alternative, and it's better when land gets added over time (Number of Islands II).

Interviewers look for: the stack-depth risk on large grids, and whether you ask before mutating the input.

IntermediateHow do you detect a cycle in a graph? Is it different for directed graphs?

So yes, the two cases need different checks. In an undirected graph, DFS finds a cycle if it reaches an already-visited node that isn't the parent I just came from. In a directed graph, "already visited" isn't enough. I need three colours, and a cycle exists only if I reach a node that is still on the current DFS path.

Let me show you with a quick example.

// undirected: skip the edge back to my parent
static boolean dfsUndirected(List<List<Integer>> adj, int u, int parent, boolean[] seen) {
    seen[u] = true;
    for (int v : adj.get(u)) {
        if (v == parent) continue;                  // the edge I came in on
        if (seen[v] || dfsUndirected(adj, v, u, seen)) return true;
    }
    return false;
}

// directed: 0 = unvisited, 1 = on current path, 2 = finished
static boolean dfsCycle(List<List<Integer>> adj, int u, int[] color) {
    color[u] = 1;
    for (int v : adj.get(u)) {
        if (color[v] == 1) return true;             // back edge to the current path
        if (color[v] == 0 && dfsCycle(adj, v, color)) return true;
    }
    color[u] = 2;
    return false;
}

// directed 0->1, 1->2, 0->2, 2->3   -> no cycle
// add 3->1                           -> cycle (1 -> 2 -> 3 -> 1)

The directed example shows why two states aren't enough. From 0, I visit 1, then 2, then 3. Back at 0, I try the edge to 2, which is already visited. With a plain visited set that looks like a cycle, but 2 is finished (black), so it's just a second route into the same place. Only a grey node, still on the stack, closes a loop.

Both run in O(V + E), and I wrap them in a loop over all vertices in case the graph is disconnected.

So for directed graphs I often use Kahn's algorithm instead: a BFS topological sort by in-degree. If it can't output every node, there's a cycle. That's the Course Schedule approach. For undirected graphs, Union-Find also works: an edge joining two nodes already in the same set closes a cycle.

Interviewers look for: the parent check for undirected, the grey state for directed, and an example where the naive check gives a false positive.

AdvancedEvery minute, rotten oranges rot their fresh neighbours. How long until all are rotten?

So this is multi-source BFS. Instead of starting from one node, I put every rotten orange in the queue at the start. BFS levels then equal minutes, and each fresh orange is reached at the minute it would actually rot. I count the fresh ones up front so I can tell if any are unreachable.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int orangesRotting(int[][] grid) {
    int rows = grid.length, cols = grid[0].length, fresh = 0;
    Queue<int[]> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++)
        for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++) {
            if (grid[r][c] == 2) queue.offer(new int[]{r, c});   // every source at once
            else if (grid[r][c] == 1) fresh++;
        }
    int minutes = 0;
    int[][] dirs = {{1,0},{-1,0},{0,1},{0,-1}};
    while (!queue.isEmpty() && fresh > 0) {
        for (int i = queue.size(); i > 0; i--) {                // one level = one minute
            int[] cell = queue.poll();
            for (int[] d : dirs) {
                int r = cell[0] + d[0], c = cell[1] + d[1];
                if (r < 0 || c < 0 || r >= rows || c >= cols || grid[r][c] != 1) continue;
                grid[r][c] = 2;                                  // mark on enqueue
                fresh--;
                queue.offer(new int[]{r, c});
            }
        }
        minutes++;
    }
    return fresh == 0 ? minutes : -1;
}

// [[2,1,1],[1,1,0],[0,1,1]]  -> 4
// [[2,1,1],[0,1,1],[1,0,1]]  -> -1   bottom-left orange is cut off
// [[0,2]]                    -> 0    nothing to rot

Starting from all sources at once is like adding one imaginary "super source" joined to every rotten orange. That's why it's correct: each fresh orange is reached from its nearest rotten one.

The fresh > 0 condition in the loop matters. Without it, I'd count one extra minute for the last level, which spreads to nothing.

The naive approach runs a BFS from each rotten orange separately, which is O((R × C)²). This is O(R × C) time and space.

So the same pattern solves "walls and gates", "distance to nearest 0" (01 Matrix), and in real life, things like distance from every house to its nearest store.

Interviewers look for: seeding all sources at once, and the -1 case. Word Ladder is the other classic BFS here: words are nodes, and I generate neighbours by changing one letter at a time.

17. Union-Find (Disjoint Set)

BasicWhat is Union-Find, and what operations does it support?

So Union-Find, or Disjoint Set Union, tracks items split into groups that never overlap. It supports two operations: find(x), which returns the leader of x's group, and union(a, b), which merges two groups. Two items are connected exactly when they have the same leader.

Let me show you with a quick example. The whole thing is two int arrays.

class UnionFind {
    private final int[] parent, size;
    private int components;
    UnionFind(int n) {
        parent = new int[n]; size = new int[n]; components = n;
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) { parent[i] = i; size[i] = 1; }
    }
    int find(int x) {
        while (parent[x] != x) {
            parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];   // path halving
            x = parent[x];
        }
        return x;
    }
    boolean union(int a, int b) {
        int ra = find(a), rb = find(b);
        if (ra == rb) return false;          // already connected
        if (size[ra] < size[rb]) { int t = ra; ra = rb; rb = t; }
        parent[rb] = ra;                     // smaller tree under larger
        size[ra] += size[rb];
        components--;
        return true;
    }
    int components() { return components; }
}
// n = 5: union(0,1), union(3,4) -> find(1)==find(0) true, find(0)==find(3) false, components() = 3

What happens here is each group is a small tree, and the root points to itself. find walks up to the root and flattens the path as it goes. union links the two roots, never the original nodes.

I like returning a boolean from union. "False" means the two were already connected, and that is exactly the signal for cycle detection. The counter gives me "how many groups" for free.

Interviewers look for: linking roots, not nodes. parent[a] = b is the classic bug; it has to be parent[find(a)] = find(b).

BasicWhen would you use Union-Find instead of BFS or DFS for connectivity?

So if the graph is fixed and I'm asked one question about it, BFS or DFS is fine and simpler. I reach for Union-Find when edges arrive over time and I need an answer after each one, or when I only care about "same group or not", never the actual path.

Let me show you with a quick comparison.

SituationMy pickWhy
Static graph, count components onceEitherBoth O(V + E)
Edges added one by one, answer after eachUnion-FindDFS would restart each time: O(k(V + E))
Need the path, or the shortest pathBFS / DFSUnion-Find only knows the leader
Kruskal's MST, cycle check in undirected graphUnion-Find"Would this edge join two things already joined?"
Edges get removedBFS / DFSUnion-Find cannot split

The reason I'd pick Union-Find for the online case is that each new edge is one near-O(1) union, instead of a whole re-traversal. Number of Islands II is the classic example: land cells appear one at a time, and each new cell unions with its land neighbours.

So in practice I ask two questions: does the data change over time, and do I need paths? Changing plus no paths means Union-Find.

IntermediateExplain path compression and union by rank. What's the resulting complexity?

So without any tricks, Union-Find can degrade into a long chain and find becomes O(n). The two optimisations fix that from both sides. Union by size or rank keeps trees shallow when merging. Path compression flattens trees when reading.

Let me show you with the numbers.

VersionCost per operation
No tricksO(n) worst case
Union by size / rank onlyO(log n)
Path compression onlyO(log n) amortized
BothO(α(n)) amortized, effectively O(1)

Union by size means I always attach the smaller tree under the bigger root. A node only gets one level deeper when its tree at least doubles, so no tree is taller than log n. Path compression means that after find(x) discovers the root, every node on the way points straight at it, so that work is never repeated.

α is the inverse Ackermann function. It grows so slowly that it is at most 4 for any input that fits in the universe, which is why people call it "effectively constant".

int find(int x) {                                    // recursive, full compression
    if (parent[x] != x) parent[x] = find(parent[x]);
    return parent[x];
}

One gotcha I've hit: the recursive version can go very deep on the first call before anything is compressed. On big inputs I use the iterative path-halving loop instead.

Interviewers look for: that you say "amortized". A single find can still be slow; it's the sequence of m operations that costs O(m α(n)).

IntermediateHow would you detect a cycle in an undirected graph using Union-Find?

So I process the edges one by one and union their endpoints. If an edge connects two nodes that already have the same root, there was already a path between them, so this edge closes a cycle.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static boolean hasCycle(int n, int[][] edges) {
    UnionFind uf = new UnionFind(n);
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        if (!uf.union(e[0], e[1])) return true;   // ends already connected
    }
    return false;
}
// {{0,1},{1,2},{2,0}} -> true      {{0,1},{1,2},{2,3}} -> false

What happens here is: edge 0-1 merges two singletons, edge 1-2 merges again, and now 0, 1 and 2 share a root. When 2-0 arrives, union sees the same root and returns false. That's the cycle.

This is exactly LeetCode's Redundant Connection: return the first edge where union fails. Time is O(E α(V)), space O(V).

The one thing I'd point out is that this only works for undirected graphs. In a directed graph, A to B and C to B is not a cycle, but Union-Find would say those nodes are "connected". For directed graphs I use DFS with three colours, or Kahn's topological sort.

Interviewers look for: the directed-graph caveat. It's the most common follow-up to this question.
