IntermediateWalk me through Kruskal's algorithm. Where does Union-Find come in?

So Kruskal builds a minimum spanning tree greedily: sort all edges by weight, then take each edge in order unless it would create a cycle. Union-Find is the cycle check. An edge is safe exactly when its two endpoints are in different components.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int kruskal(int n, int[][] edges) {        // edge = {u, v, weight}
    Arrays.sort(edges, Comparator.comparingInt(e -> e[2]));
    UnionFind uf = new UnionFind(n);
    int total = 0, used = 0;
    for (int[] e : edges) {
        if (uf.union(e[0], e[1])) {           // skip edges that would form a cycle
            total += e[2];
            if (++used == n - 1) break;        // tree is complete
        }
    }
    return used == n - 1 ? total : -1;         // -1: graph not connected
}
// edges {0-1:4, 0-2:1, 1-2:2, 1-3:5, 2-3:8}, n = 4 -> 8

Tracing it: the sorted order is 0-2 (1), 1-2 (2), 0-1 (4), 1-3 (5), 2-3 (8). I take 0-2 and 1-2. Then 0-1 fails because 0 and 1 already share a root, so I skip it. I take 1-3, and that's three edges for four nodes, so I stop. Total is 1 + 2 + 5 = 8.

Complexity is O(E log E) for the sort. The Union-Find part is nearly linear, so the sort dominates.

So in practice I pick Kruskal for sparse graphs given as an edge list, and Prim's with a heap for dense graphs given as an adjacency list.

AdvancedWhat if the items aren't 0..n-1 integers, or you need to undo a union?

So for arbitrary keys like emails or strings, I either map each key to an integer id first, or I back the parent array with a HashMap and create entries lazily. Undo is a different story. Path compression rewrites the tree, so I can't cleanly reverse it. For undo I drop compression and keep only union by size, then record each union on a stack.

Let me show you the map-based version.

class DSU<T> {
    private final Map<T, T> parent = new HashMap<>();
    T find(T x) {
        parent.putIfAbsent(x, x);                  // lazily create new items
        T p = parent.get(x);
        if (!p.equals(x)) { p = find(p); parent.put(x, p); }  // path compression
        return p;
    }
    void union(T a, T b) { parent.put(find(a), find(b)); }
}
// union("a@x.com","b@x.com"), union("c@x.com","b@x.com")
// find(a) == find(c) -> true,  find(a) == find("z@x.com") -> false

This is handy for Accounts Merge. The trade-off is that a HashMap is several times slower than an int array, so if I know all the keys up front I compress them to ids first.

For rollback, the rule is: union by size only, and each union pushes "rb's parent was rb, ra's size was s" onto a stack. Undo pops and restores. Without compression, find is O(log n) instead of near O(1). That variant shows up in offline dynamic connectivity, where edges are added and removed.

Interviewers look for: that you know plain Union-Find can't delete, and why path compression is what blocks undo.

18. Segment Trees & Fenwick Trees

BasicWhat problem does a segment tree solve? Why not just use a prefix sum array?

So a segment tree answers range queries, like sum, min or max over [l, r], while the array is also being updated. Both operations cost O(log n). A prefix sum array gives O(1) queries, but a single update invalidates every prefix after it, so updates cost O(n).

Let me show you with a quick comparison.

ApproachPoint updateRange queryq mixed operations
Plain arrayO(1)O(n)O(q n)
Prefix sumsO(n)O(1)O(q n)
Segment tree / FenwickO(log n)O(log n)O(q log n)

What happens with the tree is that every node stores the answer for a slice of the array. The root covers everything, and the leaves are single elements. A query stitches together a few stored slices. An update only fixes the log n ancestors of one leaf.

With n and q both around 105, the first two rows are about 1010 operations, which times out. The tree is about 1.7 × 106.

So my rule is: if the array never changes, I use prefix sums. It's simpler and faster. When I see "update" and "range query" in the same problem, I think segment tree or Fenwick tree.

IntermediateWalk me through a segment tree range query. Why is it O(log n), and why allocate 4n?

So a query has three cases at each node. No overlap: return the neutral value. Full overlap: return the stored answer without going deeper. Partial overlap: ask both children and combine.

Let me show you with a range-minimum tree.

class MinSegTree {
    private final int n;
    private final int[] tree;
    MinSegTree(int[] a) { n = a.length; tree = new int[4 * n]; build(a, 1, 0, n - 1); }
    private void build(int[] a, int node, int lo, int hi) {
        if (lo == hi) { tree[node] = a[lo]; return; }
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
        build(a, 2 * node, lo, mid);
        build(a, 2 * node + 1, mid + 1, hi);
        tree[node] = Math.min(tree[2 * node], tree[2 * node + 1]);
    }
    int query(int l, int r) { return query(1, 0, n - 1, l, r); }
    private int query(int node, int lo, int hi, int l, int r) {
        if (r < lo || hi < l) return Integer.MAX_VALUE;      // no overlap: neutral
        if (l <= lo && hi <= r) return tree[node];            // fully inside
        int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;                              // partial: split
        return Math.min(query(2 * node, lo, mid, l, r),
                        query(2 * node + 1, mid + 1, hi, l, r));
    }
}
// {5, 2, 8, 6, 3, 7}: query(0,5) = 2, query(2,4) = 3, query(2,3) = 6

Why it's O(log n): on any level of the tree, at most two nodes are partially covered, the ones holding the left and right edges of the range. Everything strictly between them is a full overlap and returns immediately. So I visit a constant number of nodes per level, times log n levels.

Why 4n: the tree is stored like a heap, with children at 2k and 2k + 1. When n isn't a power of two, the tree isn't perfectly packed, and the highest index can go past 2n. 4n is a safe bound for every n.

Interviewers look for: the neutral value. For min it's Integer.MAX_VALUE, not 0. Returning 0 is a silent wrong answer.

IntermediateHow does a Fenwick tree work? What does i & -i do?

So a Fenwick tree, or Binary Indexed Tree, is a single 1-indexed array where bit[i] stores the sum of a block of elements ending at i. The block length is the lowest set bit of i. i & -i isolates that lowest set bit. In two's complement, -i flips every bit and adds one, so it matches i only at that bit. For example, 12 & -12 is 4, 6 & -6 is 2, and 7 & -7 is 1.

Let me show you the whole thing. It's about ten lines.

class FenwickTree {
    private final long[] bit;                  // 1-indexed, bit[0] unused
    FenwickTree(int n) { bit = new long[n + 1]; }
    void add(int i, long delta) {              // position i += delta
        for (; i < bit.length; i += i & -i) bit[i] += delta;
    }
    long prefixSum(int i) {                    // sum of 1..i
        long s = 0;
        for (; i > 0; i -= i & -i) s += bit[i];
        return s;
    }
    long rangeSum(int l, int r) { return prefixSum(r) - prefixSum(l - 1); }
}
// values 2,1,5,3,4 at 1..5: prefixSum(3) = 8, rangeSum(2,4) = 9; add(3, 5): rangeSum(2,4) = 14

What happens in prefixSum(7) is: add bit[7], strip the low bit to get 6, add bit[6], strip to 4, add bit[4], stop at 0. Three blocks cover 1..7. add goes the other way and climbs to every block that contains i. Both loops run at most about log n times.

One gotcha I've hit: calling add(0, x) loops forever, because 0 & -0 is 0. So I always shift 0-indexed input by one.

IntermediateSegment tree or Fenwick tree: which would you pick?

So for sums or counts with point updates, I pick Fenwick. It's ten lines, uses n + 1 memory, and is hard to get wrong. For min or max over arbitrary ranges, range updates, or anything custom, I pick a segment tree.

Let me show you the trade-offs.

Segment treeFenwick tree
Code size~30 lines, easy to slip~10 lines
Memory4nn + 1
OperationsAny associative op: sum, min, max, gcd, custom structsOps with an inverse (sum, xor, count), since ranges come from two prefixes
Range updatesYes, with lazy propagationRange add + point query, or two BITs for range add + range sum

The key limitation of Fenwick is that it only knows prefixes. A range sum works because sum(l..r) = prefix(r) - prefix(l-1). You can't "subtract" a min, so range min over any [l, r] needs a segment tree.

Fenwick does have one trick a segment tree makes awkward: it doubles as a frequency table indexed by value. That's why "count inversions" and "how many smaller elements have I seen so far" are almost always written with a BIT.

And if the array never changes, neither. Prefix sums for sums, a sparse table for range min. So in an interview I say the rule out loud, then write whichever one the operation needs.

Interviewers look for: the "needs an inverse" reason. Saying "Fenwick can't do min" without the why sounds memorised.

AdvancedWhat is lazy propagation, and when do you need it?

So lazy propagation lets a segment tree do range updates, like "add 10 to every element in [l, r]", in O(log n) instead of O(n). When an update fully covers a node, I update that node's sum right away and leave a note saying "my children still owe +10". I only push that note down when a later operation actually needs to go below that node.

Let me show you the core of it, for range add and range sum.

void apply(int node, int lo, int hi, long v) {
    sum[node] += v * (hi - lo + 1);       // whole segment grows by v each
    lazy[node] += v;                      // owe this to the children
}
void push(int node, int lo, int hi) {
    if (lazy[node] == 0 || lo == hi) return;
    int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
    apply(2 * node, lo, mid, lazy[node]);
    apply(2 * node + 1, mid + 1, hi, lazy[node]);
    lazy[node] = 0;
}
void add(int node, int lo, int hi, int l, int r, long v) {
    if (r < lo || hi < l) return;
    if (l <= lo && hi <= r) { apply(node, lo, hi, v); return; }  // stop here
    push(node, lo, hi);
    int mid = (lo + hi) / 2;
    add(2 * node, lo, mid, l, r, v);
    add(2 * node + 1, mid + 1, hi, l, r, v);
    sum[node] = sum[2 * node] + sum[2 * node + 1];
}
// {2, 1, 5, 3, 4}, add 10 to [1..3]: sum(0,4) = 45, sum(2,2) = 15

The reason it stays O(log n) is that an update touches the same nodes a query would. It stops at fully covered nodes and never walks their subtrees.

The bug I see most is forgetting to call push in the query path too. Then a query reads children that never received the pending add. I also use long, because v * length overflows int quickly.

Interviewers look for: "push before you go down, pull after you come back up". That's the whole invariant.

AdvancedHow would you count inversions, or "smaller numbers after self", with a Fenwick tree?

So I use the Fenwick tree as a frequency table indexed by value, not by position. I scan the array once. At each element I ask "how many values already seen are bigger than me?" That's a prefix-sum query. Then I add myself to the table. If values are large or negative, I coordinate-compress them to ranks 1..m first.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static long countInversions(int[] nums) {
    int[] sorted = Arrays.stream(nums).distinct().sorted().toArray();
    FenwickTree fw = new FenwickTree(sorted.length);
    long inversions = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        int rank = Arrays.binarySearch(sorted, nums[i]) + 1;   // 1..m
        inversions += i - fw.prefixSum(rank);   // seen so far that are bigger
        fw.add(rank, 1);
    }
    return inversions;
}
// {8, 4, 2, 1} -> 6    {2, 4, 1, 3, 5} -> 3    {3, 3, 1} -> 2

What happens here is that at index i I've seen i elements. prefixSum(rank) counts how many of them are <= me, so i minus that is how many are strictly bigger. Each of those forms an inversion with me. Equal values are not counted, which is correct.

Time is O(n log n) for the sort plus n log n for the tree. Space is O(n), and the answer goes in a long, because n = 105 can give around 5 × 109 inversions.

"Count of smaller numbers after self" is the mirror image: scan right to left and query prefixSum(rank - 1). Merge sort solves the same thing, but the BIT version is easier to adapt to online queries.

Part 4 — Algorithms

19. Sorting

BasicWhat does it mean for a sort to be stable, and when does it matter?

So a stable sort keeps equal elements in the order they had before sorting. It matters whenever the elements carry more than the sort key. The classic case is sorting by one field after already sorting by another, where I want the first ordering kept as the tie-breaker.

Let me show you with a quick example.

record Order(String id, String status, int amount) {}

List<Order> orders = new ArrayList<>(List.of(
    new Order("A", "PAID", 50), new Order("B", "NEW", 20),
    new Order("C", "PAID", 20), new Order("D", "NEW", 50)));

orders.sort(Comparator.comparingInt(Order::amount));   // first by amount
orders.sort(Comparator.comparing(Order::status));      // then by status
// B D C A: NEW before PAID, and inside each status still by amount

What happens here is that List.sort is stable, so after the second sort B (20) still comes before D (50) among the NEW orders. With an unstable sort, the amount order inside each status could come out scrambled. This is exactly what happens when a user clicks one table column and then another.

StableNot stable
Merge sort, insertion sort, TimSort, counting sort, radix sortQuicksort, heap sort, selection sort

So in practice, if I control the comparator, I'd rather write one chained comparator, comparing(status).thenComparingInt(amount). That doesn't depend on stability at all. Stability matters most when sorting happens in stages or in someone else's code.

Interviewers look for: a real use case (multi-column sort, keeping arrival order for ties), not only the definition.

BasicCompare merge sort and quicksort.

So both are O(n log n) on average. Merge sort guarantees it and is stable, but needs O(n) extra memory. Quicksort is in-place and usually faster in practice, but it isn't stable and has an O(n²) worst case.

Let me show you side by side.

Merge sortQuicksort
Average / worstO(n log n) / O(n log n)O(n log n) / O(n²)
Extra spaceO(n) bufferO(log n) stack expected
StableYesNo
Where the work isCombine step (merge)Split step (partition)
Best fitLinked lists, external sorting, stable object sortsIn-memory primitive arrays

The reason quicksort wins on arrays in practice is cache behaviour. Partitioning scans memory sequentially and swaps in place, and there's no copying to a buffer and back. Merge sort's buffer doubles the memory traffic.

But merge sort is the natural choice for linked lists. Splitting and merging are just pointer changes, so no buffer is needed, and it doesn't need random access. It's also the basis of external sorting, where I sort chunks that fit in memory and then k-way merge files from disk.

So I'd say: quicksort for raw speed on arrays, merge sort when I need a guarantee, stability, or I'm sorting something that isn't an array.

IntermediateWhat is quicksort's worst case, and how do you avoid it?

So the worst case is O(n²). It happens when the pivot is always the smallest or largest element, so every partition peels off just one element. The textbook trigger is picking the last element as pivot on an array that's already sorted.

Let me show you with a quick example. I counted comparisons with a "last element" pivot on sorted input.

static void naiveQuick(int[] a, int lo, int hi) {
    if (lo >= hi) return;
    int pivot = a[hi], i = lo;                 // always the LAST element
    for (int j = lo; j < hi; j++) { comparisons++; if (a[j] < pivot) swap(a, i++, j); }
    swap(a, i, hi);
    naiveQuick(a, lo, i - 1);
    naiveQuick(a, i + 1, hi);
}
// sorted input, n = 1,000:  499,500 comparisons   (n(n-1)/2)
// sorted input, n = 10,000: 49,995,000 comparisons

What happens here is that 10× the input gives 100× the work. That's the quadratic signature. Recursion depth also becomes n, so a big enough input throws StackOverflowError as well.

How I avoid it:

  • Random pivot (or median-of-three). Then no fixed input is bad, and the expected time is O(n log n).
  • Three-way partition (<, =, >) for many duplicates. Otherwise an all-equal array is also quadratic.
  • Recurse on the smaller side, loop on the larger. That caps the stack at O(log n).
  • Introsort fallback: if the depth passes about 2 log n, switch to heap sort. That's a hard O(n log n) guarantee, and it's what C++ std::sort does and what recent JDKs do for primitives.

Interviewers look for: the duplicates case. Many candidates fix sorted input with a random pivot and forget that all-equal keys break two-way partitioning too.

IntermediateWhat sort does Java's Arrays.sort use, and why is it different for primitives and objects?

So for primitive arrays like int[], Java uses dual-pivot quicksort, with insertion sort for tiny ranges and a heap sort fallback in modern JDKs. For objects, meaning Arrays.sort(T[]), Collections.sort and List.sort, it uses TimSort, which is a stable merge-sort hybrid. The reason is stability.

Let me show you the breakdown.

CallAlgorithmGuarantees
Arrays.sort(int[]) (any primitive)Dual-pivot quicksortO(n log n), in-place, not stable
Arrays.sort(T[]), List.sort, Collections.sortTimSortStable, O(n log n) worst, O(n) on already-sorted runs, O(n) extra memory
Arrays.parallelSortParallel merge sort (fork/join)Worth it only for large arrays

Two equal ints are indistinguishable. You can't tell which 5 came first, so stability means nothing, and Java picks the fastest in-place option. Two "equal" objects can differ in every other field, like two employees with the same salary. Users expect the earlier order to survive, so objects get a stable sort. The extra memory is acceptable there, because the array only holds references.

TimSort is also tuned for real data, which is often partly sorted. It finds existing runs and merges them, so sorted input costs O(n).

One gotcha I've hit: a comparator written as (a, b) -> a - b. Subtraction overflows.

Integer[] boxed = {Integer.MIN_VALUE, 1, 0};
Arrays.sort(boxed, (x, y) -> x - y);       // [0, 1, -2147483648]  wrong
Arrays.sort(boxed, Integer::compare);      // [-2147483648, 0, 1]  right

Interviewers look for: the stability reason, stated plainly. Bonus points for knowing TimSort throws "Comparison method violates its general contract" when your comparator isn't transitive.

IntermediateCan you sort faster than O(n log n)?

So not with a comparison sort. Any algorithm that only asks "is a < b?" needs Ω(n log n) comparisons in the worst case. But if I can look at the values themselves, and they're integers in a small range or fixed-width keys, counting sort and radix sort run in linear time.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int[] countingSort(int[] a, int k) {    // values in [0, k]
    int[] count = new int[k + 1];
    for (int x : a) count[x]++;
    int[] out = new int[a.length];
    int idx = 0;
    for (int v = 0; v <= k; v++)
        while (count[v]-- > 0) out[idx++] = v;
    return out;
}
// {4, 2, 2, 8, 3, 3, 1}, k = 8 -> [1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 4, 8]    O(n + k)

Why the lower bound holds: there are n! possible orderings of the input. Each comparison has two outcomes, so after c comparisons I can tell apart at most 2c cases. I need 2c ≥ n!, so c ≥ log2(n!), which is about n log n.

When linear sorts fit:

  • Counting sort: small range k, like ages, grades or chars. O(n + k). Useless if k is 109.
  • Radix sort: a stable counting sort per digit. O(d(n + b)). Good for 32-bit ints or phone numbers.
  • Bucket sort: floats spread evenly over a known range. O(n) on average, O(n²) if everything lands in one bucket.

So in practice, if the problem says "values are between 0 and 100" or "lowercase letters only", I think counting array first.

AdvancedFind the k-th largest element. Would you sort, use a heap, or use quickselect?

So all three work. Sorting is O(n log n) and the simplest. A min-heap of size k is O(n log k), with a guaranteed bound and it works on a stream. Quickselect is O(n) expected, but it's O(n²) in the worst case and it reorders the array. In an interview I usually offer the heap first and then quickselect as the optimisation.

Let me show you both.

static int kthLargestHeap(int[] nums, int k) {
    PriorityQueue<Integer> heap = new PriorityQueue<>();   // min-heap of the k largest
    for (int x : nums) {
        heap.offer(x);
        if (heap.size() > k) heap.poll();                  // drop the smallest
    }
    return heap.peek();
}

static int findKthLargest(int[] nums, int k) {
    int target = nums.length - k;              // index in sorted order
    int lo = 0, hi = nums.length - 1;
    while (lo < hi) {
        int p = partition(nums, lo, hi);       // random-pivot Lomuto
        if (p == target) return nums[p];
        if (p < target) lo = p + 1; else hi = p - 1;
    }
    return nums[lo];
}
// {3,2,1,5,6,4}, k=2 -> 5      {3,2,3,1,2,4,5,5,6}, k=4 -> 4   (both methods)

What happens in quickselect is that the pivot lands at its final sorted index p. If that's the target I'm done. Otherwise the answer is only on one side, so I recurse into that side and throw the other away. The expected work is n + n/2 + n/4 + ..., which is about 2n.

The heap keeps the k largest seen so far, and its top is the smallest of them, which is the k-th largest.

MethodTimeSpaceNotes
SortO(n log n)O(1) to O(n)Simplest
Min-heap size kO(n log k)O(k)Streaming, guaranteed
QuickselectO(n) expectedO(1)Mutates input

Interviewers look for: "what if the data doesn't fit in memory?" The answer is the heap. It only ever holds k elements.

20. Binary Search

BasicWrite binary search. What are the common bugs?

So binary search looks at the middle of a sorted range, decides which half the target must be in, and throws the other half away. That makes it O(log n): a million elements take about 20 steps. The code is short, but almost every bug is at the boundaries.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int binarySearch(int[] a, int target) {
    int lo = 0, hi = a.length - 1;             // closed range [lo, hi]
    while (lo <= hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;          // no overflow
        if (a[mid] == target) return mid;
        if (a[mid] < target) lo = mid + 1;
        else hi = mid - 1;
    }
    return -1;
}
// {1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11}: search 7 -> 3, search 4 -> -1

The bugs I watch for:

  • (lo + hi) / 2 overflow. With lo = 2,000,000,000 and hi = 2,100,000,000, the sum wraps and gives -97483648. lo + (hi - lo) / 2 gives 2050000000.
  • Mixing styles. A closed range [lo, hi] goes with lo <= hi and hi = mid - 1. A half-open range [lo, hi) goes with lo < hi and hi = mid. Mixing them gives infinite loops or missed elements.
  • lo = mid with a rounded-down mid loops forever when hi = lo + 1.

So I pick one style per problem and say the invariant out loud: "if the target exists, it's inside [lo, hi]".

Interviewers look for: the overflow fix without prompting. It's a famous bug that sat in the JDK's own binary search for years.

IntermediateHow do you find the first and last position of a target in a sorted array with duplicates?

So I use a lower-bound search: find the first index where a[i] >= target. That's the first occurrence. The last occurrence is one before the first index where a[i] > target. Two O(log n) searches, and no linear scan over the duplicates.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int lowerBound(int[] a, int target) {   // first i with a[i] >= target
    int lo = 0, hi = a.length;                 // half-open [lo, hi)
    while (lo < hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (a[mid] >= target) hi = mid;        // mid might be the answer
        else lo = mid + 1;
    }
    return lo;
}
static int[] searchRange(int[] a, int t) {
    int first = lowerBound(a, t);
    if (first == a.length || a[first] != t) return new int[]{-1, -1};
    int last = lowerBound(a, t + 1) - 1;       // first index > t, minus one
    return new int[]{first, last};
}
// {5, 7, 7, 8, 8, 10}, 8 -> [3, 4]     target 6 -> [-1, -1]

What happens here is that when I find an 8, I don't stop. I keep it as a candidate with hi = mid and keep looking left. The loop ends when lo == hi, and that's the boundary.

Why not find any 8 and walk outward? If the whole array is 8s, that walk is O(n).

The t + 1 trick works for ints unless t is Integer.MAX_VALUE. In that case I write a separate upper bound with >. Also note that Java's Arrays.binarySearch returns some matching index, not necessarily the first.

Interviewers look for: that this one template also gives insert position, count of t, floor and ceiling. It's the "first true" pattern.

IntermediateSearch in a rotated sorted array. Walk me through it.

So in an array like [4, 5, 6, 7, 0, 1, 2], if I cut at any mid, at least one half is normally sorted. I check which half that is, and whether the target falls inside its range. If it does, I go there. If not, it can only be in the other half. Still O(log n).

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int searchRotated(int[] a, int target) {
    int lo = 0, hi = a.length - 1;
    while (lo <= hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (a[mid] == target) return mid;
        if (a[lo] <= a[mid]) {                             // left half sorted
            if (a[lo] <= target && target < a[mid]) hi = mid - 1;
            else lo = mid + 1;
        } else {                                           // right half sorted
            if (a[mid] < target && target <= a[hi]) lo = mid + 1;
            else hi = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return -1;
}
// {4,5,6,7,0,1,2}, 0 -> 4      target 3 -> -1

Tracing target 0: lo=0, hi=6, mid=3 with value 7. Since a[0]=4 <= 7, the left half is sorted. Is 0 in [4, 7)? No, so lo=4. Now mid=5 with value 1. a[4]=0 <= 1, so the left half [0, 1] is sorted. Is 0 in [0, 1)? Yes, so hi=4. Then mid=4 finds 0.

The <= in a[lo] <= a[mid] matters. When lo == mid, the "left half" is a single element and counts as sorted.

So the key idea I'd say out loud is that binary search needs a rule that tells me which half keeps the answer. It doesn't need a fully sorted array.

Interviewers look for: the follow-up "what if there are duplicates?" With [1, 1, 1, 3, 1] you can't tell which side is sorted, so you shrink with lo++, and the worst case becomes O(n).

IntermediateWhat is "binary search on the answer"? Give an example.

So instead of searching an array, I search the range of possible answers. It works when there's a yes/no check that's monotonic: once some value works, every bigger value also works. Then the answers look like F F F T T T, and I binary search for the first T. The signal words are "minimum speed", "smallest capacity" or "minimise the maximum".

Let me show you with Koko Eating Bananas: find the slowest speed k that finishes all piles within h hours.

static int minEatingSpeed(int[] piles, int h) {
    int lo = 1, hi = Arrays.stream(piles).max().getAsInt();
    while (lo < hi) {
        int k = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (hours(piles, k) <= h) hi = k;       // fast enough: try slower
        else lo = k + 1;                        // too slow
    }
    return lo;
}
static long hours(int[] piles, int k) {
    long total = 0;
    for (int p : piles) total += (p + k - 1) / k;   // ceil(p / k)
    return total;
}
// {3,6,7,11}, h=8 -> 4     {30,11,23,4,20}, h=5 -> 30     h=6 -> 23

What happens here is the range is [1, max pile]. Speed 1 might be too slow, and max pile always finishes in n hours. Each check is O(n), and I do log(max) of them, so the total is O(n log M).

The same shape solves Capacity to Ship Packages, Split Array Largest Sum, and Minimum Days to Make Bouquets. Only the check function changes.

One gotcha I've hit is summing hours in an int. With big piles and speed 1, it overflows, so I use long.

AdvancedCan binary search work on an unsorted array? When exactly is it valid?

So yes. Binary search needs a rule that, looking only at mid, guarantees which half still contains an answer. Sortedness is one such rule, but not the only one. Find Peak Element is the classic example on unsorted data.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int findPeak(int[] a) {
    int lo = 0, hi = a.length - 1;
    while (lo < hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (a[mid] < a[mid + 1]) lo = mid + 1;   // uphill: peak on the right
        else hi = mid;                           // downhill: peak at mid or left
    }
    return lo;
}
// {1, 2, 3, 1} -> 2      {1, 2, 1, 3, 5, 6, 4} -> 5

Why it works: if a[mid] < a[mid + 1], I'm walking uphill to the right. Either it keeps rising to the end, and the last element is a peak because the edges count as minus infinity, or it drops somewhere, and that turn is a peak. Either way the right side has one. The invariant is "a peak exists in [lo, hi]".

So the general condition is a monotonic predicate over the index or value space: F F F T T T. Before coding, I write the F/T pattern out for a small example. If it looks like F T F T, binary search will give random wrong answers.

Note it finds a peak, not the highest one. Finding the global maximum of an unsorted array still needs O(n).

Interviewers look for: the invariant stated in words, and the distinction between "a peak" and "the maximum".

AdvancedHow would you binary search when you don't know the size of the data, like a huge or unbounded sorted stream?

So I use exponential search, also called galloping. I probe indexes 1, 2, 4, 8, and so on until I hit a value >= target or run past the end. That gives me a bracket [hi/2, hi] that must contain the target. Then I do a normal binary search inside it. Both phases are O(log p), where p is the target's position, not the total size.

Let me show you with a quick example. Here the reader returns Integer.MAX_VALUE past the end, like LeetCode's ArrayReader.

static int searchUnknownSize(IntUnaryOperator get, int target) {
    int hi = 1;
    while (get.applyAsInt(hi) < target) hi *= 2;    // gallop: 1, 2, 4, 8, ...
    int lo = hi / 2;
    while (lo <= hi) {                              // normal search in [hi/2, hi]
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        int v = get.applyAsInt(mid);
        if (v == target) return mid;
        if (v < target) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid - 1;
    }
    return -1;
}
// data = 0, 3, 6, ..., 2997 (1000 items): 2997 -> 999, 300 -> 100, 301 -> -1, 0 -> 0

The same idea shows up in real systems. Searching a sorted log file by timestamp, I don't read the whole file. I seek to byte offsets, and I can binary search offsets directly because I know the file size. Databases do the same with B-tree pages, where each node is a sorted array searched with binary search.

TimSort uses galloping too. When one run keeps winning during a merge, it gallops ahead instead of comparing one element at a time.

So when someone says "the input is too huge", my first question is whether it's sorted and seekable. If yes, logarithmic probes beat loading everything.

21. Recursion & Backtracking

BasicWhat is backtracking, and how is it different from plain recursion or brute force?

So backtracking is organised brute force. I build a solution one choice at a time. At each step I choose an option, recurse to explore everything that follows, then undo the choice and try the next one. What makes it better than blind brute force is pruning: the moment a partial solution can't possibly work, I abandon that whole branch.

Let me show you with the template I use.

void backtrack(List<Integer> path, int start) {
    if (isComplete(path)) {
        result.add(new ArrayList<>(path));    // record a COPY
        return;
    }
    for (int choice : choices(start)) {
        if (!isValid(path, choice)) continue;  // prune
        path.add(choice);                      // choose
        backtrack(path, choice + 1);           // explore
        path.remove(path.size() - 1);          // un-choose
    }
}

What happens here is a depth-first walk over a decision tree. The root is the empty solution, each edge is a choice, and each leaf is either an answer or a dead end. The "un-choose" line is what makes it backtracking. It restores the shared state, so the next sibling branch starts clean.

Every backtracking solution is recursion, but not every recursion is backtracking. Merge sort recurses without choices or undoing.

The signal I look for is "return all ..." or "does any arrangement exist?" with small n, around 10 to 20. The output itself is exponential, so nothing faster exists.

IntermediateGenerate all subsets of an array. What's the complexity, and what's the classic bug?

So every node of the decision tree is a subset. I record the current path on entry, then for each later element I add it, recurse, and remove it. There are 2n subsets and each is copied in O(n), so it's O(n · 2n) time.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static void backtrack(int[] nums, int start, List<Integer> path, List<List<Integer>> res) {
    res.add(new ArrayList<>(path));          // record a COPY of this node
    for (int i = start; i < nums.length; i++) {
        path.add(nums[i]);                   // choose
        backtrack(nums, i + 1, path, res);   // explore
        path.remove(path.size() - 1);        // un-choose
    }
}
// {1, 2, 3} -> [[], [1], [1, 2], [1, 2, 3], [1, 3], [2], [2, 3], [3]]

The start index is what prevents duplicates. I only ever add elements to the right of the last one picked, so [2, 1] is never built. Each subset appears in exactly one order.

The classic bug is res.add(path) instead of res.add(new ArrayList<>(path)). I ran it to be sure:

// res.add(path)  ->  [[], [], [], [], [], [], [], []]

All eight entries are references to the same list. After every un-choose it ends up empty. So I always store a snapshot.

Space is O(n) for recursion and path, not counting the output. The alternative is bitmasks: loop mask from 0 to 2n - 1 and take the elements whose bits are set. Same complexity, no recursion.

Interviewers look for: the copy bug, and "what if the input has duplicates?" (Subsets II: sort, then skip nums[i] == nums[i-1] when i > start).

IntermediateGenerate permutations when the input has duplicates. How do you avoid repeated results?

So permutations differ from subsets because order matters. Every position may take any unused element, so I loop from 0 and track used[]. With duplicates, I sort first so equal values are adjacent. Then I skip a value if it equals the previous one and that previous copy isn't currently in the path. That forces equal values to be used left to right, so each distinct arrangement is built once.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static List<List<Integer>> permuteUnique(int[] nums) {
    Arrays.sort(nums);                               // duplicates side by side
    List<List<Integer>> res = new ArrayList<>();
    permBt(nums, new boolean[nums.length], new ArrayList<>(), res);
    return res;
}
static void permBt(int[] nums, boolean[] used, List<Integer> path, List<List<Integer>> res) {
    if (path.size() == nums.length) { res.add(new ArrayList<>(path)); return; }
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        if (used[i]) continue;
        if (i > 0 && nums[i] == nums[i - 1] && !used[i - 1]) continue;   // skip repeat
        used[i] = true; path.add(nums[i]);
        permBt(nums, used, path, res);
        path.remove(path.size() - 1); used[i] = false;
    }
}
// {1, 1, 2} -> [[1, 1, 2], [1, 2, 1], [2, 1, 1]]   (3, not 6)

What happens here is that at the top level, choosing the second 1 first would build exactly the same trees as choosing the first 1. The skip condition cuts that branch. Without it I'd get 6 results with each one twice.

I could also dump everything into a HashSet, but that still does all n! work plus hashing. Pruning avoids the work entirely.

Time is O(n · n!) in the worst case. Note that used[] is part of the state, so it has to be un-chosen too.

IntermediateHow would you solve N-Queens? How do you check attacks in O(1)?

So I place exactly one queen per row, so the only choice is the column. For the attack check I keep three boolean arrays: columns, "/" diagonals, and "\" diagonals. On a "/" diagonal, r + c is constant. On a "\" diagonal, r - c is constant, and I shift it by n - 1 to make it a valid index. So each check is three array lookups.

Let me show you with a quick example that counts solutions.

static int place(int r, int n, boolean[] cols, boolean[] diag, boolean[] anti) {
    if (r == n) return 1;                              // all rows filled
    int count = 0;
    for (int c = 0; c < n; c++) {
        int d = r + c, x = r - c + n - 1;              // diagonal ids
        if (cols[c] || diag[d] || anti[x]) continue;   // attacked: prune
        cols[c] = diag[d] = anti[x] = true;
        count += place(r + 1, n, cols, diag, anti);
        cols[c] = diag[d] = anti[x] = false;           // un-choose
    }
    return count;
}
// place(0, n, new boolean[n], new boolean[2n-1], new boolean[2n-1])
// n = 1..8: 1 0 0 2 10 4 40 92

What happens here is that row conflicts are impossible by construction. Column and diagonal conflicts are rejected before placing, so every board that reaches r == n is valid. I never have to check a full board.

A naive approach scans the board for each placement, which is O(n) per check. The arrays make it O(1). The time is bounded by O(n!), because row 0 has n options, row 1 at most n - 1, and so on. Pruning makes the real number far smaller.

Interviewers look for: the r + c / r - c diagonal trick. For a senior follow-up, the three arrays can become three int bitmasks.

AdvancedYour backtracking solution times out. How do you speed it up, and when do you switch to DP?

So first I prune harder, and then I ask whether the question really needs every solution. If it only asks to count them, or for the best one, and the same sub-state keeps coming back, that's DP, and memoising the recursion can drop the cost from exponential to polynomial.

Let me show you a prune with Combination Sum.

static void comb(int[] c, int remain, int start, List<Integer> path, List<List<Integer>> res) {
    if (remain == 0) { res.add(new ArrayList<>(path)); return; }
    for (int i = start; i < c.length; i++) {
        if (c[i] > remain) break;               // sorted: everything after is too big
        path.add(c[i]);
        comb(c, remain - c[i], i, path, res);   // i, not i + 1: reuse allowed
        path.remove(path.size() - 1);
    }
}
// sorted {2,3,6,7}, 7 -> [[2, 2, 3], [7]]     {2,3,5}, 8 -> [[2,2,2,2], [2,3,3], [3,5]]

The break kills a whole subtree at once. That only works because I sorted first. My checklist of speed-ups:

  • Sort and break early once candidates exceed the remaining budget.
  • Order choices well: most constrained first. In Sudoku, fill the cell with the fewest options.
  • O(1) validity checks with boolean arrays or bitmasks instead of rescanning.
  • Precompute: in Palindrome Partitioning, a table of which substrings are palindromes.
  • Memoise when the state repeats.

The DP switch: Combination Sum IV asks only for the count. The state is just "remaining amount", so there are only target + 1 states, and dp[t] = sum of dp[t - c] is O(target · n). Listing all combinations would be exponential.

Interviewers look for: "list all" means backtracking, "count or optimise" means check for overlapping subproblems.

22. Greedy

BasicWhat is a greedy algorithm, and when does it work?

So a greedy algorithm builds the answer step by step and at each step takes the option that looks best right now, without ever revisiting that choice. It works only when two properties hold. First, the greedy choice property: some optimal solution starts with the greedy choice. Second, optimal substructure: after that choice, what's left is a smaller version of the same problem.

Let me show you with a quick example: fractional knapsack, where I can take part of an item.

// items (weight, value): (10, 60), (20, 100), (30, 120); capacity 50
// sort by value/weight: 6.0, 5.0, 4.0
// take all of item 1 (60), all of item 2 (100), 20/30 of item 3 (80)
// total = 240.0

Greedy by value-per-kilo is optimal here. Any kilo spent on a worse ratio could be swapped for a better one without losing value.

But 0/1 knapsack, where I can't split items, breaks the same greedy. With capacity 50 it takes items 1 and 2 for 160, while items 2 and 3 give 220. That one needs DP.

What I like about greedy is that when it works, it's usually "sort, then one pass", O(n log n), with tiny code. The risk is that a greedy idea that feels right can be wrong. So I always try to break it with a small counterexample before coding.

Interviewers look for: fractional vs 0/1 knapsack as the example of the same problem flipping from greedy to DP.

BasicWhy does greedy fail for coin change?

So "always take the biggest coin that fits" is only optimal for certain coin systems, called canonical ones, like US or Indian coins. For arbitrary denominations it can lose, because taking the big coin now can force several small coins later.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int greedyCoins(int[] coinsDesc, int amount) {
    int count = 0;
    for (int c : coinsDesc) { count += amount / c; amount %= c; }
    return amount == 0 ? count : -1;
}
// coins {4, 3, 1}, amount 6: greedy 4 + 1 + 1 = 3 coins, optimal 3 + 3 = 2 coins
// coins {25, 10, 5, 1}, amount 63: greedy 6 coins, which is optimal

What happens here is that greedy commits to the 4 and never reconsiders. Once 4 is used, the remaining 2 can only be made with two 1s. The greedy choice property fails: no optimal solution for 6 starts with a 4.

Greedy can even fail to find any answer at all. With coins {5, 3} and amount 9, it takes 5, then 3, and is left with 1 it can't pay. Meanwhile 3 + 3 + 3 works.

The fix is DP: dp[a] = min over coins c of dp[a - c] + 1, in O(amount × coins). It tries every "last coin" instead of committing to one.

So in practice this is my go-to sanity check. If I'm tempted by a greedy rule, I spend a minute hunting for a tiny input like {1, 3, 4} that breaks it.

IntermediateFor interval scheduling, why sort by end time and not start time?

So to keep the most non-overlapping intervals, I always keep the one that finishes first. It leaves the most room for everything after it. Sorting by start time fails because one long interval that starts early blocks many short ones.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int maxNonOverlapping(int[][] intervals) {
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]));  // by END
    int kept = 0, lastEnd = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
    for (int[] in : intervals) {
        if (in[0] >= lastEnd) { kept++; lastEnd = in[1]; }
    }
    return kept;
}
// {1,10}, {2,3}, {4,5}, {6,7}
// by end   -> keeps [2,3], [4,5], [6,7] = 3
// by start -> keeps [1,10] only         = 1

Why it's correct, with the exchange argument: take any optimal schedule. Its first interval ends at or after the earliest-ending interval overall. If I swap that first interval for the earliest-ending one, nothing after it conflicts, because it ends even sooner. So an optimal schedule exists that starts with the greedy choice. Then I repeat on what's left.

LeetCode 435, Non-overlapping Intervals, asks for the minimum number to remove. That's just n - kept. The whole thing is O(n log n) for the sort plus one pass.

Interviewers look for: the counterexample for start-time sorting, and the exchange argument in your own words. Also watch >= vs >: whether [1,2] and [2,3] overlap depends on the problem.

IntermediateJump Game: can you reach the last index, and what's the minimum number of jumps?

So for "can I reach the end", I track the farthest index reachable so far. If I ever stand on an index beyond it, I'm stuck. For "minimum jumps", I treat it like BFS in levels without a queue. All indexes reachable in one jump are level 1, and so on, and I count how many levels it takes.

Let me show you both.

static boolean canJump(int[] nums) {
    int farthest = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        if (i > farthest) return false;               // can't even stand here
        farthest = Math.max(farthest, i + nums[i]);
    }
    return true;
}
static int jump(int[] nums) {
    int jumps = 0, curEnd = 0, farthest = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length - 1; i++) {       // no jump needed from the last
        farthest = Math.max(farthest, i + nums[i]);
        if (i == curEnd) { jumps++; curEnd = farthest; }   // used up this level
    }
    return jumps;
}
// canJump {2,3,1,1,4} -> true, {3,2,1,0,4} -> false;   jump {2,3,1,1,4} -> 2

The insight for canJump is that every index up to farthest is reachable, because I can always jump shorter than the max. So I don't need to know which jumps to take, only how far the reachable region extends. That's O(n) time and O(1) space. A DP over "can I reach i" would be O(n²).

For jump, curEnd is the edge of the current level. When I reach it, I must spend another jump, and the new edge is the farthest point seen in this level.

One gotcha I've hit: looping to nums.length instead of nums.length - 1. If a level ends exactly on the last index, that counts one extra jump.

IntermediateGas Station: explain the one-pass solution.

So two facts make it one pass. First, if total gas >= total cost, a valid start exists. Second, if I start at s and run dry before reaching station i + 1, then no station between s and i can be the start either. So I jump the start straight to i + 1 and reset the tank.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int canCompleteCircuit(int[] gas, int[] cost) {
    int total = 0, tank = 0, start = 0;
    for (int i = 0; i < gas.length; i++) {
        int diff = gas[i] - cost[i];
        total += diff;
        tank += diff;
        if (tank < 0) { start = i + 1; tank = 0; }   // nothing in [start..i] works
    }
    return total >= 0 ? start : -1;
}
// gas {1,2,3,4,5}, cost {3,4,5,1,2} -> 3      gas {2,3,4}, cost {3,4,3} -> -1

Why the second fact holds: any station k between start and i was reached with a tank >= 0. Starting at k with an empty tank can only be worse, so it would also fail at i. That's why I never need to go back and retry them.

Tracing the first example: the diffs are -2, -2, -2, 3, 3. The tank goes negative at 0, 1 and 2, moving start to 3. Then 3 + 3 = 6 stays positive. The total is 0, which is >= 0, so the answer is 3.

So the brute force of trying every start is O(n²), and this is O(n) time, O(1) space.

Interviewers look for: the proof of why skipping to i + 1 is safe. The code is easy to memorise, the reasoning is what they're testing.

AdvancedMinimum meeting rooms. How would you solve it, and how do you prove a greedy correct in general?

So the answer is the maximum number of meetings happening at the same moment. I sort by start time and keep a min-heap of end times, one per busy room. For each meeting, if the earliest-ending room is free by then, I reuse it. Otherwise I open a new room. The heap size at the end is the answer.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int minMeetingRooms(int[][] meetings) {
    Arrays.sort(meetings, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));   // by start
    PriorityQueue<Integer> ends = new PriorityQueue<>();            // end time per busy room
    for (int[] m : meetings) {
        if (!ends.isEmpty() && ends.peek() <= m[0]) ends.poll();     // reuse earliest-free room
        ends.offer(m[1]);
    }
    return ends.size();
}
// {{0,30},{5,10},{15,20}} -> 2      {{1,5},{2,6},{3,7},{5,8}} -> 3

In the second example, [5, 8] reuses the room freed by [1, 5], so three rooms are enough. Time is O(n log n), space O(n).

How I prove a greedy in general, with the exchange argument: take any optimal solution. Find the first place it differs from the greedy one. Show that swapping its choice for the greedy choice keeps the solution valid and no worse. Repeat, and the optimal solution becomes the greedy one without getting worse, so greedy is optimal. Here the proof is even simpler. A new room only opens when every existing room is busy at that start time, so at that moment there really are that many overlapping meetings. No schedule can use fewer.

Interviewers look for: the lower-bound argument ("at time t, k meetings overlap, so k rooms is necessary"). It shows you understand why the answer is optimal, not just how to compute it.

23. Divide & Conquer

BasicWhat is divide and conquer, and how is it different from dynamic programming?

So divide and conquer solves a problem in three moves: split it into smaller pieces of the same kind, solve each piece recursively, and combine the answers. The difference from DP is whether the pieces overlap. In D&C they're independent, like the two halves in merge sort, so nothing is solved twice and there's nothing to cache. In DP the same subproblem comes up again and again, so I store it.

Let me show you with a quick comparison.

AlgorithmSplitCombineSubproblems overlap?
Merge sortMiddle, O(1)Merge, O(n)No
QuicksortPartition, O(n)NothingNo
Binary searchCompare with middleNothing (one side only)No
Naive Fibonaccifib(n-1), fib(n-2)AddYes, so use DP

What happens with naive Fibonacci is that it looks like D&C, but fib(n-1) and fib(n-2) both recompute fib(n-3), and so on down. The call tree blows up to O(2n). A memo fixes it. That's the moment it becomes DP.

Notice the work sits in different places. Merge sort's split is trivial and the merge is the real work. Quicksort's partition is the real work and there's no combine.

So my quick test is: draw the recursion tree for a small input. If the same arguments appear in two branches, I add a memo. If not, plain recursion is already optimal.

Interviewers look for: "overlapping subproblems" as the one-line difference.

IntermediateHow do you find the time complexity of a recursive algorithm? Explain the master theorem.

So I write the running time as a recurrence, T(n) = a T(n/b) + f(n): a subproblems, each 1/b the size, plus f(n) work to split and combine. Then I either sum the recursion tree level by level, or use the master theorem as a shortcut. For f(n) = O(nd), I compare d with logb a.

Let me show you with the three cases and common examples.

CaseResultMeaning
d > logb aO(nd)The top-level work dominates
d = logb aO(nd log n)Every level costs the same
d < logb aO(nlogb a)The leaves dominate
AlgorithmRecurrenceResult
Binary searchT(n/2) + O(1)O(log n)
Merge sort2T(n/2) + O(n)O(n log n)
Quickselect (average)T(n/2) + O(n)O(n)
Balanced tree traversal2T(n/2) + O(1)O(n)
Karatsuba3T(n/2) + O(n)O(n1.585)

For merge sort, a = 2, b = 2, d = 1, and log2 2 = 1 = d. So it's the middle case, O(n log n). Intuitively, each level does n total work, because the pieces double in number while halving in size, and there are log n levels.

One gotcha: the theorem needs equal-sized splits. Quicksort's worst case is T(n) = T(n-1) + O(n), which isn't covered. I sum it directly: n + (n-1) + ... + 1 = O(n²).

IntermediateImplement pow(x, n) in O(log n).

So I use xn = (xn/2)² when n is even, and x · (xn/2)² when it's odd. That's one half-size subproblem per level, so O(log n). The key detail is computing the half once and squaring it.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static double myPow(double x, int n) {
    long N = n;                          // -Integer.MIN_VALUE overflows int
    if (N < 0) { x = 1 / x; N = -N; }
    return pow(x, N);
}
static double pow(double x, long n) {
    if (n == 0) return 1.0;
    double half = pow(x, n / 2);         // ONE recursive call
    return (n % 2 == 0) ? half * half : half * half * x;
}
// myPow(2.0, 10) = 1024.0    myPow(2.0, -2) = 0.25    myPow(1.0, Integer.MIN_VALUE) = 1.0

Two gotchas I watch for. First, writing pow(x, n/2) * pow(x, n/2) makes two calls per level. That's T(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(1), which is O(n) again. I counted it: for n = 1024 that version makes 4,095 calls, while the version above makes 12. Second, n = Integer.MIN_VALUE has no positive int counterpart, so -n overflows. That's why I widen to long before negating.

The iterative version walks the bits of n. 13 is 1101 in binary, so x13 = x8 · x4 · x1. That's O(1) space. With % mod after each multiply, it becomes the modPow used in almost every "answer mod 109+7" problem.

Interviewers look for: the Integer.MIN_VALUE edge case and the "two calls makes it linear" trap.

IntermediateSolve Maximum Subarray with divide and conquer. How does it compare to Kadane's?

So the best subarray is in one of three places: entirely in the left half, entirely in the right half, or crossing the middle. The first two come from recursion. The crossing one is the best suffix of the left half plus the best prefix of the right half, which I find with two linear scans. That's T(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(n), so O(n log n).

Let me show you both.

static int best(int[] a, int lo, int hi) {
    if (lo == hi) return a[lo];
    int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
    int left = best(a, lo, mid), right = best(a, mid + 1, hi);
    int sum = 0, bestLeft = Integer.MIN_VALUE;          // best suffix of left half
    for (int i = mid; i >= lo; i--) { sum += a[i]; bestLeft = Math.max(bestLeft, sum); }
    sum = 0;
    int bestRight = Integer.MIN_VALUE;                  // best prefix of right half
    for (int j = mid + 1; j <= hi; j++) { sum += a[j]; bestRight = Math.max(bestRight, sum); }
    return Math.max(Math.max(left, right), bestLeft + bestRight);
}
static int kadane(int[] a) {
    int cur = a[0], best = a[0];
    for (int i = 1; i < a.length; i++) {
        cur = Math.max(a[i], cur + a[i]);   // extend the run or restart here
        best = Math.max(best, cur);
    }
    return best;
}
// {-2,1,-3,4,-1,2,1,-5,4} -> 6 (both)      {-3,-1,-2} -> -1 (both)

Kadane is O(n) time and O(1) space, so in an interview it's the answer I'd give. It's really a one-dimensional DP: the best run ending at i is either the previous run extended, or a fresh start at a[i].

The D&C version is still worth knowing. It parallelises naturally, and if each node also stores its total, best prefix and best suffix, it becomes a segment tree that answers "max subarray in [l, r]" with updates in O(log n). Kadane can't do that.

Interviewers look for: the all-negative case. Initialising best = 0 returns 0 for {-3, -1, -2}, but the answer is -1.

AdvancedCount inversions in O(n log n). Why does merge sort make it possible?

So an inversion is a pair i < j with a[i] > a[j]. Brute force checks every pair, which is O(n²). With merge sort, inversions inside each half are counted recursively. The ones that cross the halves are counted during the merge: when I take an element from the right half, it's smaller than every element still waiting in the left half, so all of those are inversions with it at once.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static long countInv(int[] a, int[] tmp, int lo, int hi) {
    if (lo >= hi) return 0;
    int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
    long count = countInv(a, tmp, lo, mid) + countInv(a, tmp, mid + 1, hi);
    int i = lo, j = mid + 1, k = lo;
    while (i <= mid && j <= hi) {
        if (a[i] <= a[j]) tmp[k++] = a[i++];
        else { count += mid - i + 1; tmp[k++] = a[j++]; }   // a[i..mid] all > a[j]
    }
    while (i <= mid) tmp[k++] = a[i++];
    while (j <= hi) tmp[k++] = a[j++];
    System.arraycopy(tmp, lo, a, lo, hi - lo + 1);
    return count;
}
// {2, 4, 1, 3, 5} -> 3   (pairs 2>1, 4>1, 4>3)

This shows when D&C actually wins: the combine step must be cheaper than brute-forcing across the split. Checking every left-right pair is O(n²). But because both halves are already sorted, mid - i + 1 counts a whole block of pairs in O(1). So the combine is O(n), and the total is O(n log n).

Details I care about: <= in the comparison, so equal elements aren't counted as inversions. A long for the count, because n = 105 can give about 5 × 109. And sorting a copy if the caller still needs the original.

In practice, inversion count measures how far apart two rankings are, like comparing two users' movie preferences.

AdvancedHow does divide and conquer relate to parallelism? How would you use it in Java?

So because D&C subproblems are independent, they can run on different cores at the same time with no locking. Java's fork/join framework is built exactly for this. A RecursiveTask splits until the piece is small enough, forks one half, computes the other itself, and joins. The pool uses work stealing, so idle threads take pending halves from busy ones.

Let me show you with a quick example: a parallel sum.

class SumTask extends RecursiveTask<Long> {
    private static final int THRESHOLD = 10_000;
    private final int[] a; private final int lo, hi;     // sums a[lo..hi)
    SumTask(int[] a, int lo, int hi) { this.a = a; this.lo = lo; this.hi = hi; }
    @Override protected Long compute() {
        if (hi - lo <= THRESHOLD) {                      // small: just loop
            long s = 0;
            for (int i = lo; i < hi; i++) s += a[i];
            return s;
        }
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        SumTask left = new SumTask(a, lo, mid);
        left.fork();                                     // run left half in parallel
        long right = new SumTask(a, mid, hi).compute();  // do right half ourselves
        return left.join() + right;                      // combine
    }
}
// 5,000,000 ints (i % 100): ForkJoinPool.commonPool().invoke(new SumTask(a, 0, a.length)) = 247500000

What happens here is the threshold matters a lot. Splitting down to single elements would spend more time creating tasks than adding numbers. The pattern of fork one half, compute the other in the current thread, then join, avoids wasting a thread that would otherwise just wait.

This is what Arrays.parallelSort and parallel streams use underneath. At a bigger scale, MapReduce is the same idea across machines: split the data, process pieces independently, combine.

So in practice I only reach for it with large inputs and CPU-bound work. For small arrays the overhead makes it slower than a plain loop.

Interviewers look for: the threshold and the "why not fork both" detail. Those show you've actually used fork/join.

24. Dynamic Programming — the Fundamentals

BasicWhat is dynamic programming?

So dynamic programming is recursion with a memory. I break a problem into smaller versions of itself, solve each distinct one once, store the answer, and reuse it. It applies when two things hold: overlapping subproblems, meaning the same subproblem comes up many times, and optimal substructure, meaning the best answer is built from the best answers to smaller pieces.

Let me show you with a quick example: Fibonacci.

static int fibNaive(int n) {
    if (n <= 1) return n;
    return fibNaive(n - 1) + fibNaive(n - 2);
}
// fibNaive(30) = 832040, but it makes 2,692,537 calls

static long fibMemo(int n, long[] memo) {
    if (n <= 1) return n;
    if (memo[n] != 0) return memo[n];                 // already solved
    return memo[n] = fibMemo(n - 1, memo) + fibMemo(n - 2, memo);
}
// fibMemo(50) = 12586269025 instantly: only 51 distinct states

What happens in the naive version is that fib(28) is computed twice, fib(27) three times, and so on. The call tree grows roughly like 1.6n, but there are only n + 1 distinct questions. The memo turns each repeat into an array lookup, so it's O(n).

On optimal substructure: shortest path has it, since every piece of a shortest path is itself a shortest path. Longest simple path doesn't, because the best sub-paths can reuse the same vertex, so you can't just glue them together.

So the way I describe DP in one line is: exponential recursion, made polynomial by never solving the same state twice. The cost becomes number of states × work per state.

Interviewers look for: the two property names, and "states × transitions" as the way you compute DP complexity.

BasicHow do you know a problem is a DP problem?

So I look for three things. First, the question asks for a count, a min or max, or a yes/no, not a list of every solution. Second, at each step I make a choice that affects what comes after. Third, when I sketch the brute-force recursion, the same arguments show up in different branches. If all three are there, it's DP.

Let me show you the signals I use.

Signal in the questionExamples
"Count the number of ways..."Climbing stairs, unique paths, decode ways, coin change II
"Minimum / maximum cost, profit, length..."Coin change, house robber, LIS, edit distance
"Is it possible to...?"Partition equal subset sum, word break
A choice at each step: take/skip, buy/sell, left/rightKnapsack, stock problems
Sequence, string or grid with n up to ~103–104O(n²) DP fits; n around 20 hints at bitmask DP

Then I rule out the neighbours. If it says "return all combinations", the output is exponential, so it's backtracking. If a simple local rule works, it's greedy. I try to break the greedy with a tiny counterexample, like coins {1, 3, 4} for amount 6, where greedy gives 3 coins and the answer is 2. If the subproblems don't overlap, like merge sort's halves, it's divide and conquer.

The most reliable test is concrete. I write the recursive function, say its parameters out loud, and ask "can two different paths reach the same parameters?" If yes, those parameters are my DP state.

Interviewers look for: that you can name the state before coding. "dp[i] means the fewest coins to make amount i" shows you've recognised it, not just pattern-matched.

IntermediateMemoization vs tabulation: what are the trade-offs?

So memoization is top-down: I write the natural recursion and add a cache. Tabulation is bottom-up: I fill a table from the smallest states upward with loops. Both compute each state once, so the Big-O is usually the same. The differences are practical: stack depth, which states get computed, and how easily I can shrink memory.

Let me show you both on climbing stairs.

// Top-down
static int climbMemo(int n, Integer[] memo) {
    if (n <= 1) return 1;
    if (memo[n] != null) return memo[n];
    return memo[n] = (climbMemo(n - 1, memo) + climbMemo(n - 2, memo)) % 1_000_000_007;
}
// Bottom-up, space-optimised to two variables
static int climbTab(int n) {
    int prev2 = 1, prev1 = 1;                        // ways(i-2), ways(i-1)
    for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++) {
        int cur = (prev1 + prev2) % 1_000_000_007;
        prev2 = prev1;
        prev1 = cur;
    }
    return prev1;
}
// n = 100,000: climbTab -> 967618232,   climbMemo -> StackOverflowError
Memoization (top-down)Tabulation (bottom-up)
Writing itNatural: recursion + cacheMust work out the fill order
States computedOnly the reachable onesAll of them
Stack overflowYes, when depth ~ n is largeNever
SpeedCall overhead, boxed cachesTight loops, cache-friendly
Space optimisationHardEasy: keep only the rows you read

So in practice I start with memoization in an interview, because it falls straight out of the brute force and is easiest to get right. I switch to tabulation when n is large, when I need to cut memory to O(1) or one row, or when an interviewer asks for it. Memoization wins when the state space is sparse, for example a HashMap memo over states that are hard to enumerate in order.

Interviewers look for: the stack depth point. Java's default stack gives out somewhere in the thousands-to-tens-of-thousands of frames, depending on frame size.

IntermediateWalk me through House Robber using a DP recipe.

So I use five steps: state, recurrence, base cases, order, answer. Houses are in a row, I can't rob two neighbours, and I want the maximum total. At each house I either skip it or rob it and skip its neighbour.

Let me show you.

  • State: dp[i] is the most money from the first i houses.
  • Recurrence: dp[i] = max(dp[i-1], dp[i-2] + nums[i-1]), meaning skip house i or rob it.
  • Base: dp[0] = 0, dp[1] = nums[0].
  • Order: i from 2 up to n.
  • Answer: dp[n].
static int rob(int[] nums) {
    int prev2 = 0, prev1 = 0;                        // best up to i-2, i-1
    for (int x : nums) {
        int cur = Math.max(prev1, prev2 + x);        // skip x, or take it
        prev2 = prev1;
        prev1 = cur;
    }
    return prev1;
}
// {2, 7, 9, 3, 1} -> 12   (houses 0, 2, 4: 2 + 9 + 1)

Tracing it: after 2, best is 2. After 7, max(2, 0 + 7) = 7. After 9, max(7, 2 + 9) = 11. After 3, max(11, 7 + 3) = 11. After 1, max(11, 11 + 1) = 12.

Since dp[i] only reads the previous two values, I keep two variables. That's O(n) time and O(1) space.

The greedy idea of taking every other house fails. For [2, 1, 1, 2], both alternating picks give 3, but the answer is 2 + 2 = 4.

Interviewers look for: the follow-up "houses in a circle" (House Robber II). Run the same function twice, once without the first house and once without the last, and take the max.

IntermediateSolve Coin Change (fewest coins). What's the complexity?

So the state is dp[a], the fewest coins to make amount a. To make a, the last coin I used was some c, so dp[a] = min over coins c of dp[a - c] + 1. The base is dp[0] = 0, and I fill from 1 up to amount.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static int coinChange(int[] coins, int amount) {
    int[] dp = new int[amount + 1];                  // dp[a] = fewest coins for a
    Arrays.fill(dp, amount + 1);                     // "infinity"
    dp[0] = 0;
    for (int a = 1; a <= amount; a++)
        for (int c : coins)
            if (c <= a) dp[a] = Math.min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1);   // last coin is c
    return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount];
}
// {1, 3, 4}, 6 -> 2 (3 + 3)     {2}, 3 -> -1     {1, 2, 5}, 11 -> 3

Complexity is O(amount × coins) time and O(amount) space. That's states × transitions: amount + 1 states, each trying every coin.

One gotcha I've hit is using Integer.MAX_VALUE as infinity. Then dp[a - c] + 1 overflows to a negative number and wins the min. amount + 1 is a safe infinity, because no answer can use more than amount coins, since the smallest coin is at least 1.

This is the problem where greedy fails. Greedy on {1, 3, 4} for 6 gives 4 + 1 + 1. The DP tries every last coin instead of committing to the biggest.

Also note that it's pseudo-polynomial. It's linear in the value of amount, not the input size. With amount = 109 this table doesn't fit, and I'd need a different approach, like BFS with pruning or number theory.

AdvancedWhen you space-optimise a DP, what do you lose? How do you reconstruct the actual solution?

So rolling the table down to a few variables or one row keeps the value of the answer, but throws away the history of choices. If the question asks "which houses" or "which items", not just "how much", I keep the full table and walk it backwards. At each state I check which choice produced that value.

Let me show you with House Robber.

static List<Integer> robWhich(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    int[] dp = new int[n + 1];
    dp[1] = nums[0];
    for (int i = 2; i <= n; i++) dp[i] = Math.max(dp[i - 1], dp[i - 2] + nums[i - 1]);
    List<Integer> picked = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = n; i >= 1; ) {
        if (i == 1 || dp[i] != dp[i - 1]) { picked.add(i - 1); i -= 2; }   // house i-1 was robbed
        else i--;                                                          // house i-1 was skipped
    }
    Collections.reverse(picked);
    return picked;
}
// {2, 7, 9, 3, 1} -> [0, 2, 4]   (2 + 9 + 1 = 12)

What happens here is: if dp[i] equals dp[i-1], skipping house i was optimal, so I step back one. Otherwise I must have robbed it, so I record it and jump back two. The walk is O(n), so reconstruction doesn't change the complexity. It only costs the O(n) table.

The general rules for rolling arrays:

  • If dp[i] reads only the last k values, keep k variables.
  • If dp[i][j] reads only row i - 1, keep one or two rows. Loop direction matters so I never read a cell I already overwrote in this pass. In 0/1 knapsack I go right to left for exactly that reason.
  • If I need the path, keep the table, or store a separate "choice" array, which is smaller than the full values.

Interviewers look for: the loop-direction point in 1D knapsack. Going left to right silently turns 0/1 knapsack into unbounded knapsack.

25. DP Patterns

BasicYou've been handed a problem and you suspect DP. How do you figure out which DP pattern it is?

So I look at two things: what the input is shaped like, and what choice I make at each step. Those two usually map straight onto one of about nine patterns, and the pattern hands me the state.

What I noticePatternState
Items with a weight, a budget, "use each once"0/1 knapsackdp[i][cap], or 1D going backwards
Same, but "unlimited supply" (coins, rods)Unbounded knapsackdp[amount]
One array or string, answer builds left to rightSingle sequencedp[i] = best ending at / using first i
Two strings comparedTwo sequencesdp[i][j] over prefixes
Moves only right/down in a gridGriddp[r][c]
"Merge / burst / split a range", order mattersIntervaldp[l][r]
A binary tree with a take/skip choiceTree DPPair returned from DFS
n <= 20, "visit all", "assign each"Bitmaskdp[mask][i]
Days with modes: holding, sold, cooldownState machinedp[i][state]

The tell that it's DP at all is "count the ways", "min/max", or "is it possible", plus a brute force that re-solves the same subproblem. Then I say the state in words before I write code: "dp[i][j] is the edit distance between the first i chars of a and the first j of b." If I can't say it in one sentence, I haven't found the pattern yet.

So in practice, recognising the pattern is most of the work. The recurrence usually falls out once the state sentence is right.

BasicWalk me through Coin Change: the fewest coins to make an amount.

So this is unbounded knapsack, because I can reuse each coin as many times as I like. My state is dp[a] = the fewest coins that make exactly a. For each amount I try every coin as the "last coin" and take the best.

static int coinChange(int[] coins, int amount) {
  int[] dp = new int[amount + 1];
  Arrays.fill(dp, amount + 1);          // "infinity" that can't overflow
  dp[0] = 0;
  for (int a = 1; a <= amount; a++) {
    for (int c : coins) {
      if (c <= a) dp[a] = Math.min(dp[a], dp[a - c] + 1);
    }
  }
  return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount];
}
// coinChange([1, 2, 5], 11) -> 3   (5 + 5 + 1)
// coinChange([2], 3)        -> -1

The recurrence is dp[a] = 1 + min(dp[a - c]) over coins c. The base case is dp[0] = 0. It's O(amount x coins) time and O(amount) space.

One gotcha I've hit is using Integer.MAX_VALUE as "unreachable". The moment I do dp[a - c] + 1 it wraps to a negative number and wins every min. Using amount + 1 works because no real answer can need more than amount coins.

And I'd say why greedy doesn't work here: with coins [1, 3, 4] and amount 6, greedy takes 4+1+1 (three coins) but 3+3 is two. DP tries every last coin, so it can't be fooled that way.

Interviewers look for: the follow-up is Coin Change II, "count the ways". They want to hear that the loop order decides what you count: coins in the outer loop counts combinations (4 ways for [1, 2, 5], amount 5), amount in the outer loop counts ordered sequences (9 ways).

IntermediateFor 0/1 knapsack in one dimension, why does the capacity loop go backwards? Show me with Partition Equal Subset Sum.

So going backwards is what makes each item usable only once. When I update dp[s] from dp[s - x], I need dp[s - x] to still be the value from before this item. Going from high to low, the smaller index hasn't been touched yet in this round.

static boolean canPartition(int[] nums) {
  int total = 0;
  for (int x : nums) total += x;
  if (total % 2 != 0) return false;
  int target = total / 2;
  boolean[] dp = new boolean[target + 1];
  dp[0] = true;
  for (int x : nums) {
    for (int s = target; s >= x; s--) {  // backwards: each item once
      dp[s] = dp[s] || dp[s - x];
    }
  }
  return dp[target];
}
// [1, 5, 11, 5] -> true  (11 = 1 + 5 + 5)
// [1, 2, 3, 5]  -> false (total 11 is odd)

The problem becomes knapsack once I notice that splitting into two equal halves means "can some subset sum to total / 2?". dp[s] is "some subset of the items so far sums to s."

If I went forwards with x = 5, I'd set dp[5] true, then later in the same pass read dp[5] to set dp[10]. That uses the 5 twice. That forward order is exactly unbounded knapsack. So the direction of one loop is the whole difference between the two patterns.

It's O(n x target) time and O(target) space. That's pseudo-polynomial, so if the sum could be in the billions I'd say so and look for a different approach.

Interviewers look for: the reduction to "subset sums to half" and the one-line reason for the backwards loop. Target Sum (LC 494) is the usual follow-up, same trick.

IntermediateLongest Increasing Subsequence: give me the O(n²) idea, then get it to O(n log n).

So the O(n²) version is dp[i] = the length of the longest increasing subsequence ending at i, and I fill it by looking at every earlier j with a smaller value. The O(n log n) version keeps a different array: tails[k] = the smallest possible tail of an increasing subsequence of length k + 1.

static int lengthOfLIS(int[] nums) {
  int[] tails = new int[nums.length];
  int size = 0;
  for (int x : nums) {
    int lo = 0, hi = size;
    while (lo < hi) {                     // first tail >= x
      int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
      if (tails[mid] < x) lo = mid + 1; else hi = mid;
    }
    tails[lo] = x;
    if (lo == size) size++;
  }
  return size;
}
// [10, 9, 2, 5, 3, 7, 101, 18] -> 4   (2, 3, 7, 18)

Why keep the smallest tail? A smaller tail is always at least as good, because it leaves more room for the next number to extend it. tails stays sorted, so for each x I binary search the first tail that is >= x and replace it. If x is bigger than every tail, it extends the longest one.

One gotcha: tails is not the actual subsequence. For the example it ends as [2, 3, 7, 18] by luck, but in general it's a mix. If they want the sequence itself, I store a parent index for each element.

For strictly increasing I search >= x. For non-decreasing I'd search > x instead.

IntermediateWalk me through Edit Distance. Where do the three operations show up in the recurrence?

So dp[i][j] is the minimum edits to turn the first i characters of a into the first j characters of b. If the last characters match, they cost nothing. If they don't, I pay 1 and take the best of replace, delete, or insert, each of which is a neighbouring cell.

static int minDistance(String a, String b) {
  int m = a.length(), n = b.length();
  int[][] dp = new int[m + 1][n + 1];
  for (int i = 0; i <= m; i++) dp[i][0] = i;   // delete all
  for (int j = 0; j <= n; j++) dp[0][j] = j;   // insert all
  for (int i = 1; i <= m; i++) {
    for (int j = 1; j <= n; j++) {
      if (a.charAt(i - 1) == b.charAt(j - 1)) {
        dp[i][j] = dp[i - 1][j - 1];
      } else {
        dp[i][j] = 1 + Math.min(dp[i - 1][j - 1],     // replace
                       Math.min(dp[i - 1][j],         // delete
                                dp[i][j - 1]));       // insert
      }
    }
  }
  return dp[m][n];
}
// minDistance("horse", "ros") -> 3

The mapping is: replace fixes both last chars, so diagonal dp[i-1][j-1]. Delete drops a's last char, so up, dp[i-1][j]. Insert adds b's last char, so left, dp[i][j-1]. The first row and column are the "one string is empty" cases.

I use size m + 1 by n + 1 so index 0 means "empty prefix". That removes all the edge checks, and the price is remembering charAt(i - 1).

It's O(mn) time. Each row only reads the row above, so I can drop to two rows, O(n) space, if memory matters. LCS is the same grid, with +1 on the diagonal for a match and max instead of min.

AdvancedHow would you do Best Time to Buy and Sell Stock with Cooldown? Why call it a state machine?

So each day I'm in one of three states: holding a share, just sold (so tomorrow is a cooldown), or resting with no share and free to buy. I write down how each state can be reached from yesterday's states, and that is the recurrence. Drawing the three boxes and arrows first is why people call it a state machine.

static int maxProfitCooldown(int[] prices) {
  int hold = Integer.MIN_VALUE / 2;  // own a share
  int sold = 0;                      // just sold today (cooldown next)
  int rest = 0;                      // free to buy
  for (int p : prices) {
    int prevHold = hold, prevSold = sold, prevRest = rest;
    hold = Math.max(prevHold, prevRest - p);   // keep, or buy from rest
    sold = prevHold + p;                       // sell today
    rest = Math.max(prevRest, prevSold);       // wait, or cooldown ends
  }
  return Math.max(sold, rest);
}
// [1, 2, 3, 0, 2] -> 3   (buy 1, sell 2, cooldown, buy 0, sell 2)

The cooldown lives in one arrow: you can only buy from rest, never straight from sold. That's the whole rule, encoded by which arrows exist.

The gotcha I watch for is updating in place. If I wrote hold first and then used the new hold to compute sold, I'd be buying and selling on the same day. So I copy yesterday's values first.

The nice thing is the whole stock family is the same machine with different arrows. A transaction fee subtracts on the sell arrow. "At most k transactions" adds the count to the state, dp[i][k][holding]. It's O(n) time and O(1) space here.

Interviewers look for: the states named and drawn before code, and the "use yesterday's values" point. They often follow with "now at most two transactions" (LC 123).

26. Shortest Paths

BasicHow do you choose between BFS, Dijkstra, Bellman-Ford and Floyd-Warshall?

So I decide from the edge weights and from whether I need one source or all pairs. The fastest algorithm that is still correct for those weights wins.

GraphAlgorithmTime
UnweightedBFSO(V + E)
Weights only 0 or 10-1 BFS with a dequeO(V + E)
Non-negative weights, one sourceDijkstra with a heapO((V + E) log V)
Negative weights, or "at most K edges"Bellman-FordO(V x E)
All pairs, small V (a few hundred)Floyd-WarshallO(V³)
DAG, any weightsTopological order, then relaxO(V + E)

They all share one move called relaxation: "if going through u gives v a cheaper cost than it has, update dist[v]." What differs is the order in which they relax edges. BFS goes by layers. Dijkstra always finishes the closest unfinished node next. Bellman-Ford relaxes every edge, round after round. Floyd-Warshall tries every node as a middle stop.

A mistake I see is people using Dijkstra on an unweighted grid. It works, but BFS gives the same answer without the log factor, so I'd pick BFS there.

So in practice Dijkstra is my default for weighted graphs, and I move away from it only when I see negative weights, a limit on the number of edges, or a request for all pairs.

BasicWrite Dijkstra for me and tell me its complexity.

So I keep a dist array and a min-heap of (node, distance). I always pop the closest node, and relax its edges. Java's PriorityQueue has no decrease-key, so I push a new entry instead and skip stale ones when they come out.

// graph.get(u) holds int[]{to, weight}
static int[] dijkstra(List<List<int[]>> graph, int src) {
  int n = graph.size();
  int[] dist = new int[n];
  Arrays.fill(dist, Integer.MAX_VALUE);
  dist[src] = 0;
  PriorityQueue<int[]> pq = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]));
  pq.offer(new int[]{src, 0});
  while (!pq.isEmpty()) {
    int[] cur = pq.poll();
    int u = cur[0], d = cur[1];
    if (d > dist[u]) continue;               // stale entry, skip
    for (int[] e : graph.get(u)) {
      int v = e[0], nd = d + e[1];
      if (nd < dist[v]) {                    // relax
