        dist[v] = nd;
        pq.offer(new int[]{v, nd});
      }
    }
  }
  return dist;
}

For Network Delay Time (LC 743) I run this from k and return the max of dist, or -1 if any node stays at MAX_VALUE. For edges 2->1, 2->3, 3->4 all weight 1, starting at 2, that's 2.

Complexity is O((V + E) log V). Each edge can push at most one entry, so the heap holds up to E items, and log E is at most 2 log V. Space is O(V + E).

Two gotchas. I use Integer.compare in the comparator, not a[1] - b[1], which can overflow. And the d > dist[u] check matters: without it, stale entries re-expand nodes and the run slows down badly on dense graphs.

Interviewers look for: the lazy-deletion check, and an honest complexity. They may ask why you don't need a visited set: the stale check does the same job.

IntermediateWhy does Dijkstra fail with negative edge weights? Give me a concrete example.

So Dijkstra assumes that once a node is the closest thing in the heap, its distance is final. That's only true if no path can get cheaper by going further. A negative edge breaks that: a longer detour can end up cheaper.

// A = 0, B = 1, C = 2
// A -> B cost 2
// A -> C cost 3
// C -> B cost -2
//
// Dijkstra (marking nodes final when popped):
//   pop A (0): dist B = 2, dist C = 3
//   pop B (2): B is final at 2
//   pop C (3): C -> B gives 1, but B is already final
// result:  [0, 2, 3]
// correct: [0, 1, 3]   (A -> C -> B = 3 - 2)

I ran exactly this. Dijkstra with a "done" array returns B = 2, and Bellman-Ford returns B = 1.

The lazy version without a done array sometimes "gets lucky" on small cases because it keeps relaxing. But then it can re-process nodes many times, and the running time can blow up to exponential in bad cases. So it isn't a real fix.

A trick people suggest is "add a constant to every edge to make them positive". That's wrong too. It punishes paths with more edges, so it changes which path is shortest.

So when I see negative weights, I go to Bellman-Ford. If the graph is a DAG, I use topological order and relax once, which handles negatives in linear time.

Interviewers look for: a three-node counterexample drawn on the spot, and that you reject the "add a constant" trick.

IntermediateExplain Bellman-Ford. Why V - 1 rounds, and how does it detect a negative cycle?

So Bellman-Ford just relaxes every edge, again and again. After round k, every shortest path that uses at most k edges is correct. A shortest path with no cycle visits each node at most once, so it has at most V - 1 edges. That's why V - 1 rounds are enough.

static long[] bellmanFord(int n, int[][] edges, int src) {
  long INF = Long.MAX_VALUE / 4;
  long[] dist = new long[n];
  Arrays.fill(dist, INF);
  dist[src] = 0;
  for (int round = 0; round < n - 1; round++) {
    boolean changed = false;
    for (int[] e : edges) {
      int u = e[0], v = e[1], w = e[2];
      if (dist[u] != INF && dist[u] + w < dist[v]) {
        dist[v] = dist[u] + w;
        changed = true;
      }
    }
    if (!changed) break;                     // early exit
  }
  for (int[] e : edges) {                    // one more round
    if (dist[e[0]] != INF && dist[e[0]] + e[2] < dist[e[1]]) {
      return null;                           // negative cycle reachable
    }
  }
  return dist;
}

For the negative cycle: after V - 1 rounds, everything should be settled. If one more pass can still make something cheaper, the only explanation is a cycle whose total is negative, because you can go round it forever and keep getting cheaper. So I return a signal instead of distances.

The dist[u] != INF check matters. Without it, "infinity plus a negative weight" looks smaller than infinity, and unreachable nodes get fake distances.

It's O(V x E), much slower than Dijkstra, so I only use it when I need what it offers: negative weights, cycle detection, or a limit on the number of edges.

IntermediateCheapest Flights Within K Stops: why not just use Dijkstra, and what's the bug people hit with Bellman-Ford?

So plain Dijkstra finalises each city at its cheapest cost, but the cheapest route might use too many stops. A pricier route with fewer stops might be the only legal one, and Dijkstra has already thrown it away. Bellman-Ford fits naturally, because round i means "paths with at most i edges". K stops is K + 1 flights, so I run K + 1 rounds.

static int findCheapestPrice(int n, int[][] flights, int src, int dst, int k) {
  int INF = Integer.MAX_VALUE / 2;
  int[] dist = new int[n];
  Arrays.fill(dist, INF);
  dist[src] = 0;
  for (int i = 0; i <= k; i++) {             // k stops = k + 1 edges
    int[] next = dist.clone();               // read old, write new
    for (int[] f : flights) {
      int u = f[0], v = f[1], w = f[2];
      if (dist[u] + w < next[v]) next[v] = dist[u] + w;
    }
    dist = next;
  }
  return dist[dst] >= INF ? -1 : dist[dst];
}
// flights 0->1 (100), 1->2 (100), 0->2 (500), src 0, dst 2
// k = 1 -> 200,  k = 0 -> 500

The bug is skipping the clone(). If I update dist in place, one round can chain several edges: it sets city 1 to 100, and then in the same round uses that new 100 to reach city 2. I tested it: with k = 0 the in-place version returns 200, which uses a stop it isn't allowed. The copy makes sure each round adds exactly one edge.

It's O(K x E). Dijkstra can also work if the state is (city, stops used), but the Bellman-Ford version is shorter and harder to get wrong.

Interviewers look for: the "read old, write new" copy. It's the single line that makes or breaks this problem.

AdvancedIn Floyd-Warshall, why must the k loop be outermost? And when would you use 0-1 BFS instead?

So Floyd-Warshall is a DP. After the k-th outer step, d[i][j] is the shortest path from i to j that uses only nodes 0..k as middle stops. Each step allows one more middle node. That only works if every pair is fully updated for node k before we allow node k + 1, so k has to be the outer loop.

static void floydWarshall(long[][] d) {      // d[i][j] = edge weight, or a big INF
  int n = d.length;
  for (int k = 0; k < n; k++)                // k MUST be outermost
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++)
      for (int j = 0; j < n; j++)
        if (d[i][k] + d[k][j] < d[i][j])
          d[i][j] = d[i][k] + d[k][j];
}

If I put k inside, d[i][j] gets its final value while d[i][k] and d[k][j] are still unfinished, and it never gets revisited. The answer comes out wrong with no error. After it runs, a negative d[i][i] means node i is on a negative cycle. I use Long.MAX_VALUE / 4 as INF so that adding two of them doesn't overflow.

0-1 BFS is for a different case: single source, and every weight is 0 or 1. Think "free to move along an arrow, cost 1 to break a wall". I use a deque. A 0-edge pushes to the front and a 1-edge to the back. The deque then stays sorted by distance, like Dijkstra's heap, but each operation is O(1), so the total is O(V + E).

So I'd pick Floyd-Warshall only when V is a few hundred and I really need all pairs. V³ at 400 is 64 million steps, which is fine. At 5,000 it's far too slow.

27. Topological Sort, MST & More

BasicWhat is a topological sort, and when does one exist?

So a topological sort is an ordering of the nodes of a directed graph where every edge u -> v has u before v. It's "an order that respects every dependency." One exists exactly when the graph has no directed cycle, so a DAG.

Let me show you with a quick example. Courses: 0 is required for 1 and 2, and both 1 and 2 are required for 3.

0 -> 1 -> 3
0 -> 2 -> 3

valid orders: [0, 1, 2, 3] and [0, 2, 1, 3]
add 3 -> 0 and there is no valid order (0 needs 3 needs 1 needs 0)

Two things to notice. First, the order is usually not unique. Any node whose dependencies are done can go next. If the problem wants a specific one, like the smallest-first order, I swap the queue for a min-heap. Second, a cycle makes it impossible, and the algorithm gives me that check for free.

The signals that tell me to reach for it: "prerequisites", "build order", "tasks that depend on other tasks", "is it possible to finish", or "derive an ordering from pairwise comparisons" like Alien Dictionary.

There are two standard ways to compute it: Kahn's algorithm, which is BFS on in-degrees, and DFS, where I add each node after all its descendants and reverse. Both are O(V + E).

BasicWalk me through Course Schedule II using Kahn's algorithm.

So Kahn's algorithm repeatedly takes a node with no remaining prerequisites, outputs it, and removes its outgoing edges. That may free up new nodes. If I output all n nodes, that's my order. If I get stuck early, the leftover nodes are in a cycle.

static int[] findOrder(int n, int[][] prereq) {
  List<List<Integer>> g = new ArrayList<>();
  for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) g.add(new ArrayList<>());
  int[] indeg = new int[n];
  for (int[] p : prereq) {                 // [course, pre] means pre -> course
    g.get(p[1]).add(p[0]);
    indeg[p[0]]++;
  }
  Deque<Integer> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
  for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) if (indeg[i] == 0) q.offer(i);
  int[] order = new int[n];
  int idx = 0;
  while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    int u = q.poll();
    order[idx++] = u;
    for (int v : g.get(u)) {
      if (--indeg[v] == 0) q.offer(v);
    }
  }
  return idx == n ? order : new int[0];     // fewer than n means a cycle
}
// n = 4, [[1,0],[2,0],[3,1],[3,2]] -> [0, 1, 2, 3]
// n = 2, [[1,0],[0,1]]             -> []

The step people get wrong is the edge direction. [1, 0] means "to take 1 you need 0", so the edge is 0 -> 1. If I reverse it, I get a valid-looking order that's backwards.

The cycle check is the idx == n line. Nodes on a cycle all keep in-degree at least 1, so they never enter the queue. Course Schedule I is the same code returning idx == n as a boolean.

It's O(V + E) time and space. Each node is enqueued once, and each edge decrements once.

IntermediateFor cycle detection in a directed graph with DFS, why isn't a single visited array enough?

So in a directed graph, reaching an already-visited node doesn't mean there's a cycle. It might just be a node I finished earlier through another branch. I need to know if that node is on my current path. So I use three colours: white (not seen), gray (on the current recursion path), black (fully done).

static boolean hasCycle(List<List<Integer>> g) {
  int[] color = new int[g.size()];          // 0 white, 1 gray, 2 black
  for (int i = 0; i < g.size(); i++)
    if (color[i] == 0 && dfs(g, i, color)) return true;
  return false;
}

static boolean dfs(List<List<Integer>> g, int u, int[] color) {
  color[u] = 1;                             // on the current path
  for (int v : g.get(u)) {
    if (color[v] == 1) return true;         // back edge: cycle
    if (color[v] == 0 && dfs(g, v, color)) return true;
  }
  color[u] = 2;                             // fully explored, safe
  return false;
}
// diamond 0->1, 0->2, 1->3, 2->3 : false
// triangle 0->1->2->0           : true

The diamond is the case that trips a plain visited array. I go 0 -> 1 -> 3, finish, then 0 -> 2 -> 3. Node 3 is visited, but it's black, not gray, so it isn't a cycle. A single boolean would wrongly say "cycle" there.

In an undirected graph, one visited array plus "skip the parent" is enough, because every edge goes both ways anyway.

Bonus: if I append each node when it turns black and then reverse the list, I get a topological order. One gotcha is recursion depth. With 100,000 nodes in a chain, Java's default stack can overflow, so for big inputs I prefer Kahn's.

Interviewers look for: the diamond counterexample, and the directed vs undirected difference.

IntermediateKruskal vs Prim for a minimum spanning tree: how do they work, and which would you pick?

So both build the cheapest set of edges that connects every node with no cycle. Kruskal looks at all edges globally, cheapest first, and keeps an edge if it joins two different components. Prim grows one tree from a start node, always adding the cheapest edge that leaves the tree.

static long kruskal(int n, int[][] edges) {  // edges: {u, v, w}
  parent = new int[n];
  rank = new int[n];
  for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) parent[i] = i;
  Arrays.sort(edges, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[2], b[2]));
  long total = 0;
  int used = 0;
  for (int[] e : edges) {
    if (union(e[0], e[1])) {                // false if already connected
      total += e[2];
      if (++used == n - 1) break;           // tree is complete
    }
  }
  return used == n - 1 ? total : -1;        // -1: graph not connected
}
// 4 nodes, edges (0-1,1) (1-2,2) (0-2,3) (2-3,4) (1-3,5) -> 7

Here union is the standard union-find with path compression and union by rank. It returns false when both ends already have the same root, which is exactly "this edge would make a cycle". In the example, 0-2 with cost 3 is skipped for that reason.

Kruskal is O(E log E), and sorting is most of that. Prim with a binary heap is O(E log V). On paper they're close. How I pick: if I already have an edge list, or the graph is sparse, Kruskal. If the graph is dense or implicit, like "every pair of points is an edge" in Min Cost to Connect All Points, I use Prim. The simple array version of Prim is O(V²) and never builds the V² edges at all.

Both are greedy, and both are correct because of the cut property: the cheapest edge crossing any cut is safe to include.

Interviewers look for: union-find for cycle checks in Kruskal, and a reason to prefer one over the other that depends on density.

IntermediateHow would you approach Alien Dictionary?

So I turn the sorted word list into a graph of letter rules, then topologically sort the letters. Each pair of adjacent words gives at most one rule: the first position where they differ says which letter comes first.

static String alienOrder(String[] words) {
  Map<Character, Set<Character>> g = new HashMap<>();
  Map<Character, Integer> indeg = new HashMap<>();
  for (String w : words)
    for (char c : w.toCharArray()) { g.putIfAbsent(c, new HashSet<>()); indeg.putIfAbsent(c, 0); }
  for (int i = 0; i + 1 < words.length; i++) {
    String a = words[i], b = words[i + 1];
    if (a.length() > b.length() && a.startsWith(b)) return "";   // "abc" before "ab"
    for (int j = 0; j < Math.min(a.length(), b.length()); j++) {
      char x = a.charAt(j), y = b.charAt(j);
      if (x != y) {
        if (g.get(x).add(y)) indeg.merge(y, 1, Integer::sum);
        break;                              // only the FIRST difference counts
      }
    }
  }
  Deque<Character> q = new ArrayDeque<>();
  for (var e : indeg.entrySet()) if (e.getValue() == 0) q.offer(e.getKey());
  StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
  while (!q.isEmpty()) {
    char c = q.poll();
    sb.append(c);
    for (char nx : g.get(c)) if (indeg.merge(nx, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) q.offer(nx);
  }
  return sb.length() == indeg.size() ? sb.toString() : "";
}
// ["wrt","wrf","er","ett","rftt"] -> "wertf"

Three traps I watch for. Only the first difference gives a rule. After that, the rest of the letters tell you nothing. A longer word before its own prefix, like "abc" then "ab", is invalid input, so I return empty. And I add each edge once, which is why I check add(y) before bumping the in-degree. Otherwise duplicate rules inflate in-degrees and the sort gets stuck.

Every letter that appears must be in the output, even if no rule mentions it. That's why I register all letters first. It's O(total characters) to build and O(V + E) to sort, where V is at most 26.

AdvancedHow do you find bridges in a graph, like in Critical Connections in a Network?

So a bridge is an edge whose removal disconnects the graph. Tarjan's idea is one DFS that records, for every node, its discovery time disc and a low value: the earliest discovery time it can reach using its subtree plus one back edge. An edge u -> v in the DFS tree is a bridge when low[v] > disc[u]. That means nothing below v has another way back up to u or above.

static void bridgeDfs(List<List<Integer>> g, int u, int parent) {
  disc[u] = low[u] = timer++;
  for (int v : g.get(u)) {
    if (v == parent) continue;              // don't use the edge we came in on
    if (disc[v] == -1) {
      bridgeDfs(g, v, u);
      low[u] = Math.min(low[u], low[v]);
      if (low[v] > disc[u]) bridges.add(List.of(u, v));
    } else {
      low[u] = Math.min(low[u], disc[v]);   // back edge
    }
  }
}
// edges 0-1, 1-2, 2-0, 1-3  -> bridges [[1, 3]]

In the example, the triangle 0-1-2 has back edges, so every node in it gets low = 0, and none of its edges are bridges. Node 3 only hangs off 1, so low[3] = disc[3], which is greater than disc[1]. So 1-3 is the bridge.

Articulation points (cut vertices) use the same arrays with >= instead of >, and a special rule that the DFS root is a cut vertex only if it has two or more children.

It's O(V + E). The gotcha in Java is recursion depth. LC 1192 allows 100,000 nodes, and a long chain can overflow the default stack, so in production I'd write it iteratively or run it on a thread with a bigger stack. Skipping the parent by node id also breaks if there are parallel edges. In that case I skip by edge id instead.

Interviewers look for: a clear meaning for low, and the > vs >= difference between bridges and articulation points.

28. String Algorithms

BasicWhy is building a string with s += c in a loop slow in Java, and what do you use instead?

So Java strings are immutable. Every s += c creates a brand new string and copies all the old characters into it. Doing that n times copies 1 + 2 + ... + n characters, which is O(n²). A StringBuilder appends into a growable buffer, so each append is amortised O(1) and the whole loop is O(n).

// O(n^2): a new String on every pass
String s = "";
for (char c : chars) s += c;

// O(n): one buffer, doubled when full
StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
for (char c : chars) sb.append(c);
String result = sb.toString();

What happens under the hood is that the compiler turns each += into its own concatenation. It can't reuse the buffer across loop iterations, so you pay the full copy every time. With 100,000 characters that's around 5 billion character copies, which is why it times out.

A few related things I keep in mind. substring copies too, so it's O(length), not free. In a hot loop I compare characters with charAt rather than building substrings. For counting letters I use an int[26] or int[128] instead of a HashMap<Character, Integer>, which avoids boxing. And toCharArray() is handy when I want to sort or swap characters in place.

So in practice, any time I build a string in a loop, I reach for StringBuilder without thinking about it.

BasicWhat's the complexity of naive string matching, and what input makes it slow?

So the naive approach tries every start position in the text and compares the pattern character by character. That's O(n x m) in the worst case, for text length n and pattern length m.

static int naive(String text, String p) {
  for (int i = 0; i + p.length() <= text.length(); i++) {
    int j = 0;
    while (j < p.length() && text.charAt(i + j) == p.charAt(j)) j++;
    if (j == p.length()) return i;
  }
  return -1;
}
// worst case: text = "aaaa...a" (n chars), p = "aaa...ab" (m chars)
// every start matches m - 1 chars, then fails on the 'b'

The slow case is lots of near-misses. With a text of all as and a pattern of as ending in b, every start position gets almost all the way through before failing. The waste is that after a failure, we go back and re-read text characters we have already matched.

On random or normal text it's fast, because most attempts fail on the first or second character. That's why Java's String.indexOf is basically this naive loop and still works fine in everyday code.

So in an interview I'd say: indexOf is fine unless the input is adversarial or huge. If they want a guaranteed O(n + m), I'd use KMP, which never moves backwards in the text. Or I'd use Rabin-Karp, which compares hashes instead of characters.

IntermediateWalk me through KMP. What is the LPS array?

So KMP avoids re-reading the text. When a mismatch happens after matching j characters, it already knows what those characters were: the first j of the pattern. The LPS array tells it how much of that is still usable. lps[i] is the length of the longest proper prefix of p[0..i] that is also a suffix of it.

static int[] buildLps(String p) {
  int[] lps = new int[p.length()];
  int len = 0;                               // length of current border
  for (int i = 1; i < p.length(); ) {
    if (p.charAt(i) == p.charAt(len)) {
      lps[i++] = ++len;
    } else if (len > 0) {
      len = lps[len - 1];                    // fall back, don't move i
    } else {
      lps[i++] = 0;
    }
  }
  return lps;
}

static int kmpSearch(String text, String p) {
  if (p.isEmpty()) return 0;
  int[] lps = buildLps(p);
  int j = 0;                                 // chars of p matched so far
  for (int i = 0; i < text.length(); i++) {
    while (j > 0 && text.charAt(i) != p.charAt(j)) j = lps[j - 1];
    if (text.charAt(i) == p.charAt(j)) j++;
    if (j == p.length()) return i - j + 1;   // first match
  }
  return -1;
}
// buildLps("ababaca") -> [0, 0, 1, 2, 3, 0, 1]
// kmpSearch("ababcabcabababd", "ababd") -> 10

Take "ababaca". At index 4 the prefix is "ababa", and "aba" is both its prefix and its suffix, so lps[4] = 3. If a mismatch happens right after matching "ababa", I don't restart. I keep 3 characters matched and try again from there.

The index i in the text only moves forward. j can fall back, but it can only fall back as much as it has gone up, so the total work is O(n + m). That's amortised analysis, and it's the part interviewers want explained.

Interviewers look for: a clear one-line definition of LPS and the "text pointer never goes back" argument. Writing it from memory is less important than explaining it.

IntermediateHow does Rabin-Karp work, and how do you deal with hash collisions?

So Rabin-Karp turns each window of the text into a number (a polynomial hash) and compares numbers instead of strings. The trick is the rolling hash: moving the window one step right is O(1). I subtract the leaving character, shift everything by the base, and add the new one.

static List<Integer> rabinKarp(String text, String p) {
  List<Integer> res = new ArrayList<>();
  int n = text.length(), m = p.length();
  if (m == 0 || m > n) return res;
  final long MOD = 1_000_000_007L, BASE = 131;
  long pow = 1;                              // BASE^(m-1)
  for (int i = 0; i < m - 1; i++) pow = pow * BASE % MOD;
  long hp = 0, hw = 0;
  for (int i = 0; i < m; i++) {
    hp = (hp * BASE + p.charAt(i)) % MOD;
    hw = (hw * BASE + text.charAt(i)) % MOD;
  }
  for (int i = 0; ; i++) {
    if (hp == hw && text.regionMatches(i, p, 0, m)) res.add(i);  // confirm
    if (i + m == n) break;
    hw = (hw - text.charAt(i) * pow % MOD + MOD) % MOD;          // drop left
    hw = (hw * BASE + text.charAt(i + m)) % MOD;                 // add right
  }
  return res;
}
// rabinKarp("abracadabra", "abra") -> [0, 7]

Collisions are real: two different strings can hash to the same value. So when the hashes match, I confirm with a real comparison, regionMatches. That keeps the answer always correct. The expected time is O(n + m). The worst case is O(nm) if everything collides, which a good modulus makes very unlikely.

Two details that bite in Java. The + MOD after subtracting, because % can return a negative. And every product is at most about 109 times a char or the base, far below the long limit, so nothing overflows.

Where Rabin-Karp beats KMP is when I'm matching many patterns of the same length, or comparing lots of substrings to each other. The hashes go into a set. If I can't confirm every match and need extra safety, I use two different mods (double hashing).

IntermediateFind the longest palindromic substring. What's your approach and complexity?

So I expand around every center. A palindrome mirrors around its middle, and there are 2n - 1 possible middles: each character (odd length) and each gap between two characters (even length). From each one, I grow outwards while the ends match.

static String longestPalindrome(String s) {
  int start = 0, best = 0;
  for (int c = 0; c < s.length(); c++) {
    int odd = expand(s, c, c);               // center on a char
    int even = expand(s, c, c + 1);          // center between chars
    int len = Math.max(odd, even);
    if (len > best) {
      best = len;
      start = c - (len - 1) / 2;
    }
  }
  return s.substring(start, start + best);
}

static int expand(String s, int l, int r) {
  while (l >= 0 && r < s.length() && s.charAt(l) == s.charAt(r)) { l--; r++; }
  return r - l - 1;
}
// "babad" -> "bab"   "cbbd" -> "bb"

It's O(n²) time in the worst case, like a string of all the same letter, and O(1) extra space. That beats the DP table version, which is also O(n²) time but uses O(n²) memory for no gain.

The fiddly part is the start index. When the loop stops, l and r are one step past the palindrome, so the length is r - l - 1. c - (len - 1) / 2 works for both odd and even lengths. I usually check it on "cbbd": center 1, even length 2, start = 1.

If they push for linear time, that's Manacher's algorithm, O(n). I'd name it and explain that it reuses mirrored radii, but I wouldn't write it unless asked.

AdvancedHow would you find the longest substring that appears at least twice?

So I binary search on the length, and check each length with a rolling hash. If some substring of length L repeats, then a shorter one repeats too (just take its prefix). That monotonic yes/no means binary search is valid. Each check slides a window of length L, stores hashes, and looks for a repeat.

static String longestDupSubstring(String s) {
  int lo = 1, hi = s.length() - 1;
  int bestStart = -1, bestLen = 0;
  while (lo <= hi) {
    int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
    int start = findDup(s, mid);
    if (start != -1) { bestStart = start; bestLen = mid; lo = mid + 1; }
    else hi = mid - 1;
  }
  return bestStart == -1 ? "" : s.substring(bestStart, bestStart + bestLen);
}

static int findDup(String s, int len) {       // start of a repeated length-len substring, or -1
  final long M = 1_000_000_007L, B = 131;
  long pow = 1, h = 0;
  for (int i = 0; i < len; i++) { h = (h * B + s.charAt(i)) % M; if (i > 0) pow = pow * B % M; }
  Map<Long, List<Integer>> seen = new HashMap<>();
  seen.computeIfAbsent(h, k -> new ArrayList<>()).add(0);
  for (int i = 1; i + len <= s.length(); i++) {
    h = (h - s.charAt(i - 1) * pow % M + M) % M;
    h = (h * B + s.charAt(i + len - 1)) % M;
    List<Integer> starts = seen.computeIfAbsent(h, k -> new ArrayList<>());
    for (int j : starts)
      if (s.regionMatches(i, s, j, len)) return i;   // real match, not a collision
    starts.add(i);
  }
  return -1;
}
// "banana" -> "ana"   "abcd" -> ""

Expected cost is O(n log n): log n binary search steps, each an O(n) scan. I keep a list of start positions per hash and confirm with regionMatches, so a collision can't give a wrong answer. It only costs extra time.

The pattern here is useful beyond this problem: "binary search on the answer, plus a linear check". I also mention the textbook alternative, a suffix array with an LCP array, which gives the answer directly as the max LCP value. But it's much more code for an interview.

Interviewers look for: the argument for why the check is monotonic, and how you handle collisions. On LeetCode 1044, a single weak hash without verification fails on purpose-built tests.

29. Math & Number Theory

BasicHow do you compute GCD and LCM, and why does Euclid's algorithm work?

So Euclid's algorithm uses one fact: gcd(a, b) = gcd(b, a % b). Any number that divides both a and b also divides a - k*b, which is the remainder. So I keep replacing the pair until the second number is zero, and the first is the answer.

static long gcd(long a, long b) {
  while (b != 0) {
    long t = a % b;
    a = b;
    b = t;
  }
  return Math.abs(a);
}

static long lcm(long a, long b) {
  return a / gcd(a, b) * b;                  // divide first to avoid overflow
}
// gcd(48, 18): (48,18) -> (18,12) -> (12,6) -> (6,0) -> 6
// lcm(4, 6) = 12

It's O(log min(a, b)). The quick reason is that every two steps the bigger number at least halves. The worst case is two consecutive Fibonacci numbers.

For LCM, the formula is a * b / gcd, but I write it as a / gcd * b. Multiplying first can overflow even when the final LCM fits. Dividing first keeps the middle value small.

Where I've used GCD in problems: simplifying fractions, "GCD of strings" (LC 1071), checking whether a jug amount is reachable (it has to be a multiple of the GCD), and reducing a slope dy/dx to a canonical key when counting points on a line.

BasicCount the primes below n. Walk me through the Sieve of Eratosthenes.

So instead of testing each number, I cross out multiples. I walk from 2 upward. Any number that is still not crossed out is prime, and I cross out all its multiples. By the end, everything unmarked is prime.

static int countPrimes(int n) {              // primes strictly less than n
  if (n < 3) return 0;
  boolean[] composite = new boolean[n];
  int count = 0;
  for (int i = 2; i < n; i++) {
    if (composite[i]) continue;
    count++;
    for (long j = (long) i * i; j < n; j += i) {   // long: i*i can overflow
      composite[(int) j] = true;
    }
  }
  return count;
}
// countPrimes(10) -> 4   (2, 3, 5, 7)
// countPrimes(1_000_000) -> 78498

Why start crossing at i * i? Any smaller multiple, like 3 * i, has a smaller prime factor, so it was already crossed out by that factor. That's also why I only need to sieve with primes up to sqrt(n).

The complexity is O(n log log n), which is basically linear in practice, and O(n) memory. Checking each number by trial division would be O(n sqrt n).

The gotcha is the i * i. For i around 50,000, i * i is bigger than Integer.MAX_VALUE and wraps to a negative number. As an int, the loop might then write to a negative index. Using a long for j avoids it.

For a single number I'd just do trial division up to sqrt(n). The sieve pays off when I need many primes, or many answers, up to some limit.

BasicWhat integer overflow traps do you watch for in Java?

So Java integers wrap around silently. There's no exception, just a wrong number. I watch for a handful of specific places where that happens in DSA code.

int a = 100_000, b = 100_000;
long wrong = (long) (a * b);   // 1410065408: multiplied as int, then widened
long right = (long) a * b;     // 10000000000: widened first

Integer.MAX_VALUE + 1          // -2147483648
Math.abs(Integer.MIN_VALUE)    // -2147483648 (still negative!)
-7 % 3                         // -1
Math.floorMod(-7, 3)           // 2

int lo = 2_000_000_000, hi = 2_100_000_000;
(lo + hi) / 2                  // -97483648 (overflowed)
lo + (hi - lo) / 2             // 2050000000
(lo + hi) >>> 1                // 2050000000

Math.multiplyExact(a, b)       // throws ArithmeticException

The one that catches most people is the cast. (long) (a * b) multiplies as int first, so it has already overflowed before the cast. The cast has to be on an operand.

The others I check for: the binary search midpoint; Integer.MAX_VALUE used as infinity and then having something added to it; Math.abs and negation of MIN_VALUE (which is why reversing an integer or pow(x, n) need a long); comparators written as a - b; and a % of a negative number used as an array index.

So in practice, I use long for any sum or product that could pass about 2 x 109. When I want a loud failure instead of a silent one, I use Math.addExact or multiplyExact.

Interviewers look for: the (long) (a * b) trap and abs(MIN_VALUE). Both come up in "reverse integer" and "pow" follow-ups.

IntermediateImplement Pow(x, n) in O(log n). What edge case breaks naive solutions?

So I use binary exponentiation. I read the exponent in binary. x^13 is x^8 * x^4 * x^1, because 13 is 1101. I keep squaring x to get x, x², x⁴, x⁸, and multiply into the result whenever the current bit is 1. That's O(log n) multiplications instead of n.

static double myPow(double x, int n) {
  long e = n;                                // -Integer.MIN_VALUE overflows an int
  if (e < 0) { x = 1 / x; e = -e; }
  double result = 1;
  while (e > 0) {
    if ((e & 1) == 1) result *= x;           // this bit is set: use current power
    x *= x;                                  // x, x^2, x^4, x^8 ...
    e >>= 1;
  }
  return result;
}
// myPow(2, 10) -> 1024.0    myPow(2, -2) -> 0.25

The edge case is n = Integer.MIN_VALUE. To handle a negative exponent I flip it to positive, but -(-2147483648) is still -2147483648 as an int. The loop then runs on a negative exponent and gives the wrong answer. Copying to a long first fixes it. That's the test case LC 50 includes on purpose.

The same loop with % mod after every multiply is modular exponentiation, modPow(base, exp, mod). That's what I use for things like a^b mod 1_000_000_007 and for modular inverses. With long and a mod near 109, each product stays under 1018, which fits.

A linear loop would also time out here, because n can be about two billion.

IntermediateWhy do problems ask for the answer mod 1_000_000_007, and how do you compute nCr under a mod?

So the true answer is too big for a long, and the mod keeps it small while still letting them check correctness. 1_000_000_007 is chosen because it's prime and fits in an int. The product of two values under it still fits in a long. And being prime means every non-zero number has a modular inverse, which is how I "divide".

The rules: add, subtract and multiply all commute with mod, so I mod after every step. Subtraction can go negative, so I add MOD back. Division is not allowed directly. Instead, I multiply by the inverse, which by Fermat's little theorem is a^(MOD - 2).

static final long MOD = 1_000_000_007L;

static long inverse(long a) {
  return modPow(a, MOD - 2, MOD);            // Fermat: a^(p-2) = a^-1 mod p
}
// modPow is the binary-power loop from Pow(x, n), with % MOD after each multiply

static long[] fact, invFact;
static void precompute(int n) {
  fact = new long[n + 1];
  invFact = new long[n + 1];
  fact[0] = 1;
  for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) fact[i] = fact[i - 1] * i % MOD;
  invFact[n] = inverse(fact[n]);             // one modPow
  for (int i = n; i > 0; i--) invFact[i - 1] = invFact[i] * i % MOD;
}

static long nCr(int n, int r) {
  if (r < 0 || r > n) return 0;
  return fact[n] * invFact[r] % MOD * invFact[n - r] % MOD;
}
// inverse(2) -> 500000004    nCr(5, 2) -> 10    (3 - 5) % MOD -> -2 (add MOD!)

After an O(n) precompute, each nCr is O(1). The backwards loop for invFact is a small trick: I compute one inverse with modPow, then get the rest by multiplying, since 1/(i-1)! = i/i!.

If n is small, like 1,000, I'd just use Pascal's triangle, C[n][r] = C[n-1][r-1] + C[n-1][r]. It needs no inverses at all.

Interviewers look for: "you can't divide under a mod, you multiply by the inverse", and the negative-after-subtraction fix.

AdvancedPick a uniformly random element from a stream of unknown length, using O(1) memory.

So that's reservoir sampling. I keep one "chosen" element. When the i-th element arrives (counting from 1), I replace my choice with it with probability 1/i. When the stream ends, every element has been chosen with probability exactly 1/n, even though I never knew n.

static int reservoir(Iterator<Integer> stream, Random rnd) {
  int chosen = -1, seen = 0;
  while (stream.hasNext()) {
    int x = stream.next();
    seen++;
    if (rnd.nextInt(seen) == 0) chosen = x;  // keep x with probability 1/seen
  }
  return chosen;
}
// 100,000 runs over [0..4] gave about 20,000 each:
// [20028, 20047, 20033, 19874, 20018]

The proof is a short product. For element i to be the final answer, it has to be picked when it arrives, with probability 1/i. Then it must survive every later step. At step i + 1 it survives with i/(i+1), at i + 2 with (i+1)/(i+2), and so on up to (n-1)/n. That product cancels down to 1/n.

For k samples, I fill the reservoir with the first k items. Then for item i > k, I draw r = nextInt(i), and if r < k I replace slot r. The same argument gives each item a k/n chance.

Where it shows up: Linked List Random Node (LC 382), Random Pick Index (LC 398), and in real systems, sampling log lines or events from a stream too big to store.

Interviewers look for: the survival-product proof, not just the code. The usual follow-up is "now pick k items".

Part 5 — Problem-Solving Patterns

30. Two Pointers

BasicHow do you recognise that a problem is a two-pointer problem?

So the strongest signal is a sorted array (or one I'm allowed to sort) where I need a pair or triplet with some sum. The other signals are "compare from both ends", like palindromes and reversing, and "modify in place with O(1) extra space". Underneath all of them, the brute force is two nested loops, and the ordering lets me throw away one side at every step.

There are two flavours, and I decide which one early:

FlavourPointersTypical problems
Opposite endsl = 0, r = n - 1, walk inwardsTwo Sum II, 3Sum, Container With Most Water, Valid Palindrome, Trapping Rain Water
Same directionfast reads, slow marks the next write spotRemove Duplicates, Move Zeroes, Remove Element

Let me show you the same-direction flavour quickly, since it looks different:

static int removeDuplicates(int[] nums) {    // sorted input
  if (nums.length == 0) return 0;
  int slow = 1;                              // next write position
  for (int fast = 1; fast < nums.length; fast++) {
    if (nums[fast] != nums[slow - 1]) nums[slow++] = nums[fast];
  }
  return slow;
}
// [0,0,1,1,1,2,2,3,3,4] -> 5, array starts with [0,1,2,3,4]

What happens is that fast scans everything, and slow only moves when I keep an element. Everything before slow is the finished answer.

So in practice, when I read "sorted", "pair", "in place", or "palindrome", I try two pointers first. It usually turns an O(n²) brute force into O(n) with O(1) space.

Interviewers look for: that you name the flavour and say what each pointer means. They'll ask "what if the array isn't sorted?" next.

BasicWalk me through Two Sum II on a sorted array, and convince me it can't miss the answer.

So I start with one pointer at each end. If the sum is too small, I move the left pointer right to get a bigger number. If it's too big, I move the right pointer left. I stop when they meet or I find the target.

static int[] twoSum(int[] nums, int target) {  // sorted input, 1-indexed answer
  int l = 0, r = nums.length - 1;
  while (l < r) {
    int sum = nums[l] + nums[r];
    if (sum == target) return new int[]{l + 1, r + 1};
    if (sum < target) l++;                   // need bigger: move the small end up
    else r--;                                // need smaller: move the big end down
  }
  return new int[]{-1, -1};
}
// [2, 7, 11, 15], target 9 -> [1, 2]

Why it can't miss: when nums[l] + nums[r] is too small, nums[l] paired with anything to the left of r is even smaller, because the array is sorted. So nums[l] can't be in any answer, and I can safely drop it. The same argument, mirrored, works for dropping nums[r] when the sum is too big. Each step removes one element that can't be part of the answer, so the real pair is never skipped.

It's O(n) time and O(1) space. The hash map version is also O(n), but it uses O(n) memory. With sorted input, two pointers is strictly better.

One small thing: if values can be near Integer.MAX_VALUE, I compute the sum as a long.

IntermediateWalk me through 3Sum. How do you avoid duplicate triplets?

So I sort the array, fix the first number with a loop, and run Two Sum II on the rest for target -nums[i]. That's O(n²) overall. Sorting also solves the duplicates problem, because equal values end up next to each other and I can just skip over them.

static List<List<Integer>> threeSum(int[] nums) {
  Arrays.sort(nums);
  List<List<Integer>> res = new ArrayList<>();
  for (int i = 0; i < nums.length - 2; i++) {
    if (i > 0 && nums[i] == nums[i - 1]) continue;   // skip duplicate anchors
    if (nums[i] > 0) break;                          // smallest is positive: done
    int l = i + 1, r = nums.length - 1;
    while (l < r) {
      int sum = nums[i] + nums[l] + nums[r];
      if (sum < 0) l++;
      else if (sum > 0) r--;
      else {
        res.add(List.of(nums[i], nums[l], nums[r]));
        l++;
        r--;
        while (l < r && nums[l] == nums[l - 1]) l++;  // skip duplicate lefts
      }
    }
  }
  return res;
}
// [-1, 0, 1, 2, -1, -4] -> [[-1, -1, 2], [-1, 0, 1]]
// [0, 0, 0, 0]          -> [[0, 0, 0]]

There are two places to dedupe. For the anchor, I skip i if it equals the previous anchor. I compare with i - 1, not i + 1, so that a triplet like [-1, -1, 2] can still use two equal values. For the pair, after a hit I move both pointers and skip left values equal to the one I just used. Skipping on the left is enough: once the left value is new, the matching right value must be new too.

The nums[i] > 0 break is a small optimisation. If the smallest of the three is positive, the sum can't be zero.

A HashSet of triplets also removes duplicates, but it's slower and it hides the reasoning, so I prefer the skip loops.

IntermediateContainer With Most Water: why is it safe to always move the shorter wall?

So the area is min(h[l], h[r]) x (r - l). The shorter wall caps the height. If I moved the taller wall inwards, the width drops and the height is still capped by the same short wall, so the area can only get worse. Every container that uses the short wall with a closer partner is no better than the one I just measured. So I can drop the short wall for good.

static int maxArea(int[] h) {
  int l = 0, r = h.length - 1, best = 0;
  while (l < r) {
    best = Math.max(best, Math.min(h[l], h[r]) * (r - l));
    if (h[l] < h[r]) l++;                    // the shorter wall is the limit
    else r--;
  }
  return best;
}
// [1, 8, 6, 2, 5, 4, 8, 3, 7] -> 49   (walls at index 1 and 8: min(8, 7) x 7)

Let me trace the start. l = 0 (height 1), r = 8 (height 7): area 8. The left wall is shorter, and any container using height 1 is at most 1 tall, so index 0 is done. Move l. Now h[1] = 8 and h[8] = 7: area 49, which turns out to be the answer.

When the walls are equal, I can move either one. Neither can be part of a bigger container with a closer partner.

It's O(n) instead of checking all O(n²) pairs. I've checked this against the brute force on random arrays and they always match.

Interviewers look for: the argument, not just the code. "Move the shorter one" without the reason sounds memorised.

IntermediateHow do you solve Trapping Rain Water with two pointers?

So the water above each bar is min(maxLeft, maxRight) - height. The two-pointer trick is that I don't need both maxes exactly. If h[l] < h[r], I know there's a wall on the right at least as tall as h[l]. So the water at l is decided by leftMax alone, and I can settle that bar and move on.

static int trap(int[] h) {
  int l = 0, r = h.length - 1;
  int leftMax = 0, rightMax = 0, water = 0;
  while (l < r) {
    if (h[l] < h[r]) {                       // left side is the bottleneck
      leftMax = Math.max(leftMax, h[l]);
      water += leftMax - h[l];
      l++;
    } else {
      rightMax = Math.max(rightMax, h[r]);
      water += rightMax - h[r];
      r--;
    }
  }
  return water;
}
// [0,1,0,2,1,0,1,3,2,1,2,1] -> 6

What happens is that I always process the lower side. The lower side's max is the limiting wall, because the other side already has something at least as tall. Updating leftMax before adding means a new tallest bar just adds zero, which handles that case without an extra branch.

The simpler way is two prefix arrays, maxLeft[i] and maxRight[i], then one pass. That's also O(n) time but O(n) space. I usually explain that version first, because it makes the formula obvious, then compress it to two pointers for O(1) space.

A monotonic stack also works and computes water layer by layer, which some people find easier to extend. I checked the two-pointer version against a brute force on random arrays and they matched every time.

AdvancedWhen does two pointers NOT apply? Give me examples where it's the wrong tool.

So two pointers only works when moving a pointer lets me safely throw something away. That needs some kind of order: a sorted array, or a rule that changes in one direction. Without that, moving a pointer can skip the answer.

The cases I watch for:

Unsorted input where I need original indices. Classic Two Sum returns indices. Sorting destroys them, and sorting costs O(n log n) anyway. A hash map does it in one pass.

static int[] twoSumUnsorted(int[] nums, int target) {
  Map<Integer, Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();   // value -> index
  for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    Integer j = seen.get(target - nums[i]);
    if (j != null) return new int[]{j, i};
    seen.put(nums[i], i);
  }
  return new int[]{-1, -1};
}
// [3, 2, 4], target 6 -> [1, 2]

Subarray sums with negative numbers. "Count subarrays with sum k" looks like a window, but with negatives, growing the window can make the sum smaller. So there's no safe direction to move. That's a prefix sum plus hash map problem.

Counting all pairs, not finding one. "How many pairs sum to less than k" still works with sorting (add r - l pairs at a time). But "how many pairs have a sum divisible by k" has no order to exploit, so I'd count remainders in a map instead.

Ordering is not the relevant property. For "closest pair of points in 2D", sorting by x doesn't make the pointer moves safe. It needs divide and conquer or a sweep.

So my check is: can I say, in one sentence, why the element I'm skipping can never be part of the answer? If I can't, I don't use two pointers.

Interviewers look for: the hash map fallback for unsorted Two Sum, and the negative-numbers case that pushes you to prefix sums.

31. Sliding Window

BasicHow do you recognise a sliding window problem? What's the difference between a fixed and a variable window?

So the words I listen for are "contiguous subarray" or "substring", plus "longest", "shortest", "at most k", or "every window of size k". The key requirement is that the rule is monotonic: if a window breaks the rule, making it bigger keeps it broken. That's what makes it safe to shrink from the left.

A fixed window has size k the whole time. Every step, one element comes in and one goes out.

static int maxSumK(int[] a, int k) {
  int sum = 0;
  for (int i = 0; i < k; i++) sum += a[i];
  int best = sum;
  for (int r = k; r < a.length; r++) {
    sum += a[r] - a[r - k];                  // one in, one out
    best = Math.max(best, sum);
  }
  return best;
}
// [2, 1, 5, 1, 3, 2], k = 3 -> 9   (5 + 1 + 3)

A variable window grows by moving right every step. When the window breaks the rule, left moves forward until it's valid again. Both pointers only go forward, so each element enters once and leaves once. That's O(n), even though there's a loop inside a loop.

I also watch for "subsequence". A subsequence can skip elements, and a window can't, so that's usually DP instead.

So my first question on any array or string problem is "is the answer contiguous?" If yes, I try a window. Then I ask whether the size is fixed or depends on a rule.

BasicWalk me through Longest Substring Without Repeating Characters.

So I keep a window with no repeated characters and a count of each character in it. I extend right one character at a time. If that character now appears twice, I shrink from left until it appears once. After each step, the window is valid, so I record its length.

static int lengthOfLongestSubstring(String s) {
  int[] count = new int[128];
  int left = 0, best = 0;
  for (int right = 0; right < s.length(); right++) {
    char c = s.charAt(right);
    count[c]++;
    while (count[c] > 1) {                   // window broke the rule: shrink
      count[s.charAt(left)]--;
      left++;
    }
    best = Math.max(best, right - left + 1);
  }
  return best;
}
// "abcabcbb" -> 3 ("abc")    "pwwkew" -> 3 ("wke")

Let me trace "pwwkew". The window grows to "pw". The second w makes w appear twice, so I drop p, then the first w, and I'm left with "w". Then it grows to "wke", length 3. The last w forces the window down to "kew", still 3.

I use int[128] instead of a HashMap because the input is ASCII. It's faster and avoids boxing. For full Unicode I'd switch to a map.

There's also a jump version: store each character's last index and set left = max(left, last[c] + 1) directly. The max is the part people forget. Without it, left can jump backwards to an old position. Both versions are O(n).

IntermediateWalk me through Minimum Window Substring.

So this is the "shortest" version of the window. I grow right until the window covers every character of t, with the right counts. Then, while it's still valid, I shrink from left and record the smallest valid window. When shrinking breaks it, I go back to growing.

static String minWindow(String s, String t) {
  int[] need = new int[128];
  for (char c : t.toCharArray()) need[c]++;
  int missing = t.length();                  // chars of t still not covered
  int left = 0, bestStart = 0, bestLen = Integer.MAX_VALUE;
  for (int right = 0; right < s.length(); right++) {
    if (need[s.charAt(right)]-- > 0) missing--;
    while (missing == 0) {                   // valid: try to shrink
      if (right - left + 1 < bestLen) {
        bestLen = right - left + 1;
        bestStart = left;
      }
      if (++need[s.charAt(left)] > 0) missing++;   // lost a needed char
      left++;
    }
  }
  return bestLen == Integer.MAX_VALUE ? "" : s.substring(bestStart, bestStart + bestLen);
}
// minWindow("ADOBECODEBANC", "ABC") -> "BANC"

The trick that keeps it O(n) is the single missing counter. I don't compare two maps each step. need[c] can go negative, which means the window has extra copies of c. missing only drops when I add a character that was actually needed, and only rises when I remove one that becomes needed again.

Characters not in t just go negative and never affect missing, so I don't need to special-case them.

Compare with the previous problem: there I record the answer after shrinking, because I want the longest valid window. Here I record it inside the shrink loop, because I want the shortest. That one placement is the difference between the "longest" and "shortest" templates.

Interviewers look for: the missing counter instead of comparing maps, and duplicates in t handled correctly ("AA" needs two A's).

IntermediateIn Longest Repeating Character Replacement, why don't you need to decrease maxFreq when the window shrinks?

So a window is valid if length - maxFreq <= k: I replace everything except the most common letter. When left moves, the true max frequency might drop, and I don't update it. That's fine, because I only care about finding a longer window. A longer window can only beat my best if some letter reaches a higher frequency than before. So a stale, too-high maxFreq never makes me report a wrong answer.

static int characterReplacement(String s, int k) {
  int[] count = new int[26];
  int left = 0, maxFreq = 0, best = 0;
  for (int right = 0; right < s.length(); right++) {
    maxFreq = Math.max(maxFreq, ++count[s.charAt(right) - 'A']);
    if (right - left + 1 - maxFreq > k) {    // too many to replace
      count[s.charAt(left) - 'A']--;
      left++;                                // slide, don't shrink
    }
    best = Math.max(best, right - left + 1);
  }
  return best;
}
// ("AABABBA", 1) -> 4    ("ABAB", 2) -> 4

Notice it's an if, not a while. Once I've found a window of size w, I never let it get smaller. When it's invalid I move both ends by one, so the window slides at its best size and only grows when maxFreq goes up. The window at the end may not be valid, but best was recorded when it was.

This is subtle, so in an interview I'd say the safe version first: recompute the max over 26 counts when needed, which is O(26n). Then I'd offer this as the optimisation. I checked it against a brute force on random strings.

Interviewers look for: a real explanation of the stale maxFreq. Many candidates write this code but can't say why it's correct.

IntermediateHow do you count subarrays with exactly K distinct values? A plain window doesn't seem to work.

So "exactly K" isn't monotonic. Adding an element can take the window from too few to exactly right to too many, so there's no single rule to shrink on. But "at most K" is monotonic. So I compute exactly(K) = atMost(K) - atMost(K - 1).

static int subarraysWithKDistinct(int[] nums, int k) {
  return atMost(nums, k) - atMost(nums, k - 1);
}

static int atMost(int[] nums, int k) {
  Map<Integer, Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
  int left = 0, count = 0;
  for (int right = 0; right < nums.length; right++) {
    if (freq.merge(nums[right], 1, Integer::sum) == 1) k--;   // new distinct value
    while (k < 0) {
      if (freq.merge(nums[left], -1, Integer::sum) == 0) k++;
      left++;
    }
    count += right - left + 1;               // every subarray ending at right
  }
  return count;
}
// [1, 2, 1, 2, 3], K = 2 -> 7

The counting line is the key idea. Once the window [left, right] is valid, every subarray that ends at right and starts anywhere from left to right is also valid, since it's a smaller piece. That's right - left + 1 subarrays, added in one go.

I use k itself as the "distinct slots left" budget. It goes down when a new value enters and up when a value's count hits zero.

The same trick handles "Count Number of Nice Subarrays" (exactly K odd numbers) and "Binary Subarrays With Sum". Whenever I see "exactly", I check if "at most" is monotonic and subtract. Total time is two O(n) passes.

AdvancedWhen does a sliding window NOT work, and what do you use instead?

So a window needs one thing: when the window breaks the rule, moving left forward must be the only fix, and making it bigger must never fix it. When that's false, shrinking can throw away the answer.

The classic failure is a sum target with negative numbers. Take "count subarrays summing to 0" on [1, -1, 1, -1]. There's no rule for when to shrink. A sum that's too big can come back down by adding a negative. So I switch to prefix sums plus a hash map, which is O(n) and doesn't care about signs.

// "shortest subarray with sum at least K", with negatives (LC 862):
// a plain window is wrong. Use prefix sums + a monotonic deque of indices:
//   pre[j] - pre[i] >= K  ->  pop i from the front, record j - i
//   keep deque prefix values increasing (pop the back while pre[back] >= pre[j])

Other cases where I change tools:

Problem shapeWhy a window failsWhat I use
Subarray sum = k with negativesNot monotonicPrefix sum + hash map
Max or min in every window of size kA window works, but finding the max inside it isn't O(1)Window + monotonic deque
Longest subsequence with some propertyNot contiguousDP
"Exactly k" of somethingNot monotonicatMost(k) - atMost(k - 1)
Median of every windowNeed order statisticsTwo heaps or a TreeMap

So my test before writing any window code is: "if the window is invalid, can extending it ever make it valid again?" If the answer is yes, I don't use a plain window.

Interviewers look for: the negative-numbers counterexample and the prefix-sum switch. Mentioning the monotonic deque for LC 862 is a strong senior signal.

32. Prefix Sum & Difference Arrays

BasicWhat is a prefix sum, and how do you recognise when to use one?

So a prefix sum array stores running totals, so the sum of any range is one subtraction: sum(l..r) = pre[r + 1] - pre[l]. It's O(n) to build and O(1) per query. I reach for it when I see many range-sum queries on an array that doesn't change, or "count subarrays with sum k / divisible by k / equal 0s and 1s", especially with negative numbers, where a window fails.

static int[] prefix(int[] a) {
  int[] pre = new int[a.length + 1];         // pre[i] = sum of a[0..i-1]
  for (int i = 0; i < a.length; i++) pre[i + 1] = pre[i] + a[i];
  return pre;
}

static int rangeSum(int[] pre, int l, int r) {   // inclusive l..r
  return pre[r + 1] - pre[l];
}
// a   = [-2, 0, 3, -5, 2, -1]
// pre = [0, -2, -2, 1, -4, -2, -3]
// rangeSum(0, 2) = 1    rangeSum(2, 5) = -1

The n + 1 size with pre[0] = 0 is the detail I care about most. It means a range starting at index 0 needs no special case. That's the classic off-by-one bug with size-n prefix arrays.

The same idea works for anything I can "undo": counts, XOR (pre[r+1] ^ pre[l]), and products if there are no zeros. For Product of Array Except Self, I use a prefix product from the left and a suffix product from the right, and no division at all.

So in practice, when the brute force is "for every range, add it up", I build a prefix array once and the inner loop disappears.

Interviewers look for: the n + 1 convention stated clearly, and Range Sum Query (LC 303) done with O(1) queries.

BasicWalk me through Subarray Sum Equals K. Why seed the map with 0?

So a subarray (i, j] sums to k exactly when pre[j] - pre[i] = k, which means pre[i] = pre[j] - k. So as I walk, I keep a map of how many times each prefix sum has appeared. At each position, I add the count of sum - k seen so far.

static int subarraySum(int[] nums, int k) {
  Map<Integer, Integer> seen = new HashMap<>();   // prefix sum -> how many times
  seen.put(0, 1);                            // the empty prefix
  int sum = 0, count = 0;
  for (int x : nums) {
    sum += x;
    count += seen.getOrDefault(sum - k, 0);  // earlier prefixes that leave exactly k
    seen.merge(sum, 1, Integer::sum);
  }
  return count;
}
// [1, 1, 1], k = 2 -> 2

The seed {0: 1} stands for the empty prefix before index 0. Without it, I'd miss every subarray that starts at index 0. In [1, 1, 1] with k = 2, the subarray [1, 1] at the start needs sum - k = 0 to be in the map.

The order inside the loop matters too. I look up before adding the current sum. Otherwise, when k = 0, I'd match the current prefix with itself and count an empty subarray.

It's O(n) time and O(n) space. And it works with negative numbers, which is exactly why this isn't a sliding window problem. I've checked it against an O(n²) brute force on random arrays with negatives.

Interviewers look for: the seed and the lookup-before-insert order. The follow-up is often "divisible by k" (LC 974): store floorMod(sum, k) instead.

IntermediateHow would you find the longest subarray with an equal number of 0s and 1s?

So I turn it into a prefix-sum problem by treating every 0 as -1. Then "equal 0s and 1s" means "sums to 0", and a subarray sums to 0 when the same running balance shows up twice. For the longest, I store the first index where each balance appeared.

static int findMaxLength(int[] nums) {
  Map<Integer, Integer> first = new HashMap<>();  // balance -> first index seen
  first.put(0, -1);
  int balance = 0, best = 0;
  for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
    balance += nums[i] == 1 ? 1 : -1;        // treat 0 as -1
    Integer j = first.get(balance);
    if (j != null) best = Math.max(best, i - j);
    else first.put(balance, i);              // keep only the earliest
  }
  return best;
}
// [0, 1, 0]                -> 2
// [0, 0, 1, 0, 0, 0, 1, 1] -> 6   (indices 2..7)

The differences from the last problem are worth pointing out. There I stored counts because I wanted how many subarrays. Here I store the first index because I want the longest one. If I overwrote it with later indices, I'd shorten the answer. The seed is 0 -> -1, so a balanced prefix starting at index 0 gets length i + 1.

Since the balance is always between -n and n, I could use an array of size 2n + 1 with an offset instead of a map. That's faster in practice, with the same O(n).

So the general move is: "turn the condition into a sum being zero or k, then match prefix sums." Counting vowels versus consonants, or "more 1s than 0s", reduce the same way.

IntermediateWhat's a difference array? Walk me through Car Pooling with it.

So a difference array is the reverse of a prefix sum. To add v to every element in [l, r], I write just two numbers: +v at l and -v at r + 1. After all the updates, one prefix-sum pass rebuilds the real values. So q range updates cost O(q + n) instead of O(q x n).

static boolean carPooling(int[][] trips, int capacity) {
  int[] diff = new int[1001];                // stops are 0..1000
  for (int[] t : trips) {
    diff[t[1]] += t[0];                      // get on at from
    diff[t[2]] -= t[0];                      // get off at to (not to + 1)
  }
  int onBoard = 0;
  for (int d : diff) {
    onBoard += d;
    if (onBoard > capacity) return false;
  }
  return true;
}
// trips [[2,1,5],[3,3,7]]: capacity 4 -> false, capacity 5 -> true

Each trip is "passengers on board from stop from until just before to". Passengers get off at to, so the range is [from, to - 1], and the minus goes at to. That's the off-by-one to get right in this problem. In the example, between stops 3 and 4 there are 2 + 3 = 5 people. That fails with capacity 4 and passes with 5.

The running sum is literally "how many people are on the bus right now", which is a nice way to explain it. The same trick solves Corporate Flight Bookings (LC 1109), and "how many intervals cover each point".

If the coordinates were huge, like up to 109, I couldn't make the array. Then I'd put the +v and -v events in a TreeMap and sweep over them in order. That's O(q log q).

Interviewers look for: the inclusive vs exclusive endpoint reasoning, and the sweep-line fallback for huge coordinates.

IntermediateHow do you answer sub-rectangle sum queries on a 2D grid in O(1)?

So I build a 2D prefix array where pre[r][c] is the sum of the rectangle from the top-left corner to just before (r, c). Any sub-rectangle is then four lookups, using inclusion-exclusion: take the big rectangle, subtract the strip above and the strip on the left, and add back the corner I subtracted twice.

static class NumMatrix {
  private final int[][] pre;

  NumMatrix(int[][] m) {
    int R = m.length, C = m[0].length;
    pre = new int[R + 1][C + 1];
    for (int r = 0; r < R; r++)
      for (int c = 0; c < C; c++)
        pre[r + 1][c + 1] = m[r][c] + pre[r][c + 1] + pre[r + 1][c] - pre[r][c];
  }

  int sumRegion(int r1, int c1, int r2, int c2) {
    return pre[r2 + 1][c2 + 1] - pre[r1][c2 + 1] - pre[r2 + 1][c1] + pre[r1][c1];
  }
}
// LC 304 example grid: sumRegion(2,1,4,3) = 8, (1,1,2,2) = 11, (1,2,2,4) = 12

The build uses the same inclusion-exclusion. The cell itself, plus the block above, plus the block to the left, minus the overlap that got counted twice.

Again I pad with an extra row and column of zeros, so the first row and column need no special cases. That's the same n + 1 idea as in 1D. It's O(R x C) to build and O(1) per query.

This comes up inside bigger problems too. Matrix Block Sum (LC 1314), "count submatrices that sum to target" (fix two rows, then run the 1D subarray-sum-equals-k trick on the column sums), and image processing box blurs all use it.

AdvancedWhen does a prefix sum stop being the right tool?

So a prefix sum assumes two things: the array doesn't change, and the operation can be undone by subtraction. When either one breaks, I switch tools.

The array gets updated. One point update means every prefix after it changes, so it's O(n) per update. With a mix of updates and queries, I use a Fenwick tree (Binary Indexed Tree). Both operations are O(log n).

static class Fenwick {
  private final long[] tree;
  Fenwick(int n) { tree = new long[n + 1]; }

  void add(int i, long delta) {              // 0-indexed position
    for (i++; i < tree.length; i += i & -i) tree[i] += delta;
  }

  long prefixSum(int i) {                    // sum of [0..i]
    long s = 0;
    for (i++; i > 0; i -= i & -i) s += tree[i];
    return s;
  }
}
// build from [1,2,3,4,5], then add(2, 7):
// prefixSum(3) = 17, range 2..4 = prefixSum(4) - prefixSum(1) = 19

The operation can't be undone. Min and max don't have an inverse. Knowing the max of [0..r] and of [0..l-1] tells you nothing about [l..r]. For static range-min I'd use a sparse table (O(1) query after O(n log n) build). With updates, I'd use a segment tree.

Range updates and range queries, both mixed. A difference array only gives answers after all updates are done. If queries come in between, I need a segment tree with lazy propagation, or two Fenwick trees.

Overflow. Prefix sums of large values grow fast. I use long[] as soon as the total could pass about 2 x 109.

So the order I'd reach for: prefix sum if it's static, Fenwick for point updates with sum queries, segment tree for anything more general.

Interviewers look for: "what if the array changes?" is the standard follow-up to LC 303. Naming Fenwick or a segment tree, with O(log n) for both operations, is the expected answer.

33. Fast & Slow Pointers

BasicHow do you recognise a fast and slow pointer problem?

So I reach for fast and slow pointers when I'm walking something that behaves like a linked list, where each step decides the next one, and I need to know either "does this loop?" or "where is the middle?". The giveaway is usually that the obvious answer is a HashSet of visited nodes, and the problem says O(1) extra space.

These are the signals I listen for:

What the problem saysWhy it fits
"Does the list have a cycle?", "where does the cycle start?"Floyd's tortoise and hare, the textbook case
"Find the middle", "split the list in half"When fast reaches the end, slow is halfway
A sequence x -> f(x) that might repeat (happy number)It's an implicit linked list, so it either ends or cycles
Values in 1..n used as indexes, one duplicateRead i -> nums[i] as next pointers; the duplicate is a cycle entry

The mental model I use is two runners on a track. On a straight road the fast one just finishes first. On a circular track the fast one laps the slow one and taps them on the shoulder, and that tap proves there's a loop.

So in practice, the moment I see "linked list" plus "cycle" or "middle", or I see a hidden "next" function with a space limit, I write the slow = slow.next; fast = fast.next.next loop.

Interviewers look for: spotting the implicit linked list. Happy Number and Find the Duplicate don't mention lists at all, and seeing the x -> f(x) chain is the real test.

BasicWalk me through detecting a cycle in a linked list.

So I start two pointers at the head. Slow moves one node per step, fast moves two. If fast ever hits null, the list has an end, so there's no cycle. If the two pointers ever point at the same node, there is one.

boolean hasCycle(ListNode head) {
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {   // fast needs two valid steps
        slow = slow.next;
        fast = fast.next.next;
        if (slow == fast) return true;             // same NODE, not same value
    }
    return false;                                  // fast fell off the end
}
// [3,2,0,-4] with the tail linked back to index 1 -> true
// [1,2] with no link                               -> false

The reason they must meet is simple once both are inside the loop. Think of the gap from slow to fast measured along the cycle. Every step fast gains exactly one node, so the gap goes 5, 4, 3, 2, 1, 0. It shrinks by one at a time, so it can't jump over zero. That also bounds the time: slow is caught before it finishes its first lap, so the whole thing is O(n) time and O(1) space.

Two bugs I watch for. First, checking only fast != null and then calling fast.next.next, which throws a NullPointerException on an even-length list. Second, comparing slow.val == fast.val. Two different nodes can hold the same value, so I compare references.

So in practice I'd mention the HashSet<ListNode> version first as the O(n)-space baseline, then give this as the O(1) improvement.

Interviewers look for: the follow-up "why speed 2 and not 3?". Speed 2 closes the gap by exactly 1 per step, so meeting is guaranteed. With speed 3 the gap shrinks by 2, so it can jump past 0 and take extra laps. Starting both at the head they still meet, but it's slower to argue, and the reset-to-head trick for finding the cycle start depends on fast having walked exactly twice as far.

IntermediateHow do you find the middle of a linked list, and where does that get used?

So it's the same loop with no meeting check. Fast covers the whole list while slow covers half, so when fast runs out, slow is sitting on the middle. The only real decision is which middle I want on an even-length list.

// second middle: [1,2,3,4,5,6] -> 4
ListNode secondMiddle(ListNode head) {
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {
        slow = slow.next;
        fast = fast.next.next;
    }
    return slow;
}

// first middle: [1,2,3,4,5,6] -> 3  (odd lengths give the same node)
ListNode firstMiddle(ListNode head) {       // assumes head != null
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast.next != null && fast.next.next != null) {
        slow = slow.next;
        fast = fast.next.next;
    }
    return slow;
}

The first-middle version is the one I use when I want to split the list, because I need the last node of the first half so I can set slow.next = null. That's exactly what merge sort on a linked list needs (LC 148). If I split at the second middle on a two-node list, one half is empty and the recursion never shrinks.

It's also the first step of a few combo problems. Palindrome Linked List is find the middle, reverse the second half, compare. Reorder List is find the middle, reverse the second half, weave.

So the answer I give is: same loop, and I pick the loop condition based on whether I'm returning a middle or cutting the list.

IntermediateHow do you find where the cycle starts, and why does it work?

So once slow and fast meet, I put one pointer back at the head and leave the other at the meeting point. Then I move both one step at a time. The node where they meet again is the start of the cycle.

ListNode detectCycle(ListNode head) {
    ListNode slow = head, fast = head;
    while (fast != null && fast.next != null) {
        slow = slow.next;
        fast = fast.next.next;
        if (slow == fast) {                  // phase 1: met inside the cycle
            ListNode p = head;               // phase 2
            while (p != slow) {
                p = p.next;
                slow = slow.next;
            }
            return p;
        }
    }
    return null;
}
// 1->2->3->4->5->6->7->(back to 3) -> node 3

Here's the proof I'd say out loud. Let the tail before the cycle be a nodes, the cycle length c, and say they meet b steps past the cycle start. Slow walked a + b. Fast walked twice that, 2(a + b). The extra a + b that fast walked must be whole laps, so a + b = k·c. Rearranged, a = (k-1)·c + (c - b).

In words: walking a steps from the meeting point takes you c - b steps to the cycle start, plus some full laps that land you back there. And walking a steps from the head also lands on the cycle start. So both pointers arrive together.

In the example, a = 2 and c = 5. They meet at node 6, and from there p goes 1, 2, 3 while slow goes 7, 3. Both on node 3.

Interviewers look for: the algebra, not just the code. Plenty of candidates memorise "reset to head" but can't say why. The follow-up is "how would you get the cycle length?": from the meeting point, walk once around counting until you're back.

IntermediateWhen does fast and slow NOT apply, and what would you use instead?

So it only works when there's a single "next" from each position, so the path is a chain or a rho shape. The moment a node can have several next steps, or I need more than "is there a loop" and "where's the middle", I use something else.

SituationWhy fast/slow failsWhat I use
Cycle in a general graph (course schedule)Many out-edges per nodeDFS with three colours, or Kahn's topological sort
I need every repeated node, or the order they were first seenFloyd only finds one meeting pointHashSet of visited, O(n) space
Middle of an arrayRandom access, so pointers are pointlessarr[n / 2]
"Nth node from the end"It's about a fixed gap, not speedTwo pointers at the same speed, n apart
Duplicate in 1..n and I'm allowed to modify the arrayFloyd works, but there's a simpler optionCyclic sort or negation marking

The gap variant is worth showing because people confuse the two:

ListNode removeNthFromEnd(ListNode head, int n) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0);
    dummy.next = head;
    ListNode lead = dummy, trail = dummy;
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) lead = lead.next;   // open a gap of n + 1
    while (lead != null) {                            // same speed
        lead = lead.next;
        trail = trail.next;
    }
    trail.next = trail.next.next;                     // trail is just before the target
    return dummy.next;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5], n = 2 -> [1,2,3,5]

So in practice my rule is: speed difference for cycles and middles, fixed gap for "k from the end", and a set or a graph algorithm when there's branching.

AdvancedFind the duplicate in an array of n + 1 numbers from 1..n without modifying it and in O(1) space.

So the trick is to read the array as a linked list where index i points to index nums[i]. Index 0 is never pointed to, since values start at 1, so it's the head. The duplicate value has two arrows coming into it, and a node with two incoming arrows in a functional graph is exactly the entry point of a cycle. So it's Linked List Cycle II on an array.

int findDuplicate(int[] nums) {
    int slow = nums[0], fast = nums[0];
    do {                                // phase 1: find a meeting point
        slow = nums[slow];
        fast = nums[nums[fast]];
    } while (slow != fast);
    slow = nums[0];                     // phase 2: restart one pointer
    while (slow != fast) {
        slow = nums[slow];
        fast = nums[fast];
    }
    return slow;
}
// [1,3,4,2,2] -> 2,  [3,1,3,4,2] -> 3,  [3,3,3,3,3] -> 3

Let me trace [1,3,4,2,2]. The chain is 0 -> 1 -> 3 -> 2 -> 4 -> 2 -> 4... Phase 1: slow goes 3 then 2, fast goes 2 then 2, so they meet at 2. Phase 2: slow restarts, goes 1, 3, 2 while fast goes 4, 2. Both land on 2, which is the duplicate.

I use do-while because both pointers start equal, so a plain while would exit immediately. It's O(n) time and O(1) space, and it never writes to the array.

The alternatives I'd mention: a HashSet is O(n) space; sorting modifies the array; cyclic sort also modifies it; and binary search on the value range (count how many are <= mid) keeps the array read-only in O(n log n). Floyd is the only one that hits all three constraints.

Interviewers look for: why the duplicate is the cycle start. If you can say "two arrows point into it, and the cycle entry is the only node with two incoming arrows", you've shown you understand it rather than memorised it.

34. Monotonic Stack & Queue

BasicWhat is a monotonic stack, and how do you recognise a problem that needs one?

So a monotonic stack is a normal stack with one house rule: its values stay sorted, always decreasing or always increasing from bottom to top. Before I push a new element, I pop everything that would break the order, and the pops are where the answers come from.

The recognition signal is a question about the nearest bigger or smaller element in some direction:

  • "Next greater element", "previous smaller element", "first element to the right that's bigger".
  • "How many days until a warmer day", "stock span", "how far can this bar stretch".
  • "Largest rectangle in a histogram", "trapping rain water".
  • "Max of every window of size k", which is the deque version.

The tell in the brute force is "for each i, scan right until I find something bigger". That's O(n²), and the stack makes it O(n).

The way I picture it is people in a queue each waiting for someone taller. When a tall person arrives, every shorter person still waiting has found their answer and leaves. The people left waiting are always in decreasing height, and that's the stack.

So in practice, if I catch myself writing a nested loop that looks for the first element that beats the current one, I replace it with a stack of indexes.

Interviewers look for: the word "nearest". A monotonic stack answers nearest-greater and nearest-smaller questions. It doesn't answer "second greater" or "greatest in an arbitrary range" by itself.

BasicWalk me through Daily Temperatures with a monotonic stack.

So for each day I want how many days until a warmer one. I keep a stack of the indexes of days still waiting for their answer. Their temperatures are decreasing, because a warmer day would already have answered the colder ones below it.

int[] dailyTemperatures(int[] temps) {
    int[] answer = new int[temps.length];        // default 0 = never warmer
    Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();   // indexes of waiting days
    for (int i = 0; i < temps.length; i++) {
        while (!stack.isEmpty() && temps[stack.peek()] < temps[i]) {
            int j = stack.pop();
            answer[j] = i - j;                   // distance, which is why I store indexes
        }
        stack.push(i);
    }
    return answer;
}

Let me trace [73,74,75,71,69,72,76,73], showing the values on the stack:

i=2  75  pops 74           stack [75]
i=3  71  push              stack [75, 71]
i=4  69  push              stack [75, 71, 69]
i=5  72  pops 69 (ans 1), 71 (ans 2)     stack [75, 72]
i=6  76  pops 72 (ans 1), 75 (ans 4)     stack [76]
i=7  73  push, never popped -> 0
result [1, 1, 4, 2, 1, 1, 0, 0]

The question I always get is "a while inside a for, how is that O(n)?". Each index is pushed exactly once and popped at most once, so across the whole run there are at most 2n stack operations. It's amortised O(n) time and O(n) space.

I use ArrayDeque rather than the old Stack class, because Stack extends Vector and every call is synchronized for no reason.

IntermediateHow do you decide the stack direction and whether to use < or <=?

So I decide by what I'm looking for. For "next greater", the waiting elements must be decreasing, so a bigger newcomer pops them. For "next smaller" it's the mirror. For "previous" questions I pop the useless ones first and then the top is the answer for the current index.

I wantStack order (bottom to top)Pop whileAnswer comes from
Next greaterdecreasinga[top] < a[i]the popped items get a[i]
Next smallerincreasinga[top] > a[i]the popped items get a[i]
Previous greaterdecreasinga[top] <= a[i]the top, after popping
Previous smallerincreasinga[top] >= a[i]the top, after popping

Strict versus non-strict only matters with duplicates, and it matters most in counting problems. In Sum of Subarray Minimums, I count for each element how many subarrays it's the minimum of. If I use strict on both sides, a subarray like [2,2] gets counted for both 2s:

// arr = [2, 2]; true answer: [2] + [2] + [2,2] = 6
// strict on both sides:            8  (the [2,2] subarray counted twice)
// strict left, non-strict right:   6  (each tie owned by exactly one index)
while (!stack.isEmpty() && arr[stack.peek()] > arr[i]) stack.pop();   // left pass
while (!stack.isEmpty() && arr[stack.peek()] >= arr[i]) stack.pop();  // right pass

So I write this table on the whiteboard before coding. One side strict and the other non-strict is the standard way to make ties count once.

Interviewers look for: a test with duplicates. Candidates who get Daily Temperatures right often break on [2,2] in counting problems.

IntermediateWalk me through Sliding Window Maximum with a monotonic deque.
