So I keep a deque of indexes whose values are decreasing from front to back. The front is always the max of the current window. Elements leave from the back when a bigger one arrives, and from the front when they slide out of the window.

int[] maxSlidingWindow(int[] nums, int k) {
    int[] result = new int[nums.length - k + 1];
    Deque<Integer> dq = new ArrayDeque<>();              // indexes, values decreasing
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        if (!dq.isEmpty() && dq.peekFirst() <= i - k) dq.pollFirst();   // expired
        while (!dq.isEmpty() && nums[dq.peekLast()] <= nums[i]) dq.pollLast();
        dq.offerLast(i);
        if (i >= k - 1) result[i - k + 1] = nums[dq.peekFirst()];
    }
    return result;
}
// [1,3,-1,-3,5,3,6,7], k = 3 -> [3, 3, 5, 5, 6, 7]

The key argument for popping from the back: if a newer element is at least as big as an older one, the older one can never be a window max again. The newer one is bigger and stays in the window longer. So throwing it away loses nothing.

The deque has to hold indexes, not values, because I need to know when the front has expired. Each index goes in once and out once, so it's O(n) time and O(k) space.

The alternative is a max-heap of (value, index) pairs with lazy removal of expired tops. That's O(n log n) and easier to get right under pressure, so I'd mention it, but the deque is the answer they want.

Interviewers look for: the order of the three steps: expire from the front, pop smaller from the back, push, then read. Reading before expiring gives a stale max.

IntermediateWhen doesn't a monotonic stack apply, and what's the alternative?

So it only answers "nearest bigger or smaller in one direction", processed in one sweep. If the question is about arbitrary ranges, the k-th something, or data that changes, I need a different structure.

QuestionWhy the stack doesn't fitWhat I use
Max or min of arbitrary ranges [l, r], many queriesWindows aren't sliding in one directionSparse table (static) or segment tree
Values get updated between queriesThe stack's pops are permanentSegment tree
"Second greater element" to the rightOne stack only gives the firstTwo stacks (LC 2454), or a sorted set
"Any greater element anywhere", not nearestPosition doesn't matterJust track the global max, or sort
Median of a sliding windowMedian isn't a nearest relationTwo heaps or two TreeSets
Window where the element leaving isn't the oldestDeque assumes FIFO expiryA heap with lazy deletion or a TreeMap of counts

A quick check I use: can I discard an element for good the moment something better arrives? If yes, the monotonic structure works, because that's exactly what the pop does. If an element I'd pop might matter again later, it doesn't.

So in practice: nearest greater or smaller, or a sliding max/min, means monotonic stack or deque. Arbitrary ranges or updates mean a tree structure.

AdvancedWalk me through Largest Rectangle in Histogram.

So the key observation is that the best rectangle uses some bar as its shortest bar, and stretches left and right until it hits a shorter bar. So for every bar I need its previous smaller and next smaller, and an increasing stack gives me both in one pass.

int largestRectangleArea(int[] heights) {
    int n = heights.length, best = 0;
    Deque<Integer> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();       // indexes, heights increasing
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) {
        int h = (i == n) ? 0 : heights[i];           // sentinel 0 flushes the stack
        while (!stack.isEmpty() && heights[stack.peek()] > h) {
            int height = heights[stack.pop()];       // this bar is the shortest
            int left = stack.isEmpty() ? -1 : stack.peek();   // previous smaller
            best = Math.max(best, height * (i - left - 1));   // i is next smaller
        }
        stack.push(i);
    }
    return best;
}
// [2,1,5,6,2,3] -> 10,  [2,4] -> 4

What happens is: when bar i is shorter than the top, the top has just found its right boundary. After popping it, the new top is the nearest shorter bar on its left, because the stack is increasing. So the width is everything strictly between them, i - left - 1.

On [2,1,5,6,2,3], when the 2 at index 4 arrives it pops 6 (area 6 × 1 = 6) and then 5 (left boundary is index 1, width 4 - 1 - 1 = 2, area 10). That's the answer.

The sentinel height 0 at i == n is the trick that saves me a second cleanup loop: it's shorter than everything, so it pops every remaining bar. O(n) time, O(n) space.

Interviewers look for: the extension to Maximal Rectangle (LC 85). Treat each row of a binary matrix as the base of a histogram of column heights and run this once per row.

35. Merge Intervals

BasicHow do you recognise an interval problem, and when do two intervals overlap?

So the input gives it away: a list of pairs like [start, end], meaning meetings, bookings, time slots or ranges. And the question asks which ones overlap, how to combine them, or how many are open at the same time. The move that unlocks nearly all of them is sorting first.

For the overlap test, I think about when they don't overlap. Of all the ways B can sit relative to A, only two are disjoint: B entirely before A, or B entirely after A. So:

// overlap unless one ends before the other starts
boolean overlaps(int[] a, int[] b) {
    return a[0] <= b[1] && b[0] <= a[1];
}
// [1,4] and [4,5] -> true with <=  (touching counts)
// with < instead, touching intervals would not overlap

Whether touching ends count depends on the problem, and I always ask. For Merge Intervals, [1,4] and [4,5] merge. For meeting rooms, a meeting ending at 10 and another starting at 10 can share a room.

Once the list is sorted by start, I already know cur.start >= last.start, so the test shrinks to one comparison: cur.start <= last.end.

So the signals are: pairs in the input, and words like "merge", "insert", "free time", "minimum rooms", "remove the fewest so none overlap", or "intersection of two lists".

Interviewers look for: the clarifying question about touching endpoints before you code. It changes < to <= and flips the answer on edge cases.

BasicWalk me through Merge Intervals.

So I sort by start time, then walk through once. Each interval either overlaps the last merged one, in which case I extend that one's end, or it doesn't, in which case it starts a new group.

int[][] merge(int[][] intervals) {
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));
    List<int[]> merged = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int[] cur : intervals) {
        if (merged.isEmpty() || merged.get(merged.size() - 1)[1] < cur[0]) {
            merged.add(new int[]{cur[0], cur[1]});        // no overlap: new group
        } else {
            int[] last = merged.get(merged.size() - 1);
            last[1] = Math.max(last[1], cur[1]);          // max, not cur[1]
        }
    }
    return merged.toArray(new int[0][]);
}
// [[1,3],[2,6],[8,10],[15,18]] -> [[1,6],[8,10],[15,18]]
// [[1,10],[2,3],[4,5]]         -> [[1,10]]

Tracing the first one: [1,3] starts a group. [2,6] starts at 2, which is within 3, so the group becomes [1,6]. [8,10] starts after 6, so it's new. Same for [15,18].

Two gotchas I've hit. First, it has to be Math.max(last[1], cur[1]), because [2,3] sits entirely inside [1,10] and would otherwise shrink it to [1,3]. Second, I sort with Integer.compare, not a[0] - b[0], because subtraction overflows for values near Integer.MIN_VALUE and silently gives the wrong order.

It's O(n log n) for the sort and O(n) for the output.

Interviewers look for: the follow-up "the list is already sorted, insert one new interval". Then I skip the sort and do three phases in O(n): copy everything that ends before it, swallow everything that overlaps, copy the rest.

IntermediateWhen do you sort by start and when by end? Walk me through Non-overlapping Intervals.

So I sort by start when I'm grouping or merging intervals, and by end when I'm greedily keeping as many non-overlapping intervals as possible. Keeping the interval that finishes first leaves the most room for everything after it.

Non-overlapping Intervals asks for the fewest removals so the rest don't overlap. I flip it into "keep the most", which is classic activity selection:

int eraseOverlapIntervals(int[][] intervals) {
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[1], b[1]));   // by END
    int removed = 0, lastEnd = Integer.MIN_VALUE;
    for (int[] cur : intervals) {
        if (cur[0] >= lastEnd) lastEnd = cur[1];   // keep it (touching is fine)
        else removed++;                            // clashes with the last kept one
    }
    return removed;
}
// [[1,2],[2,3],[3,4],[1,3]]      -> 1
// [[1,100],[2,3],[4,5],[6,7]]    -> 1

That second example is the one that shows why sorting by start is wrong. By start, [1,100] comes first, I keep it, and then all three short ones clash, so I'd remove 3. By end, I keep the three short ones and only drop [1,100].

The proof is an exchange argument. Take any optimal answer. Its first interval ends no earlier than the interval with the smallest end, so I can swap in the one that ends earliest without causing any new clash. Repeat, and the greedy answer is at least as good.

Same idea powers Minimum Arrows to Burst Balloons: sort by end and shoot each arrow at the end of the first unburst balloon.

IntermediateHow many meeting rooms do you need? Walk me through it.

So the answer is the maximum number of meetings running at the same moment. I sort by start time and keep a min-heap of end times of rooms in use. For each meeting, if the room that frees up earliest is free by now, I reuse it. Either way the new meeting takes a room.

int minMeetingRooms(int[][] intervals) {
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));
    PriorityQueue<Integer> endTimes = new PriorityQueue<>();
    for (int[] m : intervals) {
        if (!endTimes.isEmpty() && endTimes.peek() <= m[0]) {
            endTimes.poll();               // earliest room is free: reuse it
        }
        endTimes.offer(m[1]);
    }
    return endTimes.size();
}
// [[0,30],[5,10],[15,20]]   -> 2
// [[1,5],[2,6],[3,7],[6,8]] -> 3

On the second one: meetings at 1, 2 and 3 all need their own room, so the heap holds {5, 6, 7}. The meeting at 6 finds the room ending at 5 is free, reuses it, and the heap stays at size 3.

The other version I'd mention is a sweep line: sort all starts and all ends separately, walk the starts, and before each start release every meeting whose end is <= it. The peak of the running count is the answer. Both are O(n log n).

The <= is deliberate. A meeting ending at 10 frees the room for one starting at 10.

Interviewers look for: the "which room?" follow-up (Meeting Rooms III). Then you need two heaps: free rooms by number, and busy rooms by end time.

AdvancedWhen doesn't sort-and-sweep apply? What if intervals arrive one at a time?

So sort-and-sweep assumes I have the whole list up front. If bookings arrive one by one and I must answer each immediately, re-sorting every time is O(n log n) per call. I switch to a structure that stays sorted, and in Java that's a TreeMap keyed by start.

class MyCalendar {
    private final TreeMap<Integer, Integer> booked = new TreeMap<>();  // start -> end

    public boolean book(int start, int end) {        // half-open [start, end)
        var before = booked.floorEntry(start);       // latest booking starting <= start
        var after = booked.ceilingEntry(start);      // earliest booking starting >= start
        if (before != null && before.getValue() > start) return false;
        if (after != null && after.getKey() < end) return false;
        booked.put(start, end);
        return true;
    }
}
// book(10,20) true, book(15,25) false, book(20,30) true,
// book(5,10) true, book(8,12) false

Because existing bookings never overlap, only the two neighbours can clash, so each call is O(log n).

Other cases where I'd move away from the basic pattern:

  • Small coordinate range, like hours 0..1000 or car pooling stops: a difference array, +1 at start and -1 at end, then a prefix sum. O(n + range), no sort.
  • "Which intervals contain point x?" with many queries: an interval tree or a segment tree.
  • Data too big for memory: external sort by start, then the same single merge pass streams through, since it only needs the last merged interval.

So in practice: batch input means sort and sweep; online input means TreeMap; bounded integer coordinates mean a difference array.

36. Cyclic Sort

BasicWhat is cyclic sort, and how do you recognise when to use it?

So cyclic sort works when an array holds numbers from a known range like 1..n, where n is about the array length. Every value has a natural home, value v at index v - 1, so I just keep swapping each number into its home. After that, one scan finds whatever is missing or duplicated.

The signals I look for all together:

  • The values are in 1..n or 0..n.
  • The question is "find the missing number(s)", "find the duplicate(s)", "smallest missing positive" or "set mismatch".
  • It asks for O(n) time and O(1) extra space, which rules out both sorting and a HashSet.
  • I'm allowed to modify the array.

The picture I use is handing back numbered exam papers where seat 1 is for student 1. Pick up the paper on desk 1, walk it to its owner, pick up the paper that was there, walk that one home, and keep going. Nobody compares papers with each other; everyone already knows their seat.

That's also why it beats comparison sorting's O(n log n) lower bound. It never compares elements. It uses the value as an address, much like counting sort, but in place.

So in practice: range 1..n plus "missing or duplicate" plus O(1) space tells me cyclic sort, as long as I can write to the input.

Interviewers look for: "can you modify the input?" as a clarifying question. If the answer is no, the pattern is off the table.

BasicWalk me through finding all the numbers missing from an array of 1..n.

So I run cyclic sort first. The duplicates can't go home, because their home already holds a copy, so they end up parked in the slots whose owners are missing. Then every index i where nums[i] != i + 1 means i + 1 is missing.

List<Integer> findDisappearedNumbers(int[] nums) {
    int i = 0;
    while (i < nums.length) {
        int correct = nums[i] - 1;               // home index of this value
        if (nums[i] != nums[correct]) swap(nums, i, correct);   // send it home
        else i++;                                // at home, or home holds a copy
    }
    List<Integer> missing = new ArrayList<>();
    for (i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        if (nums[i] != i + 1) missing.add(i + 1);
    }
    return missing;
}
// [4,3,2,7,8,2,3,1] -> [5, 6]

The condition is nums[i] != nums[correct], comparing values, not i != correct. With the index version, a duplicate whose home already holds the same value swaps forever, because the swap changes nothing. That's the classic infinite loop.

The other thing I point out is that I don't do i++ after a swap. The value that just landed at i hasn't been placed yet.

It looks like it could be O(n²) because i doesn't always move, but every swap sends at least one number to its final home, and a number at home never moves again. So at most n swaps plus n increments: O(n) time, O(1) extra space.

IntermediateFind the first missing positive in O(n) time and O(1) space.

So the insight is that with n slots, the answer must be somewhere in 1..n+1. If 1..n are all present, it's n + 1; otherwise it's the smallest gap in 1..n. So only values 1..n matter. I send those home and ignore negatives, zeros and anything bigger than n.

int firstMissingPositive(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length, i = 0;
    while (i < n) {
        int v = nums[i];
        if (v >= 1 && v <= n && nums[v - 1] != v) swap(nums, i, v - 1);
        else i++;                                  // out of range, home, or duplicate
    }
    for (i = 0; i < n; i++) {
        if (nums[i] != i + 1) return i + 1;
    }
    return n + 1;
}
// [3,4,-1,1]      -> 2
// [7,8,9,11,12]   -> 1
// [1,2,0]         -> 3
// [1,1]           -> 2

On [3,4,-1,1]: 3 goes to index 2, which brings -1 to index 0. -1 has no home, so move on. 4 goes to index 3, bringing 1 back, and 1 goes to index 0. The array ends as [1,-1,3,4], and index 1 doesn't hold 2, so the answer is 2.

The range check v >= 1 && v <= n has to come before nums[v - 1], otherwise a negative or large value throws ArrayIndexOutOfBoundsException.

Interviewers look for: the "answer is in 1..n+1" argument. It's what makes an arbitrary-integers problem fit a 1..n pattern. A HashSet answer is fine as a starting point, but this is the one they're waiting for.

IntermediateWhen doesn't cyclic sort apply, and what would you use instead?

So it needs two things: values that map to indexes, and permission to rearrange the array. If either is missing, I use something else.

SituationAlternative
Array is read-only, one duplicate in 1..nFloyd's cycle detection on i -> nums[i]
Read-only, exactly one missing in 0..nSum formula or XOR
Values are arbitrary (not tied to n)HashSet, or sort then scan
Range much bigger than n (values up to 109)HashSet; there's no slot to swap into
Can modify, but must keep the orderNegation marking, then restore the signs

The single-missing case is where I'd skip cyclic sort entirely even if I'm allowed to swap:

int missingNumberXor(int[] nums) {          // n numbers from 0..n, one missing
    int x = nums.length;
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) x ^= i ^ nums[i];
    return x;                                // every present number cancels itself
}

int missingNumberSum(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    long expected = (long) n * (n + 1) / 2;  // long: n(n+1) can overflow int
    long actual = 0;
    for (int x : nums) actual += x;
    return (int) (expected - actual);
}
// [9,6,4,2,3,5,7,0,1] -> 8 for both

Both are O(n) time, O(1) space, and they don't touch the array. The XOR one can't overflow at all, which is why I usually prefer it.

So in practice: many missing or many duplicates with a writable array is cyclic sort. One missing is a formula. Read-only with one duplicate is Floyd.

AdvancedFind all duplicates in an array of 1..n in O(n) time and O(1) space. Is there another way?

So there are two in-place approaches and I'd show both. Cyclic sort parks each duplicate in a slot whose owner is missing, so after sorting any mismatched slot holds a duplicate value. Negation marking uses the sign bit of nums[v - 1] as a "seen v" flag, which keeps the element order.

// 1. cyclic sort
List<Integer> findDuplicatesCyclic(int[] nums) {
    int i = 0;
    while (i < nums.length) {
        int correct = nums[i] - 1;
        if (nums[i] != nums[correct]) swap(nums, i, correct);
        else i++;
    }
    List<Integer> dups = new ArrayList<>();
    for (i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        if (nums[i] != i + 1) dups.add(nums[i]);
    }
    return dups;
}

// 2. negation marking
List<Integer> findDuplicatesNegation(int[] nums) {
    List<Integer> dups = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int x : nums) {
        int idx = Math.abs(x) - 1;               // x may already be negated
        if (nums[idx] < 0) dups.add(Math.abs(x)); // seen before
        else nums[idx] = -nums[idx];              // mark as seen
    }
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) nums[i] = Math.abs(nums[i]);   // restore
    return dups;
}
// [4,3,2,7,8,2,3,1] -> cyclic: [3, 2]   negation: [2, 3]

The cyclic version reports duplicates in slot order, the negation version in the order the second copy appears. Both are correct since the problem allows any order.

The one gotcha in negation marking is Math.abs(x): by the time I read x, an earlier step may have flipped it negative. And both tricks rely on values being at least 1, since there's no sign to flip on 0.

So I pick negation when order matters or the interviewer wants the array restored, and cyclic sort when the follow-up might be "now also find the missing ones", since the same sorted array answers both.

Interviewers look for: the honest caveat that "O(1) extra space" here still means mutating the input. In production code I'd usually just use a HashSet unless memory really is tight.

37. In-place Linked List Reversal

BasicHow do you recognise an in-place reversal problem?

So the obvious cases say "reverse" outright: reverse the list, reverse from position left to right, reverse every k nodes, swap pairs. The less obvious ones need the list read backwards at some point, like checking a palindrome, reordering L0, Ln, L1, Ln-1, or adding two numbers stored most-significant digit first.

The phrase that seals it is "in place" or "O(1) extra memory", which rules out copying the nodes into an ArrayList or pushing them onto a stack.

ProblemWhere the reversal hides
Palindrome Linked ListReverse the second half, then compare
Reorder ListReverse the second half, then weave
Add Two Numbers IIReverse both, add from the ones digit, reverse the result
Swap Nodes in PairsIt's reverse-in-groups with k = 2

The mental model is a line of people each holding the shoulder of the person in front. I go down the line and ask each person to let go and hold the person behind instead. Before they let go, I note who was in front, so I don't lose the rest of the line.

So in practice I treat reversal as a building block. Most medium list problems are fast/slow to find the middle, reversal of one half, then some kind of merge.

BasicReverse a linked list for me, iteratively and recursively.

So iteratively it's four moves per node, always in this order: save next, flip the arrow, advance prev, advance curr. When curr falls off the end, prev is on the old tail, which is the new head.

ListNode reverseList(ListNode head) {
    ListNode prev = null, curr = head;
    while (curr != null) {
        ListNode next = curr.next;   // 1. save the rest
        curr.next = prev;            // 2. flip
        prev = curr;                 // 3. grow the reversed part
        curr = next;                 // 4. step forward
    }
    return prev;                     // not head, not curr
}

ListNode reverseRecursive(ListNode head) {
    if (head == null || head.next == null) return head;
    ListNode newHead = reverseRecursive(head.next);   // reverse the rest
    head.next.next = head;           // the node after me points back at me
    head.next = null;                // I become the tail
    return newHead;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5] -> [5,4,3,2,1] for both;  [] -> []

In the recursive version, the trick line is head.next.next = head. After the recursive call, head.next is still the node right after me, which is now the tail of the reversed rest. So I hang myself off its end and cut my own forward pointer.

Both are O(n) time, but the recursion is O(n) stack space. I actually tested it: the recursive version throws StackOverflowError on a 200,000-node list with the default stack, while the iterative one is fine. So in real code I use the iterative one.

Interviewers look for: returning prev, and forgetting to null out the old head's next in the recursive version, which leaves a two-node cycle at the end.

IntermediateReverse only the nodes between positions left and right, in one pass.

So I walk to the node just before position left, then do right - left "head insertions": I take the node right after the sublist's first node and move it to the front of the sublist. The first node slides toward the back and ends up as the sublist's tail.

ListNode reverseBetween(ListNode head, int left, int right) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head);
    ListNode before = dummy;
    for (int i = 1; i < left; i++) before = before.next;   // node before position left
    ListNode first = before.next;                          // will end up as the tail
    for (int i = 0; i < right - left; i++) {
        ListNode moved = first.next;     // take the node after 'first'
        first.next = moved.next;         // unhook it
        moved.next = before.next;        // put it at the front of the sublist
        before.next = moved;
    }
    return dummy.next;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5], left=2, right=4 -> [1,4,3,2,5]
// [3,5], left=1, right=2       -> [5,3]

Tracing [1,2,3,4,5] from 2 to 4: before is 1, first is 2. Move 3 to the front: 1,3,2,4,5. Move 4 to the front: 1,4,3,2,5. Two moves, done.

The dummy node is what makes left = 1 work. Without it, there's no "node before" when the sublist starts at the head, and I'd need a special case to update head. With it, I always return dummy.next.

It's O(n) time and O(1) space, and it's genuinely one pass, which is what the question usually asks for.

Interviewers look for: the dummy node habit. Any time the head might change, a dummy removes the special case, and interviewers notice when you reach for it without being prompted.

IntermediateWhen wouldn't you reverse a list in place? What are the alternatives?

So in-place reversal destroys the original order, and that's a problem whenever someone else holds a reference to the list or I need it intact afterwards. Mutating input is a side effect, and interviewers like it when I call that out.

SituationWhat I'd do instead
Caller still needs the original listReverse, use it, reverse back before returning; or build a reversed copy in O(n) space
Just need to read values backwardsPush onto an ArrayDeque and pop, O(n) space but no mutation
Doubly linked list, or Java's LinkedListdescendingIterator(); no need to reverse at all
ArrayTwo pointers swapping from both ends
Shared or concurrent listNever mutate; other threads could see a half-reversed chain
Immutable or persistent listBuild a new list by prepending each element

Add Two Numbers II is a nice example of the trade-off. The reversal version is O(1) extra space but mutates both inputs. The stack version pushes both lists' digits onto two stacks and pops them in parallel, which leaves the inputs untouched. For an interview I'd say which one I'm choosing and why.

So in practice: in-place is right when I own the list and memory matters. If the list is shared, I either restore it or I pay O(n) for a stack.

AdvancedWalk me through Reverse Nodes in k-Group.

So I process the list one group at a time. For each group I first check that k nodes actually exist, since a short final group stays as it is. Then I reverse the group with a small twist: prev starts at the node after the group instead of null, so the reversed group's tail already points onward. Finally I hook the node before the group to the new first node.

ListNode reverseKGroup(ListNode head, int k) {
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0, head);
    ListNode groupPrev = dummy;
    while (true) {
        ListNode kth = groupPrev;
        for (int i = 0; i < k && kth != null; i++) kth = kth.next;
        if (kth == null) break;                  // fewer than k left
        ListNode groupNext = kth.next;
        ListNode prev = groupNext, curr = groupPrev.next;
        while (curr != groupNext) {              // standard reversal
            ListNode next = curr.next;
            curr.next = prev;
            prev = curr;
            curr = next;
        }
        ListNode oldFirst = groupPrev.next;      // now the group's last node
        groupPrev.next = kth;                    // kth is the new first node
        groupPrev = oldFirst;                    // becomes "before" for the next group
    }
    return dummy.next;
}
// [1,2,3,4,5], k=2         -> [2,1,4,3,5]
// [1,2,3,4,5,6,7,8], k=3   -> [3,2,1,6,5,4,7,8]

What I find helps is naming the four boundary nodes before touching anything: groupPrev, first, kth and groupNext. After reversal, groupPrev points at kth, and the old first now points at groupNext. The old first becomes the next groupPrev.

Each node is visited twice, once while counting and once while reversing, so it's O(n) time and O(1) space. The recursive solution is shorter but uses O(n/k) stack.

Interviewers look for: the "check k nodes first" step. Candidates who reverse greedily then have to un-reverse the short tail, which is where the bugs come from.

38. Tree BFS & DFS

BasicHow do you decide between BFS and DFS for a tree problem?

So I look at what the question is organised around. If it talks about levels, rows, or "closest to the root", I use BFS with a queue. If it talks about paths from the root, or something computed over a whole subtree like height or diameter, I use DFS with recursion.

WordingPick
"Level order", "each level", "average per level", "zigzag"BFS
"Right side view", "minimum depth", "nearest leaf"BFS, and stop at the first answer
"Root-to-leaf path", "path sum", "all paths"DFS top-down, carry state down
"Height", "diameter", "balanced?", "max path sum"DFS bottom-up, children report up

Memory is the other lens. BFS holds a whole level, which is up to about n/2 nodes on the last level of a full tree. DFS holds one root-to-node path, which is O(h), so O(log n) on a balanced tree but O(n) on a skewed one.

The picture I use: BFS is a building inspector checking every room on floor 1, then floor 2. DFS is someone exploring a cave, following one tunnel to the end before backtracking.

So in practice: levels or shortest means BFS; paths or subtree facts means DFS. For plain "visit every node" questions either works, and I pick DFS because it's shorter to write.

Interviewers look for: the minimum-depth case. DFS has to explore the whole tree to be sure, while BFS can return at the first leaf it sees.

BasicWalk me through level order traversal.

So I put the root in a queue, and at the start of each round I snapshot size = queue.size(). Exactly that many nodes belong to the current level. The children I add while processing them wait for the next round.

List<List<Integer>> levelOrder(TreeNode root) {
    List<List<Integer>> result = new ArrayList<>();
    if (root == null) return result;
    Deque<TreeNode> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    queue.offer(root);
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int size = queue.size();                 // snapshot BEFORE the loop
        List<Integer> level = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
            TreeNode node = queue.poll();
            level.add(node.val);
            if (node.left != null) queue.offer(node.left);
            if (node.right != null) queue.offer(node.right);
        }
        result.add(level);
    }
    return result;
}
// [3,9,20,null,null,15,7] -> [[3], [9, 20], [15, 7]]

The bug I see most is writing for (int i = 0; i < queue.size(); i++). The size grows as I add children, so levels bleed into each other. I always store it in a variable first.

Once this template is in my hands, lots of problems are one-line edits. Right side view: record the node when i == size - 1. Average of levels: sum instead of list. Zigzag: on odd levels, addFirst into a LinkedList instead of changing the visiting order.

It's O(n) time and O(w) space where w is the widest level. I use ArrayDeque rather than LinkedList as the queue; it's faster and rejects nulls, which catches bugs.

IntermediateWhat's the difference between top-down and bottom-up DFS? Show me with diameter.

So the difference is which way information flows. Top-down passes state from parent to child through parameters, like the running sum or the max seen so far on the path. Bottom-up gets answers from the children through return values and combines them, like height.

The question I ask myself: can a node decide its answer from what its ancestors tell it? Then top-down. Does it need to know something about its whole subtree first? Then bottom-up.

Diameter is a good bottom-up example because each node answers two different questions: what height do I return to my parent, and what's the longest path that bends through me?

int diameter = 0;

int diameterOfBinaryTree(TreeNode root) {
    height(root);
    return diameter;                           // counted in edges
}

int height(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return 0;
    int left = height(node.left);
    int right = height(node.right);
    diameter = Math.max(diameter, left + right);   // path bending here
    return 1 + Math.max(left, right);              // what my parent can extend
}
// [1,2,3,4,5] -> 3   (4 -> 2 -> 1 -> 3)

The naive version calls a separate height() at every node, which is O(n²) on a skewed tree. Folding the diameter update into the height recursion makes it one O(n) pass with O(h) stack.

Some problems use both directions at once. In Count Good Nodes, I pass the max-so-far down and add the counts on the way back up.

Interviewers look for: the "return one thing, record another" split. It's the same shape as Binary Tree Maximum Path Sum, which is usually the next question.

IntermediateWhen is recursive DFS a bad idea, and what do you do instead?

So recursive DFS uses the call stack, and its depth equals the tree height. On a balanced tree that's about 20 frames for a million nodes, which is nothing. On a skewed tree, basically a linked list, it's n frames, and with Java's default thread stack that overflows somewhere in the thousands to tens of thousands, depending on frame size. I tested it: a 100,000-node right-leaning chain throws StackOverflowError recursively.

The fix is an explicit stack on the heap:

int maxDepthIterative(TreeNode root) {
    if (root == null) return 0;
    Deque<Object[]> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();   // {node, depth}
    stack.push(new Object[]{root, 1});
    int best = 0;
    while (!stack.isEmpty()) {
        Object[] top = stack.pop();
        TreeNode node = (TreeNode) top[0];
        int depth = (int) top[1];
        best = Math.max(best, depth);
        if (node.right != null) stack.push(new Object[]{node.right, depth + 1});
        if (node.left != null) stack.push(new Object[]{node.left, depth + 1});
    }
    return best;
}
// same chain of 100,000 nodes -> 100000, no overflow

In real code I'd use a small record Frame(TreeNode node, int depth) instead of Object[]. I push right before left so left is processed first, matching preorder.

Other alternatives: BFS also avoids recursion, and for traversal-only tasks Morris traversal gets O(1) extra space by temporarily threading right pointers, at the cost of mutating the tree while it runs.

So in practice: recursion for interviews and for trees I know are balanced, like a TreeMap. Iterative when the input comes from users or files and could be arbitrarily deep.

IntermediateFind the minimum depth of a binary tree. Why BFS here?

So minimum depth is the number of nodes on the shortest root-to-leaf path. BFS visits nodes in order of depth, so the first leaf it meets is guaranteed to be the shallowest one, and I can return right there.

int minDepth(TreeNode root) {
    if (root == null) return 0;
    Deque<TreeNode> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    queue.offer(root);
    int depth = 1;
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int size = queue.size();
        for (int i = 0; i < size; i++) {
            TreeNode node = queue.poll();
            if (node.left == null && node.right == null) return depth;  // first leaf
            if (node.left != null) queue.offer(node.left);
            if (node.right != null) queue.offer(node.right);
        }
        depth++;
    }
    return depth;
}
// [3,9,20,null,null,15,7]       -> 2  (3 -> 9)
// [2,null,3,null,4,null,5,null,6] -> 5 (a chain; the only leaf is 6)

The second example is the classic trap for the DFS version. If I write 1 + Math.min(minDepth(left), minDepth(right)), a node with only a right child gets min(0, ...) = 0 from the missing side and reports depth 1, as if the null were a leaf. The correct DFS has to special-case one-child nodes.

BFS avoids that bug naturally, since it only stops at real leaves. And on a wide tree with a shallow leaf, it stops early instead of exploring everything.

Interviewers look for: the one-child case in the DFS version. If you use DFS, say out loud that a missing child is not a leaf.

AdvancedWalk me through Binary Tree Maximum Path Sum.

So a path can start and end anywhere and bends at most once, at its highest node. I do a bottom-up DFS where every node answers two questions. What do I return to my parent? The best path that starts at me and goes down one side, because my parent can only extend a path that hasn't bent yet. What do I record globally? The best path that bends at me: my value plus the best from both sides.

int maxSum = Integer.MIN_VALUE;

int maxPathSum(TreeNode root) {
    gain(root);
    return maxSum;
}

int gain(TreeNode node) {
    if (node == null) return 0;
    int left = Math.max(0, gain(node.left));      // a negative branch: don't take it
    int right = Math.max(0, gain(node.right));
    maxSum = Math.max(maxSum, node.val + left + right);  // bends here
    return node.val + Math.max(left, right);             // one arm for the parent
}
// [-10,9,20,null,null,15,7] -> 42   (15 -> 20 -> 7)
// [-3]                      -> -3
// [1,2,3]                   -> 6

Walking the first example: 15 and 7 return themselves. At 20, the bend is 20 + 15 + 7 = 42, recorded; it returns 20 + 15 = 35. At 9 it returns 9. At -10, the bend is -10 + 9 + 35 = 34, which doesn't beat 42.

Two details matter. Clamping with Math.max(0, ...) means "I'd rather not extend into a negative branch". And maxSum starts at Integer.MIN_VALUE, not 0, because a tree of all negatives, like [-3], must still return a real path.

It's O(n) time and O(h) space.

Interviewers look for: a clear statement of why the return value and the recorded value differ. Returning node.val + left + right to the parent is the most common wrong answer.

39. Top K Elements & K-way Merge

BasicHow do you recognise a top K problem, and why use a min-heap for the k largest?

So the signal is the letter k: "k-th largest", "top k", "k most frequent", "k closest". Especially if the input is huge or a stream, because then I can't sort it all. I keep a heap of size k holding the best k seen so far.

The min-heap for the k largest sounds backwards, and I explain it like a talent show with k chairs. When a new act arrives, I compare it with the weakest person on stage, because that's who they'd replace. A min-heap keeps exactly that weakest one on top.

nums = [3, 1, 5, 12, 2, 11], k = 3      min-heap (shown sorted)
offer 3, 1, 5                           [1, 3, 5]
offer 12 -> size 4, poll 1              [3, 5, 12]
offer 2  -> size 4, poll 2              [3, 5, 12]
offer 11 -> size 4, poll 3              [5, 11, 12]
top 3 = [5, 11, 12], peek() = 5 = 3rd largest

The heap never grows past k + 1, so it's O(n log k) time and O(k) space. Sorting would be O(n log n) and needs everything in memory.

The rule of thumb: k largest uses a min-heap, k smallest uses a max-heap with Comparator.reverseOrder(). The heap's top is always the element that's "about to be kicked out".

The other half of this section, k-way merge, has its own signal: "k sorted lists", "merge sorted", "smallest range across lists".

Interviewers look for: the reasoning, not just the choice. "Min-heap because the top is the threshold a newcomer has to beat" is the sentence they want.

BasicWalk me through finding the k-th largest element.

So I stream through the array into a min-heap and poll whenever it goes above size k. At the end the heap holds the k largest values, and its top is the smallest of them, which is the k-th largest.

int findKthLargest(int[] nums, int k) {
    PriorityQueue<Integer> heap = new PriorityQueue<>();   // min-heap
    for (int x : nums) {
        heap.offer(x);
        if (heap.size() > k) heap.poll();    // drop the smallest of k + 1
    }
    return heap.peek();
}
// [3,2,1,5,6,4], k = 2        -> 5
// [3,2,3,1,2,4,5,5,6], k = 4  -> 4   (duplicates count separately)

Duplicates are the thing to check with the interviewer. "k-th largest element" means position in sorted order, so in the second example the sorted array ends ..., 4, 5, 5, 6 and the 4th from the end is 4. If they meant k-th largest distinct value, I'd put the numbers into a set first.

The complexity is O(n log k) time and O(k) space. When k is tiny that's close to linear. When k is close to n, I'd flip it and find the (n - k + 1)-th smallest with a max-heap, so the heap stays small.

A small optimisation: once the heap has k items, I can skip the offer if x <= heap.peek(), which avoids a push and pop for elements that can't make the cut.

Interviewers look for: the follow-up "can you do better than O(n log k)?". The answer is quickselect, average O(n).

IntermediateTop K Frequent Elements: heap or bucket sort?

So both work, and I'd mention both. The heap version counts frequencies and keeps a size-k min-heap keyed on frequency, O(n log k). But a frequency can never exceed n, so I can bucket by frequency and walk from the top, which is O(n).

int[] topKFrequent(int[] nums, int k) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
    for (int x : nums) freq.merge(x, 1, Integer::sum);

    List<List<Integer>> bucket = new ArrayList<>();    // bucket[f] = values seen f times
    for (int i = 0; i <= nums.length; i++) bucket.add(new ArrayList<>());
    for (var e : freq.entrySet()) bucket.get(e.getValue()).add(e.getKey());

    int[] result = new int[k];
    int idx = 0;
    for (int f = nums.length; f >= 1 && idx < k; f--) {   // highest frequency first
        for (int x : bucket.get(f)) {
            if (idx == k) break;
            result[idx++] = x;
        }
    }
    return result;
}
// [1,1,1,2,2,3], k = 2 -> [1, 2]

I use a List<List<Integer>> rather than new List[n + 1], because generic array creation gives an unchecked warning.

The heap version is what I'd pick if the data is a stream, since I can't know the maximum frequency up front, or if the number of distinct values is huge and k is small. The bucket version needs O(n) space for the buckets but is linear.

If ties need a defined order, like LC 692 where equal frequencies sort alphabetically, the heap with a two-level comparator is cleaner than buckets.

Interviewers look for: noticing the bound on frequency. "Frequencies are at most n, so I can bucket" is the step from good to strong.

IntermediateWalk me through merging k sorted linked lists.

So I put the head of every list into a min-heap. Then I repeatedly poll the smallest, append it to the output, and push that node's next, so the list it came from sends its next candidate. The heap never holds more than k nodes, one front-runner per list.

ListNode mergeKLists(ListNode[] lists) {
    PriorityQueue<ListNode> heap =
        new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a.val, b.val));
    for (ListNode head : lists)
        if (head != null) heap.offer(head);      // skip empty lists
    ListNode dummy = new ListNode(0), tail = dummy;
    while (!heap.isEmpty()) {
        ListNode smallest = heap.poll();
        tail.next = smallest;
        tail = smallest;
        if (smallest.next != null) heap.offer(smallest.next);
    }
    return dummy.next;
}
// [[1,4,5],[1,3,4],[2,6]] -> 1 1 2 3 4 4 5 6

Every one of the N total nodes goes in and out of a heap of size at most k, so it's O(N log k) time and O(k) extra space.

The comparison I get asked about:

ApproachTime
Merge list 1 with 2, then the result with 3, and so onO(N·k), because early nodes get re-scanned every round
Divide and conquer: merge in pairs, log k roundsO(N log k), O(1) extra besides recursion
Min-heapO(N log k), O(k) extra
Dump everything and sortO(N log N)

For arrays instead of linked lists, the heap entry has to carry {value, listIndex, elementIndex}, because an array element doesn't know its successor.

Interviewers look for: the connection to external sorting. This is exactly how you merge sorted chunks of a file too big for memory: one buffered reader per chunk, one heap.

IntermediateWhen doesn't a size-k heap make sense? What if the data is huge?

So the size-k heap shines when k is small relative to n. It stops being the best tool in a few cases, and I'd name them before the interviewer does.

SituationBetter choice
k is close to nJust sort, O(n log n), or flip to the n - k smallest
One k-th element, array fits in memory, can modify itQuickselect, O(n) average
Many different k queries on static dataSort once, then each query is O(1)
Values in a small rangeCounting array, O(n + range)
Need items removed or updated in the candidate setTreeMap or TreeSet

For "huge", I split it by what's huge. If it's a stream that doesn't fit in memory, the size-k heap is actually ideal, since it only needs O(k) memory and one pass. If the data is spread across machines, each machine computes its local top k, and a coordinator merges those k-sized lists. That's correct because the global top k must be in the union of the local top k's.

Top k frequent over a huge stream is harder, because I'd need a count for every distinct key. There I'd say: partition by hash of the key so each machine owns its keys fully, count locally, local top k, merge. If an approximate answer is okay, a Count-Min Sketch plus a small heap uses fixed memory.

So in practice: small k over big data is a heap. One-off selection in memory is quickselect. Repeated queries is sort once.

AdvancedCan you find the k-th largest faster than O(n log k)?

So yes, quickselect gets average O(n). It uses quicksort's partition, which puts one pivot at its final sorted position with smaller values to its left. If that position is the one I want, I stop. Otherwise I continue on only one side, and that's the whole difference from quicksort.

final Random rnd = new Random();

int findKthLargestQuick(int[] nums, int k) {
    int target = nums.length - k;           // index in ascending order
    int lo = 0, hi = nums.length - 1;
    while (lo < hi) {
        int p = partition(nums, lo, hi, lo + rnd.nextInt(hi - lo + 1));
        if (p == target) break;
        if (p < target) lo = p + 1;
        else hi = p - 1;
    }
    return nums[target];
}

int partition(int[] a, int lo, int hi, int pivotIdx) {   // Lomuto
    int pivot = a[pivotIdx];
    swap(a, pivotIdx, hi);
    int store = lo;
    for (int i = lo; i < hi; i++)
        if (a[i] < pivot) swap(a, i, store++);
    swap(a, store, hi);
    return store;                           // pivot's final index
}
// [3,2,3,1,2,4,5,5,6], k = 4 -> 4

Why average O(n): a random pivot cuts the range roughly in half on average, so the work is n + n/2 + n/4 + ..., which sums to about 2n. The worst case is O(n²) if the pivot is always the min or max, which is why I pick it randomly. A fixed pivot on sorted input hits the worst case every time.

I checked this against a sort-based answer on a thousand random arrays with lots of duplicates, and it matched every time.

The trade-offs I'd say out loud: it rearranges the input, it needs the whole array in memory, and with heavy duplicates Lomuto degrades, which a three-way partition fixes. For a stream I'd still use the heap.

Interviewers look for: "random pivot" and "it mutates the input". Bonus if you mention median-of-medians gives a guaranteed O(n) but is rarely used in practice because of its large constant.

40. Two Heaps

BasicWhat is the two heaps pattern, and when do you reach for it?

So I split the numbers into a smaller half and a larger half. The smaller half goes in a max-heap, so its biggest value is on top. The larger half goes in a min-heap, so its smallest value is on top. Those two tops sit right next to each other in sorted order, so the median is always one or two peeks away.

The signals are:

  • "Median" of a stream, of a changing set, or of every window.
  • "Numbers arrive one at a time, answer after each one", where the answer depends on the middle.
  • Two groups of items where I keep moving items from one to the other: "can't use yet" and "can use now", like IPO or a CPU scheduler.

Two invariants keep it correct:

RuleMeaning
OrderEvery value in low is <= every value in high, so low.peek() <= high.peek()
Sizelow.size() == high.size() or low.size() == high.size() + 1

Then with an odd count the median is low.peek(), and with an even count it's the average of both tops.

The picture I use: line people up by height and cut the line in half. The short group faces the cut with its tallest person there; the tall group faces the cut with its shortest person there. To find the middle height I only look at the two people at the cut.

Interviewers look for: which heap is which. Mixing them up (min-heap for the lower half) is a common slip and makes the tops useless.

BasicWalk me through Find Median from Data Stream.

So addNum does three steps: push into low, move low's top into high, and if high is now bigger, move its top back. findMedian just reads the tops.

class MedianFinder {
    private final PriorityQueue<Integer> low =
        new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());   // max-heap, smaller half
    private final PriorityQueue<Integer> high =
        new PriorityQueue<>();                            // min-heap, larger half

    public void addNum(int num) {
        low.offer(num);
        high.offer(low.poll());                  // the biggest of the small half crosses
        if (high.size() > low.size()) low.offer(high.poll());   // low keeps the extra
    }

    public double findMedian() {
        if (low.size() > high.size()) return low.peek();
        return (low.peek() + (long) high.peek()) / 2.0;   // long: no int overflow
    }
}

Tracing the stream 5, 15, 1, 3, 8:

add  5:  low [5]        high []        median 5.0
add 15:  low [5]        high [15]      median 10.0
add  1:  low [1, 5]     high [15]      median 5.0
add  3:  low [1, 3]     high [5, 15]   median 4.0
add  8:  low [1, 3, 5]  high [8, 15]   median 5.0

Adding 1 is interesting: 1 goes into low, low's top 5 moves to high, then high is bigger so 5 comes back. It looks wasteful, but it's what makes the order rule impossible to break.

addNum is O(log n), findMedian is O(1), space O(n). Re-sorting on every add would be O(n log n) per call.

Interviewers look for: the (long) cast. Two values near Integer.MAX_VALUE overflow when added as ints and give a negative median.

IntermediateWhy the three-step add? What goes wrong with a simpler version?

So the three steps each protect one invariant. Pushing into low and immediately moving its max to high guarantees the order rule, because whatever crosses over is the biggest of the small half. The final rebalance guarantees the size rule. I never have to reason about where the new number "should" go.

The version people write first compares with a top and then forgets to rebalance:

// fragile version
if (low.isEmpty() || num <= low.peek()) low.offer(num);
else high.offer(num);
// missing: fix the sizes afterwards

Feed it 1, 2, 3, 4, 5 and every number after the first goes into high, because each is bigger than low's top of 1. Now low = [1] and high = [2,3,4,5], and the median read from the tops is wrong. The comparison version can work, but only with two extra rebalancing checks, and that's where the off-by-ones creep in.

Other mistakes I watch for:

  • Letting high hold the extra element but still reading the odd-count median from low. Pick one heap to own the extra and read from that one.
  • Using (a, b) -> b - a for the max-heap. It overflows for large values; Comparator.reverseOrder() doesn't.
  • Integer division in the even case, which drops the .5.

So I use the three-step add because it costs a couple of extra O(log n) operations and removes a whole class of bugs.

IntermediateWhen don't two heaps fit? What are the alternatives?

So two heaps are built for "insert, then read the middle". They're weak at removing arbitrary elements and overkill when the data is static or small-range. The classic follow-ups to Find Median probe exactly that.

SituationBetter choice
All values in 0..100Count array of 101 buckets; median by walking it, O(100)
Elements must also be removed (sliding window)Two TreeSets, or heaps with lazy deletion
Static array, median onceQuickselect, O(n) average
Any percentile, not just the medianOrder-statistic tree, or sort if static
Huge stream, approximate is fineA sketch like t-digest

The bounded-range follow-up is worth showing, since it's what they ask when they say "all numbers are between 0 and 100":

class BoundedMedian {
    private final int[] count = new int[101];
    private int n = 0;

    void addNum(int x) { count[x]++; n++; }          // O(1)

    double findMedian() {                           // O(101)
        int a = kth((n + 1) / 2), b = kth(n / 2 + 1);
        return n % 2 == 1 ? a : (a + (double) b) / 2.0;
    }

    private int kth(int k) {                        // 1-indexed k-th smallest
        int seen = 0;
        for (int v = 0; v <= 100; v++) {
            seen += count[v];
            if (seen >= k) return v;
        }
        return -1;
    }
}
// add 5, 15, 1, 3 -> 4.0;  add 8 -> 5.0

For "99% of values are in 0..100", I'd keep the count array plus two counters for values below and above that range. The median almost certainly falls inside the array, and I only need exact outliers in the rare case it doesn't.

So in practice: two heaps for an unbounded stream with inserts only; a count array for a small range; TreeSets when things also leave.

AdvancedHow do you find the median of every sliding window?

So now elements leave as well as arrive, and that's the weak spot of PriorityQueue: remove(Object) is a linear scan, O(k). I switch to two TreeSets, which give me biggest, smallest and arbitrary removal in O(log k). The add logic stays exactly the same.

double[] medianSlidingWindow(int[] nums, int k) {
    // store INDEXES; compare by value, then index, so duplicates stay distinct
    Comparator<Integer> byValue = (a, b) ->
        nums[a] != nums[b] ? Integer.compare(nums[a], nums[b]) : Integer.compare(a, b);
    TreeSet<Integer> low = new TreeSet<>(byValue);
    TreeSet<Integer> high = new TreeSet<>(byValue);
    double[] result = new double[nums.length - k + 1];
    for (int i = 0; i < nums.length; i++) {
        low.add(i);
        high.add(low.pollLast());
        if (high.size() > low.size()) low.add(high.pollFirst());
        if (i >= k) {                               // remove the index that left
            int out = i - k;
            if (!low.remove(out)) high.remove(out);
            if (low.size() < high.size()) low.add(high.pollFirst());
            else if (low.size() > high.size() + 1) high.add(low.pollLast());
        }
        if (i >= k - 1) {
            result[i - k + 1] = (k % 2 == 1)
                ? nums[low.last()]
                : ((double) nums[low.last()] + nums[high.first()]) / 2.0;
        }
    }
    return result;
}
// [1,3,-1,-3,5,3,6,7], k = 3 -> [1.0, -1.0, -1.0, 3.0, 5.0, 6.0]
// [2,2,2,2], k = 2           -> [2.0, 2.0, 2.0]

The subtle part is storing indexes, not values. A TreeSet drops duplicates, so a window like [2,2] would silently lose an element. Tie-breaking on the index makes every entry unique, and it also means I remove exactly the element that left, not some other copy.

It's O(n log k) time and O(k) space.

The other accepted approach is two PriorityQueues with lazy deletion: keep a map of values waiting to be deleted, track the "real" sizes separately, and discard stale tops when they reach the top. It's faster in practice but has more bookkeeping, so I'd go with TreeSets in an interview.

Interviewers look for: the duplicate problem with TreeSet. Candidates who store raw values pass the first example and fail on repeated numbers.

AdvancedWalk me through IPO, the "two groups" version of two heaps.

So here the two heaps don't hold halves of a sorted list. They hold two groups: projects I can't afford yet, in a min-heap by required capital, and projects I can afford now, in a max-heap by profit. Each round I unlock everything my capital covers, then greedily take the most profitable.

int findMaximizedCapital(int k, int w, int[] profits, int[] capital) {
    PriorityQueue<int[]> locked =
        new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));   // {capital, profit}
    PriorityQueue<Integer> available =
        new PriorityQueue<>(Comparator.reverseOrder());               // profits
    for (int i = 0; i < profits.length; i++)
        locked.offer(new int[]{capital[i], profits[i]});
    for (int round = 0; round < k; round++) {
        while (!locked.isEmpty() && locked.peek()[0] <= w)
            available.offer(locked.poll()[1]);   // now affordable
        if (available.isEmpty()) break;          // nothing I can start
        w += available.poll();                   // take the best
    }
    return w;
}
// k=2, w=0, profits=[1,2,3], capital=[0,1,1] -> 4
// k=3, w=0, profits=[1,2,3], capital=[0,1,2] -> 6

Tracing the first one: with w = 0 only project 0 unlocks, so I take profit 1 and w = 1. Now projects 1 and 2 unlock, I take profit 3, and w = 4.

The greedy is safe because capital only goes up. Taking the most profitable affordable project can never lock me out of anything; it only unlocks more. And once a project moves to available, it stays there, so each project crosses at most once. That's O(n log n) to build plus O(k log n) for the rounds.

The same "locked versus ready" shape shows up in Single-Threaded CPU (tasks not yet arrived versus waiting), Meeting Rooms III (busy rooms versus free rooms) and Minimum Refueling Stops.

Interviewers look for: recognising that this is the same pattern as the median problem even though nothing is being split in half: two heaps, each keeping the item you care about next on top, with items moving one way between them.

41. Subsets, Permutations & Combinations

BasicHow many subsets and permutations does an array of n elements have, and what does that mean for the running time?

So an array of n distinct elements has 2n subsets and n! permutations. Those are output sizes, so no algorithm that lists them all can beat them. The best I can do is not waste work on top.

Let me show you with a quick table.

n     2^n subsets     n! permutations
3     8               6
10    1,024           3,628,800
15    32,768          ~1.3 trillion
20    ~1 million      ~2.4 * 10^18

Each subset has up to n elements, and I copy it into the result, so generating all subsets is O(n · 2n). Permutations are O(n · n!) for the same reason. Every leaf costs a copy of length n.

What this tells me in an interview is the input limit. If the constraint says n <= 20, 2n is about a million and fine. If it says n <= 10, n! is about 3.6 million and fine. If it says n <= 105, the problem is not asking me to enumerate. It wants a count via DP or a formula, and listing would never finish.

So in practice I read the constraints first. A tiny n is the hint that backtracking is expected.

Interviewers look for: the extra factor of n for copying each answer. Saying "O(2n)" alone is usually accepted but gets the follow-up "what does each leaf cost?"

BasicWhy do you write result.add(new ArrayList<>(path)) instead of result.add(path)?

So path is one list that I keep mutating as I choose and un-choose. If I add the reference itself, every entry in the result points to that same list, and by the end it's empty.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static List<List<Integer>> wrong = new ArrayList<>();

static void badBacktrack(int[] nums, int start, List<Integer> path) {
    wrong.add(path);                          // BUG: stores the same list object
    for (int i = start; i < nums.length; i++) {
        path.add(nums[i]);
        badBacktrack(nums, i + 1, path);
        path.remove(path.size() - 1);
    }
}

// badBacktrack(new int[]{1, 2, 3}, 0, new ArrayList<>());
// wrong = [[], [], [], [], [], [], [], []]

What happens here is that I do record eight answers, but they're eight references to one object. Backtracking undoes every choice on the way out, so when I print the result that one list is back to [].

The copy takes a snapshot of the path at that moment. That snapshot is also where the O(n) per answer in the complexity comes from.

The same trap shows up with a StringBuilder. I have to call sb.toString() when saving, never store the builder. And with an int[] path I'd use path.clone() or Arrays.copyOf.

So the rule I follow: the path is shared scratch space, and anything that goes into the result is a copy.

IntermediateWalk me through generating all subsets. Is there a way without recursion?

So I'd use the start-index backtracking template. Every node of the decision tree is a valid subset, so I save at every call. Then I only extend with elements to the right of the last pick, so [2,1] is never built as a duplicate of [1,2].

static List<List<Integer>> subsets(int[] nums) {
    List<List<Integer>> result = new ArrayList<>();
    backtrack(nums, 0, new ArrayList<>(), result);
    return result;
}

static void backtrack(int[] nums, int start, List<Integer> path, List<List<Integer>> result) {
    result.add(new ArrayList<>(path));       // every node is an answer
    for (int i = start; i < nums.length; i++) {
        path.add(nums[i]);                    // choose
        backtrack(nums, i + 1, path, result); // explore
        path.remove(path.size() - 1);         // un-choose
    }
}
// [1,2,3] -> [[], [1], [1, 2], [1, 2, 3], [1, 3], [2], [2, 3], [3]]

Without recursion, I use a bitmask. Each number from 0 to 2n - 1 is one subset: bit i set means "include nums[i]".

static List<List<Integer>> subsetsBits(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    List<List<Integer>> result = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int mask = 0; mask < (1 << n); mask++) {
        List<Integer> subset = new ArrayList<>();
        for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
            if ((mask & (1 << i)) != 0) subset.add(nums[i]);
        }
        result.add(subset);
    }
    return result;
}
// [1,2,3] -> [[], [1], [2], [1, 2], [3], [1, 3], [2, 3], [1, 2, 3]]

Both are O(n · 2n). The bitmask one is short, but it's limited to n <= 30 with an int, and it doesn't extend to duplicates or pruning. So in practice I write the backtracking version, because the same template becomes Subsets II and Combination Sum with a one-line change.

Interviewers look for: that you know both. The follow-up is usually "now the input has duplicates", which is only easy with the backtracking version.

IntermediateThe input has duplicates, like [1,2,2]. How do you avoid duplicate subsets? Why i > start and not i > 0?

So I sort first, which puts equal values next to each other. Then inside the loop I skip a value if it equals its left neighbour at the same level of the tree. That means I only start one branch per distinct value at each level.

static void dup(int[] nums, int start, List<Integer> path, List<List<Integer>> result) {
    result.add(new ArrayList<>(path));
    for (int i = start; i < nums.length; i++) {
        if (i > start && nums[i] == nums[i - 1]) continue;   // same value, same level: skip
        path.add(nums[i]);
        dup(nums, i + 1, path, result);
        path.remove(path.size() - 1);
    }
}
// sorted [1,2,2] -> [[], [1], [1, 2], [1, 2, 2], [2], [2, 2]]

The i > start part is the subtle bit. The loop at one call is one level of the tree. The first element of the loop, at i == start, is always allowed, even if it equals nums[i - 1], because nums[i - 1] was picked at the level above. That's how [2,2] gets built: go down with the first 2, and at the next level the second 2 is the first option.

If I wrote i > 0, I'd lose those. I actually ran it: with i > 0 the output is [[], [1], [1, 2], [2]]. [1,2,2] and [2,2] disappear.

For permutations with duplicates, the rule is i > 0 && nums[i] == nums[i-1] && !used[i-1], because there's no start index. So in practice: sort, then skip same value at the same level.

Interviewers look for: the level explanation, not just the line of code. Many candidates memorise the skip and can't say why [2,2] survives.

IntermediateCombination Sum: numbers can be reused. How does that change the template, and how do you prune?

So two changes. I recurse with i instead of i + 1, which lets the same candidate be picked again. And I track remaining, saving the path only when it hits exactly zero.

static List<List<Integer>> combinationSum(int[] candidates, int target) {
    Arrays.sort(candidates);
    List<List<Integer>> result = new ArrayList<>();
    comb(candidates, 0, target, new ArrayList<>(), result);
    return result;
}

static void comb(int[] c, int start, int remaining, List<Integer> path, List<List<Integer>> result) {
    if (remaining == 0) {
        result.add(new ArrayList<>(path));
        return;
    }
    for (int i = start; i < c.length; i++) {
        if (c[i] > remaining) break;          // sorted: everything after is bigger too
        path.add(c[i]);
        comb(c, i, remaining - c[i], path, result);   // i, not i + 1: reuse allowed
        path.remove(path.size() - 1);
    }
}
// [2,3,6,7], 7 -> [[2, 2, 3], [7]]
// [2,3,5], 8   -> [[2, 2, 2, 2], [2, 3, 3], [3, 5]]

The start index still matters even with reuse. Without it I'd get [2,2,3], [2,3,2] and [3,2,2] as three answers. Keeping picks in non-decreasing index order means each combination is built exactly once.

The pruning comes from the sort. Once c[i] is bigger than what's left, every later candidate is too, so I break instead of continue. That cuts whole subtrees, not just one branch.

So the three variants are one template with different knobs: i + 1 for use-once, i for reuse, and the duplicate skip for Combination Sum II.

AdvancedGenerate all valid parentheses for n pairs. How do you avoid generating invalid strings, and how many are there?

So instead of building all 22n strings and filtering, I only ever add a character that keeps the prefix valid. I can add ( while I've used fewer than n. I can add ) only while there are more opens than closes. Every leaf is then valid by construction.

static List<String> generateParenthesis(int n) {
    List<String> result = new ArrayList<>();
    gen(n, 0, 0, new StringBuilder(), result);
    return result;
}

static void gen(int n, int open, int close, StringBuilder sb, List<String> result) {
    if (sb.length() == 2 * n) {
        result.add(sb.toString());
        return;
    }
    if (open < n) {
        sb.append('(');
        gen(n, open + 1, close, sb, result);
        sb.deleteCharAt(sb.length() - 1);
    }
    if (close < open) {
        sb.append(')');
        gen(n, open, close + 1, sb, result);
        sb.deleteCharAt(sb.length() - 1);
    }
}
// n = 3 -> [((())), (()()), (())(), ()(()), ()()()]

The count is the Catalan number Cn = (2n)! / ((n+1)! n!): 1, 2, 5, 14, 42... For n = 3 I get 5, and for n = 4 I get 14. It grows roughly like 4n / n1.5.

For complexity, the tree has no dead ends, since every branch I take leads to at least one valid string. So the work is proportional to the output: O(Cn · n) including the toString copies. Recursion depth is 2n.

So the general lesson I'd point out: move the validity check from the leaf into the branching condition. That's the difference between filtering and pruning.

Interviewers look for: "close < open", not "close < n". The follow-up is often "just count them", which is the Catalan recurrence or a DP on (open, close).

42. Modified Binary Search

BasicWhy do you write mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2 instead of (lo + hi) / 2?

So lo + hi can overflow an int when both are large, and the mid goes negative. lo + (hi - lo) / 2 never adds the two big numbers, so it stays in range.

Let me show you with a quick example.

int lo = 2_000_000_000, hi = 2_100_000_000;
(lo + hi) / 2          // -97483648   overflowed past Integer.MAX_VALUE
lo + (hi - lo) / 2     // 2050000000
(lo + hi) >>> 1        // 2050000000  unsigned shift also works

You won't hit this with array indices under a billion. But it matters a lot for binary search on the answer, where the range is values, not indices. A capacity or a time can easily go up to 109 or more. This exact bug lived in the JDK's Arrays.binarySearch for years.

The other detail is that both forms round down, so mid leans left. That's why the while (lo < hi) template pairs with hi = mid and lo = mid + 1. If I ever need lo = mid, I have to round up with lo + (hi - lo + 1) / 2, or the loop can get stuck when hi = lo + 1.

So I use lo + (hi - lo) / 2 by habit, and I check the rounding matches how I move the bounds.

IntermediateSearch for a target in a rotated sorted array in O(log n).

So the key fact is that if I cut a rotated sorted array at mid, at least one half is fully sorted. I find which half by comparing nums[lo] with nums[mid]. Then I check whether the target is inside that sorted half's range. If yes I go there, otherwise I go to the other half.

static int search(int[] nums, int target) {
    int lo = 0, hi = nums.length - 1;
    while (lo <= hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (nums[mid] == target) return mid;
        if (nums[lo] <= nums[mid]) {                  // left half is sorted
            if (nums[lo] <= target && target < nums[mid]) hi = mid - 1;
            else lo = mid + 1;
        } else {                                      // right half is sorted
            if (nums[mid] < target && target <= nums[hi]) lo = mid + 1;
            else hi = mid - 1;
        }
    }
    return -1;
}
// [4,5,6,7,0,1,2], 0 -> 4    target 3 -> -1    [1], 0 -> -1

Tracing target 0: lo=0, hi=6, mid=3 (7). 4 <= 7, so the left half is sorted. Is 0 in [4, 7)? No, so lo = 4. Now mid=5 (1). nums[4]=0 <= 1, left sorted, and 0 is in [0, 1), so hi = 4. mid=4 is 0. Found at index 4.

One gotcha I've hit is <= in nums[lo] <= nums[mid]. When lo == mid, which happens with two elements left, the "left half" is just one element and it counts as sorted. With < it breaks on inputs like [3,1] searching for 1.

It's O(log n) time, O(1) space. With duplicates allowed (LC 81), nums[lo] == nums[mid] == nums[hi] makes it impossible to tell which side is sorted. Then I shrink by one, and the worst case becomes O(n).

Interviewers look for: the "one half is always sorted" sentence before any code. And the duplicates follow-up, where the honest answer is O(n) worst case.

IntermediateFind the first and last position of a target in a sorted array.

So I run two boundary searches instead of finding any match and scanning outward. Scanning outward is O(n) when the whole array is the target. I write one lowerBound, "first index with value >= x", and call it twice.

static int lowerBound(int[] nums, int target) {
    int lo = 0, hi = nums.length;                 // hi = n: "not found" is a valid answer
    while (lo < hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (nums[mid] >= target) hi = mid;
        else lo = mid + 1;
    }
    return lo;
}

static int[] searchRange(int[] nums, int target) {
    int first = lowerBound(nums, target);
    if (first == nums.length || nums[first] != target) return new int[]{-1, -1};
    int last = lowerBound(nums, target + 1) - 1;  // one before the first bigger value
    return new int[]{first, last};
}
// [5,7,7,8,8,10], 8 -> [3, 4]    6 -> [-1, -1]    [] , 0 -> [-1, -1]

The reason I like this shape is that the condition is monotonic: false false false TRUE TRUE. The loop always ends with lo == hi on the first true, so there's no "did I return mid or mid + 1?" guessing.

The last position is "first index with value >= target + 1, minus one". That trick needs a type that won't overflow at target + 1. If the target could be Integer.MAX_VALUE, I'd write a separate upper-bound with nums[mid] > target instead.

So it's two O(log n) searches, O(1) space, and the same helper solves "count of x" (last - first + 1) and "insert position" too.

IntermediateFind the minimum of a rotated sorted array. What changes if there are duplicates?

So I compare mid with the last element. If nums[mid] > nums[hi], the drop is to the right of mid, so the minimum is in (mid, hi]. Otherwise mid is in the lower run, and the minimum is at mid or to its left.

static int findMin(int[] nums) {
    int lo = 0, hi = nums.length - 1;
    while (lo < hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (nums[mid] > nums[hi]) lo = mid + 1;   // drop is to the right of mid
        else hi = mid;                            // mid could be the minimum
    }
    return nums[lo];
}
// [3,4,5,1,2] -> 1    [4,5,6,7,0,1,2] -> 0    [11,13,15,17] -> 11

I compare with hi rather than lo because it handles the not-rotated case for free. In [11,13,15,17], mid is always <= hi, so hi keeps moving left to index 0.

With duplicates (LC 154), nums[mid] == nums[hi] tells me nothing. In [3,1,3,3,3] the minimum is left of mid, and in [3,3,3,1,3] it's right. So I drop just hi, which is safe because mid is an equal copy:

if (nums[mid] > nums[hi]) lo = mid + 1;
else if (nums[mid] < nums[hi]) hi = mid;
else hi--;                                        // can't tell: shrink by one
// [2,2,2,0,1] -> 0    [3,1,3,3,3] -> 1

That makes the worst case O(n), for example an array of all 1s with one 0 somewhere. So without duplicates it's O(log n), and with them I say "O(log n) average, O(n) worst" and explain why no algorithm can do better.

Interviewers look for: why you compare with hi, not lo. The follow-up is "return the rotation index", which is just lo.

AdvancedWhat is "binary search on the answer"? Show me with Capacity to Ship Packages Within D Days.

So sometimes I can't compute the answer directly, but I can cheaply check a guess, and feasibility is monotonic: if capacity C works, any bigger capacity works too. Then the answers look like no no no YES YES, and I binary search for the first yes over the range of possible answers.

static int shipWithinDays(int[] weights, int days) {
    int lo = 0, hi = 0;
    for (int w : weights) {
        lo = Math.max(lo, w);                     // must fit the heaviest package
        hi += w;                                  // one day ships everything
    }
    while (lo < hi) {
        int mid = lo + (hi - lo) / 2;
        if (canShip(weights, days, mid)) hi = mid;
        else lo = mid + 1;
    }
    return lo;
}

static boolean canShip(int[] weights, int days, int capacity) {
    int needed = 1, load = 0;
    for (int w : weights) {                       // greedy: fill each day in order
        if (load + w > capacity) {
            needed++;
            load = 0;
        }
        load += w;
    }
    return needed <= days;
}
// [1..10], 5 -> 15     [3,2,2,4,1,4], 3 -> 6     [1,2,3,1,1], 4 -> 3

The bounds matter. The low end is the heaviest package, since anything smaller can't carry it. The high end is the total, which ships in one day. The check is greedy: packages must go in order, so I pack each day as full as possible.

Complexity is O(n · log(sum)). The binary search does about log(sum) rounds, and each check is one O(n) pass. With sum up to 5 * 107, that's about 26 passes.

The same shape solves Koko Eating Bananas, Split Array Largest Sum, and Minimum Days to Make Bouquets. The phrases that give it away are "minimum capacity / speed" and "minimise the maximum". So in practice, when I see those, I first ask myself "can I check a guess in O(n)?"

Interviewers look for: a stated monotonicity argument and correct bounds. A common bug is starting lo at 1, which makes canShip silently wrong for a package heavier than the capacity.

AdvancedHow do you find the median of two sorted arrays in O(log(min(m, n)))?

So I don't merge. I binary search for a partition: take i elements from the shorter array A and j = half - i from B, so the left side has half of all elements. The partition is correct when everything on the left is <= everything on the right. Because each array is sorted, I only need to check the two cross pairs.

static double findMedianSortedArrays(int[] a, int[] b) {
    if (a.length > b.length) return findMedianSortedArrays(b, a);   // search the shorter
    int m = a.length, n = b.length, half = (m + n + 1) / 2;
    int lo = 0, hi = m;
    while (lo <= hi) {
        int i = (lo + hi) / 2;                    // take i from a
        int j = half - i;                         // and the rest from b
        int aLeft  = i == 0 ? Integer.MIN_VALUE : a[i - 1];
        int aRight = i == m ? Integer.MAX_VALUE : a[i];
        int bLeft  = j == 0 ? Integer.MIN_VALUE : b[j - 1];
        int bRight = j == n ? Integer.MAX_VALUE : b[j];
        if (aLeft <= bRight && bLeft <= aRight) {
            if ((m + n) % 2 == 1) return Math.max(aLeft, bLeft);
            return (Math.max(aLeft, bLeft) + Math.min(aRight, bRight)) / 2.0;
        } else if (aLeft > bRight) {
            hi = i - 1;                           // took too many from a
        } else {
            lo = i + 1;                           // took too few from a
        }
    }
    throw new IllegalArgumentException("inputs not sorted");
}
// [1,3],[2] -> 2.0     [1,2],[3,4] -> 2.5     [],[1] -> 1.0

If aLeft > bRight, I took too many from A, so I move left. Otherwise I took too few. The sentinels MIN_VALUE and MAX_VALUE handle the edge where a partition takes nothing or everything from one array.

I search the shorter array for two reasons: it's O(log(min(m, n))), and it guarantees j stays within [0, n]. The + 1 in half puts the extra element on the left for odd totals, so the median is just the max of the left side.

In an interview I'd mention the O(m + n) merge first as the baseline. I'd do this version only if they ask for log time.

Interviewers look for: the partition idea explained in words, and the sentinel edges. Most failed attempts break on an empty array or on j going out of range.

43. Topological Sort

BasicWhat is a topological sort, and when does one exist?

So a topological sort is an ordering of the nodes of a directed graph where, for every edge A → B, A comes before B. Think of the edges as "must happen before". One exists if and only if the graph has no directed cycle, meaning it's a DAG.

Let me show you with a quick example.

edges: 0 -> 1, 0 -> 2, 1 -> 3, 2 -> 3

valid orders:   [0, 1, 2, 3]   [0, 2, 1, 3]
invalid:        [1, 0, 2, 3]   (1 before 0, but 0 -> 1)

add 3 -> 0:     0 -> 1 -> 3 -> 0 is a cycle, so no order exists

The cycle part is intuitive. If A must come before B and B before A, nobody can go first. And the other direction also holds: every DAG has at least one node with no incoming edges, so I can always pick something to go first, remove it, and repeat.

The order is usually not unique. In the example, 1 and 2 don't depend on each other, so either can come first.

Where I've actually seen this: build systems ordering compile steps, package managers resolving install order, spreadsheet cells recomputing, and database migrations. The interview versions are Course Schedule, build order and Alien Dictionary.

So when a problem says "prerequisites", "depends on" or "must be done before", I think topological sort. And "is it possible?" is really "is there a cycle?"

IntermediateWalk me through Kahn's algorithm. How does it detect a cycle?

So Kahn's algorithm is BFS on indegrees. A node's indegree is how many prerequisites are still unfinished. I start a queue with every node at indegree 0. Each time I poll a node, I add it to the order and decrement its neighbours. Any neighbour that drops to 0 joins the queue.

static int[] findOrder(int numCourses, int[][] prerequisites) {
    List<List<Integer>> graph = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numCourses; i++) graph.add(new ArrayList<>());
    int[] indegree = new int[numCourses];
    for (int[] p : prerequisites) {           // p = {course, prereq}: prereq -> course
        graph.get(p[1]).add(p[0]);
        indegree[p[0]]++;
    }
    Deque<Integer> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numCourses; i++) if (indegree[i] == 0) queue.offer(i);
