    int[] order = new int[numCourses];
    int idx = 0;
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int node = queue.poll();
        order[idx++] = node;
        for (int next : graph.get(node)) {
            if (--indegree[next] == 0) queue.offer(next);
        }
    }
    return idx == numCourses ? order : new int[0];   // fewer than n: cycle
}
// 4, [[1,0],[2,0],[3,1],[3,2]] -> [0, 1, 2, 3]     2, [[1,0],[0,1]] -> []

The cycle detection comes for free. Nodes on a cycle wait on each other, so their indegree never reaches 0 and they never enter the queue. If I processed fewer than n nodes, there's a cycle.

One gotcha I've hit is edge direction. LeetCode gives [a, b] meaning "b before a", so the edge is b → a. I always write that comment before building the graph.

It's O(V + E) time and space: each node is enqueued once, and each edge is decremented once.

Interviewers look for: the "count processed nodes" check and the edge direction. The follow-up is often "which nodes are in the cycle?", which are exactly the ones with indegree still above 0.

IntermediateHow do you do topological sort with DFS? Why do you need three colours instead of a visited boolean?

So in DFS I add a node to the order only after all its descendants finish, which is post-order. Reversing that gives a valid topological order. For cycle detection I track three states: unvisited, in progress (on the current path) and done.

static final int WHITE = 0, GRAY = 1, BLACK = 2;

static boolean dfs(int u, List<List<Integer>> graph, int[] color, Deque<Integer> stack) {
    color[u] = GRAY;                          // on the current path
    for (int v : graph.get(u)) {
        if (color[v] == GRAY) return false;   // back edge: cycle
        if (color[v] == WHITE && !dfs(v, graph, color, stack)) return false;
    }
    color[u] = BLACK;                         // fully finished
    stack.push(u);                            // post-order, pushed to the front
    return true;
}
// edges 0->1, 0->2, 1->3, 2->3 : order [0, 2, 1, 3]
// edges 0->1, 1->2, 2->0       : cycle, []

The reason one boolean isn't enough: in a directed graph, reaching an already visited node doesn't mean a cycle. In the diamond 0→1→3 and 0→2→3, I reach 3 a second time through 2, but 3 is finished, and that's fine. It's only a cycle if the node is still on my current path, which is GRAY.

For undirected graphs, one visited flag plus "ignore the parent" works. That's where people's habit comes from, and it's exactly what breaks here.

Both approaches are O(V + E). I usually pick Kahn's because it has no recursion depth problem on long chains, and it extends to "minimum number of rounds". I'd pick DFS if I already have a DFS written, or for a strongly connected components follow-up.

IntermediateWhat is the minimum number of semesters to finish all courses, if you can take any number of courses at once?

So that's Kahn's algorithm processed level by level. Everything in the queue at the same time has no unmet prerequisites, so it can all be taken in the same semester. The number of levels is the answer. It equals the number of nodes on the longest path in the DAG.

static int minimumSemesters(int n, int[][] relations) {   // 1-indexed {prev, next}
    List<List<Integer>> graph = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i <= n; i++) graph.add(new ArrayList<>());
    int[] indegree = new int[n + 1];
    for (int[] r : relations) {
        graph.get(r[0]).add(r[1]);
        indegree[r[1]]++;
    }
    Deque<Integer> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) if (indegree[i] == 0) queue.offer(i);
    int semesters = 0, taken = 0;
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        semesters++;
        for (int size = queue.size(); size > 0; size--) {   // one level = one semester
            int course = queue.poll();
            taken++;
            for (int next : graph.get(course)) if (--indegree[next] == 0) queue.offer(next);
        }
    }
    return taken == n ? semesters : -1;
}
// 3, [[1,3],[2,3]] -> 2      3, [[1,2],[2,3],[3,1]] -> -1

The trick is snapshotting queue.size() before the inner loop. New courses that unlock during this semester go to the back of the queue and get counted next round. It's the same pattern as level-order traversal of a tree.

A related question is "is the order unique?" That's true only if the queue never holds more than one node at a time. Two free nodes at once means I could take them in either order.

Still O(V + E). If there's a cap of k courses per semester (LC 1494), the greedy breaks down and it becomes bitmask DP, which I'd call out rather than try to force.

Interviewers look for: the level snapshot. And whether you notice that a per-semester limit changes the problem completely.

AdvancedAlien Dictionary: given words sorted in an unknown alphabet, find the letter order.

So it's a graph-building problem followed by a plain topological sort. Adjacent words tell me one fact each: at their first differing position, the letter in the first word comes before the letter in the second. Everything after that position tells me nothing.

static String alienOrder(String[] words) {
    Map<Character, Set<Character>> graph = new HashMap<>();
    Map<Character, Integer> indegree = new HashMap<>();
    for (String w : words) {
        for (char c : w.toCharArray()) {
            graph.putIfAbsent(c, new HashSet<>());
            indegree.putIfAbsent(c, 0);
        }
    }
    for (int i = 0; i + 1 < words.length; i++) {
        String a = words[i], b = words[i + 1];
        if (a.length() > b.length() && a.startsWith(b)) return "";   // "abc" before "ab": invalid
        for (int k = 0; k < Math.min(a.length(), b.length()); k++) {
            char x = a.charAt(k), y = b.charAt(k);
            if (x != y) {
                if (graph.get(x).add(y)) indegree.merge(y, 1, Integer::sum);  // only new edges
                break;                        // only the FIRST difference counts
            }
        }
    }
    Deque<Character> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (var e : indegree.entrySet()) if (e.getValue() == 0) queue.offer(e.getKey());
    StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        char c = queue.poll();
        sb.append(c);
        for (char next : graph.get(c)) {
            if (indegree.merge(next, -1, Integer::sum) == 0) queue.offer(next);
        }
    }
    return sb.length() == indegree.size() ? sb.toString() : "";      // cycle: no valid order
}
// ["wrt","wrf","er","ett","rftt"] -> "wertf"     ["z","x","z"] -> ""

Three edge cases are where people lose this one. First, every letter that appears must be in the output, even with no edges, so I register all characters up front. Second, a word followed by its own prefix, like "abc" then "ab", is impossible in any alphabet, so I return "". Third, the same pair can appear twice, so I use a Set and only bump the indegree on a new edge. Otherwise the count never reaches zero.

Complexity is O(C) to scan all characters, plus O(U + E) for the sort, where U is at most 26. So it's effectively linear in the total input length.

Interviewers look for: the prefix case and the duplicate-edge case. Candidates who compare every pair of words, not just adjacent ones, also get pushed on why that's unnecessary.

44. Matrix & Islands

BasicHow do you treat a grid as a graph? What is the complexity of visiting every cell?

So each cell is a node, and its neighbours are the up to four cells next to it. I don't build an adjacency list. I compute neighbours on the fly with a direction array and a bounds check. Visiting every cell with DFS or BFS is O(R × C), because each cell is marked once and checks at most four neighbours.

Let me show you with a quick example.

static final int[][] DIRS = {{1, 0}, {-1, 0}, {0, 1}, {0, -1}};   // down, up, right, left

for (int[] d : DIRS) {
    int nr = r + d[0], nc = c + d[1];
    if (nr < 0 || nr >= rows || nc < 0 || nc >= cols) continue;    // off the grid
    if (grid[nr][nc] != '1') continue;                             // not part of the region
    // visit (nr, nc)
}

The direction array replaces four copy-pasted if-blocks, and that's where most grid bugs come from. If the problem allows diagonals, I add the four diagonal pairs and nothing else changes.

In graph terms, V = R × C and E is at most 4V, so O(V + E) becomes O(R × C). Space is O(R × C) for the visited array. I can make it O(1) extra by overwriting the grid, as long as I ask first whether mutating the input is fine.

A trick I use when a set or union-find needs one key per cell: encode (r, c) as r * cols + c, and decode with / cols and % cols.

So I set up the grid scaffolding once, and every island problem becomes a question of what to do at each cell.

IntermediateYour recursive DFS for islands crashes on a 1000 × 1000 grid. Why, and how do you fix it?

So the recursion depth can reach the number of cells in one island. A grid of all land is a million nested calls, and Java's default thread stack only fits tens of thousands of frames at most. I tested it, and it throws StackOverflowError. The fix is BFS or DFS with an explicit queue or stack, which lives on the heap.

static int numIslandsBfs(char[][] grid) {
    int rows = grid.length, cols = grid[0].length, count = 0;
    Deque<int[]> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++) {
        for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++) {
            if (grid[r][c] != '1') continue;
            count++;
            grid[r][c] = '0';                 // mark when ENQUEUED
            queue.offer(new int[]{r, c});
            while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
                int[] cell = queue.poll();
                for (int[] d : DIRS) {
                    int nr = cell[0] + d[0], nc = cell[1] + d[1];
                    if (nr < 0 || nr >= rows || nc < 0 || nc >= cols || grid[nr][nc] != '1') continue;
                    grid[nr][nc] = '0';
                    queue.offer(new int[]{nr, nc});
                }
            }
        }
    }
    return count;
}
// 1000 x 1000 all land -> 1, no crash

One detail matters a lot here: I mark a cell visited when I enqueue it, not when I poll it. If I mark on poll, the same cell can be added by up to four neighbours before it's processed. The queue swells, and on big grids it's noticeably slower.

Other options are running the DFS in a thread with a bigger stack size, or union-find. But in an interview I'd say "the recursive version is fine for LeetCode sizes, and for production-sized grids I'd switch to BFS." That shows I know where the limit is.

Interviewers look for: naming the stack depth as O(R × C) in the worst case, and marking on enqueue. The follow-up is often "and BFS queue size?", which is much smaller in practice.

IntermediateRotting Oranges: how many minutes until every orange is rotten?

So it's multi-source BFS. Every rotten orange starts spreading at the same time, so I put all of them in the queue before the loop. Each BFS layer is one minute. I count fresh oranges up front so I can stop early and detect the unreachable ones.

static int orangesRotting(int[][] grid) {
    int rows = grid.length, cols = grid[0].length, fresh = 0;
    Deque<int[]> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++) {
        for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++) {
            if (grid[r][c] == 2) queue.offer(new int[]{r, c});   // every source up front
            else if (grid[r][c] == 1) fresh++;
        }
    }
    int minutes = 0;
    while (!queue.isEmpty() && fresh > 0) {
        minutes++;
        for (int size = queue.size(); size > 0; size--) {       // one layer = one minute
            int[] cell = queue.poll();
            for (int[] d : DIRS) {
                int nr = cell[0] + d[0], nc = cell[1] + d[1];
                if (nr < 0 || nr >= rows || nc < 0 || nc >= cols || grid[nr][nc] != 1) continue;
                grid[nr][nc] = 2;
                fresh--;
                queue.offer(new int[]{nr, nc});
            }
        }
    }
    return fresh == 0 ? minutes : -1;
}
// [[2,1,1],[1,1,0],[0,1,1]] -> 4    [[2,1,1],[0,1,1],[1,0,1]] -> -1    [[0,2]] -> 0

The fresh > 0 in the loop condition is deliberate. Without it I'd count one extra minute for the final layer that rots nothing. And [[0,2]] must return 0, not -1, because there was nothing to rot.

Why not run a BFS from each rotten orange separately? That's O(k × R × C), and taking the minimum per cell is messy. Seeding all sources at once is like adding a virtual super-source connected to each of them. One BFS gives every cell its distance to the nearest source.

O(R × C) time and space. The same pattern solves 01 Matrix and Walls and Gates.

IntermediateSurrounded Regions: flip every 'O' region that isn't connected to the border. How do you approach it?

So I flip the question around. Checking "is this region enclosed?" means exploring a region and only finding out at the end. Instead, I start from every border 'O' and mark everything it reaches as safe. Whatever 'O' is left unmarked is enclosed.

static void solve(char[][] board) {
    int rows = board.length, cols = board[0].length;
    for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++) {
        mark(board, r, 0);
        mark(board, r, cols - 1);
    }
    for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++) {
        mark(board, 0, c);
        mark(board, rows - 1, c);
    }
    for (int r = 0; r < rows; r++) {
        for (int c = 0; c < cols; c++) {
            if (board[r][c] == 'O') board[r][c] = 'X';        // not reached: enclosed
            else if (board[r][c] == '#') board[r][c] = 'O';   // reached from border: restore
        }
    }
}

static void mark(char[][] b, int r, int c) {
    if (r < 0 || r >= b.length || c < 0 || c >= b[0].length || b[r][c] != 'O') return;
    b[r][c] = '#';
    for (int[] d : DIRS) mark(b, r + d[0], c + d[1]);
}
// XXXX      XXXX
// XOOX  ->  XXXX
// XXOX      XXXX
// XOXX      XOXX   (bottom O touches the border, so it stays)

The temporary '#' is a third state, so I can tell "safe O" from "enclosed O" in one final sweep without an extra visited array.

This boundary trick shows up a lot. In Pacific Atlantic Water Flow I run it twice, once from each ocean's edges, flowing uphill, and the answer is the intersection. In Number of Enclaves I count what's left instead of flipping it.

It's O(R × C) time. Space is O(R × C) worst case for the recursion, so for big boards I'd use the BFS version from the previous question.

Interviewers look for: the "start from the border" flip. Candidates who DFS from each interior 'O' and try to undo changes when they hit the edge usually get tangled.

IntermediateRotate an n × n matrix 90 degrees clockwise in place.

So a clockwise rotation is a transpose followed by reversing each row. Both steps are simple in-place swaps, so I don't need a second matrix.

static void rotate(int[][] m) {
    int n = m.length;
    for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {                 // 1. transpose: swap across the diagonal
        for (int j = i + 1; j < n; j++) {
            int t = m[i][j]; m[i][j] = m[j][i]; m[j][i] = t;
        }
    }
    for (int[] row : m) {                         // 2. reverse each row
        for (int l = 0, r = n - 1; l < r; l++, r--) {
            int t = row[l]; row[l] = row[r]; row[r] = t;
        }
    }
}
// 1 2 3      1 4 7      7 4 1
// 4 5 6  ->  2 5 8  ->  8 5 2
// 7 8 9      3 6 9      9 6 3
//   transpose    reverse rows

The way I justify it: clockwise rotation sends (i, j) to (j, n-1-i). Transpose sends (i, j) to (j, i), and reversing the row sends column i to n-1-i. Put together, that's the rotation.

The bug I watch for is the inner loop starting at j = i + 1. If it starts at 0, every pair gets swapped twice and the matrix comes back unchanged.

For counter-clockwise, I transpose and then reverse each column, or equivalently reverse each row first and then transpose. For 180 degrees, I reverse the order of the rows and then reverse each row.

It's O(n2) time, which is optimal since every cell moves, and O(1) extra space. The alternative is rotating four cells at a time layer by layer. It's also O(1), but the index arithmetic is easier to get wrong under pressure.

AdvancedLand cells are added one at a time. How do you report the number of islands after each addition?

So rerunning DFS after each addition is O(k × R × C). Instead I use union-find. Each new land cell starts as its own island, so count + 1. Then for each land neighbour in a different set, I union them and count - 1. Each addition costs nearly constant time.

static int[] parent;

static int find(int x) {
    while (parent[x] != x) {
        parent[x] = parent[parent[x]];            // path halving
        x = parent[x];
    }
    return x;
}

static List<Integer> numIslands2(int m, int n, int[][] positions) {
    parent = new int[m * n];
    Arrays.fill(parent, -1);                      // -1 = water
    List<Integer> result = new ArrayList<>();
    int count = 0;
    for (int[] p : positions) {
        int id = p[0] * n + p[1];
        if (parent[id] != -1) {                   // already land: nothing changes
            result.add(count);
            continue;
        }
        parent[id] = id;
        count++;                                  // new island on its own...
        for (int[] d : DIRS) {
            int r = p[0] + d[0], c = p[1] + d[1], nid = r * n + c;
            if (r < 0 || r >= m || c < 0 || c >= n || parent[nid] == -1) continue;
            int a = find(id), b = find(nid);
            if (a != b) {                         // ...unless it touches others: merge
                parent[a] = b;
                count--;
            }
        }
        result.add(count);
    }
    return result;
}
// 3x3, [[0,0],[0,1],[1,2],[2,1]] -> [1, 1, 2, 3]
// 3x3, [[0,0],[0,2],[0,1]]       -> [1, 2, 1]   (the middle cell joins two islands)

The a != b check is what makes it correct. When a new cell touches two neighbours that already belong to the same island, only the first union decrements the count.

The duplicate-position check matters too. Adding the same cell twice must not bump the count.

With path compression and union by rank, each operation is O(α(n)), effectively constant. I only did path halving here, which is O(log n) amortised and fine in practice. Total is O(R × C + k · α). The limit is that union-find only handles additions. If land can also be removed, it doesn't support splitting, and I'd have to process offline in reverse.

Interviewers look for: recognising "dynamic connectivity, additions only" as union-find, and the r * n + c encoding. The next question is usually "what if cells can turn back into water?"

45. Design Problems

BasicHow do you approach a "design a data structure" question?

So I start by listing each operation with its required cost, because that list picks the structures for me. Usually no single structure meets every target, so I combine two and make sure every write keeps both in sync.

Let me show you with a quick table of what I reach for.

Requirement                                   Structure
find by key in O(1)                           HashMap
move/remove a known item in O(1), keep order  doubly linked list (node stored in the map)
random element in O(1)                        ArrayList + map value -> index
min so far, with pops                         stack of (value, min at that point)
nearest key <= x                              TreeMap.floorKey
top k across many lists                       PriorityQueue

Then I talk through it in a fixed order. First, the fields, one per requirement. Second, for each public method: look up via the map, update the ordering structure, update the map. Third, private helpers for fiddly pointer work, like unlink(node) and addFirst(node), so the public methods read like English.

Before coding, I ask the clarifying questions that change the design. What happens on a duplicate insert? Can capacity be zero? Does get count as a "use"? Is it single-threaded?

So in practice the interviewer is testing whether I can justify each field by the operation it speeds up. I say that out loud, "the map is for O(1) lookup, the list is for O(1) reordering", before I write anything.

IntermediateDesign an LRU cache with O(1) get and put. Write the full implementation.

So I combine a HashMap with a doubly linked list. The list keeps keys in recency order: the front is most recently used, the back is next to evict. The map goes from key to list node, so I can find any node in O(1) and unlink it in O(1), since it knows its own neighbours.

class LRUCache {
    private static class Node {
        int key, val;
        Node prev, next;
        Node(int key, int val) { this.key = key; this.val = val; }
    }

    private final int capacity;
    private final Map<Integer, Node> map = new HashMap<>();
    private final Node head = new Node(0, 0);    // sentinel, most recent side
    private final Node tail = new Node(0, 0);    // sentinel, least recent side

    public LRUCache(int capacity) {
        this.capacity = capacity;
        head.next = tail;
        tail.prev = head;
    }

    public int get(int key) {
        Node node = map.get(key);
        if (node == null) return -1;
        moveToFront(node);                        // reading counts as a use
        return node.val;
    }

    public void put(int key, int value) {
        Node node = map.get(key);
        if (node != null) {                       // existing key: update + touch
            node.val = value;
            moveToFront(node);
            return;
        }
        if (map.size() == capacity) {             // full: evict the LRU
            Node lru = tail.prev;
            unlink(lru);
            map.remove(lru.key);                  // why the node stores its key
        }
        Node fresh = new Node(key, value);
        addFirst(fresh);
        map.put(key, fresh);
    }

    private void moveToFront(Node node) {
        unlink(node);
        addFirst(node);
    }

    private void unlink(Node node) {
        node.prev.next = node.next;
        node.next.prev = node.prev;
    }

    private void addFirst(Node node) {
        node.prev = head;
        node.next = head.next;
        head.next.prev = node;
        head.next = node;
    }
}
// capacity 2: put(1,1) put(2,2) get(1)=1 put(3,3) [evicts 2] get(2)=-1
//             put(4,4) [evicts 1] get(1)=-1 get(3)=3 get(4)=4

The sentinels are the thing that keeps this bug-free. Because head and tail always exist, unlink and addFirst never check for null, and there's no special case for an empty list or a single node.

Why doubly linked? To remove a node in O(1), I need its predecessor. A singly linked list would need an O(n) walk to find it. And why store the key in the node? When I evict tail.prev, I have to delete it from the map too, and the map is keyed by key.

O(1) per operation, O(capacity) space.

Interviewers look for: sentinels, the key stored in the node, and updating an existing key without evicting. The classic bug is checking size == capacity before checking whether the key already exists.

IntermediateCan you build the LRU cache with LinkedHashMap? When would you use it in real code?

So yes. LinkedHashMap is literally a hash map plus a doubly linked list. If I construct it with accessOrder = true, every get and put moves the entry to the end. Overriding removeEldestEntry gives me eviction. The whole cache is about ten lines.

class LRUCacheShort extends LinkedHashMap<Integer, Integer> {
    private final int capacity;

    LRUCacheShort(int capacity) {
        super(16, 0.75f, true);                   // true = access order, not insertion order
        this.capacity = capacity;
    }

    public int get(int key) { return getOrDefault(key, -1); }

    public void put(int key, int value) { super.put(key, value); }

    @Override
    protected boolean removeEldestEntry(Map.Entry<Integer, Integer> eldest) {
        return size() > capacity;                 // checked after every insert
    }
}
// same sequence as before: 1, -1, -1, 3, 4

The detail people get wrong is the third constructor argument. Without true, the map is in insertion order, so reading a key doesn't refresh it, and I've built a FIFO cache instead of an LRU one. Also, removeEldestEntry runs after the insert, so the check is size() > capacity, not >=.

In an interview I mention this first and then ask, "do you want me to implement the linked list myself?" Usually they do, since that's the point of the question.

In real code I'd use this for a small single-threaded cache. It isn't thread-safe, and wrapping it in Collections.synchronizedMap puts one lock around every read, because reads mutate the order. For a real shared cache I'd reach for Caffeine, which has a smarter eviction policy (W-TinyLFU) and good concurrency, instead of writing my own.

Interviewers look for: the accessOrder flag and knowing that a get is a write in an access-ordered map. The follow-up is "make it thread-safe".

IntermediateDesign a stack that returns the minimum element in O(1).

So alongside each value, I store the minimum of the stack at the moment it was pushed. The current minimum is then always on top. When I pop, the previous minimum comes back automatically, because it was saved in the entry below.

class MinStack {
    private final Deque<int[]> stack = new ArrayDeque<>();   // {value, min at this point}

    public void push(int val) {
        int min = stack.isEmpty() ? val : Math.min(val, stack.peek()[1]);
        stack.push(new int[]{val, min});
    }
    public void pop()    { stack.pop(); }
    public int top()     { return stack.peek()[0]; }
    public int getMin()  { return stack.peek()[1]; }
}
// push -2, push 0, push -3: getMin = -3
// pop:                      top = 0, getMin = -2

A single min field doesn't work. When I pop the current minimum, I'd have no way to know the previous one without scanning, and that's O(n). Storing the minimum per level is basically an undo log.

A space-saving variant keeps a second stack that only gets a push when the new value is <= the current minimum. On pop, I pop from it too if the values match. The <= matters because of duplicates: if I push 1 twice and use <, the first pop loses the minimum.

All operations are O(1). Space is O(n) for the pairs version, and it's smaller in practice for the two-stack version.

The same idea extends to a queue with O(1) max, built from two such stacks, and to monotonic deques for sliding window maximum.

IntermediateDesign a set with insert, remove and getRandom, all in O(1) average.

So getRandom in O(1) needs index access, which means an ArrayList. remove in O(1) needs to find the value, which means a map from value to index. The problem is that removing from the middle of an ArrayList is O(n). So I swap the last element into the hole and remove from the end instead.

class RandomizedSet {
    private final List<Integer> values = new ArrayList<>();
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> indexOf = new HashMap<>();
    private final Random random = new Random();

    public boolean insert(int val) {
        if (indexOf.containsKey(val)) return false;
        indexOf.put(val, values.size());
        values.add(val);
        return true;
    }

    public boolean remove(int val) {
        Integer i = indexOf.get(val);
        if (i == null) return false;
        int last = values.get(values.size() - 1);
        values.set(i, last);                      // move the last value into the hole
        indexOf.put(last, i);
        values.remove(values.size() - 1);         // removing the LAST slot is O(1)
        indexOf.remove(val);
        return true;
    }

    public int getRandom() {
        return values.get(random.nextInt(values.size()));
    }
}

The order of the last two map operations matters. If val is itself the last element, indexOf.put(last, i) writes it back, and then indexOf.remove(val) deletes it. If I did them the other way around, I'd leave a stale entry for a removed value.

Also, I use values.remove(values.size() - 1) with an int index. With a List<Integer>, calling remove with an Integer would remove by value in O(n). That overload trap is a real Java gotcha.

Swapping breaks ordering, which is fine because a set has none. Each value is equally likely, since the list has no gaps.

Interviewers look for: the swap-with-last trick and the self-removal edge case. The follow-up is LC 381, which allows duplicates, where the map value becomes a set of indices.

AdvancedHow would you design an LFU cache with O(1) operations?

So I group keys by use count. buckets[f] holds the keys used exactly f times, in LRU order for tie-breaking, and a minFreq variable points at the lowest non-empty bucket. Using a key moves it from bucket f to f + 1. Eviction takes the oldest key in the minFreq bucket.

class LFUCache {
    private final int capacity;
    private int minFreq = 0;
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> vals = new HashMap<>();     // key -> value
    private final Map<Integer, Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();     // key -> use count
    private final Map<Integer, LinkedHashSet<Integer>> buckets = new HashMap<>();

    LFUCache(int capacity) { this.capacity = capacity; }

    public int get(int key) {
        if (!vals.containsKey(key)) return -1;
        touch(key);
        return vals.get(key);
    }

    public void put(int key, int value) {
        if (capacity == 0) return;
        if (vals.containsKey(key)) {
            vals.put(key, value);
            touch(key);
            return;
        }
        if (vals.size() == capacity) {
            Iterator<Integer> it = buckets.get(minFreq).iterator();
            int victim = it.next();               // oldest key in the lowest bucket
            it.remove();
            vals.remove(victim);
            freq.remove(victim);
        }
        vals.put(key, value);
        freq.put(key, 1);
        buckets.computeIfAbsent(1, f -> new LinkedHashSet<>()).add(key);
        minFreq = 1;                              // a new key always has count 1
    }

    private void touch(int key) {
        int f = freq.get(key);
        freq.put(key, f + 1);
        buckets.get(f).remove(key);
        if (f == minFreq && buckets.get(f).isEmpty()) minFreq++;
        buckets.computeIfAbsent(f + 1, x -> new LinkedHashSet<>()).add(key);
    }
}
// cap 2: put(1) put(2) get(1)=1 put(3) [evicts 2] get(2)=-1 get(3)=3
//        put(4) [evicts 1: count 2 vs 3's count 2, 1 is older] get(1)=-1 get(3)=3 get(4)=4

The reason minFreq is O(1) to maintain: it only changes in two ways. A new key resets it to 1. Or the key I just touched was the last one in the minimum bucket, and then the minimum is exactly f + 1, because that's where the key went. I never have to search.

LinkedHashSet gives me O(1) add, remove, and "oldest element" through its iterator. That's the per-bucket LRU for free.

So it's O(1) per operation and O(capacity) space. In an interview I'd first explain why a heap keyed on (count, time) is O(log n), and then present this as the O(1) upgrade.

Interviewers look for: why minFreq never needs a scan, and the capacity-zero edge. Getting tie-breaking wrong, by evicting any key with the minimum count instead of the oldest, is the most common miss.

Practice

Complexity Questions

BasicWhat's the complexity? (nested loops with a dependent bound) 
int count = 0;
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    for (int j = 0; j < i; j++) {
        count++;
    }
}

Answer: Time O(n2), Space O(1)

So I count how many times the inner body runs. When i is 0 it runs 0 times, when i is 1 it runs once, and so on up to n - 1. That's 0 + 1 + ... + (n - 1) = n(n - 1) / 2. I actually checked it with n = 1000 and got 499,500, which matches.

n(n - 1) / 2 is about n2 / 2, and I drop the constant, so it's O(n2). The fact that the inner loop is "only half" doesn't change the class. Half of quadratic is still quadratic.

Space is O(1): just the counters.

This is the shape of every "compare each pair once" loop, like the brute force for Two Sum or checking all pairs for duplicates. So when I see j < i or j = i + 1, I think "n choose 2", which is O(n2).

Interviewers look for: the arithmetic series, not "two loops so n squared". Two loops are not always n2, as the halving and harmonic questions show.

BasicWhat's the complexity? (a loop that halves) 
int steps = 0;
while (n > 0) {
    n /= 2;
    steps++;
}

Answer: Time O(log n), Space O(1)

So I ask how many times I can halve n before it hits 0. After k steps, n has become n / 2k, and the loop stops when that drops below 1, which is when 2k > n. So k is about log2 n, plus one for the final step. I checked it: for n = 1,000,000 it runs 20 times, and log2(106) is about 19.9.

The base of the log doesn't matter in big-O. log2 n and log10 n only differ by a constant factor, so I just write O(log n).

The same count shows up whenever the problem size is multiplied or divided by a constant each step: binary search, i *= 2 loops, counting the bits of a number, and climbing a balanced tree.

What would change it: if the loop body did O(n) work each time, like scanning the current range, the total would be n + n/2 + n/4 + ..., which is O(n), not O(n log n). That's a nice trap to remember.

BasicWhat's the complexity? (loop while i * i <= n) 
static boolean isPrime(int n) {
    if (n < 2) return false;
    for (int i = 2; i * i <= n; i++) {
        if (n % i == 0) return false;
    }
    return true;
}

Answer: Time O(√n), Space O(1)

So the loop variable goes up by one each step, and it stops when i2 passes n, which means i passes √n. That's about √n iterations in the worst case, which is when n is prime. For n = 109, that's around 31,623 checks instead of a billion.

The reason stopping at √n is correct: if n = a × b with a <= b, then a <= √n. So any composite number has a factor at or below its square root, and if I haven't found one by then, there isn't one.

One gotcha I've hit: i * i can overflow for n near Integer.MAX_VALUE. When i reaches 46,341, i2 goes negative, which is still <= n, so the loop keeps going. For large n I'd write i <= n / i or use long.

Best case is O(1) for an even number. But I state the worst case unless asked otherwise.

Interviewers look for: the "a factor pair has one side at most √n" reason. The follow-up is "primes up to n", where the Sieve of Eratosthenes is O(n log log n).

IntermediateWhat's the complexity? (recursive Fibonacci without memoisation) 
static int fib(int n) {
    if (n <= 1) return n;
    return fib(n - 1) + fib(n - 2);
}

Answer: Time O(2n), tighter O(φn) ≈ O(1.618n), Space O(n)

So I draw the call tree. Each call makes two calls, and the tree is at most n levels deep, so there are at most 2n nodes. That's where O(2n) comes from, and it's a valid upper bound.

It's not tight, though, because the right branch, n - 2, is shallower than the left. The number of calls satisfies the same recurrence as Fibonacci itself, so it grows like φn, where φ is the golden ratio, about 1.618. Either answer is usually accepted. I mention φ to show I know 2n is loose.

For space, the tree is huge, but it's never all in memory at once. The call stack only holds one root-to-leaf path at a time, and the longest path is fib(n) → fib(n-1) → ... → fib(1). So that's O(n) stack.

The real problem is repeated work. fib(n - 2) is computed once directly and again inside fib(n - 1), and it gets worse deeper down. fib(40) makes over 300 million calls.

Interviewers look for: time from the size of the tree, space from its depth. Mixing those up, by saying space is O(2n), is the common mistake.

IntermediateWhat's the complexity? (the same Fibonacci with memoisation) 
static long fibMemo(int n, long[] memo) {
    if (n <= 1) return n;
    if (memo[n] != 0) return memo[n];
    return memo[n] = fibMemo(n - 1, memo) + fibMemo(n - 2, memo);
}
// fibMemo(50, new long[51]) = 12586269025

Answer: Time O(n), Space O(n)

So the way I count memoised recursion is: number of distinct states times work per state. The only state is n, and there are n + 1 of them, from 0 to n. Each one does O(1) work besides its recursive calls, and it's computed only once. After that, any call for that state returns straight from the memo. So the total is O(n).

Another way to see it: fib(n) goes all the way down the left spine first, filling in memo[2], memo[3], and so on as it returns. By the time any right-branch call happens, its answer is already cached, so each right branch is O(1).

Space is O(n) for the memo array plus O(n) for the recursion depth, which is still O(n).

If they push further, I'd go bottom-up with two variables, prev and curr. That's O(n) time and O(1) space. And matrix exponentiation gets it to O(log n).

One small thing I changed: the return type is long. fib(47) already overflows an int, so that's worth mentioning.

IntermediateWhat's the complexity? (building a String with + in a loop) 
String s = "";
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) {
    s += "a";
}

Answer: Time O(n2), Space O(n)

So Java strings are immutable. s += "a" doesn't append in place. It creates a new string and copies all the current characters plus one. On iteration i, that copy is about i characters, so the total is 1 + 2 + ... + n = n(n + 1) / 2 character copies. That's O(n2).

The loop looks O(n), which is the trap. The hidden cost is inside the +=.

Space: at any moment only the current string, about n chars, is live, so it's O(n). The total allocated over the run is O(n2), which is garbage the GC has to clean up. So it's also slow in practice.

The fix is StringBuilder. It keeps a growable char array and doubles its capacity when full, so appends are amortised O(1) and the whole loop is O(n).

StringBuilder sb = new StringBuilder();
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) sb.append('a');
String s = sb.toString();

The compiler optimises a + b + c in a single expression, but it can't merge across loop iterations. So in any loop, I use a builder.

Interviewers look for: immutability as the reason. The same trap exists with list = concat(list, x) patterns and with repeated substring calls in recursion.

IntermediateWhat's the complexity? (ArrayList.remove(0) in a loop) 
List<Integer> list = new ArrayList<>();
for (int i = 0; i < n; i++) list.add(i);
while (!list.isEmpty()) {
    list.remove(0);
}

Answer: Time O(n2), Space O(n)

So ArrayList is backed by an array, and removing index 0 shifts every remaining element one slot left with System.arraycopy. With k elements left, that's k - 1 moves. Summed over the loop, it's (n - 1) + (n - 2) + ... + 0, which is O(n2).

The filling loop is O(n) amortised, so the removal dominates.

Space is O(n) for the list itself. The removal allocates nothing new.

This comes up in real code when someone uses an ArrayList as a queue in a BFS. The fix is ArrayDeque: poll() from the front is O(1) because it's a circular buffer, so it just moves a head index. LinkedList also gives O(1) at the ends, but ArrayDeque is faster in practice because of memory locality.

The opposite end is fine. list.remove(list.size() - 1) is O(1), since nothing shifts. That's why a stack built on ArrayList works well, and why the RandomizedSet trick swaps with the last element.

IntermediateWhat's the complexity? (merge sort) 
static void mergeSort(int[] a, int lo, int hi, int[] tmp) {   // sorts a[lo, hi)
    if (hi - lo < 2) return;
    int mid = (lo + hi) >>> 1;
    mergeSort(a, lo, mid, tmp);
    mergeSort(a, mid, hi, tmp);
    int i = lo, j = mid, k = lo;
    while (i < mid && j < hi) tmp[k++] = a[i] <= a[j] ? a[i++] : a[j++];
    while (i < mid) tmp[k++] = a[i++];
    while (j < hi)  tmp[k++] = a[j++];
    System.arraycopy(tmp, lo, a, lo, hi - lo);
}

Answer: Time O(n log n), Space O(n)

So I count it level by level. The recursion splits the array in half each time, so there are about log2 n levels. At every level, the pieces together cover all n elements, and merging is linear in the piece size. So each level costs O(n), and O(n) per level times log n levels gives O(n log n).

Formally it's T(n) = 2T(n/2) + O(n), which is the standard Master theorem case giving n log n.

That's the same in the best, average and worst case. Merge sort doesn't care about the input order, unlike quicksort.

Space is O(n) for the temp array, which I allocate once and pass down instead of creating one per call. The recursion stack adds O(log n), which is smaller.

That's also the reason Java's Arrays.sort on objects uses TimSort, a merge sort variant: it's stable and has a guaranteed n log n. For primitives it uses dual-pivot quicksort, which is in place and fast on average.

Interviewers look for: the level-by-level picture, not just the recurrence. The follow-up is "can you do it with O(1) extra space?", and the practical answer is heap sort, which isn't stable.

IntermediateWhat's the complexity? (BFS shortest path on a grid) 
static int bfs(int[][] grid) {                    // 0 = open, 1 = wall
    int rows = grid.length, cols = grid[0].length;
    int[][] dist = new int[rows][cols];
    for (int[] row : dist) Arrays.fill(row, -1);
    Deque<int[]> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    queue.offer(new int[]{0, 0});
    dist[0][0] = 0;
    int[][] dirs = {{1, 0}, {-1, 0}, {0, 1}, {0, -1}};
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int[] cur = queue.poll();
        for (int[] d : dirs) {
            int r = cur[0] + d[0], c = cur[1] + d[1];
            if (r < 0 || r >= rows || c < 0 || c >= cols) continue;
            if (grid[r][c] == 1 || dist[r][c] != -1) continue;
            dist[r][c] = dist[cur[0]][cur[1]] + 1;
            queue.offer(new int[]{r, c});
        }
    }
    return dist[rows - 1][cols - 1];
}

Answer: Time O(R × C), Space O(R × C)

So the key is that each cell gets its dist set once, and only then is it enqueued. So each cell is enqueued and polled at most once. When it's polled, it looks at 4 neighbours, which is constant work. That's O(4 × R × C), which is O(R × C).

In graph terms, V = R × C and E <= 4V, so the usual O(V + E) for BFS becomes O(R × C).

Space is O(R × C) for the dist array. The queue holds the current frontier, which is usually much smaller than the grid, but I still bound it by R × C.

What would break the bound is checking visited when I poll instead of when I enqueue. Then a cell can be enqueued by several neighbours before it's processed. It's still correct, but the queue does repeated work.

So on any grid question, I say O(R × C) and explain "each cell once, four neighbours each."

AdvancedWhat's the complexity? (HashMap operations in a loop, including the worst case) 
Map<Integer, Integer> freq = new HashMap<>();
for (int x : nums) {
    freq.merge(x, 1, Integer::sum);
}

Answer: Time O(n) average, O(n log n) worst case on Java 8+, Space O(n)

So each merge is a hash lookup plus an insert or update. On average that's O(1), assuming the hash spreads keys evenly and the table resizes to keep the load factor under 0.75. Resizing is amortised O(1) because the table doubles. So n operations are O(n) on average.

The worst case is when many keys land in the same bucket. Before Java 8, a bucket was a linked list, so each operation could be O(n), and the loop would be O(n2). Since Java 8, a bucket with more than 8 entries (in a table of at least 64) turns into a red-black tree. For Comparable keys like Integer, that makes each operation O(log n), so the loop is O(n log n).

The catch is that the tree needs to order the keys. I tested it: with a key class where every hash collides but that's not Comparable, 20,000 puts took about 2 seconds and 40,000 took about 8. That's quadratic. The same keys made Comparable took milliseconds.

Space is O(n) for the entries plus a table of about n / 0.75 buckets.

Interviewers look for: "O(1) average", with the word average, and knowing about treeification. The deeper follow-up is hash-flooding attacks on web servers, which is why the Java 8 change happened.

AdvancedWhat's the complexity? (the harmonic loop: j += i) 
for (int i = 1; i <= n; i++) {
    for (int j = i; j <= n; j += i) {
        work(i, j);                               // O(1)
    }
}

Answer: Time O(n log n), Space O(1)

So it looks like two nested loops up to n, but the inner loop steps by i, not by 1. For a given i, it runs about n / i times. Summing over all i, that's n/1 + n/2 + n/3 + ... + n/n = n × (1 + 1/2 + ... + 1/n).

That bracket is the harmonic series Hn, which grows like ln n. So the total is about n ln n, which is O(n log n). I checked it for n = 1,000,000: the inner body ran 13,970,034 times, and n ln n is 13,815,510. That's very close.

Space is O(1) beyond whatever work uses.

Where this shows up: the Sieve of Eratosthenes, where marking multiples of each i is this exact pattern. The sieve is O(n log log n) because it only loops over primes, which is even smaller. It also shows up when precomputing divisors for all numbers up to n, and in "for each number, visit its multiples" DP.

So the lesson I take from it: don't count loops, count how many times the innermost line runs. Two nested loops can be O(n2), O(n log n) or even O(n), depending on how the inner bound moves.

Interviewers look for: naming the harmonic series. Candidates who say O(n2) by pattern-matching on "two loops" are exactly who this question is built to catch.

AdvancedWhat's the complexity? (backtracking to generate all subsets) 
static void subsets(int[] nums, int start, List<Integer> path, List<List<Integer>> out) {
    out.add(new ArrayList<>(path));
    for (int i = start; i < nums.length; i++) {
        path.add(nums[i]);
        subsets(nums, i + 1, path, out);
        path.remove(path.size() - 1);
    }
}

Answer: Time O(n · 2n), Space O(n) auxiliary, O(n · 2n) including the output

So I count the calls first. Every call adds exactly one subset to out, and there are 2n subsets, so there are 2n calls. For n = 4 I get 16.

Then I count the work per call. The new ArrayList<>(path) copy costs up to n. The loop's add and remove are O(1) per child, and each child is its own call, so they're already counted. That gives 2n calls times O(n) copy, which is O(n · 2n). A tighter count is that the average subset has n/2 elements, but that's still the same class.

Space depends on what I count. The recursion depth is at most n, and path holds at most n elements, so the auxiliary space is O(n). The output itself holds 2n lists of average length n/2, which is O(n · 2n). I say both and make clear which is which.

For permutations the same reasoning gives O(n · n!): n! leaves, each copied at length n, plus the internal nodes, which are fewer than the leaves times a constant.

So for any backtracking question my recipe is: number of nodes in the tree, times work per node, then state output space separately.

Coding Rounds

IntermediateLC 3 · Longest Substring Without Repeating Characters

So before I start, a few clarifying questions. What characters can appear, only lowercase or any ASCII? That decides between an int[128] and a map. Is it a substring, meaning contiguous, and not a subsequence? And for an empty string, should I return 0? I'll assume any characters, contiguous, and 0 for empty.

Examples: "abcabcbb" gives 3 ("abc"), "bbbbb" gives 1, "pwwkew" gives 3 ("wke", not "pwke", because that's not contiguous). Edge cases I'd keep in mind are "" giving 0, " " giving 1, and "abba", which I'll come back to.

The brute force is to check every substring for uniqueness with a set. That's O(n2) substrings, and O(n) to check each, so O(n3). If I extend from each start and stop at the first repeat, it's O(n2).

The insight is that when I extend and hit a repeat, I don't need to restart. The valid window just needs its left edge moved past the earlier copy of that character. So it's a sliding window where both pointers only move forward. If I store each character's last index, I can jump left in one step instead of shrinking one at a time.

static int lengthOfLongestSubstring(String s) {
    Map<Character, Integer> lastSeen = new HashMap<>();
    int best = 0, left = 0;
    for (int right = 0; right < s.length(); right++) {
        char c = s.charAt(right);
        Integer prev = lastSeen.get(c);
        if (prev != null && prev >= left) {
            left = prev + 1;                  // jump past the earlier copy
        }
        lastSeen.put(c, right);
        best = Math.max(best, right - left + 1);
    }
    return best;
}
// "abcabcbb" -> 3   "bbbbb" -> 1   "pwwkew" -> 3   "" -> 0   "abba" -> 2   " " -> 1

Dry run on "abcabcbb": right 0 to 2 builds "abc" with left = 0, so best = 3. At right = 3 ('a'), the previous 'a' is at 0, so left becomes 1 and the window is "bca". At right = 4 ('b'), left becomes 2. At right = 5 ('c'), left becomes 3. At right = 6 ('b'), the previous 'b' is at 4, so left = 5. At right = 7, left = 7. The window never beats 3.

The prev >= left check is why I mentioned "abba". At the last 'a', its previous index is 0, but left is already 2 because of the second 'b'. Without the check, left would jump back to 1 and I'd return 3. With it, I correctly get 2.

Final complexity: O(n) time, since each character is visited once by right and left only jumps forward. Space is O(min(n, alphabet)). If they confirm ASCII, I'd swap the map for an int[128] filled with -1, which is faster.

IntermediateLC 56 · Merge Intervals

So first the clarifying questions. Is the input sorted? I'll assume not. Do touching intervals like [1,4] and [4,5] count as overlapping? LeetCode says yes, so they merge into [1,5]. Can I modify the input array, for example sort it in place? And is it guaranteed that start <= end?

Examples: [[1,3],[2,6],[8,10],[15,18]] gives [[1,6],[8,10],[15,18]]. [[1,4],[4,5]] gives [[1,5]]. Edge cases I'd test are unsorted input like [[4,7],[1,4]], giving [[1,7]], and containment like [[1,10],[2,3]], which must give [[1,10]] and not shrink the end to 3.

The brute force is to repeatedly scan all pairs, merge any two that overlap, and restart until nothing changes. That's O(n2) per pass and up to n passes, so O(n3), and it's fiddly.

The insight is to sort by start. After sorting, any interval that overlaps the current merged block must come right after it. There can't be one hiding further down that I'd miss. So one pass is enough: either the next interval starts after the current block ends, which is a gap, or it overlaps, and I extend the end.

static int[][] merge(int[][] intervals) {
    Arrays.sort(intervals, (a, b) -> Integer.compare(a[0], b[0]));
    List<int[]> merged = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int[] cur : intervals) {
        if (merged.isEmpty() || merged.get(merged.size() - 1)[1] < cur[0]) {
            merged.add(new int[]{cur[0], cur[1]});        // gap: start a new block
        } else {
            int[] last = merged.get(merged.size() - 1);
            last[1] = Math.max(last[1], cur[1]);          // overlap: stretch the end
        }
    }
    return merged.toArray(new int[0][]);
}
// [[1,3],[2,6],[8,10],[15,18]] -> [[1,6],[8,10],[15,18]]
// [[1,4],[4,5]] -> [[1,5]]   [[4,7],[1,4]] -> [[1,7]]   [[1,10],[2,3]] -> [[1,10]]

Dry run on the first example: [1,3] starts a block. [2,6]: 3 is not < 2, so it overlaps, and the end becomes max(3, 6) = 6. [8,10]: 6 < 8, so it's a gap and starts a new block. [15,18]: 10 < 15, another gap. Result [[1,6],[8,10],[15,18]].

Two details I'm deliberate about. The Math.max handles containment. And I use Integer.compare rather than a[0] - b[0], because subtraction overflows for large negative and positive starts.

Final complexity: O(n log n) time for the sort, and the merge pass is O(n). Space is O(n) for the output, plus O(log n) for the sort. If they asked for no sorting, like a stream of intervals, I'd switch to a TreeMap keyed by start.

IntermediateLC 200 · Number of Islands

So my clarifying questions. Is connectivity 4-directional only, or are diagonals included? I'll assume 4. Is the grid char '1' and '0'? Can I modify the grid, or should I leave it unchanged? And how large can it be? That tells me whether recursion depth is a risk.

Examples: a grid with one big blob of land gives 1. The grid 11000 / 11000 / 00100 / 00011 gives 3. Edge cases are all water, giving 0, all land, giving 1, and a single cell.

A brute-force-ish approach would be, for every land cell, to check whether it connects to any previously counted cell by searching. That's O((R × C)2) in the worst case.

The insight is that the grid is a graph, and an island is a connected component. I scan every cell. When I find unvisited land, that's a new island: I count it and flood-fill the whole island so none of its cells is counted again. Every cell is visited a constant number of times.

static int numIslands(char[][] grid) {
    int count = 0;
    for (int r = 0; r < grid.length; r++) {
        for (int c = 0; c < grid[0].length; c++) {
            if (grid[r][c] == '1') {
                count++;
                sink(grid, r, c);
            }
        }
    }
    return count;
}

static void sink(char[][] grid, int r, int c) {
    if (r < 0 || r >= grid.length || c < 0 || c >= grid[0].length || grid[r][c] != '1') return;
    grid[r][c] = '0';                             // mark visited by sinking it
    sink(grid, r + 1, c);
    sink(grid, r - 1, c);
    sink(grid, r, c + 1);
    sink(grid, r, c - 1);
}
// ["11110","11010","11000","00000"] -> 1
// ["11000","11000","00100","00011"] -> 3

Dry run on the second grid: (0,0) is land, count = 1, and sink clears (0,0), (0,1), (1,0), (1,1). The scan passes over the zeros. At (2,2), count = 2, and it sinks alone. At (3,3), count = 3, and sink clears (3,3) and (3,4). At (3,4) it's already '0', so it's skipped. The answer is 3.

I mark the cell before recursing. If I marked after, two neighbours could recurse into each other forever.

Final complexity: O(R × C) time. Space is O(R × C) worst case for the recursion stack, when the grid is all land in a snake shape. If they said the grid can be 1000 × 1000, I'd switch to BFS with an ArrayDeque, because the recursive version overflows the stack at that size. If mutating the input isn't allowed, I'd use a boolean[][] visited instead.

IntermediateLC 238 · Product of Array Except Self

So my clarifying questions. Can I use division? The problem says no, but I'd ask anyway, because it shapes the approach. Can there be zeros, and negative numbers? Is the product guaranteed to fit in an int? And does the output array count toward extra space?

Examples: [1,2,3,4] gives [24,12,8,6]. [-1,1,0,-3,3] gives [0,0,9,0,0]: only the position of the zero gets a non-zero product. Edge cases are two zeros, where everything is 0, and a length-2 array, which just swaps the elements.

The brute force is, for each i, to multiply all other elements. That's O(n2). The division trick, total product divided by nums[i], is O(n), but it's banned and breaks on zeros anyway.

The insight is that "everything except i" is "everything left of i" times "everything right of i". I can compute all prefix products in one pass and all suffix products in another. To get O(1) extra space, I store the prefix products directly in the answer array, then sweep from the right with a single running variable for the suffix.

static int[] productExceptSelf(int[] nums) {
    int n = nums.length;
    int[] answer = new int[n];
    answer[0] = 1;
    for (int i = 1; i < n; i++) {
        answer[i] = answer[i - 1] * nums[i - 1];      // product of everything to the left
    }
    int right = 1;
    for (int i = n - 1; i >= 0; i--) {
        answer[i] *= right;                            // times everything to the right
        right *= nums[i];
    }
    return answer;
}
// [1,2,3,4] -> [24,12,8,6]     [-1,1,0,-3,3] -> [0,0,9,0,0]

Dry run on [1,2,3,4]. The left pass gives answer = [1, 1, 2, 6]: at index 3, that's 1 × 2 × 3. The right pass: i = 3, answer = 6 × 1 = 6, right = 4. i = 2, answer = 2 × 4 = 8, right = 12. i = 1, answer = 1 × 12 = 12, right = 24. i = 0, answer = 1 × 24 = 24. Final [24, 12, 8, 6].

The order inside the second loop matters. I multiply answer[i] by right before including nums[i] in right, since index i must be excluded.

Zeros need no special handling. A zero just flows into the prefix or suffix of everyone except itself, which is exactly right.

Final complexity: O(n) time with two passes, and O(1) extra space, since the output array doesn't count by the problem's convention. With separate prefix and suffix arrays it would be O(n) space. I'd mention that as the stepping stone.

IntermediateLC 347 · Top K Frequent Elements

So my clarifying questions. Is the answer guaranteed unique, meaning no tie at the k-th place? LeetCode says yes. Does the output order matter? It doesn't. Is k always valid, between 1 and the number of distinct values? And roughly how big is n compared with the number of distinct values?

Examples: [1,1,1,2,2,3], k = 2 gives [1,2]. [1], k = 1 gives [1]. Edge cases are k equal to the number of distinct values, where I return all of them, and all elements the same.

The brute force is to count with a HashMap, sort the distinct values by count, and take the first k. That's O(n + d log d), where d is the number of distinct values, so O(n log n) worst case. That's actually a fine first answer, but the problem asks for better than n log n.

There are two improvements. A min-heap of size k keeps only the top k seen so far, which is O(n log k). Even better, I can use bucket sort by frequency. A frequency can only be between 1 and n, so I make an array of n + 1 buckets indexed by count and walk it from the top. That's O(n).

static int[] topKFrequent(int[] nums, int k) {
    Map<Integer, Integer> count = new HashMap<>();
    for (int x : nums) count.merge(x, 1, Integer::sum);

    @SuppressWarnings("unchecked")
    List<Integer>[] buckets = new List[nums.length + 1];   // index = frequency
    for (var e : count.entrySet()) {
        int f = e.getValue();
        if (buckets[f] == null) buckets[f] = new ArrayList<>();
        buckets[f].add(e.getKey());
    }

    int[] result = new int[k];
    int idx = 0;
    for (int f = nums.length; f >= 1 && idx < k; f--) {
        if (buckets[f] == null) continue;
        for (int x : buckets[f]) {
            if (idx == k) break;
            result[idx++] = x;
        }
    }
    return result;
}
// [1,1,1,2,2,3], 2 -> [1, 2]     [1], 1 -> [1]

Dry run on the first example: counts are {1:3, 2:2, 3:1}. Buckets: [3] = [1], [2] = [2], [1] = [3]. I walk f from 6 down. Buckets 6, 5 and 4 are empty. At f = 3 I take 1, and at f = 2 I take 2. idx reaches k, so I stop. Result [1, 2].

Final complexity: O(n) time, since counting is O(n) and the bucket walk touches at most n + 1 buckets plus d values. Space is O(n) for the map and buckets. In practice I'd also mention the heap version:

PriorityQueue<Integer> heap = new PriorityQueue<>((a, b) -> count.get(a) - count.get(b));
for (int x : count.keySet()) {
    heap.offer(x);
    if (heap.size() > k) heap.poll();             // drop the least frequent
}

That's O(n log k) and uses only O(k) extra beyond the counts. It's the one I'd pick for a stream where n isn't known up front.

IntermediateLC 207 · Course Schedule

So my clarifying questions. What does a pair [a, b] mean exactly? Here it's "to take a you must first take b", so the edge is b → a. Can there be duplicate pairs, or a course that requires itself? Are courses numbered 0 to n - 1? And I only need a yes or no, not the order, right?

Examples: n = 2, [[1,0]] gives true, since I take 0 and then 1. n = 2, [[1,0],[0,1]] gives false, because they wait on each other. Edge cases are no prerequisites at all, which is true, a self-loop [0,0], which is false, and disconnected groups of courses.

The brute force is to try orderings, or, for every course, DFS to see if it can reach itself. The per-node cycle check is O(V × (V + E)).

The insight is that "can I finish everything?" is the same question as "does the dependency graph have no cycle?", which is "does a topological order exist?" Kahn's algorithm answers it in one pass. I repeatedly take a course with no remaining prerequisites. If I can take all n that way, there's no cycle. Courses on a cycle never reach indegree 0.

static boolean canFinish(int numCourses, int[][] prerequisites) {
    List<List<Integer>> graph = new ArrayList<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numCourses; i++) graph.add(new ArrayList<>());
    int[] indegree = new int[numCourses];
    for (int[] p : prerequisites) {               // {a, b}: take b before a
        graph.get(p[1]).add(p[0]);
        indegree[p[0]]++;
    }
    Deque<Integer> queue = new ArrayDeque<>();
    for (int i = 0; i < numCourses; i++) {
        if (indegree[i] == 0) queue.offer(i);
    }
    int taken = 0;
    while (!queue.isEmpty()) {
        int course = queue.poll();
        taken++;
        for (int next : graph.get(course)) {
            if (--indegree[next] == 0) queue.offer(next);
        }
    }
    return taken == numCourses;
}
// 2, [[1,0]] -> true     2, [[1,0],[0,1]] -> false

Dry run on the first: the graph is 0 → 1, with indegree [0, 1]. The queue starts as [0]. I poll 0, so taken = 1, and 1's indegree drops to 0, so I push it. I poll 1, so taken = 2. 2 == 2, so true.

On the cycle case, the indegrees are [1, 1] and nothing starts in the queue. taken stays 0, so false.

A self-loop gives the course an indegree of 1 from itself, so it never enters the queue. That's handled without special code.

Final complexity: O(V + E) time, since each course is enqueued once and each edge is relaxed once. Space is O(V + E) for the adjacency list. The DFS version with three colours has the same complexity. I pick Kahn's because there's no recursion depth issue, and the follow-up, LC 210 "return the order", is a two-line change: record each polled course.

AdvancedLC 322 · Coin Change

So my clarifying questions. Is the supply of each coin unlimited? Yes, here. What if the amount can't be made? Return -1. Amount 0? That needs 0 coins. How large can the amount be? That decides whether an O(amount) array is OK. It's up to 104 here, so it's fine.

Examples: coins = [1,2,5], amount = 11 gives 3 (5 + 5 + 1). [2], 3 gives -1. [1], 0 gives 0. The edge case I'd bring up myself is [1,3,4], 6. Greedy takes 4 + 1 + 1, which is 3 coins, but the right answer is 3 + 3, which is 2 coins.

That example is why the first idea, greedy by largest coin, is wrong for arbitrary denominations. The real brute force is recursion: minCoins(a) = 1 + min over coins of minCoins(a - coin). Without memo, that's exponential, roughly O(kamount / min coin).

The insight is overlapping subproblems. minCoins(a) only depends on a, and a only takes amount + 1 values. So I fill a table bottom-up, where dp[a] is the fewest coins to make a. Each entry tries every coin as the last coin used.

static int coinChange(int[] coins, int amount) {
    int[] dp = new int[amount + 1];
    Arrays.fill(dp, amount + 1);                  // "infinity": more coins than possible
    dp[0] = 0;
    for (int a = 1; a <= amount; a++) {
        for (int coin : coins) {
            if (coin <= a) {
                dp[a] = Math.min(dp[a], dp[a - coin] + 1);
            }
        }
    }
    return dp[amount] > amount ? -1 : dp[amount];
}
// [1,2,5], 11 -> 3     [2], 3 -> -1     [1], 0 -> 0     [1,3,4], 6 -> 2

Dry run for [1,2,5], 11. The table comes out as dp = [0, 1, 1, 2, 2, 1, 2, 2, 3, 3, 2, 3]. For example, dp[5] = 1, just the 5 coin. dp[10] = dp[5] + 1 = 2. dp[11] = min(dp[10] + 1, dp[9] + 1, dp[6] + 1) = min(3, 4, 3) = 3.

I use amount + 1 as infinity instead of Integer.MAX_VALUE on purpose. MAX_VALUE + 1 overflows to a negative number and would win every min. amount + 1 is safe, because no answer can use more than amount coins, since the smallest coin is 1.

Final complexity: O(amount × k) time for k coins, and O(amount) space. If they asked for the number of ways instead (LC 518), I'd swap the loops, with coins outer, so each combination is counted once and not once per ordering.

AdvancedLC 42 · Trapping Rain Water

So my clarifying questions. Are the heights non-negative integers? Is the width of each bar 1? What's the answer for fewer than 3 bars? It's 0, since there's nothing to hold water. And can the array be empty?

Examples: [0,1,0,2,1,0,1,3,2,1,2,1] gives 6. [4,2,0,3,2,5] gives 9. Edge cases are a strictly increasing or decreasing array, which traps nothing, and an empty array, which gives 0.

The key observation for any approach is that water above bar i is min(tallest to the left, tallest to the right) - height[i]. The lower wall decides the level. The brute force computes both maxima by scanning for every i, which is O(n2).

The first improvement is to precompute leftMax[] and rightMax[] in two passes. That's O(n) time and O(n) space. Then the insight for O(1) space is two pointers. If height[left] < height[right], then there's a wall at least as tall as height[left] on the right. So the water at left is decided only by leftMax, and I can settle it now and move left. The same logic applies from the other side.

static int trap(int[] height) {
    int left = 0, right = height.length - 1;
    int leftMax = 0, rightMax = 0, water = 0;
    while (left < right) {
        if (height[left] < height[right]) {
            leftMax = Math.max(leftMax, height[left]);
            water += leftMax - height[left];      // right side is taller: leftMax decides
            left++;
        } else {
            rightMax = Math.max(rightMax, height[right]);
            water += rightMax - height[right];
            right--;
        }
    }
    return water;
}
// [0,1,0,2,1,0,1,3,2,1,2,1] -> 6     [4,2,0,3,2,5] -> 9     [] -> 0

Dry run on [4,2,0,3,2,5]. left = 0 (4), right = 5 (5). 4 < 5, so leftMax = 4, water += 0, and left moves on. Height 2 < 5: water += 4 - 2 = 2. Height 0: water += 4, total 6. Height 3: water += 1, total 7. Height 2: water += 2, total 9. Then left = right = 5, and I stop. The answer is 9.

I update the max before adding water. That way leftMax - height[left] is never negative: when the current bar is the new max, it just adds 0.

The empty array works because right = -1 and the loop never runs.

Final complexity: O(n) time with one pass, and O(1) space. I'd present the prefix-max version first, since it's easier to prove correct, and then the two-pointer version as the space optimisation. A monotonic stack also solves it in O(n), filling water layer by layer horizontally. I'd mention that as the alternative if they ask.
